Friedman-Ramanujan functions in random hyperbolic geometry and application to spectral gaps II
Nalini Anantharaman 1 and Laura Monk 2

Abstract
. The core focus of this series of two articles is the study of the distribution of the length spectrum of closed hyperbolic surfaces of genus g g , sampled randomly with respect to the Weil–Petersson probability measure. In the first article [ 2 ] , we introduced a notion of local topological type 𝐓 {\mathbf{T}} , and established the existence of a density function V g 𝐓 ​ ( ℓ ) V_{g}^{\mathbf{T}}(\ell) describing the distribution of the lengths of all closed geodesics of type 𝐓 \mathbf{T} in a genus g g hyperbolic surface. We proved that V g 𝐓 ​ ( ℓ ) V_{g}^{\mathbf{T}}(\ell) admits an asymptotic expansion in powers of 1 / g 1/g . We introduced a new class of functions, called Friedman–Ramanujan functions , and related it to the study of the spectral gap λ 1 \lambda_{1} of the Laplacian. In this second part, we provide a variety of new tools allowing to compute and estimate the volume functions V g 𝐓 ​ ( ℓ ) V_{g}^{\mathbf{T}}(\ell) . Notably, we construct new sets of coordinates on Teichmüller spaces, distinct from Fenchel–Nielsen coordinates, in which the Weil–Petersson volume has a simple form. These coordinates are tailored to the geodesics we study, and we can therefore prove nice formulae for their lengths. We use these new ideas, together with a notion of pseudo-convolutions, to prove that the coefficients of the expansion of V g 𝐓 ​ ( ℓ ) V_{g}^{\mathbf{T}}(\ell) in powers of 1 / g 1/g are Friedman–Ramanujan functions, for any local topological type 𝐓 \mathbf{T} . We then exploit this result to prove that, for any ϵ > 0 \epsilon>0 , λ 1 ≥ 1 4 − ϵ \lambda_{1}\geq\frac{1}{4}-\epsilon with probability going to one as g → + ∞ g\rightarrow+\infty , or, in other words, typical hyperbolic surfaces have an asymptotically optimal spectral gap.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 58J50, 32G15; Secondary 05C80, 11F72

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Main results and motivations

For an integer
g\geq 2
, let
S_{g}
be a fixed compact oriented surface of genus
g
with no
boundary. This is the second of a two-part article, studying the distribution of the length spectrum
of a random hyperbolic metric
X
on
S_{g}
. Our probability space is the moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{g}
of
hyperbolic structures on
S_{g}
, endowed with the Weil–Petersson measure
\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}
normalized to be a
probability measure.

1.1.1.
Local topological type and associated density

In the first part
[
2
]
, we defined a notion of
local topological type
for a loop

\mathbf{c}

[
2
, §4]
. Intuitively,
\mathbf{T}
corresponds to the topological datum of a
loop
\mathbf{c}
filling a surface
\mathbf{S}
, so that we know the topology of
\mathbf{c}
and
its regular neighbourhood, but not the way it is embedded in the ambient surface of genus

g
. Examples of local topological types include the type
\mathbf{s}
“simple” (loops with no
self-intersections), or the figure-eight (with exactly one self-intersection). We developed new
tools allowing to study the following averages.

Definition 1.1
.

Let
{\mathbf{T}}=[{\mathbf{S,c}}]_{\mathrm{loc}}
be a local topological type. For any measurable function

F:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
, bounded and compactly supported, we define the

{\mathbf{T}}
-average
of
F
to be

(1.1)

\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{\mathbf{T}}}:=\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\Bigg[\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\mathcal{G}(X)\\
\gamma\in{\mathbf{T}}\end{subarray}}F(\ell_{X}(\gamma))\Bigg]

where
\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}
denotes the expectation on the moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{g}
when the hyperbolic structure

X\in\mathcal{M}_{g}
is chosen randomly according to the normalized Weil–Petersson measure
\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}
, and

•

\mathcal{G}(X)
is the set of primitive oriented closed geodesics for the metric
X
;

•

for any
\gamma\in\mathcal{G}(X)
,
\ell_{X}(\gamma)
is the length of
\gamma
for
the hyperbolic metric
X
;

•

\gamma\in{\mathbf{T}}
means that the sum is restricted to closed geodesics of local
topological type
{\mathbf{T}}
.

Taking
F
to be the indicator function
{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-4.0mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-4.5mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-5.0mu{\rm{l}}}}_{[a,b]}
of an interval
[a,b]
, the average

\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{\mathbf{T}}}
is the expected number of closed primitive geodesics of local topological
type
{\mathbf{T}}
and length in the interval
[a,b]
. In the case of the local topological type
“simple”, denoted as
\mathbf{s}
, the condition
\gamma\in\mathbf{s}
means that the sum is
restricted to all simple primitive closed geodesics.

We proved in
[
2
, Prop. 5.9]
that the averages
\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{\mathbf{T}}}
can be written in an
integral form, against a density function
V_{g}^{\mathbf{T}}(\ell)
which is, informally, the
“proportion” of surfaces
X\in\mathcal{M}_{g}
containing a primitive closed geodesic of length
\ell
and
local topological type
\mathbf{T}
. The formal definition is as follows.

Proposition 1.2
.

For any local topological type
{\mathbf{T}}
, there exists a unique measurable function

V_{g}^{\mathbf{T}}:\mathbb{R}_{>0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
such that, for any admissible test function

F
,

\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{\mathbf{T}}}=\frac{1}{V_{g}}\int_{0}^{+\infty}F(\ell)\,V_{g}^{{\mathbf{T}}}(\ell)\,\mathrm{d}\ell.

The functions
V_{g}^{{\mathbf{T}}}(\ell)
are a fundamental object for the study of geodesics in
random hyperbolic surface. However they have been little studied, except in the case of the local
topological type “simple”. For simple closed geodesics, Mirzakhani showed in
[
8
]

that the functions
V_{g}^{\mathbf{T}}(\ell)
are polynomial in
\ell
(hence they are called

volume polynomials
), and that they can be computed explicitly for all genus
g
by
topological recursion. Otherwise, we call
\ell\mapsto V_{g}^{{\mathbf{T}}}(\ell)
the
volume
function
associated with local topological type
{\mathbf{T}}
on surfaces of genus
g
.

The precise nature of the functions
V_{g}^{\mathbf{T}}
is not known. One can deduce from

[
10
, Theorem 8.1]
that
V_{g}^{{\mathbf{T}}}(\ell)
behaves like a power of
\ell
to
leading order as
\ell\rightarrow+\infty
with
g
fixed. We believe that, for local topological
type other than “simple”, the function
\ell\mapsto V_{g}^{\mathbf{T}}(\ell)
is not polynomial,
but is exponentially close to one as
\ell\rightarrow+\infty
with
g
fixed.

1.1.2.
Large-genus asymptotic expansion

The object of the paper is the large genus behaviour of the volume functions
V_{g}^{\mathbf{T}}
. We
proved in the first article
[
2
]
that they admit expansions to any order in powers of

g^{-1}
, in the following sense.

Theorem 1.3

(
[
2
, Thm. 1.5 and 5.14]
)
.

For any local topological type
{\mathbf{T}}=[{\mathbf{S,c}}]_{\mathrm{loc}}
, there exists a unique
family of measurable functions
(f_{k}^{{\mathbf{T}}})_{k\geq 0}
such that, for any integer

K\geq 0
and
g\geq 2
,

(1.2)

\Big|\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{\mathbf{T}}}-\sum_{k=0}^{K}\frac{1}{g^{k}}\int_{0}^{+\infty}F(\ell)\,f_{k}^{{\mathbf{T}}}(\ell)\,\mathrm{d}\ell\Big|\leq\frac{C_{K,\mathbf{S}}}{g^{K+1}}\sup_{\ell\geq 0}\{(\ell+1)^{\alpha_{K}^{\mathbf{S}}}|F(\ell)|e^{\ell}\}

for any measurable function
F:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
bounded with compact support.

As the notation indicates, the exponents
\alpha_{K}^{\mathbf{S}}
and the constant

C_{K,\mathbf{S}}
depends on the filled surface
{\mathbf{S}}
and the order
K
of the expansion,
but not on the loop
{\mathbf{c}}
filling
\mathbf{S}
. In forthcoming applications of our results
to the spectral gap of the Laplacian, this uniform behaviour with respect to all loops filling the
same surface is important.

1.1.3.
Statement of the main results

The main technical result of this paper may be summarised as follows, answering positively the
question asked in the first part of this article
[
2
]
.

Theorem 1.4
.

Let
{\mathbf{T}}
be a local topological type other than “simple”. For any
k\geq 0
, the
function
\ell\mapsto f_{k}^{{\mathbf{T}}}(\ell)
is a Friedman–Ramanujan function in the weak
sense.

The notion of Friedman–Ramanujan function was introduced in
[
2
]
. A measurable function

f:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is said to be a Friedman–Ramanujan function if there exists a
polynomial function
p
such that
f(\ell)-p(\ell)e^{\ell}
grows at most like a polynomial
multiple of
e^{\ell/2}
. Precise definitions follow in §
1.2
, together with a
quantitative versions of our main result (Theorem
1.16
).

Remark 1.5
.

The case of the local topological type
\mathbf{s}
is excluded because, in this case, the
function
f_{k}^{\mathbf{s}}
have pole of order one at
0
. We prove that

\ell\mapsto\ell f_{k}^{\mathbf{s}}(\ell)
are Friedman–Ramanujan functions
in
[
1
]
(see
[
2
, Proposition 3.3]
for more details).

Theorem
1.4
has already been obtained when
\mathbf{T}
is a figure-eight, or more
generally when the filled surface
\mathbf{S}
has Euler characteristic
-1
, in the first part of
this article
[
2
]
. For this relatively simple topological situation, we could use almost
explicit expressions of the functions
(f_{k}^{{\mathbf{T}}})_{k\geq 0}
.

The present paper deals with general local topological types and uses entirely new methods,
involving finding explicit expressions for the Weil–Petersson measure in coordinates other than the
Fenchel–Nielsen ones, and for the lengths of non-simple geodesics in those coordinates.

This work is largely motivated by the question of estimating the gap at the bottom of the spectrum
of the Laplacian, for typical hyperbolic surfaces, as detailed in
[
2
,
4
]
. In the first
part of this article
[
2
]
, we used the result when
{\mathbf{S}}
has Euler characteristic

-1
to prove that typical surfaces have spectral gap at least
2/9-\epsilon
. More generally, our
theorem (together with the variant presented in §
12
) implies the optimal lower
bound
1/4-\epsilon
on the spectral gap.

Theorem 1.6
.

Denote by
\lambda_{1}(X)
the first non-trivial eigenvalue of Laplacian on
the hyperbolic surface
X
. Then, for any
\epsilon>0
,

(1.3)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\left(\lambda_{1}(X)\geq\frac{1}{4}-\epsilon\right)\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits_{g\rightarrow+\infty}1.

1.2.
Full statement of the Friedman–Ramanujan property

Let us introduce a few definitions, and present our main result more precisely.

1.2.1.
Definitions

We recall from
[
2
]
the
strong
and
weak
definitions of the class of
Friedman–Ramanujan functions, directly inspired by J. Friedman’s seminal work on random graphs

[
6
, Definition 2.1]
where he proves the Alon conjecture.

Definition 1.7
.

Let
\mathrm{N}\geq 1
. We define the class of functions
\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{N}}
as the set of locally integrable
functions
f:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
such that there exists
c>0
satisfying

(1.4)

\forall\ell\geq 0,\quad|f(\ell)|\leq c(\ell+1)^{\mathrm{N}-1}e^{\frac{\ell}{2}}.

We let
\mathcal{R}=\bigcup_{\mathrm{N}\geq 1}\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{N}}
.
A locally integrable function
f:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
is said to be a
Friedman–Ramanujan
function
if there exists a polynomial function
p
such that

f(\ell)-p(\ell)e^{\ell}\in\mathcal{R}.

We denote as
\mathcal{F}
the set of Friedman–Ramanujan function. For
f\in\mathcal{F}
, the function

\ell\mapsto p(\ell)e^{\ell}
is uniquely defined, we call it the
principal term
of

f
.

Notation 1.8
.

For an integer
\mathrm{K}\geq 0
, we denote as
\mathbb{C}_{\mathrm{K}-1}[\ell]
the space of polynomials of degree
strictly less than
\mathrm{K}
, and by
\mathbb{C}_{\mathrm{K}-1}[\ell]\,e^{\ell}
the class of functions of the form

p(\ell)\,e^{\ell}
where
p\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathrm{K}-1}[\ell]
. Note that these sets are reduced to the
constant function equal to
0
if
\mathrm{K}=0
.

Definition 1.9
.

For
\mathrm{K}\geq 0
, we denote as
\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{K}}
the subset of Friedman–Ramanujan functions of principal
term in
\mathbb{C}_{\mathrm{K}-1}[\ell]\,e^{\ell}
, and for
\mathrm{N}\geq 1
,

\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N}}:=\mathbb{C}_{\mathrm{K}-1}[\ell]\,e^{\ell}\oplus\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{N}}
.

Note that the space
\mathcal{R}
can be identified with the subset
\mathcal{F}^{0}
of Friedman–Ramanujan
functions with principal term
p=0
, and similarily
\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{N}}=\mathcal{F}^{0,\mathrm{N}}
.

In our proof of Theorem
1.4
we will rather use the following weak version of the
classes
\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{F}
. Remark that in the work of Friedman, where the functions are defined
on
\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
instead of
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, this distinction between weak and strong definition does not
exist.

Definition 1.10
.

Let
\mathrm{N}\geq 1
. We define the class of functions
\mathcal{R}_{w}^{\mathrm{N}}
as the set of locally
integrable functions
f:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
such that there exists
c>0
satisfying

(1.5)

\displaystyle\forall\ell\geq 0,\quad\int_{0}^{\ell}|f(s)|\,\mathrm{d}s\leq c(\ell+1)^{\mathrm{N}-1}e^{\frac{\ell}{2}}.

We let
\mathcal{R}_{w}=\bigcup_{\mathrm{N}\geq 1}\mathcal{R}_{w}^{\mathrm{N}}
.
A locally integrable function
f:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
is said to be a

Friedman–Ramanujan function in the weak sense
if there exists a polynomial function
p

such that

f(\ell)-p(\ell)e^{\ell}\in\mathcal{R}_{w}.

For
\mathrm{K}\geq 0
, we write
\mathcal{F}_{w}^{\mathrm{K}}:=\mathbb{C}_{\mathrm{K}-1}[\ell]\,e^{\ell}\oplus\mathcal{R}_{w}
and for

\mathrm{N}\geq 1
,
\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N}}_{w}:=\mathbb{C}_{\mathrm{K}-1}[\ell]\,e^{\ell}\oplus\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{N}}_{w}
.

As the name suggests, the strong definition
implies the weak one.

Remark 1.11
.

The property
f\in\mathcal{R}_{w}
is equivalent to the definition given in
[
2
]
, that is,
the existence of
a>0
such that

(1.6)

\int_{0}^{+\infty}\frac{|f(\ell)|}{(\ell+1)^{a}e^{\frac{\ell}{2}}}\,\mathrm{d}\ell<+\infty.

More precisely, if
f\in\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{N}}_{w}
, then (
1.6
) holds for any exponent

a>\mathrm{N}
. Conversely if (
1.6
) holds, then
f\in\mathcal{R}^{a+1}_{w}
.

1.2.2.
Norms on the set of Friedman–Ramanujan functions

Let us define a norm on the spaces
\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N}}
and
\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N}}_{w}
, which captures the size
of the principal term and the remainder of the Friedman–Ramanujan function.

Notation 1.12
.

For a polynomial function
p\in\mathbb{C}[\ell]
, we define
\|p\|_{\ell^{\infty}}
to be the maximum modulus
of the coefficients of
p
.

Definition 1.13
.

For
\mathrm{N}\geq 1
, we define a norm on
\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{N}}
and
\mathcal{R}_{w}^{\mathrm{N}}
by

\displaystyle\lVert f\rVert_{\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{N}}}:=\sup_{\ell\geq 0}\frac{|f(\ell)|}{(\ell+1)^{\mathrm{N}-1}e^{\frac{\ell}{2}}}\quad\text{and}\quad\lVert f\rVert^{w}_{\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{N}}}:=\sup_{\ell\geq 0}\frac{\int_{0}^{\ell}|f(s)|\,\mathrm{d}s}{(\ell+1)^{\mathrm{N}-1}\,e^{\frac{\ell}{2}}}.

If now
\mathrm{K}\geq 0
, we equip
\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N}}
with the norm

\|f\|_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N}}}:=\|p\|_{\ell^{\infty}}+\|f(\ell)-p(\ell)e^{\ell}\|_{\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{N}}}

where
p(\ell)\,e^{\ell}
is the principal term of
f
. The norm
\|f\|_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N}}}^{w}
is defined
the same way replacing the last norm by its weak version.

It will be convenient to adopt the following convention.

Notation 1.14
.

We denote as
\mathrm{fn}((u_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{V}})
any quantity that is a
function solely of some set of variables
(u_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{V}}
(but for which we do not need
to write an explicit expression).

Remark 1.15
.

By definition, if
f\in\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{N}}
, then for all
\ell\geq 0
,

(1.7)

\displaystyle|f(\ell)|\leq\lVert f\rVert_{\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{N}}}\,(1+\ell)^{\mathrm{N}-1}e^{\ell/2}.

If we rather assume that
f\in\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N}}
, then for
\ell\geq 0
,

(1.8)

\displaystyle|f(\ell)|\leq\mathrm{fn}(\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N})\lVert f\rVert_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N}}}\,(1+\ell)^{\mathrm{K}-1}e^{\ell}

for some constant
\mathrm{fn}(\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N}):=1+\sup_{\ell\geq 0}(1+\ell)^{\mathrm{N}-\mathrm{K}}e^{-\ell/2}
.

1.2.3.
Precise statement

The main technical result of this paper is the following precised version of Theorem
1.4
.

Theorem 1.16
.

Let
{\mathbf{T}}=[{\mathbf{S,c}}]_{\mathrm{loc}}
be a local type other than “simple”. Then, for any

k\geq 0
, there exist
\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N}
and
C
, depending on
k
and
{\mathbf{S}}
but not on

{\mathbf{c}}
, such that
f_{k}^{{\mathbf{T}}}\in\mathcal{F}_{w}^{\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N}}
and

\lVert f_{k}^{{\mathbf{T}}}\rVert_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N}}}^{w}\leq C
.

Notation 1.17
.

We denote as
(g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}})
the signature of
\mathbf{S}
, and
\chi(\mathbf{S}):=2g_{\mathbf{S}}-2+n_{\mathbf{S}}
its
absolute Euler characteristic. We denote as
\partial\mathbf{S}
the set of boundary components
of
\mathbf{S}
, which is identified with
\{1,\ldots,n_{\mathbf{S}}\}
through the labelling of the
boundary of
\mathbf{S}
.

Remark 1.18
.

The proof gives a seemingly optimal estimate on
\mathrm{K}
, and a non-optimal estimate on
\mathrm{N}
and

C
: we may take
\mathrm{K}=2k+2\chi(\mathbf{S})
and
\mathrm{N}=n_{\mathbf{S}}(a_{k+1}+1)
, where
a_{k+1}
is an explicit
sequence coming from
[
1
]
which is a priori not optimal either. Remark

1.25
show how we may optimise further the value of
\mathrm{K}
, depending on the
topology of the multi-loop
\mathbf{c}
.

1.3.
Outline of the proof

Let
{\mathbf{T}}=[{\mathbf{S,c}}]_{\mathrm{loc}}
be a local topological type, i.e. the topological datum
of a loop
\mathbf{c}
filling a surface
\mathbf{S}
. We present prior results on the volume
function
V_{g}^{\mathbf{T}}
and the coefficients
(f_{k}^{\mathbf{T}})_{k\geq 0}
of its asymptotic
expansion in powers of
1/g
. We then explain the key ideas behind the proof of the main theorem.

1.3.1.
Teichmüller spaces and the Weil–Petersson measure

For
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n_{\mathbf{S}}})\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{n_{\mathbf{S}}}
, we denote as

\mathcal{T}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}(\mathbf{x})
the Teichmüller space of hyperbolic surfaces of genus
g_{\mathbf{S}}
with
n_{\mathbf{S}}

labelled geodesic boundary components of respective lengths
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n_{\mathbf{S}}}
. This space is a
real-analytic manifold of dimension
6g_{\mathbf{S}}-6+2n_{\mathbf{S}}
. It is equipped with the
Weil–Petersson
symplectic form
, which induces a volume that we call the
Weil–Petersson measure
and
denote as
\mathrm{Vol}^{\mathrm{WP}}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}},\mathbf{x}}
.

In this article, it will be more interesting to view the boundary lengths as free variables, and
hence introduce the Teichmüller space

\mathcal{T}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}^{*}=\{(\mathbf{x},Y)\,:\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{n_{\mathbf{S}}},Y\in\mathcal{T}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}(\mathbf{x})\},

which is the space of compact hyperbolic metrics on the surface
\mathbf{S}
. The space

\mathcal{T}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}^{*}
is a real-analytic manifold of dimension
6g_{\mathbf{S}}-6+3n_{\mathbf{S}}=3\chi(\mathbf{S})
. It
may be endowed with the positive measure

\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}^{\mathrm{WP}}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}(\mathbf{x},Y):=\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}^{\mathrm{WP}}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}(Y)
,
where
\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}
is the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{n_{\mathbf{S}}}
, which we also call
Weil–Petersson
measure
. We will sometimes omit the mention of
\mathbf{x}
and simply write
Y\in\mathcal{T}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}^{*}
when it
is not useful to emphasize the boundary length of
Y
.

1.3.2.
Level-set desintegration

The map
\mathcal{T}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}^{*}\ni Y\mapsto\ell_{Y}({\mathbf{c}})
is a
non-constant analytic function on the Teichmüller space
\mathcal{T}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}^{*}
. We can therefore
desintegrate the Weil–Petersson measure
\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}^{\mathrm{WP}}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}
on its
level-sets. This yields a measure
\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}^{\mathrm{WP}}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}(Y)/\,\mathrm{d}\ell
defined by the
property that, for any integrable function
F:\mathcal{T}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}^{*}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{T}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}^{*}}F(Y)\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}^{\mathrm{WP}}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}(Y)=\int_{0}^{+\infty}\Big(\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell_{Y}({\mathbf{c}})=\ell\end{subarray}}F(Y)\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}^{\mathrm{WP}}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}(Y)}{\,\mathrm{d}\ell}\Big)\,\mathrm{d}\ell.

In particular, if
F(\mathbf{x},Y)=\tilde{F}(\ell_{Y}({\mathbf{c}}))\phi(\mathbf{x})
is a multiplicative function
of
\ell_{Y}({\mathbf{c}})
and
\mathbf{x}
, this equation takes the special form

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{T}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}^{*}}\tilde{F}(\ell_{Y}({\mathbf{c}}))\phi(\mathbf{x})\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}^{\mathrm{WP}}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}(\mathbf{x},Y)=\int_{0}^{+\infty}\tilde{F}(\ell)\Big(\int_{\ell_{Y}({\mathbf{c}})=\ell}\phi(\mathbf{x})\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}^{\mathrm{WP}}_{g,n}(\mathbf{x},Y)}{\,\mathrm{d}\ell}\Big)\,\mathrm{d}\ell.

1.3.3.
Description of the volume function and coefficients

We have proven in
[
2
]
that the volume function
V_{g}^{{\mathbf{T}}}(\ell)
and the
coefficients
f_{k}^{{\mathbf{T}}}(\ell)
are all of the form

(1.9)

\displaystyle\ell\mapsto\int_{\ell_{Y}({\mathbf{c}})=\ell}\phi(\mathbf{x})\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}^{\mathrm{WP}}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}(\mathbf{x},Y)}{\,\mathrm{d}\ell},

with particular choices of
\phi(\mathbf{x})
.

More precisely, the volume function
V_{g}^{{\mathbf{T}}}(\ell)
is obtained by replacing
\phi
with
the following explicit polynomial function:

(1.10)

\displaystyle\Phi_{g}^{\mathbf{T}}(\mathbf{x}):=\frac{1}{m(\mathbf{T})}\frac{x_{1}\ldots x_{n_{\mathbf{S}}}}{V_{g}}\sum_{\mathfrak{R}\in R_{g}({\mathbf{S}})}V_{\mathfrak{R}}(\mathbf{x}),

which enumerates all possible embeddings of
\mathbf{S}
in a surface of genus
g
(the coefficient

m(\mathbf{T})
is a combinatorial factor, and
V_{\mathfrak{R}}(\mathbf{x})
are products of volumes of moduli spaces,
enumerated by configurations
{\mathfrak{R}}\in R_{g}(\mathbf{S})
).

From this expression, the functions
f_{k}^{{\mathbf{T}}}(\ell)
are then obtained by performing the
asymptotic expansion of
\Phi_{g}^{\mathbf{T}}(\mathbf{x})
in powers of
1/g
, using the results of

[
1
,
2
]
. It follows that
f_{k}^{{\mathbf{T}}}(\ell)
has the form
(
1.9
) for a
\phi(\mathbf{x})
which is a linear combination of functions

(1.11)

\prod_{i\in V_{0}}x_{i}^{k_{i}}\prod_{i\in V_{+}}x_{i}^{k_{i}}\cosh\left(\frac{x_{i}}{2}\right)\prod_{i\in V_{-}}x_{i}^{k_{i}}\sinh\left(\frac{x_{i}}{2}\right)\prod_{j=1}^{k}x_{i_{j}}\delta(x_{i_{j}}-x_{i_{j}^{\prime}}),

where:

•

V_{0}
,
V_{+}
,
V_{-}
are three disjoint subsets of
\partial\mathbf{S}
;

•

\mathrm{m}=\bigsqcup_{j=1}^{k}\{i_{j},i_{j}^{\prime}\}
is a perfect matching of

\partial\mathbf{S}\setminus(V_{0}\sqcup V_{+}\sqcup V_{-})
;

•

\delta
is the usual Dirac mass.

The degree
k_{i}
is odd if
i\in V_{0}\cup V_{+}
and even if
i\in V_{-}
. In
[
1
]

we proved
1
1

1

It is shown in
[
1
, §5.4]
that

\sum_{i\in V_{+}\cup V_{-}}k_{i}\leq 2k
. There is an imprecision in
[
1
, §5.4]

in the estimate of
\sum_{i\in V_{+}\cup V_{-}}m_{i}
because
m_{i}
is either
k_{i}
or
k_{i}-1

depending on the parity of
k_{i}
, but this has no impact on the original estimate of

\sum_{i\in V_{+}\cup V_{-}}k_{i}
.
that
\sum_{i\in V_{+}\cup V_{-}}k_{i}\leq 2k
, and that for

i\in V_{0}
, we have
k_{i}\leq a_{k+1}
for a certain sequence
a_{k+1}
(which is explicit but a
priori not optimal).

1.3.4.
Brief reminder about the method followed in
[
1
,
2
]

It is worth now recalling the main steps in the proof of the asymptotic expansion of

\Phi_{g}^{\mathbf{T}}(\mathbf{x})
.

Notation 1.19
.

Throughout this article, we shall use the Landau notation
\mathcal{O}
, i.e. we will write
A=\mathcal{O}\left(B\right)
if
there exists a constant
c>0
such that, for any choice of parameters,
|A|\leq cB
. If the
constant
c
depends on some parameters, we shall write them as a subscript of the
\mathcal{O}
.

•

First, we note that, although the cardinality of
R_{g}({\mathbf{S}})
in (
1.10
)
grows with
g
, we can identify a set of embeddings
R_{g}({\mathbf{S}},K)
of bounded cardinality,
such that

\displaystyle\Phi_{g}^{\mathbf{T}}(\mathbf{x})=\frac{1}{m(\mathbf{T})}\frac{x_{1}\ldots x_{n_{\mathbf{S}}}}{V_{g}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{R}}\in R_{g}({\mathbf{S}},K)}V_{\mathfrak{R}}(\mathbf{x})+\mathcal{O}_{K,n_{\mathbf{S}}}\left(\frac{(\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert+1)^{\alpha_{K}^{\mathbf{S}}}e^{\frac{x_{1}+\ldots+x_{n_{\mathbf{S}}}}{2}}}{g^{K+1}}\right).

This is done in
[
2
, Proposition 5.21]
and its proof.

•

Then, for each realisation
{\mathfrak{R}}\in R_{g}({\mathbf{S}},K)
, we observe that there exist integers

n_{1}\leq n_{\mathbf{S}}
and
m\leq(K+n_{\mathbf{S}})/2
such that the ratio
V_{\mathfrak{R}}(\mathbf{x})/V_{\mathfrak{R}}(0)
coincides (modulo
permutation of the variables) with
V_{g-m,n_{1}}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n_{1}})/V_{g-m,n_{1}}

multiplied by a fixed polynomial in
(x_{n_{1}+1},\ldots,x_{n_{\mathbf{S}}})
. We recall that, for any

g,n
with
2g-2+n>0
,
V_{g,n}
denotes the constant coefficient of the volume polynomial

V_{g,n}(\cdot)
, i.e. its value at
(0,\ldots,0)
.

•

The main result of
[
1
]
says that

V_{g-m,n_{1}}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n_{1}})/V_{g-m,n_{1}}
coincides, modulo a remainder in

\mathcal{O}_{K,n_{1}}\left((\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert+1)^{\alpha_{K}^{n_{1}}}e^{\frac{x_{1}+\ldots+x_{n_{1}}}{2}}/g^{K+1}\right)
, with
a linear combination of the functions (
1.11
), with degree
k_{i}\leq a_{K+1}
, the
coefficients being of the form

A_{V_{0},V_{+},V_{-}}(n_{1},m,K,k_{1},\ldots,k_{n_{1}})\,\,\frac{c_{g-m,n_{1}}(\alpha)}{V_{g-m,n_{1}}}

and
\|\alpha\|_{\infty}\leq 2K+a_{K+1}
. Here
a_{K+1}
is a sequence introduced in
[
2
]
, and

c_{g-m,n_{1}}(\alpha)
is our notation for the coefficients of volume polynomials.

•

At the end we inject the result of Mirzakhani–Zograf
[
11
, Theorem 4.1]
, showing
the existence of an asymptotic expansion in powers of
g^{-1}
for

c_{g-m,n_{1}}(\alpha)/V_{g-m,n_{1}}
and for
V_{\mathfrak{R}}(0)/V_{g}
, and hence for

c_{g-m,n_{1}}(\alpha)/V_{g}
.

Remark 1.20
.

The last step is only necessary if we insist on obtaining an expansion
V_{g}^{{\mathbf{T}}}(\ell)
in powers of
g^{-1}
. The three first steps, together with the fact that
\frac{c_{g-m,n_{1}}(\alpha)}{V_{g-m,n_{1}}}
and
\frac{V_{\mathfrak{R}}(0)}{V_{g}}
are bounded functions of
g
, are actually sufficient to obtain the spectral gap (Theorem
1.6
).

Until §
12
, the surface
\mathbf{S}
is fixed and we do not need to know how

A_{V_{0},V_{+},V_{-}}(n_{1},m,K,k_{1},\ldots,k_{n_{1}})
depend on
n_{1}
. In §
12
, we
will need to come back to the proof of the asymptotic expansion, in order to have a very rough
control of the dependency on
n_{1},n_{\mathbf{S}}
of those coefficients, as well as the constants involved in

\mathcal{O}_{K,n_{\mathbf{S}}}{}
and
\mathcal{O}_{K,n_{1}}{}
.

1.3.5.
Unshielded simple portions

The following topological quantity, associated to a local topological type, will appear in our
abstract statement.

Definition 1.21
.

Let
\mathbf{T}=[\mathbf{S},\mathbf{c}]_{\mathrm{loc}}
be a local topological type.

(1)

A
simple portion
of
\mathbf{c}
is a maximal open subsegment of
\mathbf{c}
which
does not contain any self-intersection point of
\mathbf{c}
.

(2)

A simple portion is said to be
shielded
if it belongs to the boundary of a contractible
component of
\mathbf{S}\setminus\mathbf{c}
, and
unshielded
otherwise.

(3)

We say
\mathbf{c}
is
double-filling
if all simple portions are
shielded.

We denote by
\rho[{\mathbf{T}}]
the number of unshielded simple portions of
\mathbf{T}
.

The notion of simple portion and double-filling multi-loop appeared in our previous paper

[
2
, Notation 8.3]
. We have the inequality
\rho[{\mathbf{T}}]\leq 2\chi(\mathbf{S})
, which means
that the number of unshielded simple portions is bounded uniformly in
\mathbf{c}
.

1.3.6.
Main abstract statement

We can use (
1.11
) to reduce the proof of Theorem
1.16
to a general
abstract statement, which we shall now state and comment. Because our main theorem requires quite
a few notation, we group them below.

Notation 1.22

(Assumptions for main theorem)
.

•

Let
\mathbf{T}=[\mathbf{S},\mathbf{c}]_{\mathrm{loc}}
be a local type of signature
(g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}})
. We
denote as
\rho[{\mathbf{T}}]
be the number of unshielded simple portions of
{\mathbf{T}}
.

•

Let
V
be a subset of
\partial\mathbf{S}
such that
n_{\mathbf{S}}-\#V=2k
is even, and
\mathrm{m}

be a perfect matching of
\partial\mathbf{S}\setminus V
, that is, a partition

\{1,\ldots,n_{\mathbf{S}}\}\setminus V=\bigsqcup_{j=1}^{k}\{i_{j},i_{j}^{\prime}\}
.

•

We fix a smooth function
\chi_{\textrm{z}}
that vanishes at order
1
at
x=0
and is constant equal
to
1
on
[1,+\infty)
, and such that

(1.12)

\Big|\frac{\chi_{\textrm{z}}(y)^{2}}{(1-e^{-y})^{2}}-1\Big|\leq 8e^{-y}.

•

For
j\in V
, let
f_{j}
be a function such that

\tilde{g}_{j}(x):=f_{j}(x)\sinh\left(\frac{x}{2}\right)/\chi_{\textrm{z}}(x)^{2}
is continuous and lies in
\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{K}_{j},\mathrm{N}_{j}}

for some integers
\mathrm{K}_{j}
and
\mathrm{N}_{j}
. We assume w.l.o.g. that
\mathrm{K}_{j}\leq\mathrm{N}_{j}
. We set

\mathrm{K}=\sum_{j\in V}(\mathrm{K}_{j}-1)+\rho[\mathbf{T}]\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathrm{N}=\sum_{j\in V}\mathrm{N}_{j}+3\chi(\mathbf{S})+1.

Theorem 1.23
.

With the assumptions from Notation
1.22
, the function

(1.13)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}=\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{T},V,\mathrm{m},f}:\ell\mapsto\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathbf{x},Y)\in\mathcal{T}^{*}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}\\
\ell_{Y}({\mathbf{c}})=\ell\end{subarray}}\,\,\prod_{j\in V}f_{j}(x_{j})\prod_{j=1}^{k}x_{i_{j}}\delta(x_{i_{j}}-x_{i_{j}^{\prime}})\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}^{\mathrm{WP}}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}(\mathbf{x},Y)}{\,\mathrm{d}\ell}

is a Friedman–Ramanujan function in the weak sense.
More precisely,
\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{F}_{w}^{\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N}}
and

(1.14)

\displaystyle\lVert\mathcal{J}\rVert^{w}_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N}}}\leq\mathrm{fn}(g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}},(\mathrm{K}_{j},\mathrm{N}_{j})_{j\in V})\,\prod_{j\in V}\lVert\tilde{g}_{j}\rVert_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{K}_{j},\mathrm{N}_{j}}}.

Remark 1.24
.

Since
\rho[{\mathbf{T}}]\leq 2\chi(\mathbf{S})
, our Friedman–Ramanujan exponents
\mathrm{K}
and
\mathrm{N}
can
be made uniform amongst all loops filling
\mathbf{S}
.

Remark 1.25
.

In the proof of Theorem
1.23
, we have tried to optimise the value of
\mathrm{K}
, but not the
value of
\mathrm{N}
. One can further improve the value of
\mathrm{K}
depending on the loop
\mathbf{c}
,
and prove that it can be taken to be
\sum_{j\in V^{\prime}}(\mathrm{K}_{j}-1)+\rho[\mathbf{T}]
, where

V^{\prime}\subseteq V
is the set of boundary components containing unshielded simple portions of

\mathbf{c}
. See details in Remark
11.24
.

We notice that in the special case when the loop
\mathbf{c}
is double-filling, we have

V^{\prime}=\emptyset
and
\rho[\mathbf{T}]=0
. In other words, the function
\mathcal{J}
is actually an
element of
\mathcal{R}_{w}
as soon as
\mathbf{c}
is double-filling.

Let us now show how to go from Theorem
1.23
to Theorem
1.16
.

Proof of Theorem
1.16
.

We simply use the expression
(
1.11
) for the functions
f_{k}^{\mathbf{T}}
, and apply our main technical result to
V=V_{0}\sqcup V_{+}\sqcup V_{-}
and the functions

f_{j}(x)=\begin{cases}x_{j}^{k_{j}}&\text{if }j\in V_{0}\\
x_{j}^{k_{j}}\cosh\left(\frac{x_{j}}{2}\right)&\text{if }j\in V_{+}\\
x_{j}^{k_{j}}\sinh\left(\frac{x_{j}}{2}\right)&\text{if }j\in V_{-}\end{cases}

which clearly satisfy the hypotheses with

\begin{cases}\mathrm{K}_{j}=\mathrm{N}_{j}=k_{j}+1&\text{if }j\in V_{+}\cup V_{-}\\
\mathrm{K}_{j}=0,\mathrm{N}_{j}=a_{k+1}+1&\text{if }j\in V_{0},\end{cases}

using the parity condition on the integers
k_{j}
to obtain the needed
cancellation at
0
.
∎

Remark 1.26
.

The proof above allows us to see that, in Theorem
1.16
, we may take the
Friedman–Ramanujan parameters to be

\displaystyle\mathrm{K}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in V_{+}\cup V_{-}}k_{j}+\rho[{\mathbf{T}}]\leq 2k+\rho[{\mathbf{T}}]\leq 2k+2\chi(\mathbf{S})

\displaystyle\mathrm{N}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in V_{0}\cup V_{+}\cup V_{-}}\mathrm{N}_{j}+3\chi(\mathbf{S})+1\leq n_{\mathbf{S}}(a_{k+1}+1)+3\chi(\mathbf{S})+1.

The proof of Theorem
1.6
about the spectral gap of random surfaces requires variants of Theorem
1.23
.
A first straightforward variant is that Theorem
1.23
also applies in the case where
\mathbf{c}
is a multi-loop, defined as a collection of loops (§
2.1.1
).
Another variant will be given in Theorem
9.4
(where some of the loops in
\mathbf{c}
are constrained to have bounded lengths).
The final variant needed for the spectral gap is described in §
12
.

1.3.7.
Friedman–Ramanujan functions as solutions of an integral equation

A first ingredient in the proof of Theorem
1.23
is the fact that Friedman–Ramanujan
functions are fully characterised as solutions (modulo
\mathcal{R}
) of a certain integral equation. We
define two operators acting on locally integrable functions,
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{P}_{x}=\int_{0}^{x}
(taking the
primitive vanishing at
0
) and
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}_{x}=\Id-\mathcal{P}_{x}
(where
\Id
stands for the identity
operator). It is trivial, nevertheless useful, to note that
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{L}
preserve

\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F}_{w},\mathcal{R},\mathcal{R}_{w}
. More precisely,
\mathcal{L}
is a continuous operator from
\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{K}}
to

\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{K}-1}
and from
\mathcal{R}
to itself (and this is also true for the weak spaces).

We will make use of the following characterization of Friedman–Ramanujan functions:

Proposition 1.27
.

For
\mathrm{K}\geq 1
, any locally integrable function
f
,

\begin{cases}f\in\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{K}}\Leftrightarrow\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{K}}f\in\mathcal{R}\\
f\in\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{K}}_{w}\Leftrightarrow\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{K}}f\in\mathcal{R}_{w}.\end{cases}

Furthermore, if
\mathrm{N}\geq 1
is so that
\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{K}}f\in\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{N}}_{w}
, then

(1.15)

\displaystyle\lVert f\rVert_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N}}}^{w}\leq\mathrm{fn}(\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N})\,\lVert\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{K}}f\rVert^{w}_{\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{N}}}.

The “only if” part is a straightforward consequence of the fact that
\mathcal{L}
sends
\mathcal{R}_{w}
to
itself,
\mathcal{R}
to itself, and that
\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{K}}
sends
\mathbb{C}_{\mathrm{K}-1}[t]e^{t}
to

\mathbb{C}_{\mathrm{K}-1}[t]\subset\mathcal{R}
. The “if” part is proven by induction on
\mathrm{K}
, iterating the
following lemma:

Lemma 1.28
.

Let
\mathrm{K}\geq 1
be an integer. If
\mathcal{L}f=b+c
, with
b\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathrm{K}-1}[t]e^{t}
and

c\in\mathcal{R}
or
\mathcal{R}_{w}
, then there exists a constant
C
such that

(1.16)

f(t)=Ce^{t}+b(t)+c(t)+\int_{0}^{t}b(s)e^{t-s}\,\mathrm{d}s+\int_{t}^{+\infty}c(s)e^{t-s}\,\mathrm{d}s.

As a consequence, if
c\in\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{N}}_{w}
, then
f
belongs in
\mathcal{F}_{w}^{\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N}}
and

(1.17)

\displaystyle\lVert f\rVert_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N}}_{w}}\leq\mathrm{fn}(\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N})\,(\lVert b\rVert_{\mathcal{F}}+\lVert c\rVert_{\mathcal{R}_{w}^{\mathrm{N}}}).

The same holds with the strong forms.

Proof.

The lemma is proven by the usual method for solving linear ODEs : let
F=\mathcal{P}f
, then
F
solves

F^{\prime}-F=b+c
, so there exists a constant
C
such that

F(t)=Ce^{t}+\int_{0}^{t}b(s)e^{t-s}\,\mathrm{d}s+\int_{t}^{+\infty}c(s)e^{t-s}\,\mathrm{d}s.

Here, because of the different
behaviours of
b
and
c
at infinity, we have chosen different boundary conditions for the
primitives of
b(s)e^{-s}
and
c(s)e^{-s}.
Since
f
is the derivative of
F
, this
implies (
1.16
). Assume that
c\in\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{N}}_{w}
: then

\int_{0}^{t}b(s)e^{t-s}\,\mathrm{d}s\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathrm{K}-1}[t]e^{t}
,
\int_{t}^{+\infty}c(s)e^{t-s}\,\mathrm{d}s\in\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{N}}
,
so
f\in\mathcal{F}_{w}^{\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N}}
, and

\displaystyle\lVert f\rVert^{w}_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N}}_{w}}\leq\mathrm{fn}(\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N})\,\Big(\lVert c\rVert^{w}_{\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{N}}}+|C|+\lVert b\rVert_{\mathcal{F}}\Big).

Evaluation at
t=0
yields
C=-\int_{0}^{+\infty}c(s)e^{-s}\,\mathrm{d}s
so

|C|\leq\left(\int_{0}^{+\infty}(1+s)^{\mathrm{N}-1}e^{-s/2}\,\mathrm{d}s\right)\lVert c\rVert^{w}_{\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{N}}}.

Inequality (
1.17
) follows.
∎

Remark 1.29
.

We can extend Remark
1.15
to the images of a Friedman–Ramanujan function
f\in\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N}}
by
\mathcal{L}
and obtain a constant
M_{\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N}}
such that, for all
\ell\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}

and any
0\leq m<\mathrm{K}
,

\displaystyle|\mathcal{L}^{m}f(\ell)|\leq M_{\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N}}\|f\|_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N}}}(1+\ell)^{\mathrm{K}-1}e^{\ell}

\displaystyle|\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{K}}f(\ell)|\leq M_{\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N}}\|f\|_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N}}}(1+\ell)^{\mathrm{N}-1}e^{\frac{\ell}{2}}.

Here

M_{\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N}}=\max_{m\leq\mathrm{K}}(\mathrm{fn}(\mathrm{K}-m,\mathrm{N})\lVert\mathcal{L}^{m}\rVert_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N}}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{K}-m,\mathrm{N}}})
with
\mathrm{fn}(\mathrm{K}-m,\mathrm{N})
the constant in (
1.8
).

1.3.8.
Stability under convolution

Another idea behind our proof is the stability of the classes
\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F}_{w},\mathcal{R},\mathcal{R}_{w}
under convolution.
This was proven in
[
2
, Proposition 3.5]
by direct algebraic calculation, following
[
6
, Theorem 7.2]
. Another way to understand this is to refer to the usual property of convolution (classically known for differential operators, but valid also for the operator
\mathcal{L}
): given two functions
f_{1},f_{2}
and integers
\mathrm{K}_{1},\mathrm{K}_{2}
, we have

(1.18)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{K}_{1}+\mathrm{K}_{2}}(f_{1}*f_{2})=\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{K}_{1}}f_{1}*\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{K}_{2}}f_{2}.

If
f_{i}\in\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{K}_{i}}
, then
\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{K}_{i}}f_{i}\in\mathcal{R}
. Thus, using the stability
of
\mathcal{R}
under convolution (a straightforward upper bound), we obtain that
\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{K}_{1}+\mathrm{K}_{2}}(f_{1}*f_{2})\in\mathcal{R}
, implying

f_{1}*f_{2}\in\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{K}_{1}+\mathrm{K}_{2}}
. The same method holds with
\mathcal{F}_{w}
instead of
\mathcal{F}
.

In the proof of Theorem
1.23
, we show that the function
\mathcal{J}
defined by the integral
(
1.13
) can be expressed in terms of “convolutions” of Friedman–Ramanujan functions. This
requires to express the measure
\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}^{\mathrm{WP}}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}(Y)
in a well chosen set of coordinates on

\mathcal{T}^{*}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}
, adapted to the loop
{\mathbf{c}}
. These
coordinates are not the well known Fenchel–Nielsen coordinates: their description will be the focus
of §
3
.

The reason why (
1.13
) looks roughly like a convolution is that the loop
{\mathbf{c}}

can be decomposed into a union of simple curves
\beta=(\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{n})
, by “opening”
all the intersections (this operation is described in §
2.2
). In the case of
curvature
-\infty
(i.e. graphs) we would have

\ell_{Y}(\mathbf{c})=\ell_{Y}(\beta_{1})+\ldots+\ell_{Y}(\beta_{n}),

and (
1.13
) would
exactly be a convolution. But we are in finite negative curvature, and
\ell_{Y}(\mathbf{c})
differs
from
\ell_{Y}(\beta_{1})+\ldots+\ell_{Y}(\beta_{n})
: the expression (
1.13
) is not exactly a
convolution. We will nevertheless see that
\ell_{Y}(\mathbf{c})
is close to

\ell_{Y}(\beta_{1})+\ldots+\ell_{Y}(\beta_{n})
in a certain topology. In §
6
, we define a
notion of
pseudo convolutions
and we develop a sophisticated variant of (
1.18
),
first in an abstract setting – with the aim of later applying it to the study of the integral
(
1.13
).

1.3.9.
Geometric comparison estimates

A key ingredient of the proof of Theorem
1.23
is the use of geometric comparison estimates,
which we shall now comment on.

The following straightforward bound, comparing the length of a loop
\mathbf{c}
and the boundary of
the surface
\mathbf{S}
it fills, is a simple case of a geometric comparison estimate.

Proposition 1.30
.

Let
\mathbf{T}=[\mathbf{S},\mathbf{c}]_{\mathrm{loc}}
be a local type. For any
(\mathbf{x},Y)\in\mathcal{T}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}^{*}
,

(1.19)

\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\mathbf{S}}}x_{i}\leq\ell(\mathbf{c})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{I}\text{ unshielded}\\
\text{simple
portion}\end{subarray}}\ell(\mathcal{I})\leq 2\ell(\mathbf{c}).

The estimate
\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\mathbf{S}}}x_{i}\leq 2\ell(\mathbf{c})
is a classical consequence of the fact
that
\mathbf{c}
is assumed to fill the surface
\mathbf{S}
, see e.g.
[
2
, Lemma 4.4]
. In the special case of double-filling loops, Proposition
1.30
tells us that
this can be improved by dropping the factor of
2
, which was the crucial observation made in

[
2
, Lemma 8.7]
. Proposition
1.30
is a straightforward generalisation of
this lemma.

Comparison estimates are a crucial step in the proof of our main results: they give upper bounds for
terms such as
\prod_{j\in V}f_{j}(x_{j})
, appearing in the definition of
\mathcal{J}(\ell)
in Theorem

1.23
. Proposition
1.30
also holds for multi-loops, which will be defined as
collections of loops (§
2.1.1
).

The advantage of gaining a factor of
2
in the comparison estimate can clearly be seen from the
statement of Theorem
1.23
: the function
\prod_{j\in V}f_{j}(x_{j})
grows like

\exp(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j\in V}x_{j})
(up to some polynomial factors, allowed in the definition of
Friedman–Ramanujan functions), so the bound
\sum_{j=1}^{n_{\mathbf{S}}}x_{j}\leq 2\ell(\mathbf{c})
implies
the upper bound

(1.20)

\displaystyle\prod_{j\in V}|f_{j}(x_{j})|\leq C(1+\ell(\mathbf{c}))^{\alpha}e^{\ell(\mathbf{c})}

for some
C,\alpha\geq 0
. This ensures that
|\mathcal{J}(\ell)|\leq C^{\prime}(1+\ell)^{\alpha^{\prime}}e^{\ell}
in a
weak sense (see (
3.28
) for a precise bound). However, the Friedman–Ramanujan property
requires an
exact expansion
rather than an exponential upper bound. Therefore, we cannot
directly deduce the Friedman–Ramanujan property from the trivial inequality

\sum_{j=1}^{n_{\mathbf{S}}}x_{j}\leq 2\ell(\mathbf{c})
.

The fundamental challenge adressed in this article consists in refining the approach to study

\mathcal{J}(\ell)
, hence providing an exact expansion instead of the straightforward upper bound above.

In the special case when the loop
\mathbf{c}
is double-filling, then (
1.20
) may be
replaced by

(1.21)

\displaystyle\prod_{j\in V}|f_{j}(x_{j})|\leq(1+\ell(\mathbf{c}))^{\alpha}e^{\frac{\ell(\mathbf{c})}{2}},

which is enough to ensure that
\mathcal{J}(\ell)
is in the class
\mathcal{R}_{w}
, and thus in particular is a weak
Friedman–Ramanujan function (see Corollary
3.27
). In other words, Theorem

1.23
can be obtained by a straightforward upper bound in the case of double-filling
loops. In general, the presence of shielded segments should imply less work to prove Theorem

1.23
, thanks to Proposition
1.30
. On the other end of the spectrum, the worse
possible scenario should be the case when none of the simple portions are shielded: we call such
loops
generalized eights
. Most of the paper is dedicated to proving Theorem
1.23
in
the case of generalized eights. For other multi-loops we will indicate how to modify our discussion
at the end of the paper (§
11
).

1.3.10.
Plan of the paper

In §
2
we “open” all the intersections of
\mathbf{c}
to obtain a new
representative
{\mathbf{c}}^{\mathrm{op}}
of the same homotopy class, decomposed into simple curves, joined by “bars”
replacing the intersections. The whole discussion applies to multi-loops, defined as finite
collections of loops. We define a class of multi-loops called
generalized eights
. They admit
a rather simple geometric description but offer the highest degree of difficulty, as far as the
proof of Theorem
1.23
is concerned.

In §
3
we define a coordinate system on
\mathcal{T}^{*}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}
, adapted to the study of the
function
Y\mapsto\ell_{Y}(\mathbf{c})
, and we express the Weil–Petersson measure in those new
coordinates. There are few results expressing the Weil–Petersson measure in coordinates other than
the Fenchel–Nielsen ones, so we believe that Theorem
3.11
has an intrinsic interest, and
probably deserves further investigation.

In §
4
, we use the representative
{\mathbf{c}}^{\mathrm{op}}
to find an algebraic expression for

\ell_{Y}({\mathbf{c}})
, as well as the lengths of all boundary curves of
\mathbf{S}
, in the new
coordinates. After this change of coordinates, it becomes apparent that the integral
(
1.13
) looks like a convolution of Friedman–Ramanujan functions, see §
5
. As already mentioned, the fact that we do not have exactly a convolution is
related to the fact that the curvature is finite. As a result, we need to develop a theory of

pseudo-convolutions
in an abstract setting, which is the focus of §
6
.

In order to apply our theoretical results on pseudo-convolutions to our particular problem,
additional geometric and analytic estimates on the function
Y\mapsto\ell_{Y}(\mathbf{c})
are
required. They are provided in §
7
and §
8
. Most of §
7
can be skipped in the purely non-crossing case (defined in §
2.6
).
The proof is finished in §
9
, except for the “Crucial comparison estimate” which is a
variant of Proposition
1.30
, proven in §
10
. We indicate in §
11
how to treat arbitrary topologies (i.e. multi-loops which are not generalized
eights). In §
12
we state a variant of Theorem
1.23
, and use it in §
13

to deduce Theorem
1.6
about the optimal spectral gap.

Acknowledgements.
Discussions with J. Friedman were of invaluable help to surmount several
obstacles in this project.

This research has received funding from the European Research Council
(ERC) under the European Union’s Horizon 2020 research and innovation programme (Grant agreement
No. 101096550), the EPSRC Standard Grant EP/W007010/1 and the Royal Society Dorothy Hodgkin
Fellowship.

2.
Loops and generalized eights

This section starts by setting a number of conventions related to loops on surfaces, segments,
orientations and geodesics on the hyperbolic plane. We then construct in §
2.2
a
procedure allowing to open the intersections of a loop, and following on this some associated
concepts (bars, simple portions, set
\Theta
, diagrams, cyclic orderings, u-turns and
crossings). Importantly, in §
2.3
, we introduce a class of loops called

generalized eights
, which will be the focus of the bulk of the paper (§
3
-
10
).

2.1.
Definitions

Let
{\mathbf{S}}
be a smooth surface (possibly with boundary). Throughout this article, we will
assume that
{\mathbf{S}}
is connected and has negative Euler characteristic. Indeed, the case
where
{\mathbf{S}}
is a cylinder has already been addressed in
[
2
, Proposition 3.3]
. In
applications, the surface
\mathbf{S}
we apply Theorem
1.23
to will often not be connected;
however, the proof for disconnected
{\mathbf{S}}
only requires minor modifications from the proof
in the connected case.

2.1.1.
Multi-loops

Let us clarify what we mean by a loop or a multi-loop
\mathbf{c}
.

Definition 2.1
.

A
loop
is a piecewise smooth map
{\mathbf{c}}:\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow{\mathbf{S}}
with nowhere
vanishing derivative (we allow loops to be contractible). A loop is called a
curve

if it is non-contractible and has no self-intersection.

From now on we extend the discussion to
multi-loops
. This is not just for the sake of maximal
generality, but also because, when we later “remove” some self-intersections from
{\mathbf{c}}
,
this operation will inevitably turn a loop into a multi-loop.

Definition 2.2
.

A
multi-loop
is a collection of loops

{\mathbf{c}}=({\mathbf{c}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{c}}_{\mathfrak{n}}):\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow{\mathbf{S}}^{\mathfrak{n}}
. The
loops
{\mathbf{c}}_{i}
are called the
components
of
{\mathbf{c}}
.

(1)

A multi-loop is said to be
in minimal position
if it has only transverse
self-intersections, and minimal number of self intersections in its free homotopy class.

(2)

A multi-loop is said to be
simple
if it has no self-intersections.

(3)

A multi-loop is called a
multi-curve
if it is simple, its components are
non-contractible and, for
i\not=j
,
{\mathbf{c}}_{i}
is not freely homotopic to

{\mathbf{c}}_{j}
nor
{\mathbf{c}}^{-1}_{j}
.

Remark 2.3
.

Although the multi-loop
{\mathbf{c}}
is originally assumed to be in minimal position, the
reader will notice that we will work with representatives of its homotopy class that do not have
this property, but are
\mathcal{C}^{0}
-limits of multi-loops in minimal position.

We will always assume that the multi-loop
{\mathbf{c}}
fills the surface
{\mathbf{S}}
, that is
to say, if
\mathcal{N}
is a regular neighbourhood of
{\mathbf{c}}
, then all the connected components
of
{\mathbf{S}}\setminus\mathcal{N}
are disks or annular regions bordered by a boundary component of

{\mathbf{S}}
(see
[
2
, §2.2.4]
).

Multi-loops are parameterized and oriented, but often, we will only be interested in their
geometric image as subsets of
{\mathbf{S}}
. Importantly, when we say that two multi-loops

{\mathbf{c}}=({\mathbf{c}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{c}}_{\mathfrak{n}})
and

{\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}=({\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}})
are freely homotopic, we take the
numberings and the orientations into account: we mean that they have the same number of components
(
\mathfrak{n}=\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
) and that
{\mathbf{c}}_{i}
is freely homotopic to
{\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}_{i}
for every

i
. Homotopies must respect orientation, and in particular a loop is not a priori homotopic to its
inverse.

2.1.2.
Segments and concatenation

Throughout this article, we will decompose our loops in successions of segments.

Definition 2.4
.

We call
J\subset{\mathbf{S}}
an
(oriented) segment
if it is of the form
J=p([0,T])
,
where
p:[0,T]\rightarrow{\mathbf{S}}
is a smooth oriented path with nowhere vanishing
derivative.

Notation 2.5

(Origin and terminus)
.

Let
J=p([0,T])
an oriented segment.
The
origin
of
J
is the point
o(J):=p(0)
, and its
terminus

t(J):=p(T)
.

Notation 2.6

(Concatenation of paths)
.

If
p:[0,T]\mapsto{\mathbf{S}}
and

q:[0,T^{\prime}]\mapsto{\mathbf{S}}
are two piecewise smooth paths such that
p(T)=q(0)
, we define
a piecewise smooth path
p\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}q:[0,T+T^{\prime}]\rightarrow{\mathbf{S}}
by

\displaystyle p\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}q(t):=\begin{cases}p(t)\mbox{ if }t\in[0,T]\\
q(t-T)\mbox{ if }t\in[T,T+T^{\prime}].\end{cases}

This is an associative and non-commutative operation. Most of the time, we shall only be interested
in the geometric images of the paths, and not in their actual parametrizations.

Definition 2.7
.

Let
\beta
be a multi-loop on
\mathbf{S}
, and
J=p([0,T])
be an oriented segment with
endpoints on
\beta
. We say an oriented segment
K=q([0,T])
is
homotopic to
J
with
gliding endpoints
if there exists a continuous function

h:[0,T]\times[0,1]\rightarrow\mathbf{S}
such that
h(\cdot,0)=p
,
h(\cdot,1)=q
, and the
endpoints
h(0,t)
and
h(T,t)
lie on
\beta
for any
t\in[0,1]
.

Remark 2.8
.

In the following,
\beta
will (for the most part) be a multi-curve. In this case, when we equip
the surface
\mathbf{S}
with a metric
Y
, we will systematically do so by picking a
representative in the Teichmüller space
\mathcal{T}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}^{*}
so that
\beta
is a simple
multi-geodesic. In this case, any non-trivial homotopy class of segments
J
with endpoints
gliding on
\beta
contains a uniquely defined length-minimizing element
\bar{J}
, which is
an orthogeodesic (a geodesic segment orthogonal to
\beta
at its endpoints). In pictures, in an
effort to simplify the usual notation, we will often mark the endpoints of orthogeodesics with a
black dot to emphasise the fact that there is a right angle there.

2.1.3.
Left and right

We take the following conventions in terms of the orientation of segments.

Definition 2.9
.

Let
J=p([0,T])
an oriented segment on
\mathbf{S}
.

•

A tangent vector
v\in T_{x}\mathbf{S}
with
x=p(t)\in J
is said to be
on the left
(resp. right) of
J
if the angle from
\dot{p}(t)
to
v
belongs to
[0,\pi]

(resp.
[-\pi,0]
).

•

Let
K=q([0,T^{\prime}])
be an oriented segment. We say that
K

leaves
J
from the left
(resp. right)
if the origin of
K
lies in
J
and if the tangent vector
\dot{q}(0)
is on
the left (resp. right) of
J
. Similarly, we say that
K

arrives on
J
from the left
(resp. right)
if the terminus of
K
lies on
J
and
\dot{q}(T^{\prime})
is on the right
(resp. left) of
J
.

2.1.4.
Infinite geodesics on
\mathbb{H}^{2}

We now briefly focus on infinite geodesics in the hyperbolic plane
\mathbb{H}^{2}
. For two infinite
geodesics in the hyperbolic plane, with endpoints at infinity
(a,b)
and
(c,d)
, the
cross-ratio
(a:b:c:d)
can be used as a convenient way of describing their respective positions:

•

A cross-ratio
(a:b:c:d)\in(0,1)
means that the two geodesics intersect.

•

Whenever
(a:b:c:d)\not\in(0,1)
we shall speak of
parallel geodesics
.

–

A cross-ratio in
[1,+\infty)
means that the two geodesics are parallel and
oriented “head-to-tail”.

–

On the opposite,
(a:b:c:d)\leq 0
means that the two geodesics are parallel and are
oriented “in the same direction”. We will then say that there are
aligned
.

Since the hyperbolic plane is oriented, an infinite oriented geodesic determines two half-spaces,
one on its left and one on its right.

Definition 2.10

(Algebraic distance
\vec{\mathrm{d}}
)
.

If two points
x,y
lie on an oriented geodesic
\gamma
, we will say
y
is on the right
of
x
along
\gamma
to mean that
y
is after
x
along
\gamma
. We denote by
\vec{\mathrm{d}}(x,y)

the signed distance between
x
and
y
along
\gamma
, measured positively if
y
is on the right
of
x
along
\gamma
and negatively otherwise.

This notion is named according to the tradition of orienting lines from left to right. Although it
would be more rigourous to denote this distance by
\vec{\mathrm{d}}_{\gamma}(x,y)
, the notation will always
be used in the universal cover
\mathbb{H}^{2}
and the reference geodesic will always be clear from the
context.

The following key observation, illustrated in Figure
1
, will be used on multiple
occasions throughout this article.

Remark 2.11

(The Useful Remark)
.

Figure 1
.
Illustration of the Useful Remark.

Take
3
distinct non-intersecting geodesics
(\gamma_{i})_{0\leq i\leq 2}
in the hyperbolic
plane
\mathbb{H}^{2}
. Assume that for
i=1,2
we have a piecewise smooth oriented path
p_{i}
going from

\gamma_{0}
to
\gamma_{i}
, intersecting
\gamma_{0}
and
\gamma_{i}
only at its
endpoints. Therefore, the complement
\mathbb{H}^{2}\setminus(\gamma_{0}\cup\gamma_{i}\cup p_{i})
has four
connected components, that we respectively call north of
\gamma_{i}
, south of
\gamma_{0}
and
east/west of
p_{i}
, following the compass. Assume in addition that
p_{2}
only visits the east
of
p_{1}
, and
p_{1}
only visits the west of
p_{2}
. Then:

(1)

the oriented orthogeodesics
\bar{p}_{i}
from
\gamma_{0}
to
\gamma_{i}
are disjoint;

(2)

\bar{p}_{2}
entirely lies east of
\bar{p}_{1}
;

(3)

\bar{p}_{1}
entirely lies west of
\bar{p}_{2}
.

2.1.5.
Length of a path or a geodesic

If
\gamma
is a 1-dimensional Riemannian manifold, we will denote by
\ell(\gamma)
its
length. If now
\gamma
is a smooth path in a Riemannian manifold
Y
, we will denote by

\ell(\gamma)
its length in
Y
(which coincides with its length for the metric on
\gamma

induced by
Y
). If
\gamma
is a loop in a hyperbolic surface
Y
, we will denote by

\ell_{Y}(\gamma)
the minimal length of a representative of the free homotopy class of
\gamma

(if
\gamma
is contractible,
\ell_{Y}(\gamma)=0
, otherwise it is the length of the unique
geodesic representative of the free homotopy class of
\gamma
). In general,
\ell(\gamma)
and

\ell_{Y}(\gamma)
are different, but they are of course equal if
\gamma
is a closed geodesic in

Y
.

If
{\mathbf{S}}
is endowed with a hyperbolic structure
Y
, then each component
{\mathbf{c}}_{i}

of
\mathbf{c}
is freely homotopic to a periodic geodesic, which is unique if
{\mathbf{c}}_{i}
is
non-contractible, but may be reduced to a point otherwise. We denote

\ell_{Y}({\mathbf{c}})=\sum_{i=1}^{\mathfrak{n}}\ell_{Y}({\mathbf{c}}_{i})
the total length of a geodesic
representative.

2.2.
Opening the intersections of
{\mathbf{c}}

In this paragraph, we explain how to
open the self-intersections of
{\mathbf{c}}
to replace
them by bars
. Our construction yields a new picture which consists of a simple multi-loop
\beta

and a collection of bars
B
with endpoints on
\beta
, replacing the intersections.

Notation 2.12
.

Throughout the paper, the letter
r
stands for the number of self-intersection points of the
multi-loop
{\mathbf{c}}
. We identify the set of intersection points of the multi-loop

\mathbf{c}
with the set
\{1,\ldots,r\}
by picking an arbitrary numbering.

2.2.1.
The construction

If
r=0
then
{\mathbf{c}}
is a simple loop and we simply take
\beta={\mathbf{c}}
, and no
bars. Now assume
r\geq 1
. Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{r}
be the intersection points of
\mathbf{c}
and

D_{1},\ldots,D_{r}
be disjoint open disks around them. We can take the disks small enough
so that, for each
j
,
{\mathbf{c}}\cap D_{j}
is the union of two oriented segments

I_{j},I^{\prime}_{j}
intersecting transversally at
a_{j}
. We open the intersection at each
a_{j}
and replace
the two intersecting segments
I_{j},I^{\prime}_{j}
by two smooth disjoint segments, joined by a transversal
segment
B_{j}
traversed twice, replacing the intersection.
There are two distinct ways to perform this operation, represented in Figure
2(b)
and

2(c)
respectively.

(a)

The initial intersection.

(b)

Opening the first way.

(c)

Opening the second way.

Figure 2
.
Opening an intersection to replace it by a bar, two ways.

Opening all of the intersections (having made an arbitrary choice between the two ways for each
intersection) yields a new representative
{\mathbf{c}}^{\mathrm{op}}
of the homotopy class of
{\mathbf{c}}
,
represented in Figure
3
. The operation of going from
{\mathbf{c}}
to the new
representative
{\mathbf{c}}^{\mathrm{op}}
will be referred to as
opening the intersections of
{\mathbf{c}}
(to
replace them by bars)
.

(a)

The first way.

(b)

The second way.

Figure 3
.
A close deformation of the multi-loop
{\mathbf{c}}^{\mathrm{op}}
. Notice that each bar is
traversed twice (in the directions we will later denote
B_{j}^{+}
and
B_{j}^{-}
).

By convention, the bars
B_{j}
are closed, i.e. contain their endpoints. If
\dot{B_{j}}
is the open
segment, i.e. the segment without its endpoints, we call
\beta
the connected components of

{\mathbf{c}}^{\mathrm{op}}\setminus(\bigcup_{j=1}^{r}\dot{B_{j}})
. We observe that
\beta
is a simple multi-loop. The
geometric image of
{\mathbf{c}}^{\mathrm{op}}
is the simple multi-loop
\beta
joined by the collection of bars

B=(B_{1},\ldots,B_{r})
.

Note that
\beta
is not necessarily a multi-curve – see Figure
4(b)
for an
example where one of the components of
\beta
is contractible. When we later specialise our
discussion to the class of multi-loops called
generalized eights
in §
2.3
,
the family
\beta
will actually be a multi-curve (see Lemma
2.21
).

(a)

A generalized eight.

(b)

Two non-isotopic representatives of a loop.

Figure 4
.
An example of the construction for three loops filling a surface of signature

(0,4)
. Here, we opened all the intersections the first way.

Remark 2.13
.

The bars are naturally numbered as
(B_{1},\ldots,B_{r})
thanks to our choice of numbering of the
intersection points of
\mathbf{c}
. However, there is no canonical way to number the components
of
\beta
based on our information so far, and because picking a numbering is not very useful for
our purposes, we do not do so. We will denote as
\Lambda^{\beta}
the set of components of
\beta

(this notation will make more sense after §
3.3
), so that
\beta=(\beta_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda^{\beta}}
.

2.2.2.
Orientation of the bars

Let us define orientations
B_{j}^{\pm}
on the bars
B_{j}
for
1\leq j\leq r
. The convention will
depend on the type of opening.

We first observe that the endpoints of the bars
B_{1},\ldots,B_{r}
partition the multi-loop
\beta

into
2r
segments, reflecting the partition of
{\mathbf{c}}
into the simple portions defined in
Definition
1.21
. We will call
simple portions
of
{\mathbf{c}}^{\mathrm{op}}
these subsegments of

\beta
. Each simple portion is naturally oriented by the original orientation of
{\mathbf{c}}^{\mathrm{op}}
. Each bar

B_{j}
touches four (non necessarily distinct) simple portions: two at each endpoint.

We then settle on the following convention, illustrated in Figure
3
.

(1)

In the
first way
, we choose the orientation
B_{j}^{+}
of
B_{j}
such that
B_{j}^{+}
arrives
on the right of the two simple portions touching its terminus
t(B_{j}^{+})
: one of these simple
portions originates in
t(B_{j}^{+})
and the other one terminates in
t(B_{j}^{+})
. Similarly,
B_{j}^{+}

leaves from the left of the two simple portions touching its origin
o(B_{j}^{+})
; one of them
originates in
o(B_{j}^{+})
and the other one terminates in
o(B_{j}^{+})
. In this case we denote by

B_{j}^{-}
the opposite orientation of
B_{j}
.

(2)

In the
second way
, we choose the orientation
B_{j}^{+}
of
B_{j}
such that the two simple
portions touching its terminus
t(B_{j}^{+})
originate in
t(B_{j}^{+})
, and the two simple portions
touching its origin
o(B_{j}^{+})
terminate in
o(B_{j}^{+})
. In this case, we define the orientation

B_{j}^{-}
to be identical to
B_{j}^{+}
.

2.2.3.
Labelling of simple portions

Let us label the simple portions and define a few useful notations. Our most important set of
indices will be the set

(2.1)

\Theta=\{1,\ldots,r\}\times\{+,-\},

which will naturally label the simple portions. Indeed, for
(j,\epsilon)\in\Theta
, let

\mathcal{I}_{j}^{\epsilon}
be the oriented simple portion originating from the terminus of
B_{j}^{\epsilon}
, so that

B^{+}_{j}
arrives on
\mathcal{I}_{j}^{+}
from the right at the point
o(\mathcal{I}_{j}^{+})=t(B_{j}^{+})
and
B^{-}_{j}

arrives on
\mathcal{I}_{j}^{-}
from the left at the point
o(\mathcal{I}_{j}^{-})=t(B_{j}^{-})
, as represented in Figure

3
.

Remark 2.14
.

Note that, out of the four simple portions touching the bar
B_{j}
, only two are labelled

\mathcal{I}_{j}^{\pm}
by the above convention. The other two simple portions are denoted as

\mathcal{I}_{j^{\prime}}^{\pm}
for another (possibly equal) integer
j^{\prime}
.

We shall most often refer to the elements of
\Theta
by the letter
q
, so that if

q=(j,\epsilon)
is an elements of
\Theta
, then the bar
B_{q}
stands for
B_{j}^{\epsilon}
,
\mathcal{I}_{q}

for
\mathcal{I}_{j}^{\epsilon}
, etc.

The signs in
\Theta
will play an important role throughout the paper.

Notation 2.15
.

We define the sign function on
\Theta
by:

(2.2)

\displaystyle\forall q=(j,\epsilon)\in\Theta,\quad\sign(q)=\epsilon.

We denote as
\iota:\Theta\rightarrow\Theta
the involution defined by
\iota(j,\epsilon)=(j,-\epsilon)
.

It will be helpful to relate the elements of
\Theta
and the components of the multi-loop
\beta
.

Notation 2.16
.

For
q\in\Theta
, let
\lambda(q)\in\Lambda^{\beta}
denote the component of
\beta
on which

B_{q}
terminates, i.e. such that the terminus
t(B_{q})
belongs to the loop
\beta_{\lambda(q)}
.

For
\lambda\in\Lambda^{\beta}
(i.e. for a component
\beta_{\lambda}
of the multi-loop
\beta
), we
define the set of indices

(2.3)

\Theta_{t}(\lambda):=\{q\in\Theta:t(B_{q})\in\beta_{\lambda}\}\subseteq\Theta.

In other words, this is the set of indices
q\in\Theta
such that
\lambda(q)=\lambda
, or,
equivalently, such that the bar
B_{q}
terminates on
\beta_{\lambda}
. We extend this notation to
any subset
W\subseteq\Lambda^{\beta}
by letting

(2.4)

\Theta_{t}(W):=\bigcup_{\lambda\in W}\Theta_{t}(\lambda)=\{q\in\Theta:t(B_{q})\in W\}.

Remark 2.17
.

We make the key observation that, since the multi-loop
\beta
is decomposed in the simple
portions
\mathcal{I}_{q}
with
q\in\Theta
, the sets
(\Theta_{t}(\lambda))_{\lambda\in\Lambda^{\beta}}

generate a partition of
\Theta
:

\Theta=\bigsqcup_{\lambda\in\Lambda^{\beta}}\Theta_{t}(\lambda).

Remark 2.18
.

It will be useful to keep in mind from the construction that each
component
\beta_{\lambda}
of
\beta
is freely homotopic to an “initial” piecewise smooth curve

\beta_{\lambda,\mathrm{init}}
, included in the original multi-loop
{\mathbf{c}}
, made of
smooth segments joining some of the intersection points of
\mathbf{c}
. The partition of

\beta_{\lambda}
into the union of a collection of segments
(\mathcal{I}_{q})_{q\in\Theta_{t}(\lambda)}
is
inherited from a partition of
\beta_{\lambda,\mathrm{init}}
into segments

(\mathcal{I}_{q,\mathrm{init}})_{q\in\Theta_{t}(\lambda)}
which are the simple portions of Definition

1.21
.

2.2.4.
Diagrams

The result of opening all of the intersections of the multi-loop
\mathbf{c}
is what we call a
diagram.

Definition 2.19
.

A
diagram

\mathbf{D}
in
\mathbf{S}
is the data (modulo isotopy) of a simple multi-loop

\beta
, together with a collection of disjoint bars
B=(B_{1},\ldots,B_{r})
defined as closed,
mutually disjoint segments, having their endpoints in
\beta
and disjoint from
\beta

otherwise.

Starting with a multi-loop
\mathbf{c}
and opening all intersections (either way), we obtain a
diagram
\mathbf{D}
, which is said to
represent

\mathbf{c}
. Conversely, the multi-loop

\mathbf{c}
is said to
stem from

\mathbf{D}
if
\mathbf{D}
represents
\mathbf{c}
.

Different representatives of the same homotopy class may give rise to non-isotopic diagrams: see
Figure
4(b)
. The paper by Graaf and Schrijver
[
7
]
precisely
describes when two multi-loops can be homotopic without being isotopic: this may happen if and only
if one can be sent to the other by an isotopy followed by a finite succession of Reidemeister moves
of type III.

2.2.5.
First v.s. second way

Throughout this article, we will be in particular interested in the case when we open all of the
intersections the first way. We denote by
\mathbf{D}^{(1)}
the diagram obtained by opening all
intersections the first way. One possible reason for preferring the first way is the fact that the
multi-loop
\beta
then inherits a global orientation that fits with the orientation of the simple
portions
(\mathcal{I}_{q})_{q\in\Theta}
, in other words with the initial orientation of
{\mathbf{c}}
. If
we open the intersections the second way, the orientations of the simple portions

(\mathcal{I}_{q})_{q\in\Theta}
do not fit into a global orientation of
\beta
, which is not a problem,
but can sometimes be slightly cumbersome in terms of notations.

Unless explicitely stated, for the sake of simplicity, we invite the reader to consider that the
construction above has been done by
opening every intersection the first way
. However, many
of our results, notably our change of variable presented in §
3
, hold for whichever choice
of opening is made – different openings yielding different interesting results. We will be careful
to point out the few places where the choice of opening actually does matter.

2.3.
Generalized eights

We introduce a class of multi-loops, called
generalized eights
, on which we focus attention for most of the paper. Their name comes from the fact
that they are generalizations of the basic figure-eight loop, the loop with exactly one
self-intersection (
r=1
).

Definition 2.20
.

Let
\mathcal{N}
be a regular neighbourhood of
{\mathbf{c}}
in

{\mathbf{S}}
. We say that
{\mathbf{c}}
is a
generalized eight
if no connected
components of
\partial\mathcal{N}
is contractible in
{\mathbf{S}}
.

This definition has the following consequence on the surface
\mathbf{S}
and the multi-loop
\beta
.

Lemma 2.21
.

If
\mathbf{c}
is a generalized eight, then
\chi(\mathbf{S})=r
and
\beta
is a multi-curve.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{N}
be a regular neighbourhood of
\mathbf{c}
in
\mathbf{S}
. Since
\mathbf{c}
fills

\mathbf{S}
and no boundary component of
\mathcal{N}
is contractible,
\mathbf{S}\setminus\mathcal{N}
is a
union of cylinders, and hence
\chi(\mathbf{S})=\chi(\mathcal{N})
. We then conclude by observing that
the regular neighbourhood
\mathcal{N}
retracts on the
4
-regular graph with vertices the
r

intersections points of
\mathbf{c}
, and edges its
2r
simple portions, of Euler
characteristic
r-2r=-r
.

The definition of generalized eight directly implies that none of the components of
\beta
is
contractible. To prove that
\beta_{i}
cannot be freely homotopic to
\beta_{j}
or its inverse for

i\neq j
two components of
\beta
, we remark that if
\beta_{i}
is freely homotopic to

\beta_{j}
or its inverse, then there is a cylinder in
{\mathbf{S}}
bordered by
\beta_{i}
and

\beta_{j}
. This cylinder may contain in its interior other components
\beta_{k}
homotopic to

\beta_{i}^{\pm 1}
. We pick
k
such that the cylinder
C
bordered by
\beta_{i}
and

\beta_{k}
does not contain in its interior any other component of the multi-loop
\beta
. Because

{\mathbf{c}}
fills
{\mathbf{S}}
, the cylinder
C
must contain bars
B_{l}
going from

\beta_{i}
to
\beta_{k}
. These bars, together with
\beta_{i}
and
\beta_{k}
, cut
C
into
connected components homeomorphic to disks, contradicting the fact that
\partial\mathcal{N}
does not
have contractible components.
∎

Remark 2.22
.

If
\mathbf{c}
is a generalized eight, Reidemeister moves of type III are not possible and hence
any multi-loop homotopic to
\mathbf{c}
is also isotopic. As a consequence, the diagram

\mathbf{D}^{(1)}
obtained by opening the intersections the first way is uniquely defined up to
isotopy.

2.4.
Removing bars and intersections

We define two ways of
removing
a bar from a diagram: either by just deleting it (we call this
the
first way
of removing a bar), or the
second way
, shown on
Figure
5(a)
.

(a)

A bar in a diagram.

(b)

An intersection on a multi-loop.

Figure 5
.
The two ways of removing a bar on a diagram or an intersection point on a multi-loop.

If the diagram was obtained by opening all intersections of a multi-loop
{\mathbf{c}}
, removing
a bar corresponds to removing an intersection from
{\mathbf{c}}
. Correspondingly, there are
two ways to remove an intersection from
{\mathbf{c}}
(the first and the second way), shown on
Figure
5(b)
.

We make the following straightforward observation, which will enable us to argue by induction on
r
in the proof of Theorem
3.11
.

Lemma 2.23
.

If
{\mathbf{c}}
is a generalized eight, the multi-loop
{\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}
obtained by removing an
intersection point is still a generalized eight, filling a surface of absolute Euler
characteristic
r-1
.

Note that the number of components of the multi-loop
{\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}
is, a priori, different from
the number of components of
\mathbf{c}
; this is one of the motivations for studying multi-loops
instead of mere loops.

2.5.
Reconstitution of
{\mathbf{c}}^{\mathrm{op}}
and relabelling of
\Theta

Let us explain how to retrieve the multi-loop
\mathbf{c}
from the diagram, and define some
convenient notations to express the representant
{\mathbf{c}}^{\mathrm{op}}
of the homotopy class of
\mathbf{c}
in
terms of a succession of bars and simple portions.

2.5.1.
Cycle-decomposition and rewriting of
{\mathbf{c}}^{\mathrm{op}}

For
q\in\Theta
, we define

(2.5)

\displaystyle p(q):=B_{q}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\mathcal{I}_{q}

to be the concatenation of the oriented segments
B_{q}
and
\mathcal{I}_{q}
(which do, by definition,
connect). The representative
{\mathbf{c}}^{\mathrm{op}}
of the homotopy class of
{\mathbf{c}}
is the concatenation of
the
2r
paths
p(q)
for
q\in\Theta
. More precisely, there is a unique permutation
\sigma
of
the set
\Theta
, decomposed into
\mathfrak{n}
disjoint cycles

\sigma=\prod_{i=1}^{\mathfrak{n}}(q_{1}^{i}\,q_{2}^{i}\,\ldots\,q_{n_{i}}^{i})
such that, for all

i\in\{1,\ldots,\mathfrak{n}\}
, the
i
th component
{\mathbf{c}}^{\mathrm{op}}_{i}
of
{\mathbf{c}}^{\mathrm{op}}
coincides with the concatenation

(2.6)

\displaystyle{\mathbf{c}}^{\mathrm{op}}_{i}=p(q_{1}^{i})\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}p(q_{2}^{i})\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\ldots\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}p(q_{n_{i}}^{i})

(or a circular permutation thereof).

The length of the
i
-th cycle is denoted as
n_{i}
, and we have
\sum_{i=1}^{\mathfrak{n}}n_{i}=2r
. Because
of the invariance of the free homotopy class of (
2.6
) under circular permutation, it
is convenient to consider that the elements
q_{k}^{i}=(j^{i}_{k},\epsilon^{i}_{k})\in\Theta
are numbered by

k\in\mathbb{Z}_{n_{i}}
where
\mathbb{Z}_{n_{i}}:=\mathbb{Z}\diagup n_{i}\mathbb{Z}
.

Remark 2.24
.

By construction, we have the following orientation rules in (
2.6
), see Figure

3
:

•

if
\epsilon^{i}_{k}=+
then
B_{q^{i}_{k}}
arrives at
\mathcal{I}_{q^{i}_{k}}
from the right and leaves
from
\mathcal{I}_{q^{i}_{k-1}}
from the left;

•

otherwise,
B_{q^{i}_{k}}
arrives at
\mathcal{I}_{q^{i}_{k}}
from the left and leaves from

\mathcal{I}_{q^{i}_{k-1}}
from the right.

2.5.2.
Re-labelling of
\Theta

For
q\in\Theta
, let
\comp(q)=i\in\{1,\ldots,\mathfrak{n}\}
be the label of the
component
{\mathbf{c}}_{i}
of
\mathbf{c}
in which
q
appears in the decomposition
(
2.6
). We set

\Theta^{i}=\{q\in\Theta,\comp(q)=i\}.

Then, for any
q\in\Theta^{i}
, we can write using the permutation
\sigma
:

(2.7)

\displaystyle{\mathbf{c}}^{\mathrm{op}}_{i}=p(q)\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}p(\sigma q)\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}p(\sigma^{2}q)\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\ldots\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}p(\sigma^{n_{i}-1}q).

We now consider the set

\Theta^{\prime}=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{\mathfrak{n}}\mathbb{Z}_{n_{i}}=\{(i,k),i\in\{1,\ldots,\mathfrak{n}\},k\in\mathbb{Z}_{n_{i}}\}.

The map

(2.8)

\displaystyle\phi:\begin{cases}\Theta^{\prime}&\rightarrow\Theta\\
(i,k)&\mapsto q^{i}_{k}=(j^{i}_{k},\epsilon^{i}_{k})\end{cases}

is a bijection, and the cyclic ordering of
\mathbb{Z}_{n_{i}}
is compatible with the cycling ordering of

q^{i}_{k}
in the path (
2.6
). We shall identify
\Theta
with
\Theta^{\prime}
through the
map
\phi
, thus writing
(i,k)
for
1\leq i\leq\mathfrak{n}
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{n_{i}}
to enumerate the
elements of
\Theta
. Doing so,
\Theta^{i}
can be identified with
\{(i,k),k\in\mathbb{Z}_{n_{i}}\}
, that
is to say, with
\mathbb{Z}_{n_{i}}
. After this identification, the permutation
\sigma
is given by
(i,k)\mapsto(i,k+1)
.

2.5.3.
Periodic extensions

Similarly define

{\mathbf{\Theta}}=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{\mathfrak{n}}\mathbb{Z}=\{(i,k),i\in\{1,\ldots,\mathfrak{n}\},k\in\mathbb{Z}\}=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{\mathfrak{n}}{\mathbf{\Theta}}^{i},

where
{\mathbf{\Theta}}^{i}=\{(i,k),k\in\mathbb{Z}\}
.
When needed, the sequence
(i,k)\mapsto q^{i}_{k}
can also be defined for

(i,k)\in{\mathbf{\Theta}}
by pre-composing with the projection map
\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{n_{i}}
.
In accordance with the previous discussion, we will denote

\sigma:{\mathbf{\Theta}}\rightarrow{\mathbf{\Theta}}
the shift map
(i,k)\mapsto(i,k+1)
.

2.6.
U-turns and crossings

The following definition will be useful to our analysis.

Definition 2.25
.

For
q\in\Theta
, we say that
q
is a
crossing index
if

\sign(q)\sign(\sigma q)=+1
, and a
U-turn index
otherwise. We denote by

\mathcal{C}\subseteq\Theta
the set of crossing indices and by
\mathcal{U}\subseteq\Theta
the set of U-turn
indices. If
\Theta=\mathcal{U}
, we say that we are in the
purely non-crossing case
.

(a)

U-turn.

(b)

Crossing.

Figure 6
.
A U-turn v.s. crossing index (here
\sign(q)=+
).

The terminology is motivated by the fact that if
q
is a crossing index, then to go from
B_{q}
to

B_{\sigma q}
we have to cross the component
\beta_{\lambda(q)}
of
\beta
, while in the other case,
we make a U-turn when arriving at
\beta_{\lambda(q)}
. See Figure
6
.

3.
Coordinate system adapted to a generalized eight

We assume until §
11
that the multi-loop
{\mathbf{c}}
is a generalized
eight filling a surface
{\mathbf{S}}
.
We open the intersections of
{\mathbf{c}}
by the
procedure defined in the previous section (in this section, we allow every intersection to be opened
in the first or second way). By hypothesis, the simple multi-loop
\beta
is a multi-curve. Let us
show how the bars
B_{1},\ldots,B_{r}
and the simple portions
(\mathcal{I}_{q})_{q\in\Theta}
can be used to

define new coordinates
on the Teichmüller space
\mathcal{T}^{*}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}
of marked hyperbolic
metrics on
\mathbf{S}
, well-adapted to expressing the length function of
{\mathbf{c}}
.

First, we notice that the dimension of
\mathcal{T}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}^{*}
is equal to
6g_{\mathbf{S}}-6+3n_{\mathbf{S}}=3\chi(\mathbf{S})
. We proved in Lemma
2.21
that, for a generalized eight,
\chi(\mathbf{S})=r
. As a consequence, the dimension of the Teichmüller space
\mathcal{T}^{*}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}
is
3r
, so this
is the number of coordinates we expect to need to describe a point on
\mathcal{T}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}^{*}
.

3.1.
Coordinates
\vec{L}=(L_{1},\ldots,L_{r})
and
