\vec{\theta}=(\theta_{1}^{\pm},\ldots,\theta_{r}^{\pm})
on the Teichmüller
space

Consider a point
Y\in\mathcal{T}^{*}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}
, i.e. a compact hyperbolic metric on
\mathbf{S}
. As
explained in Remark
2.8
, we take a representative of
Y
in the Teichmüller space
such that the multi-curve
\beta
is a simple multi-geodesic.

For
q\in\Theta
, we replace the bar
B_{q}
by the representative
\overline{B}_{q}
of minimal
length in its homotopy class with endpoints gliding on
\beta
. While the endpoints
t(B_{q})
glide
to the new endpoints
t(\overline{B}_{q})
along
\beta
, it is important to note that the segments

\mathcal{I}_{q}
glide to new segments
\overline{\mathcal{I}}_{q}
of
\beta_{\lambda(q)}
. Although the original
segments
\mathcal{I}_{q}
were positively oriented along
\beta_{\lambda(q)}
, it can happen that

\overline{\mathcal{I}}_{q}
goes in the reverse direction, or that
\overline{\mathcal{I}}_{q}
wraps an arbitrary
number of times around
\beta_{\lambda(q)}
.

We are now ready to define our new coordinates on the Teichmüller space
\mathcal{T}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}^{*}
.

Definition 3.1

(Coordinates
(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})
)
.

Let
Y\in\mathcal{T}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}^{*}
.

(1)

For
j\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
, we denote by
L_{j}=L_{j}(Y)
the (positive) length of the
orthogeodesic
\overline{B}_{j}
. We write
\vec{L}=(L_{1},\ldots,L_{r})
.

(2)

For
q\in\Theta
, we denote by
\theta_{q}=\theta_{q}(Y)
the
algebraic
length of

\overline{\mathcal{I}}_{q}
: positive if it goes in the same direction as
\mathcal{I}_{q}
, negative
otherwise. We write
\vec{\theta}=(\theta_{q})_{q\in\Theta}
.

Figure 7
.
An example of coordinates
(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})
in a case when
r=2
. We have
additionally represented the pair of pants decomposition constructed in §
3.2
as
dashed curves (here,
\#\Lambda^{\beta}=3
and
\#\Lambda^{\Gamma}=2
).

Remark 3.2

(Crossing and U-turn variables)
.

If
q\in\mathcal{C}
is a crossing index, then we say that
\theta_{q}
is a
crossing variable
; if

q\in\mathcal{U}
is a U-turn variable, then we say that
\theta_{q}
is a
U-turn variable
. By the
Useful Remark (Remark
2.11
), U-turn variables are always positive: if
q\in\mathcal{U}
, then

\theta_{q}\geq 0
. Thus, the discussion concerning U-turn variables is a little simpler than for
crossing variables.

Notation 3.3
.

It will often be convenient to use the notation
L(q)=L_{j}
for

q=(j,\epsilon)\in\Theta
(note that
L(q)
does not depend on the sign component of
q
). We
will also benefit from introducing
\epsilon L(q):=\sign(q)L(q)
. The vector

(\epsilon L(q))_{q\in\Theta}
then contains each length
L_{j}
exactly twice, once positively
and once negatively. This corresponds to the fact that each bar is explored exactly twice when
going along
{\mathbf{c}}^{\mathrm{op}}
, following its two orientations, as illustrated in Figure
3
.

We can already note that
(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})
belongs to

\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{r}\times\mathbb{R}^{\Theta}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3r}
, a space of same dimension as the Teichmüller space

\mathcal{T}^{*}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}
. The remainder of this section is devoted to proving that

(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})
is a coordinate system on
\mathcal{T}^{*}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}
, finding the expression of the
Weil–Petersson measure in these coordinates, and describing its range.

We do not start from scratch, but use the fact that the expression for the Weil–Petersson measure
is already known in Fenchel–Nielsen coordinates, thanks to Wolpert’s theorem

[
14
]
. In order to apply Wolpert’s result, we use an auxiliary decomposition of

{\mathbf{S}}
into pairs of pants, determined by the multi-curve
\beta
and the bars

B_{1},\ldots,B_{r}
. We find relations between the Fenchel–Nielsen coordinates and the parameters

(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})
, and calculate the determinant.

Remark 3.4
.

This approach seems a bit artificial, and it would be interesting to study directly the
Weil–Petersson measure in coordinates
(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})
, without resorting to an
auxiliary system of Fenchel–Nielsen coordinates.

3.2.
Decomposition into pairs of pants

Using the multi-curve
\beta
and the bars
B_{1},\ldots,B_{r}
, we define a decomposition of

{\mathbf{S}}
into pairs of pants (3-holed spheres) that will serve as an auxiliary tool in several
places.

3.2.1.
Pair of pants determined by two simple curves joined by a segment

Let
(C_{+},C_{-})
be a multi-curve on
\mathbf{S}
, and
B
be a simple segment going from
C_{-}
to

C_{+}
, not intersecting
C_{-}\cup C_{+}
except at its endpoints. The topological pair of pants
determined by
C_{-}
,
C_{+}
and
B
is defined as a one-sided regular neighbourhood of

C_{-}\cup C_{+}\cup B
; that is to say, a regular neighbourhood
\mathcal{N}
of

C_{-}\cup C_{+}\cup B
, from which we remove the cylinders bordered by one of the curves
C_{\pm}
and
one component of
\partial\mathcal{N}
.

This also applies when
C=C_{+}=C_{-}
as soon as
B
is not homotopically trivial with gliding
endpoints along
C
. We have either a pair of pants if
B
leaves and arrives on the same side
of
C
, or a once-holed torus otherwise.

The three cases are shown in Figure
8
.

Figure 8
.
The three possible pairs of pants (or once-holed tori) determined by two simple curves
joined by a segment.

3.2.2.
Construction of the pair of pants decomposition

The (arbitrary) numbering of
B_{1},\ldots,B_{r}
determines a decomposition

({\mathbf{P}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{P}}_{r})
of
{\mathbf{S}}
into connected surfaces of Euler characteristic

-1
, defined inductively below. This decomposition is non-canonical, as it depends on the
numbering of the bars
B_{1},\ldots,B_{r}
.

The construction provides us with an increasing family of multi-curves

\Sigma_{0}\subset\Sigma_{1}\subset\ldots\subset\Sigma_{r}
, and an increasing family of surfaces

{\mathbf{S}}_{j}={\mathbf{S}}_{j-1}\cup{\mathbf{P}}_{j}
for
1\leq j\leq r
. It will also
produce an auxiliary family of sets
\widetilde{{\mathbf{S}}}_{j}
.

•

To initiate, we let
{\mathbf{S}}_{0}=\emptyset
and
\widetilde{{\mathbf{S}}}_{0}=\Sigma_{0}
be
the multi-curve
\beta
.

•

Let
j\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
. The intersection of the
j
-th bar
B_{j}
with

\widetilde{{\mathbf{S}}}_{j-1}
is the union of two non-empty closed segments
I_{+}
,
I_{-}

(possibly reduced to single points) containing the endpoints of
B_{j}
. The complement of

I_{+}\cup I_{-}
in
B_{j}
is a non-empty sub-segment
B^{\prime}_{j}
of
B_{j}
which meets one or two
components of
\partial\widetilde{{\mathbf{S}}}_{j-1}
at its endpoints; call
C_{+},C_{-}
these
components. We let
{\mathbf{P}}_{j}
be the pair of pants determined by
B^{\prime}_{j}
,
C_{+}
and
C_{-}
.

•

We then let
\Sigma_{j}=\Sigma_{j-1}\cup\partial{\mathbf{P}}_{j}
(it is a multi-curve),

\widetilde{{\mathbf{S}}}_{j}=\widetilde{{\mathbf{S}}}_{j-1}\cup{\mathbf{P}}_{j}
(the disjoint union
of a surface and a multi-curve) and

{\mathbf{S}}_{j}={\mathbf{S}}_{j-1}\cup{\mathbf{P}}_{j}={\mathbf{P}}_{1}\cup\ldots\cup{\mathbf{P}}_{j}
(a bona-fide
surface). They are related by

\widetilde{{\mathbf{S}}}_{j}={\mathbf{S}}_{j}\cup\beta
.

By an argument similar to Remark
2.21
, because
\mathbf{c}
is a generalized eight, the
boundary of each pair of pants
\mathbf{P}_{j}
is a multi-curve in
\mathbf{S}
. The construction
finishes for
j=r
, and we have
\widetilde{{\mathbf{S}}}_{r}={{\mathbf{S}}}_{r}={\mathbf{S}}
,
because the multi-loop
\mathbf{c}
fills the surface
\mathbf{S}
.

Remark 3.5
.

For each
j
, the surface
\mathbf{S}_{j}
is filled by the multi-loop
\mathbf{c}^{\prime}
obtained by
removing the intersections
j+1,\ldots,r
from
\mathbf{c}
the first way, according to
the procedure described in §
2.4
. The pairs of pants decomposition

\mathbf{P}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{P}_{j}
associated to the multi-loops
\mathbf{c}
and
\mathbf{c}^{\prime}

are the same.

3.2.3.
Reduction to Case (a) and (b)

We shall assume (without loss of generality) that the numbering of
B_{1},\ldots,B_{r}
is such that
the first bars
B_{1},\ldots,B_{r_{0}}
are in the third situation of Figure
8
(that is,
depart and arrive on one unique component of
\beta
, on opposite sides) and the remaining

B_{r_{0}+1},\ldots,B_{r}
, either join two different components of
\beta
, or depart and arrive on
one component of
\beta
on the same side, or leave from a component of
\beta
already touched by

B_{1},\ldots,B_{r_{0}}
. Then,
{\mathbf{P}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{P}}_{r_{0}}
are disjoint once-holed tori, and

{\mathbf{P}}_{r_{0}+1},\ldots,{\mathbf{P}}_{r}
are
bona fide
pairs of pants falling into one of the first
two cases of Figure
8
.

As a consequence, for
j>r_{0}
, there are only two possible scenarios that can happen at the
j
-th
step in the construction of the pair of pants decomposition, which we shall call Case (a) and Case
(b); they are represented in Figure
9
.

(a)

C_{-}
and
C_{+}
are distinct (i.e. the bar
B_{j}^{\prime}
has endpoints on different
components of
\partial\tilde{\mathbf{S}}_{j}
), and hence
\mathbf{S}_{j}
has one more genus
and one less boundary component than
\mathbf{S}_{j-1}
.

(b)

C_{-}=C_{+}
(i.e. the bar
B_{j}^{\prime}
has endpoints on one unique component of

\partial\tilde{\mathbf{S}}_{j}
), and hence
\mathbf{S}_{j}
has one more boundary component than

\mathbf{S}_{j-1}
, and the same genus.

We will refer to these two cases in proofs by induction.

(a)

Case (a).

(b)

Case (b).

Figure 9
.
The two possible scenarios when constructing
\mathbf{P}_{j}
.

3.3.
Set of boundary components of the pair of pants decomposition

3.3.1.
Definition

Let us denote as
\Gamma=(\Gamma_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
the multi-curve

\Sigma_{r}
in the construction above, i.e. the multi-curve obtained by taking the boundary of the
pair of pants decomposition
\textbf{P}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{P}_{r}
of
\mathbf{S}
. It is indexed by
a set
\Lambda
with
3g_{\mathbf{S}}-3+2n_{\mathbf{S}}
elements.

Remark 3.6
.

The construction above provides a special representative of the homotopy class of each boundary
component
\Gamma_{\lambda}
, as a simple piecewise smooth curve made of a concatenation of bars

\overline{B}_{q}
and simple portions of
\beta
(see the forthcoming Figure
20
for an
example). More precisely, we will show later that the
\Gamma_{\lambda}
are what we call

polygonal curves
, as defined in Definition
8.1
. This property will play a key role
in §
4.4
and §
8
.

Remark 3.7
.

Extending Remark
2.18
, it will also be convenient to keep
in mind that each boundary component
\Gamma_{\lambda}
is freely homotopic to a piecewise smooth
curve
\Gamma_{\lambda,\mathrm{init}}
, included in the original
{\mathbf{c}}
, made of smooth
segments joining some of the intersection points of
\mathbf{c}
.

3.3.2.
Partitions of
\Lambda

Some of the elements of
\Lambda
are the boundary components of
\mathbf{S}
– we denote as

\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}
this set, which is in natural bijection with

\partial\mathbf{S}=\{1,\ldots,n_{\mathbf{S}}\}
. The other elements are inner curves, and we denote this
set as
\Lambda_{\mathrm{in}}:=\Lambda\setminus\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}
. We have
\#\Lambda_{\mathrm{in}}=3g_{\mathbf{S}}-3+n_{\mathbf{S}}
.

We notice that the multi-curve
\beta
is part of this pair of pants decomposition (because

\beta=\Sigma_{0}\subset\Sigma_{r}
). To reflect this, we define
\Lambda^{\beta}\subseteq\Lambda

to be the set of components of
\Gamma
which are also a component of the multi-curve

\beta
. This motivates the writing of the multi-curve
\beta
as

(\beta_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda^{\beta}}
introduced before, where

\Gamma_{\lambda}=\beta_{\lambda}
for
\beta\in\Lambda^{\beta}
.

The components of
\Gamma
which are
not
part of the multi-loop
\beta
, and have therefore
been created in the process of creating Fenchel–Nielsen parameters, will be denoted as

\Lambda^{\Gamma}:=\Lambda\setminus\Lambda^{\beta}
. The partition
\Lambda=\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}\sqcup\Lambda_{\mathrm{in}}
between boundary/inner components naturally induces two partitions
of
\Lambda^{\beta}
and
\Lambda^{\Gamma}
, defined by

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}^{\beta}:=\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}\cap\Lambda^{\beta}

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}^{\Gamma}:=\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}\cap\Lambda^{\Gamma}

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\mathrm{in}}^{\beta}:=\Lambda_{\mathrm{in}}\cap\Lambda^{\beta}

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\mathrm{in}}^{\Gamma}:=\Lambda_{\mathrm{in}}\cap\Lambda^{\Gamma}.

3.3.3.
Order on
\Lambda

We do not fix a numbering of the set
\Lambda
. However, it will be convenient to equip
\Lambda

with an order relation in the following way.

Definition 3.8
.

For
\lambda\in\Lambda
, we denote as
\mathrm{step}(\lambda)
the step of the induction when
the component
\Gamma_{\lambda}
is created, i.e.

\mathrm{step}(\lambda):=\min\{j\in\{1,\ldots,r\}:\,\Gamma_{\lambda}\text{ is a boundary component of }\mathbf{P}_{j}\}.

This induces an order on
\Lambda
, defined by writing, for
\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}\in\Lambda
,

\lambda<\lambda^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow\mathrm{step}(\lambda)<\mathrm{step}(\lambda^{\prime}).

The relation
\lambda<\lambda^{\prime}
simply mean that
\Gamma_{\lambda}
appeared earlier than

\Gamma_{\lambda^{\prime}}
in our recursive construction of the pants decomposition.

Remark 3.9
.

This order is strongly connected (for any
\lambda
,
\lambda^{\prime}
, either
\lambda\leq\lambda^{\prime}

or
\lambda^{\prime}\leq\lambda
) but a priori not anti-symmetric (there would typically be distinct
elements
\lambda\neq\lambda^{\prime}
with
\mathrm{step}(\lambda)=\mathrm{step}(\lambda^{\prime})
, since we
often create several boundary components at a given step of the induction).

Remark 3.10
.

In the proof by induction of §
3.4.4
, it
will be convenient to assume that the arbitrary choice of the numbering
\{1,\ldots,n_{\mathbf{S}}\}
of
the boundary of
\mathbf{S}
has been made so that the function

\lambda\mapsto\mathrm{step}(\lambda)
is non-decreasing as a function of

\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}=\{1,\ldots,n_{\mathbf{S}}\}
, i.e. for every
i<j
in
\{1,\ldots,n_{\mathbf{S}}\}
, the

j
-th boundary component of
\partial\mathbf{S}
appears after (or at the same time as) the

i
-th boundary component in the construction of the pair of pants decomposition. In
particular, the new components appearing in the last step, when we add the final pair of pants

\mathbf{P}_{r}
, are labelled
n_{\mathbf{S}}
in Case (a), or
n_{\mathbf{S}}
and
n_{\mathbf{S}}-1
in Case (b).

3.4.
Expression of the Weil–Petersson measure

We are now ready to state and prove our new expression for the Weil–Petersson measure in the
coordinates
(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})
.

3.4.1.
Expression in Fenchel–Nielsen coordinates

For given boundary lengths
\mathbf{x}
, once a decomposition of
\mathbf{S}
into pairs of pants

{\mathbf{P}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{P}}_{r}
is given, it is standard to consider Fenchel–Nielsen coordinates

(y_{\lambda},\alpha_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{in}}}
on

\mathcal{T}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}(\mathbf{x})
. The coordinates
y_{\lambda}
are the lengths

\ell_{Y}(\Gamma_{\lambda})
of the closed geodesics homotopic to
\Gamma_{\lambda}
for the metric

Y
. The coordinates
\alpha_{\lambda}
are twist parameters, defined up to translation. We will
explain in the proof of Theorem
3.11
how to adequately choose the origin of twist
parameters in relation with the multi-loop
{\mathbf{c}}
that we want to study. By work of
Wolpert
[
14
]
, we know that

(3.1)

\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}^{\mathrm{WP}}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}},\mathbf{x}}(Y)=\prod_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{in}}}\,\mathrm{d}y_{\lambda}\,\mathrm{d}\alpha_{\lambda}.

To obtain coordinates on
\mathcal{T}^{*}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}
, we simply multiply this by the
Lebesgue measure for the length parameters
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n_{\mathbf{S}}})
, in other words the collection
of
y_{\lambda}
for
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}
. Since
\Lambda=\Lambda_{\mathrm{in}}\cup\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}
,
we obtain the expression

(3.2)

\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}^{\mathrm{WP}}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}(\mathbf{x},Y)=\prod_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\,\mathrm{d}y_{\lambda}\prod_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{in}}}\,\mathrm{d}\alpha_{\lambda}.

3.4.2.
Main statement

We shall prove the following.

Theorem 3.11
.

Let
\mathbf{c}
be a generalized eight. For any diagram
\mathbf{D}
representing
\mathbf{c}
,
the map

(3.3)

\begin{cases}\mathcal{T}^{*}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}&\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{r}\times\mathbb{R}^{\Theta}\\
(\mathbf{x},Y)&\mapsto(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})\end{cases}

is a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-diffeomorphism onto its image
\mathfrak{D}
,
and

(3.4)

\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{n_{\mathbf{S}}}\sinh\left(\frac{x_{j}}{2}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}^{\mathrm{WP}}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}(\mathbf{x},Y)={2^{r}}\prod_{\lambda\in\Lambda^{\beta}}\sinh^{2}\left(\frac{y_{\lambda}}{2}\right)\prod_{i=1}^{r}\sinh(L_{i})\,\mathrm{d}^{r}\vec{L}\,\mathrm{d}^{2r}\vec{\theta}

where
y_{\lambda}=\ell_{Y}(\beta_{\lambda})
,
\,\mathrm{d}^{r}\vec{L}=\prod_{i=1}^{r}\,\mathrm{d}L_{i}
and

\,\mathrm{d}^{2r}\vec{\theta}=\prod_{i=1}^{r}\,\mathrm{d}\theta_{i}^{+}\,\mathrm{d}\theta_{i}^{-}
.

We insist on the fact that this formula holds for any coordinate system
(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})

obtained by opening the intersections of
\mathbf{c}
, meaning that intersection can be open in the
first or second way. This yields different new sets of coordinates.
We postpone to §
3.5
the description of the image
\mathfrak{D}
of the change of variable.

Remark 3.12
.

Earlier in the paper we assumed that
\mathbf{S}
was connected, but since the formula is multiplicative,
it will also hold for disconnected
\mathbf{S}
.

3.4.3.
Dirac variant

The integral that we want to evaluate, (
1.13
), actually relates to the measures

\prod_{j=1}^{k}x_{i_{j}}\delta(x_{i_{j}}-x_{i_{j}^{\prime}}){\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}^{\mathrm{WP}}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}(\mathbf{x},Y)},

where
V
is a subset of
\partial\mathbf{S}=\{1,\ldots,n_{\mathbf{S}}\}
and

\mathrm{m}=\bigsqcup_{j=1}^{k}\{i_{j},i_{j}^{\prime}\}
is a matching of
\partial\mathbf{S}\setminus V
.
Note that, by our conventions,
\partial\mathbf{S}
is identified with
\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}
, so that
V
can
be viewed as a subset of
\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}
. Theorem
3.11
allows us to write these measures in the new
coordinates.

Corollary 3.13
.

For any set
V\subseteq\partial\mathbf{S}
and any perfect
matching
\mathrm{m}=\bigsqcup_{j=1}^{k}\{i_{j},i_{j}^{\prime}\}
of
\partial\mathbf{S}\setminus V
,

(3.5)

\begin{split}&\prod_{j=1}^{k}x_{i_{j}}\delta(x_{i_{j}}-x_{i_{j}^{\prime}})\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}^{\mathrm{WP}}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}(\mathbf{x},Y)\\
&=2^{r}\frac{\prod_{\lambda\in V^{\beta}}\sinh\left(\frac{y_{\lambda}}{2}\right)\prod_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{in}}^{\beta}}\sinh^{2}\left(\frac{y_{\lambda}}{2}\right)}{\prod_{\lambda\in V^{\Gamma}}\sinh\left(\frac{y_{\lambda}}{2}\right)}T_{V,\mathrm{m}}(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})\prod_{i=1}^{r}\sinh(L_{i})\,\mathrm{d}^{r}\vec{L}\,\,\mathrm{d}^{2r}\vec{\theta}\end{split}

where
y_{\lambda}=\ell_{Y}(\Gamma_{\lambda})
for
\lambda\in\Lambda
,

V^{\beta}=V\cap\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}^{\beta}
,
V^{\Gamma}=V\cap\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}^{\Gamma}
, and

T_{V,\mathrm{m}}(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})
is the expression of the distribution

\frac{\prod_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}^{\beta}\setminus V^{\beta}}\sinh\left(\frac{y_{\lambda}}{2}\right)}{\prod_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}^{\Gamma}\setminus V^{\Gamma}}\sinh\left(\frac{y_{\lambda}}{2}\right)}\prod_{j=1}^{k}x_{i_{j}}\delta(x_{i_{j}}-x_{i_{j}^{\prime}})

in the new coordinates.

Proof.

We simply divide the previous expression by

\prod_{j=1}^{n_{\mathbf{S}}}\sinh\left(\frac{x_{j}}{2}\right)=\prod_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}}\sinh\left(\frac{y_{\lambda}}{2}\right)
, and
simplify the factors
\prod_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}^{\beta}}\sinh\left(\frac{y_{\lambda}}{2}\right)
which appear
simultanously at the numerator and denominator of the resulting fraction.
∎

We shall never need to develop the full explicit expression of
T_{V,\mathrm{m}}(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})
.

Note that, in the expression above, the lengths
y_{\lambda}
for

\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}\cup\Lambda^{\beta}
should all be expressed in terms of the variables

(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})
. Obtaining explicit expressions will be the goal of §
4
. The expression of
y_{\lambda}
for
\lambda\in\Lambda^{\beta}
is just a sum of the
variables
\theta_{q}
, but the expressions for
y_{\lambda}
when
\lambda\in\Lambda^{\Gamma}
will require more
work.

Whilst the lengths
y_{\lambda}
for
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{in}}^{\Gamma}
do not appear explicitely in the expression
(
3.5
), they are implicitely present in the domain of definition
\mathfrak{D}
of the
change of variable, as we will see in Proposition
3.21
.

3.4.4.
Proof of Theorem
3.11

The proof goes by induction on the number of intersections
r
.

The case
r=1
.

The case
r=1
corresponds to two possible situations:
{\mathbf{c}}

is either a figure-eight, or
{\mathbf{c}}=(c_{1},c_{2})
where
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
are two curves
intersecting once, filling a once-holed torus.

The first case is represented in Figure
10(a)
. The set of boundary components of the pair
of pants decomposition is
\Lambda=\partial\mathbf{S}=\{1,2,3\}
, and

\Lambda^{\beta}=\{1,2\}
so that
x_{1}=y_{1}=\theta_{1}^{+}
and
x_{2}=y_{2}=\theta_{1}^{-}
. Noting that, by
cutting the pair of pants along its orthogeodesic
B_{1}
, we obtain a convex right-angled hexagon,
the classic trigonometric formula
[
5
, Theorem 2.4.1 (i)]
yields

\cosh(L_{1})=\frac{\cosh\left(\frac{x_{1}}{2}\right)\cosh\left(\frac{x_{2}}{2}\right)+\cosh\left(\frac{x_{3}}{2}\right)}{\sinh\left(\frac{x_{1}}{2}\right)\sinh\left(\frac{x_{2}}{2}\right)},

hence
\sinh\left(\frac{x_{3}}{2}\right)\,\mathrm{d}x_{3}=2\sinh(L_{1})\sinh\left(\frac{x_{1}}{2}\right)\sinh\left(\frac{x_{2}}{2}\right)\,\mathrm{d}L_{1}
, or equivalently

\sinh\left(\frac{x_{1}}{2}\right)\sinh\left(\frac{x_{2}}{2}\right)\sinh\left(\frac{x_{3}}{2}\right)\,\mathrm{d}x_{1}\,\mathrm{d}x_{2}\,\mathrm{d}x_{3}=2\sinh^{2}\left(\frac{y_{1}}{2}\right)\sinh^{2}\left(\frac{y_{2}}{2}\right)\sinh(L_{1})\,\mathrm{d}L_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\theta_{1}^{-}\,\mathrm{d}\theta_{1}^{+}

as announced.

(a)

The pair of pants.

(b)

The once-holed torus.

Figure 10
.
The two cases where
r=1
.

In the second case, the surface
\mathbf{S}
is a once-holed torus,
\beta
is a curve on

\mathbf{S}
, and
\Lambda
has two components,
\beta
and
\partial\mathbf{S}
. The length of

\beta
is
y_{1}=\theta_{1}^{+}+\theta_{1}^{-}
, and we notice that the parameter
\theta_{1}^{+}
can be
chosen to be a twist parameter. The Weil–Petersson measure for a once-holed torus of boundary
length
x_{1}
can then be written

\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}^{\mathrm{WP}}_{1,1,x_{1}}(Y)=\,\mathrm{d}y_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\theta_{1}^{+}=\,\mathrm{d}\theta_{1}^{-}\,\mathrm{d}\theta_{1}^{+}.

We can conclude by observing that

\cosh\left(\frac{x_{1}}{2}\right)=\cosh^{2}\left(\frac{y_{1}}{2}\right)-\sinh^{2}\left(\frac{y_{1}}{2}\right)\cosh(L_{1})
, as in the pair of pants case
above, and hence

\sinh\left(\frac{x_{1}}{2}\right)\,\mathrm{d}x_{1}=2\sinh^{2}\left(\frac{y_{1}}{2}\right)\sinh(L_{1})\,\mathrm{d}L_{1}.

∎

Let us now perform the induction.

Proof of the induction of Theorem
3.11
.

Let
r\geq 2
, and
\mathbf{c}
be a generalized eight with
r
self-intersections filling a
surface
\mathbf{S}
, with a choice of opening for each intersection point. Let
{\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}

denote the multi-loop obtained by removing the last intersection point from
{\mathbf{c}}
the
first way (as described in §
2.4
). Then,
\mathbf{c}^{\prime}
is a generalized eight with

r-1
intersections, and fills the surface
\mathbf{S}_{r-1}
. We assume that the proposition is
true for the surface
\mathbf{S}_{r-1}
and the multi-loop
\mathbf{c}^{\prime}
(with intersections
opened as they are for
\mathbf{c}
), and want to prove it for
\mathbf{S}=\mathbf{S}_{r}
by
attaching the pair of pants
\mathbf{P}_{r}
.

If
\mathbf{S}_{r}
is a disjoint union of once-holed tori, then the property is a direct
consequence of the result for
r=1
, thanks to its multiplicativity. We hence assume that it
is not the case. We therefore have two cases to consider, Case (a) and Case (b).

Let
\vec{L}^{\prime}
and
\vec{\theta}^{\prime}=(\theta_{1}^{\prime\pm},\ldots,\theta_{r-1}^{\prime\pm})
be the
parameters associated to the surface
\mathbf{S}_{r-1}
and to
{\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}
, as constructed in
§
3.1
. We observe that
\vec{L}^{\prime}=(L_{1},\ldots,L_{r-1})
are the lengths of

\overline{B}_{1},\ldots,\overline{B}_{r-1}
, and in particular
\vec{L}^{\prime}
is the same as

\vec{L}
with the last component removed.

The parameters
(\theta_{q}^{\prime})_{q\in\{1,\ldots,r-1\}\times\{\pm\}}
, are defined as the lengths
of simple portions
\overline{\mathcal{I}}_{q}^{\prime}
contained in
\beta
and delimited by the endpoints of the
bars
\overline{B}_{1},\ldots,\overline{B}_{r-1}
. We can therefore easily relate
\vec{\theta}^{\prime}

and
\vec{\theta}
: among the
2(r-1)
segments
\overline{\mathcal{I}}_{q}^{\prime}
, two of them contain the
endpoints of
\overline{B}_{r}
. All the other
\overline{\mathcal{I}}_{q}^{\prime}
are also intervals

\overline{\mathcal{I}}_{q}
. As a consequence, for
k\in\{1,\ldots,r-1\}
,
\epsilon=\pm
, we have

\theta_{k}^{\epsilon}=\theta_{k}^{\prime\epsilon}
for all except two values of
(k,\epsilon)
, namely

(n_{+},\epsilon_{+}),(n_{-},\epsilon_{-})
, for which we have the simple relations

(3.6)

\displaystyle\theta_{n_{+}}^{\prime\epsilon_{+}}=\theta_{n_{+}}^{\epsilon_{+}}+\theta_{r}^{+}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\theta_{n_{-}}^{\prime\epsilon_{-}}=\theta_{n_{-}}^{\epsilon_{-}}+\theta_{r}^{-}.

We now proceed differently depending on the case to consider, (a) or (b).

Case (a).

In this case,
\mathbf{S}_{r}
is obtained from
\mathbf{S}_{r-1}
by gluing
\mathbf{P}_{r}
to
two of its boundary components. Let
\lambda_{\pm}\in\Lambda
denote the indices of the two
boundary components of
\mathbf{S}_{r-1}
on which we glue the pair of pants
\mathbf{P}_{r}
,
which satisfy
\mathrm{step}(\lambda_{\pm})<r
. By Remark
3.10
, these two boundary
components of
\mathbf{S}_{r-1}
are replaced by the
n_{\mathbf{S}}
-th boundary component of

\mathbf{S}=\mathbf{S}_{r}
when
{\mathbf{P}}_{r}
is attached to
\mathbf{S}_{r-1}
.

Because the endpoints of the orthogeodesic
\overline{B}^{+}_{r}
are on the multi-loop
\beta
,
there exist
i_{\pm}
such that the bar
\overline{B}_{r}^{+}
goes from the component
\beta_{i_{-}}

of
\beta
to the component
\beta_{i_{+}}
of
\beta
. It is homotopic (with endpoints gliding
along
\beta
) to a succession of
5
orthogonal segments of respective lengths

t_{r}^{-},\alpha_{r}^{-},T,\alpha_{r}^{+},t_{r}^{+}
, as shown on Figure
11
. More precisely,
we can lift the four geodesics
\beta_{i_{-}}
,
\Gamma_{\lambda_{-}}
,
\Gamma_{\lambda_{+}}
and

\beta_{i_{+}}
to the hyperbolic plane, and orient them so that they are aligned and the bar

\overline{B}^{+}_{r}
arrives on the right of
\beta_{i_{+}}
. Then, we define:

•

H^{\pm}
to be the orthogeodesics between
\beta_{i_{\pm}}
and
\Gamma_{\lambda_{\pm}}
, and

H
between
\Gamma_{\lambda_{-}}
and
\Gamma_{\lambda_{+}}
;

•

t_{r}^{\pm}
and
T
to be the respective (positive) lengths of
H^{\pm}
and
H
;

•

the quantities
\alpha_{r}^{+}
and
\alpha_{r}^{-}
to denote the algebraic distance between the
endpoints of
H^{+}
and
H
along
\Gamma_{\lambda_{+}}
and between the endpoints of
H
and

H^{-}
along
\Gamma_{\lambda_{-}}
respectively.

It is important in our discussion to remark that
H^{\pm}
are contained in
\mathbf{S}_{r-1}
, so
that their lengths depend only on
Y_{\mathbf{S}_{r-1}}
, the restriction of the metric
Y
to

\mathbf{S}_{r-1}
.

Figure 11
.
The geometric construction in Case (a). The black dots symbolize the right-angles
between the different segments.

In order to express the Weil–Petersson measure for
\mathbf{S}_{r}
, and relate it to the
Weil–Petersson measure for
\mathbf{S}_{r-1}
, we need to define twist parameters along the
glued components
\Gamma_{\lambda_{\pm}}
. We choose them to be the algebraic lengths

\alpha_{r}^{\pm}
. As a consequence, the expression of the Weil–Petersson measure in
Fenchel–Nielsen coordinates recalled in §
3.4
implies the factorization

(3.7)

\displaystyle\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}^{\mathrm{WP}}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}},\mathbf{x}}(Y)=\,\mathrm{d}\alpha_{r}^{+}\,\mathrm{d}\alpha_{r}^{-}\,\mathrm{d}y_{\lambda_{+}}\,\mathrm{d}y_{\lambda_{-}}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}^{\mathrm{WP}}_{g_{\mathbf{S}}-1,n_{\mathbf{S}}+1,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}(Y_{\mathbf{S}_{r-1}}),

where
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n_{\mathbf{S}}})
and

\mathbf{x}^{\prime}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n_{\mathbf{S}}-1},y_{\lambda_{+}},y_{\lambda_{-}})
correspond to the respective
length-vectors of
\mathbf{S}_{r}
and
\mathbf{S}_{r-1}
.

We can now, as in the case
r=1
, use the classic trigonometric formula for convex right-angled
hexagons
[
5
, Theorem 2.4.1 (i)]
, which yields

(3.8)

\displaystyle\cosh(T)=\frac{\cosh\left(\frac{y_{\lambda_{+}}}{2}\right)\cosh\left(\frac{y_{\lambda_{-}}}{2}\right)+\cosh\left(\frac{x_{n_{\mathbf{S}}}}{2}\right)}{\sinh\left(\frac{y_{\lambda_{+}}}{2}\right)\sinh\left(\frac{y_{\lambda_{-}}}{2}\right)},

which implies that the map
x_{n_{\mathbf{S}}}\mapsto T
is a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-diffeomorphism onto its image,
and

(3.9)

\sinh\left(\frac{x_{n_{\mathbf{S}}}}{2}\right)\,\mathrm{d}x_{n_{\mathbf{S}}}=2\sinh\left(\frac{y_{\lambda_{+}}}{2}\right)\sinh\left(\frac{y_{\lambda_{-}}}{2}\right)\sinh(T)\,\mathrm{d}T.

It follows that

\prod_{j=1}^{n_{\mathbf{S}}}\sinh\left(\frac{x_{j}}{2}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}^{\mathrm{WP}}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}(\mathbf{x},Y)
is equal to

\displaystyle 2\sinh(T)\,\mathrm{d}T\,\mathrm{d}\alpha_{r}^{+}\,\mathrm{d}\alpha_{r}^{-}\prod_{j=1}^{n_{\mathbf{S}}-1}\sinh\left(\frac{x_{j}}{2}\right)\sinh\left(\frac{y_{\lambda_{+}}}{2}\right)\sinh\left(\frac{y_{\lambda_{-}}}{2}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}^{\mathrm{WP}}_{g_{\mathbf{S}}-1,n_{\mathbf{S}}+1}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},Y_{\mathbf{S}_{r-1}})

which, using the induction hypothesis, implies that

(3.10)

\begin{split}&\prod_{j=1}^{n_{\mathbf{S}}}\sinh\left(\frac{x_{j}}{2}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}^{\mathrm{WP}}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}(\mathbf{x},Y)\\
&={2^{r}}\sinh(T)\,\mathrm{d}T\,\mathrm{d}\alpha_{r}^{+}\,\mathrm{d}\alpha_{r}^{-}\prod_{\lambda\in\Lambda^{\beta}}\sinh^{2}\left(\frac{y_{\lambda}}{2}\right)\prod_{i=1}^{r-1}\sinh(L_{i})\,\mathrm{d}L_{i}\prod_{k=1}^{r-1}\,\mathrm{d}\theta_{k}^{\prime+}\,\mathrm{d}\theta_{k}^{\prime-}\end{split}

where we use the observation that the length-vector
\vec{L}^{\prime}
is equal to

(L_{1},\ldots,L_{r-1})
.

Let us now denote by
\epsilon_{r}^{+}
and
\epsilon^{-}_{r}
the algebraic distance from the endpoint of
H^{+}

to the endpoint of
\overline{B}_{r}
along
\beta_{i_{+}}
, and from the endpoint of

\overline{B}_{r}
to the endpoint of
H^{-}
along
\beta_{i_{-}}
respectively, as represented in
Figure
11
. Let us temporarily admit the following formula (which will be
checked in §
3.4.5
):

Lemma 3.14
.

Given a fixed metric
Y_{\mathbf{S}_{r-1}}
, the map

(\alpha_{r}^{-},\alpha_{r}^{+},T)\mapsto(\epsilon_{r}^{-},\epsilon_{r}^{+},L_{r})
is a

\mathcal{C}^{1}
-diffeomorphism onto its image, and

(3.11)

\displaystyle\sinh(T)\,\mathrm{d}T\,\mathrm{d}\alpha_{r}^{-}\,\mathrm{d}\alpha_{r}^{+}=\sinh(L_{r})\,\mathrm{d}L_{r}\,\mathrm{d}\epsilon_{r}^{-}\,\mathrm{d}\epsilon_{r}^{+}.

We can now remark that

(3.12)

\displaystyle\theta_{r}^{+}+\epsilon_{r}^{+}=\mathrm{fn}(Y_{\mathbf{S}_{r-1}})\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\theta_{r}^{-}-\epsilon_{r}^{-}=\mathrm{fn}(Y_{\mathbf{S}_{r-1}}).

Indeed, for instance, the first quantity is the signed
distance (measured along
\beta_{i_{+}}
) between the extremity of
H^{+}
lying in
\beta_{i_{+}}
, and
one of the bars
\overline{B}_{m_{+}}
with
m_{+}<r
. See Figure
12
.

Figure 12
.
The relations between the different algebraic lengths along
\beta_{i_{+}}
.

The relation (
3.12
) implies that in (
3.11
), we can change variables

(\epsilon_{r}^{-},\epsilon_{r}^{+})\rightsquigarrow(\theta_{r}^{-},\theta_{r}^{+})
and replace

\,\mathrm{d}\epsilon_{r}^{-}\,\mathrm{d}\epsilon_{r}^{+}
by
\,\mathrm{d}\theta_{r}^{-}\,\mathrm{d}\theta_{r}^{+}
. Our expression (
3.10
) then
becomes

2^{r}\sinh(L_{r})\,\mathrm{d}L_{r}\,\mathrm{d}\theta_{r}^{-}\,\mathrm{d}\theta_{r}^{+}\prod_{\lambda\in\Lambda^{\beta}}\sinh^{2}\left(\frac{y_{\lambda}}{2}\right)\prod_{i=1}^{r-1}\sinh(L_{i})\,\mathrm{d}L_{i}\prod_{k=1}^{r-1}\,\mathrm{d}\theta_{k}^{\prime+}\,\mathrm{d}\theta_{k}^{\prime-}.

Now recall the relations between
\theta_{k}^{\epsilon}
and
\theta_{k}^{\prime\epsilon}
. For
k\leq r-1

and
\epsilon=\pm
, those parameters are equal, except from two sets of indices, for which these
values differ by a translation by (
3.6
). Hence, our final expression for the measure
is the announced one.
∎

Remark 3.15
.

Part of this discussion is irrelevant when
\beta_{i_{-}}=\Gamma_{\lambda_{-}}
, because in this
case,
H^{-}
and
\alpha_{r}^{-}
are ill-defined. The previous discussion has to be modified as
follows.

•

If
\beta_{i_{-}}
is a boundary curve, then there is no twist parameter attached to it and
there is no bar
\overline{B}_{k}
arriving at
\beta_{i_{-}}
on the other side of

\overline{B}_{r}
: we have
\theta_{r}^{-}=x_{i_{-}}=y_{i_{-}}
.

•

If
\beta_{i_{-}}
is not a boundary curve, then we can take directly
\theta_{r}^{-}
as twist
parameter, instead of the ill-defined
\alpha_{r}^{-}
.

In all cases, the final outcome of the calculation is the same, with less intermediate steps.
Similar remarks can be made when
\beta_{i_{+}}=\Gamma_{\lambda_{+}}
. We skip details specific to
these cases, similar and simpler than the one we treated.

Case (b).

In this case, we attach
\mathbf{P}_{r}
to one boundary component

\Gamma_{\lambda}
of
\mathbf{S}_{r-1}
(with
\mathrm{step}(\lambda)<r
), and get the two new
boundary components of
\mathbf{S}_{r}
labelled
n_{\mathbf{S}}
and
n_{\mathbf{S}}-1
.

Figure 13
.
The geometric construction in Case (b).

The situation is quite similar to Case (a) with a small difference, namely the fact that

\lambda_{+}=\lambda_{-}
; we therefore denote them
\lambda
. There exist
i_{\pm}
such that the
orthogeodesic
\overline{B}_{r}^{+}
goes from
\beta_{i_{-}}
to
\beta_{i_{+}}
. It is homotopic to a
succession of
5
orthogonal segments of respective lengths
t_{r}^{-},\alpha_{r}^{-},T,\alpha_{r}^{+},t_{r}^{+}
,
as shown on Figure
13
. Note that, when lifting Figure
11
and

13
in the universal cover, we obtain the same picture, the only difference being
that the lifts of
\Gamma_{\lambda_{\pm}}
now are two different lifts of the same periodic geodesic

\Gamma_{\lambda}
. We therefore define the orthogeodesics
H^{\pm}
and
H
, as well as the lengths

t_{r}^{\pm}
,
\alpha_{r}^{\pm}
and
T
as in Case (a).

We choose the twist parameter to be
\alpha_{r}^{+}
. As a consequence, the expression of the
Weil–Petersson measure in Fenchel–Nielsen coordinates implies the factorization

(3.13)

\displaystyle\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}^{\mathrm{WP}}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}},\mathbf{x}}(Y)=\,\mathrm{d}\alpha_{r}^{+}\,\mathrm{d}y_{\lambda}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}^{\mathrm{WP}}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}-1,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}(Y_{\mathbf{S}_{r-1}})

where
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n_{\mathbf{S}}-2},y_{\lambda})
.

We can now use the following formulas:

(3.14)

\displaystyle\cosh\left(\frac{x_{n_{\mathbf{S}}}}{2}\right)=\sinh\left(\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)\sinh\left(\frac{T}{2}\right)

(3.15)

\displaystyle\cosh\left(\frac{x_{n_{\mathbf{S}}-1}}{2}\right)=\sinh\left(\frac{y_{\lambda}-\alpha}{2}\right)\sinh\left(\frac{T}{2}\right),

where
\alpha
is the distance between the two endpoints of the orthogeodesic
H
measured along

\Gamma_{\lambda}
, as shown on Figure
14
.

Figure 14
.
The length
\alpha
in the pair of pants
\mathbf{P}_{r}
.

As a consequence, we make the following observation, replacing equation (
3.9
) in
Case (a), obtained by calculating a
2\times 2
determinant: for fixed
y_{\lambda}
, the map

(x_{n_{\mathbf{S}}-1},x_{n_{\mathbf{S}}})\mapsto(\alpha,T)
is a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-diffeomorphism onto its image, and

\displaystyle\sinh\left(\frac{x_{n_{\mathbf{S}}-1}}{2}\right)\sinh\left(\frac{x_{n_{\mathbf{S}}}}{2}\right)\,\mathrm{d}x_{n_{\mathbf{S}}-1}\,\mathrm{d}x_{n_{\mathbf{S}}}=2\sinh\left(\frac{y_{\lambda}}{2}\right)\sinh(T)\,\mathrm{d}\alpha\,\mathrm{d}T.

The rest of the proof goes along the same lines as in Case (a) and we skip details.
∎

Finally, to conclude, in all cases, the fact that the map

(\mathbf{x},Y)\in\mathcal{T}^{*}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}\mapsto(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})
is a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-diffeomorphism onto
its image can also obtained by induction, since at each step we checked that the maps were

\mathcal{C}^{1}
-diffeomorphisms onto their images.
∎

3.4.5.
Proof of Lemma
3.14

To finish this section,
there remains to prove Lemma
3.14
. We also start investigating the range of
definition of
\vec{L}
and
\vec{\theta}
.

Let us consider a family of
M
aligned geodesics
\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{M}
on the hyperbolic
plane, such that, for all
i
,
\gamma_{i+1}
is on the left of
\gamma_{i}
(for now, we will only
take
M=3
and
4
, but more general situations will arise in §
7
). For indices

i\neq j
, let
H_{ij}
denote the orthogeodesic between
\gamma_{i}
and
\gamma_{j}
and
z_{ij}
the
endpoint of
H_{ij}
lying on
\gamma_{j}
.
Let us first prove one intermediate lemma in a three-geodesic configuration, illustrated in Figure

15
.

Lemma 3.16
.

Let
M=3
. We denote as
L
,
t
,
T
the following orthogeodesic lengths

L=\ell(H_{13})\qquad t=\ell(H_{23})\qquad T=\ell(H_{12})

and
\epsilon
,
\alpha
the algebraic distances

\epsilon=\vec{\mathrm{d}}(z_{23},z_{13})\qquad\qquad\alpha=\vec{\mathrm{d}}(z_{32},z_{12}).

Then, for fixed
t>0
, the map

\displaystyle\psi_{t}:\begin{cases}\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}&\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}\\
(\alpha,T)&\mapsto(\epsilon,L)\end{cases}

is a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-diffeomorphism onto the set of real numbers

(\epsilon,L)
such that

(3.16)

\begin{cases}\cosh(t)\cosh(L)-\cosh(\epsilon)\sinh(t)\sinh(L)>1\\
L>t,\end{cases}

and its Jacobian can be expressed as

(3.17)

\displaystyle\sinh(T)\,\mathrm{d}T\,\mathrm{d}\alpha=\sinh(L)\,\mathrm{d}L\,\mathrm{d}\epsilon.

Figure 15
.
The three geodesics and the lengths we will study. The dots mark the points
z_{ij}
,
where the geodesics meet perpendicularly.

Proof.

The fact that the image of
\{\alpha\in\mathbb{R},T>0\}
is included in the set of
(\epsilon,L)

satisfying the conditions (
3.16
) can be read from standard formulas for right-angled
hexagons in hyperbolic geometry, see e.g.
[
5
, Ch. 2 §4]
:

(3.18)

\displaystyle\cosh(T)=\cosh(t)\cosh(L)-\cosh(\epsilon)\sinh(t)\sinh(L)

(3.19)

\displaystyle\cosh(L)=\cosh(t)\cosh(T)+\cosh(\alpha)\sinh(t)\sinh(T).

Conversely, if (
3.16
) holds for parameters
(L,\epsilon)
, then there exists
T\not=0

such that (
3.18
) holds. This defines
T
up to the sign, and we choose the positive
solution. Another classical hyperbolic trigonometry formula states that, for this geometric
situation to hold, we must have

(3.20)

\frac{\sinh(\epsilon)}{\sinh(T)}=\frac{\sinh(\alpha)}{\sinh(L)}.

One can then define
\alpha
so that the relation (
3.20
) holds. Note that
\alpha
has the
same sign as
\epsilon
.
By using
\cosh^{2}(L)=1+\sinh^{2}(L)
and the two relations (
3.18
) and (
3.20
),
one then deduces that

(\cosh(L)-\cosh(t)\cosh(T))^{2}=(\cosh(\alpha)\sinh(t)\sinh(T))^{2}

or,
in other words,

\cosh(L)=\cosh(t)\cosh(T)\pm\cosh(\alpha)\sinh(t)\sinh(T).

Because we
assumed that
L>t
, we actually obtain that (
3.19
) holds,
which means that
L
is indeed the length of the orthogeodesic
H_{13}
on Figure

15
.

The determinant calculation (
3.17
) follows from (
3.19
) and (
3.20
), but
it is worth writing some details. Start writing the jacobian of the change of variable as the
determinant of the matrix of partial derivatives:

\displaystyle\cosh(\epsilon)\,\frac{\,\mathrm{d}L\,\mathrm{d}\epsilon}{\,\mathrm{d}T\,\mathrm{d}\alpha}=\frac{\partial L}{\partial T}\frac{\partial(\sinh(\epsilon))}{\partial\alpha}-\frac{\partial L}{\partial\alpha}\frac{\partial(\sinh(\epsilon))}{\partial T}.

Because of the relation (
3.20
), we can rewrite this as

\displaystyle\cosh(\epsilon)\,\frac{\,\mathrm{d}L\,\mathrm{d}\epsilon}{\,\mathrm{d}T\,\mathrm{d}\alpha}=\frac{\partial L}{\partial T}\frac{\cosh(\alpha)\sinh(T)}{\sinh(L)}-\frac{\partial L}{\partial\alpha}\frac{\sinh(\alpha)\cosh(T)}{\sinh(L)}.

An elementary calculation of
\partial L/\partial T
and
\partial L/\partial\alpha
from
(
3.19
) yields that

\displaystyle\cosh(\epsilon)\,\frac{\,\mathrm{d}L\,\mathrm{d}\epsilon}{\,\mathrm{d}T\,\mathrm{d}\alpha}=\frac{\sinh(T)}{\sinh^{2}(L)}(\sinh(t)\cosh(T)+\cosh(t)\sinh(T)\cosh(\alpha)).

Using another classical trigonometric formula, we recognize the quantity in the bracket to be

\cosh(\epsilon)\sinh(L)
, and hence this simplifies to

\displaystyle\frac{\,\mathrm{d}L\,\mathrm{d}\epsilon}{\,\mathrm{d}T\,\mathrm{d}\alpha}=\frac{\sinh(T)}{\sinh(L)}

which is the desired relation.
∎

Remark 3.17
.

We actually have
L\geq t+T
, so for any given
a>0
, the set
\{\alpha\in\mathbb{R},T>a\}
is
sent to a subset of the set of the set of values
(\epsilon,L)
such that

\begin{cases}\cosh(t)\cosh(L)-\cosh(\epsilon)\sinh(t)\sinh(L)\geq\cosh(a)\\
L\geq t+a.\end{cases}

Notation 3.18
.

This result provides us with a map
\phi_{t}:(\epsilon,L)\mapsto\vec{\mathrm{d}}(z_{31},z_{21})
, which
corresponds to the algebraic distance along the bottom geodesic.

In order to prove Lemma
3.14
, we simply apply Lemma
3.16
twice in a
four-geodesic configuration, as done below. See also Figure
16
.

Figure 16
.
The four-geodesic configuration we consider. We have represented the quantities

\tilde{T}^{+}
and
\theta^{+}
appearing in the statement.

Lemma 3.19
.

Let
M=4
. We denote as
L
,
t^{\pm}
and
T
the orthogeodesic lengths

L=\ell(H_{14})\qquad t^{+}=\ell(H_{34})\qquad t^{-}=\ell(H_{12})\qquad T=\ell(H_{23})

and
\epsilon^{\pm}
,
\alpha^{\pm}
the algebraic lengths

\epsilon^{+}=\vec{\mathrm{d}}(z_{34},z_{14})\qquad\alpha^{+}=\vec{\mathrm{d}}(z_{43},z_{23})\qquad\alpha^{-}=\vec{\mathrm{d}}(z_{32},z_{12})\qquad\epsilon^{-}=\vec{\mathrm{d}}(z_{41},z_{21}).

For fixed
t^{-},t^{+}>0
, the map

\begin{cases}\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}&\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}\\
(\alpha^{-},\alpha^{+},T)&\mapsto(\epsilon^{-},\epsilon^{+},L)\end{cases}

is a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-diffeomorphism onto the set of real numbers

(\epsilon^{-},\epsilon^{+},L)
such that

(3.21)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\cosh(t^{+})\cosh(L)-\cosh(\epsilon^{+})\sinh(t^{+})\sinh(L)>1\\
\cosh(t^{-})\cosh(\tilde{T}^{+})-\cosh(\epsilon^{-}-\theta^{+})\sinh(t^{-})\sinh(\tilde{T}^{+})>1\\
L>t^{+}\quad\text{and}\quad\tilde{T}^{+}>t^{-},\end{cases}

where
\tilde{T}^{+}
is the second component of
\psi_{t^{+}}^{-1}(\epsilon^{+},L)
and

\theta^{+}:=\phi_{t^{+}}(\epsilon^{+},L)
. Its Jacobian can be expressed as

(3.22)

\displaystyle\sinh(T)\,\mathrm{d}T\,\mathrm{d}\alpha^{-}\,\mathrm{d}\alpha^{+}=\sinh(L)\,\mathrm{d}L\,\mathrm{d}\epsilon^{-}\,\mathrm{d}\epsilon^{+}.

Proof.

We simply apply the previous result twice, once to the three geodesics
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{3},\gamma_{4}
, and another time to
\gamma_{3},\gamma_{2},\gamma_{1}
with reverse orientation.
∎

Remark 3.20
.

We always have
L\geq t^{+}+T+t^{-}
and
\tilde{T}^{+}\geq t^{-}+T
. Hence, for
any
a>0
, the set
\{\alpha^{-},\alpha^{+}\in\mathbb{R},T>a\}
corresponds to a subset of the set of real
numbers
(\epsilon^{-},\epsilon^{+},L)
such that

\begin{cases}\cosh(t^{+})\cosh(L)-\cosh(\epsilon^{+})\sinh(t^{+})\sinh(L)\geq\cosh(a)\\
\cosh(t^{-})\cosh(\tilde{T}^{+})-\cosh(\epsilon^{-}-\theta^{+})\sinh(t^{-})\sinh(\tilde{T}^{+})\geq\cosh(a)\\
L>t^{+}+a\quad\text{and}\quad\tilde{T}^{+}>t^{-}+a.\end{cases}

3.5.
Domain of definition of the new coordinates

In this section, we discuss the range of definition
\mathfrak{D}
of the coordinates

(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})
, which will be the domain of the integral (
1.13
) in the new
coordinates. We prove the following statement, which describes the boundary of
\mathfrak{D}
.

Proposition 3.21
.

The boundary of
\mathfrak{D}
is contained in

\bigcup_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\{y_{\lambda}=0\}
.

Proof.

We once again proceed by induction, closely following the proof of Theorem
3.11
but
making the domain of definition of the variables more explicit.

The result is trivial for
r=1
. Let us fix a metric

Y_{\mathbf{S}_{r-1}}\in\mathcal{T}_{g_{\mathbf{S}_{r-1}},n_{\mathbf{S}_{r-1}}}^{*}
and describe the
domain of definition of the three new coordinates
(\theta_{r}^{-},\theta_{r}^{+},L_{r})
defined when
attaching the pair of pants
{\mathbf{P}}_{r}
. We note that there exists a unique hyperbolic metric
associated to any set of Fenchel–Nielsen coordinates. Hence, the constraints on the new variables
are:

•

(\epsilon_{r}^{+},\epsilon_{r}^{-},L_{r})
need to belong to the image of the
diffeomorphism in Lemma
3.19
– we call this the
local constraint
;

•

the new boundary components of the pair of pants need to be of positive
length, i.e.
x_{n_{\mathbf{S}}}>0
in Case (a) and
x_{n_{\mathbf{S}}},x_{n_{\mathbf{S}}-1}>0
in Case (b) – we call this the

global constraint
.

We prove Proposition
3.21
by proving that the global constraints are stronger than the
local constraints.
Indeed, in Case (a), because of (
3.8
), the global constraint is equivalent to

\cosh(T)>\frac{\cosh\left(\frac{y_{\lambda_{+}}}{2}\right)\cosh\left(\frac{y_{\lambda_{-}}}{2}\right)+1}{\sinh\left(\frac{y_{\lambda_{+}}}{2}\right)\sinh\left(\frac{y_{\lambda_{-}}}{2}\right)}.

Note that the quantity

a:=\argcosh\Big(\frac{\cosh\left(\frac{y_{\lambda_{+}}}{2}\right)\cosh\left(\frac{y_{\lambda_{-}}}{2}\right)+1}{\sinh\left(\frac{y_{\lambda_{+}}}{2}\right)\sinh\left(\frac{y_{\lambda_{-}}}{2}\right)}\Big)>0,

only depends
y_{\lambda_{+}}
and
y_{\lambda_{-}}
, i.e. on the metric
Y_{\mathbf{S}_{r-1}}
in

\mathcal{T}_{g_{\mathbf{S}}-1,n_{\mathbf{S}}+1}^{*}
that we are considering to be fixed. Hence, the global constraint implies the

T>a
, where
a
is fixed. By Remark
3.20
, this implies strictly stronger conditions than
the original local constraints, which is what we wanted to check.

In Case (b), the formulas (
3.14
) and (
3.15
)
are equivalent to

\cosh(T)=\frac{\cosh^{2}\left(\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)+\cosh(x_{n_{\mathbf{S}}})}{\sinh^{2}\left(\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\cosh({{T}})=\frac{\cosh^{2}\left(\frac{y_{\lambda}-\alpha}{2}\right)+\cosh(x_{n_{\mathbf{S}}-1})}{\sinh^{2}\left(\frac{y_{\lambda}-\alpha}{2}\right)}.

Thus the new global constraints are equivalent to

\cosh({{T}})>\frac{\cosh^{2}\left(\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)+1}{\sinh^{2}\left(\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)}=1+\frac{2}{\sinh^{2}\left(\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\cosh({{T}})>1+\frac{2}{\sinh^{2}\left(\frac{y_{\lambda}-\alpha}{2}\right)}.

Define
a:=\argch(1+2/\sinh^{2}\left(\frac{y_{\lambda}}{2}\right))
. Since

0<\alpha<y_{\lambda}
, the two global constraints imply that
T>a>0
, which allows to
conclude as in Case (a).
∎

3.6.
A priori bounds on Fenchel–Nielsen parameters

We shall need some rough a priori bounds on the parameters
y_{\lambda}=\ell_{Y}(\Gamma_{\lambda})
for

\lambda\in\Lambda
and
\theta_{q}
for
q\in\Theta
, that grow at most linearly with respect
to
\ell_{Y}({\mathbf{c}})
.

Proposition 3.22
.

On the level set

\{Y\in\mathcal{T}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}^{*}\,:\,\ell_{Y}({\mathbf{c}})=\ell\}
, we have

(3.23)

\displaystyle\forall\lambda\in\Lambda,

\displaystyle\quad 0\leq y_{\lambda}\leq\ell

(3.24)

\displaystyle\forall q\in\Theta,

\displaystyle\quad|\theta_{q}|\leq 2\ell

and the twist parameters
(\alpha_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{in}}}
constructed in the proof of
Theorem
3.11
satisfy

(3.25)

\displaystyle\forall\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{in}},\quad|\alpha_{\lambda}|\leq 3\ell.

The proof relies on elementary hyperbolic trigonometry, and in particular the following lemma.

Lemma 3.23
.

Let
Y
be a compact hyperbolic surface with two simple, closed geodesics
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}
. We assume that
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
are either disjoint or equal. Let
H
be a
simple orthogeodesic segment going from
\gamma_{1}
to
\gamma_{2}
, not intersecting
\gamma_{1}

and
\gamma_{2}
in its interior. Then,
\sinh(\ell(\gamma_{1})/2)\sinh(\ell(H))\geq 1
.

Proof.

If
\gamma_{1}=\gamma_{2}
, this is a direct consequence of the collar lemma
[
5
, Theorem
4.1.1]
. If the two geodesics are disjoint, then the surface filled by
\gamma_{1}
,

\gamma_{2}
and
H
is a pair of pants. Cutting this pair of pants along its orthogeodesics, we
obtain a convex right-angled hexagon with consecutive sides of length
\ell(\gamma_{1})/2
,

\ell(H)
and
\ell(\gamma_{2})/2
. We can further cut this hexagon and obtain a right-angled
pentagon with consecutive side-lengths
\ell(\gamma_{1})/2
and
\ell(H)
, which implies the
claim by
[
5
, Lemma 2.3.5]
.
∎

We use this lemma to prove the following.

Lemma 3.24
.

Let
Y
be a compact hyperbolic surface with:

•

three simple oriented closed geodesics
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3}
;

•

an orthogeodesic
H_{12}
leaving from the right of
\gamma_{1}
and arriving at the left
of
\gamma_{2}
;

•

an orthogeodesic
H_{23}
leaving from the right of
\gamma_{2}
and arriving at the left
of
\gamma_{3}
.

Let
J\subset\gamma_{2}
be the segment joining
t(H_{12})

to
o(H_{23})
and
H
be the orthogeodesic from
\gamma_{1}
to
\gamma_{3}
, homotopic with
gliding endpoints to
H_{12}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}J\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}H_{23}
. Then, if
\gamma
denotes the
geodesic representative of the homotopy class

\gamma_{1}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}H\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\gamma_{3}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}H^{-1}
, we have

(3.26)

\displaystyle\cosh\left(\frac{\ell(\gamma)}{2}\right)\geq\cosh(\ell(J)).

Of course, the orientations of
\gamma_{1}
,
\gamma_{2}
and
\gamma_{3}
are irrelevant in the length
estimate, but serve to describe the isotopy class of the picture

\gamma_{1}\cup\gamma_{2}\cup\gamma_{2}\cup H_{12}\cup H_{23}
.

Proof.

The geodesic
\gamma
is a figure eight, going around the two closed geodesics
\gamma_{1}
and

\gamma_{3}
, connected by the orthogeodesic
H
. It then follows that

\displaystyle\cosh\left(\frac{\ell(\gamma)}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\cosh\left(\frac{\ell(\gamma_{1})}{2}\right)\cosh\left(\frac{\ell(\gamma_{3})}{2}\right)+\cosh(H)\sinh\left(\frac{\ell(\gamma_{1})}{2}\right)\sinh\left(\frac{\ell(\gamma_{3})}{2}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\cosh(H)\sinh\left(\frac{\ell(\gamma_{1})}{2}\right)\sinh\left(\frac{\ell(\gamma_{3})}{2}\right).

Expressing the length of
H
in terms of the lengths of
J
and
H_{12}
,
H_{23}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\cosh(H)

\displaystyle=\cosh(\ell(H_{12}))\cosh(\ell(H_{23}))+\cosh(\ell(J))\sinh(\ell(H_{12}))\sinh(\ell(H_{23}))

\displaystyle\geq\cosh(\ell(J))\sinh(\ell(H_{12}))\sinh(\ell(H_{23})).

By Lemma
3.23
,
\sinh(\ell(\gamma_{1})/2)\sinh(\ell(H_{12}))\geq 1
and
\sinh(\ell(\gamma_{3})/2)\sinh(\ell(H_{23}))\geq 1
, which
leads to our claim.
∎

We are now ready to proceed to the proofs of the bounds.

Proof of Proposition
3.22
.

The upper bound on
y_{\lambda}
is a direct consequence of Remark
3.7
, which states
that
\Gamma_{\lambda}
is freely homotopic to a path
\Gamma_{\lambda,\mathrm{init}}
on

\mathbf{c}
, and hence

y_{\lambda}=\ell_{Y}(\Gamma_{\lambda})\leq\ell(\Gamma_{\lambda,\mathrm{init}})\leq\ell({\mathbf{c}})=\ell.

Let us now prove the bound on
\theta_{q}
for an index
q\in\Theta
.

•

If
q
is a U-turn parameter, then we simply observe that

0\leq\theta_{q}\leq\ell(\beta_{\lambda(q)})\leq\ell
.

•

If
q
is a crossing parameter, we apply Lemma
3.24
to the simple closed
geodesics
\gamma_{1}=\beta_{\lambda(\sigma^{-1}q)}
,
\gamma_{2}=\beta_{\lambda(q)}
and

\gamma_{3}=\beta_{\lambda(\sigma q)}
, with the orthogeodesics
H_{12}=\overline{B}_{q}
and

H_{23}=\overline{B}_{\sigma q}
, so that
J=\overline{\mathcal{I}}_{q}
. We check that the figure-eight

\gamma
produced by the lemma is freely homotopic to a loop
\gamma_{\mathrm{init}}
drawn on

\mathbf{c}
, going along
\beta_{\lambda(\sigma^{-1}q),\mathrm{init}}
,

\beta_{\lambda(\sigma q),\mathrm{init}}
and twice
\mathcal{I}_{q,\mathrm{init}}
. We conclude that

\cosh(\theta_{q})\leq\cosh(\ell(\gamma))\leq\cosh(2\ell).

The bounds on the twist parameters are obtained the same way, constructing for each case (Case (a)
and (b)) a figure-eight
\gamma
such that
|\alpha_{\lambda}|\leq\ell(\gamma)\leq 3\ell
(the
factor of
3
being there for Case (b), due to repeated portions of
\mathbf{c}
used to describe
the homotopy class of
\gamma
).
∎

3.7.
Polynomial bounds on volumes and the case of double-filling loops

One can straightforwardly deduce from the apriori bound on Fenchel–Nielsen parameters,
Proposition
3.22
, a rough polynomial upper bound on the volume of the set of hyperbolic
surfaces
Y\in\mathcal{T}^{*}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}
such that
\ell_{Y}({\mathbf{c}})\leq\ell
.
A similar statement was obtained when
\mathbf{S}
is a once-holed torus in

[
2
, eq. (8.4)]
.

Corollary 3.25
.

For any family of distinct indices
(i_{j},i_{j}^{\prime})_{1\leq j\leq k}
in

\partial\mathbf{S}
, any
\ell\geq 0
,

(3.27)

\displaystyle\int_{\ell_{Y}({\mathbf{c}})\leq\ell}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\delta(x_{i_{j}}-x_{i_{j}^{\prime}})\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}^{\mathrm{WP}}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}(\mathbf{x},Y)\leq(3\ell)^{3\chi(\mathbf{S})}.

Proof.

We simply write the expression of

\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}^{\mathrm{WP}}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}(\mathbf{x},Y)
in Fenchel–Nielsen
coordinates as in (
3.1
), and use the bounds given by Proposition
3.22
on the
lengths and twist angles.
∎

Remark 3.26
.

We have assumed at the beginning of §
3
that the loop
\mathbf{c}
is a generalized
eight, and therefore, as of now, we have only proven Corollary
3.25
for generalized
eights. For the sake of the following application, it will be useful to observe that this result
can be extended to any multi-loop
\mathbf{c}
. This proof relies on definitions from Section

11
and can be omitted at first read; it is only presented here for completeness
and to allow to state the interesting Lemma
3.27
below.

Proof of Corollary
3.25
for general loops.

Let
\mathbf{c}^{\prime}
be a skeleton of
\mathbf{c}
, and
\mathbf{S}^{\prime}
be the surface filled by

\mathbf{c}^{\prime}
. If
\mathbf{S}^{\prime}=\mathbf{S}
, then by Proposition
11.13
,

\ell_{Y}({\mathbf{c}}^{\prime})\leq\ell_{Y}({\mathbf{c}})
, from which we deduce the claim. Otherwise,

\mathbf{S}
is obtained from
\mathbf{S}^{\prime}
by gluing a finite number of cylinders and adding
cylindrical bars. We add the corresponding twist parameters to our coordinate system and remove
the
\theta_{q}
that have become redundant. Corollary
3.25
persists because we can
choose those twist parameters so that they still satisfy (
3.25
), by the same proof.
∎

As in
[
2
, §8.1]
, we can use polynomial volume bounds to prove the following special case
of our main result, Theorem
1.23
, for double-filling loops (defined in Definition

1.21
).

Lemma 3.27
.

If
\mathbf{c}
is double-filling, then
\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{R}_{w}^{\mathrm{N}}
and

\lVert\mathcal{J}\rVert_{\mathcal{R}_{w}^{\mathrm{N}}}\leq\mathrm{fn}(g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}},\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N})\prod_{j\in V}\|\tilde{g}_{j}\|_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{K}_{j},\mathrm{N}_{j}}}.

Proof of Lemma
3.27
.

Let us write

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\ell}|\mathcal{J}(s)|\,\mathrm{d}s\leq\int_{\ell_{Y}({\mathbf{c}})\leq\ell}\,\prod_{j\in V}|f_{j}(x_{j})|\prod_{j=1}^{k}x_{i_{j}}\delta(x_{i_{j}}-x_{i_{j}^{\prime}})\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}^{\mathrm{WP}}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}(\mathbf{x},Y).

By hypothesis on the functions
f_{j}
and using equation (
1.8
) on
\tilde{g}_{j}
, we
have for each
j\in V
,

|f_{j}(x_{j})|\leq\mathrm{fn}(\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N})\|\tilde{g}_{j}\|_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{K}_{j},\mathrm{N}_{j}}}(1+x_{j})^{\mathrm{K}_{j}-1}e^{x_{j}/2}

and for each
1\leq j\leq k
,
x_{i_{j}}\leq e^{x_{i_{j}}/2}
.
By Proposition
1.30
, if
\ell_{Y}({\mathbf{c}})\leq\ell
and
\mathbf{c}
is
double-filling, then
\sum_{j=1}^{n_{\mathbf{S}}}x_{j}\leq\ell
and hence

\displaystyle\prod_{j\in V}|f_{j}(x_{j})|\prod_{j=1}^{k}x_{i_{j}}\leq\mathrm{fn}(\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N})\prod_{j\in V}\|\tilde{g}_{j}\|_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{K}_{j},\mathrm{N}_{j}}}(1+\ell)^{\sum_{j\in V}(\mathrm{K}_{j}-1)}e^{\ell/2}.

With Corollary
3.25
, using the hypothesis
\mathrm{K}_{j}\leq\mathrm{N}_{j}
and the definition

\mathrm{N}=\sum_{j\in V}\mathrm{N}_{j}+3\chi(\mathbf{S})+1
, this implies that

\int_{0}^{\ell}|\mathcal{J}(s)|\,\mathrm{d}s\leq\mathrm{fn}(g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}},\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N})\prod_{j\in V}\|\tilde{g}_{j}\|_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{K}_{j},\mathrm{N}_{j}}}(1+\ell)^{\mathrm{N}-1}e^{\ell/2},

which allows us to conclude and to obtain the claimed bound.
∎

Remark 3.28
.

Had we not assumed that
\mathbf{c}
was double-filling, Proposition
1.30

would only have implied that

(3.28)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\ell}|\mathcal{J}(s)|\,\mathrm{d}s\leq\mathrm{fn}(g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}},\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N})\prod_{j\in V}\|\tilde{g}_{j}\|_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{K}_{j},\mathrm{N}_{j}}}(1+\ell)^{\mathrm{N}-1}e^{\ell},

i.e. a similar upper bound but without the factor
1/2
in the exponential, which is not enough to
conclude to Theorem
1.23
.

4.
Expression of length functions in the new coordinate system

The aim of this section is to give explicit expressions in the coordinates
(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})

for the length
\ell_{Y}({\mathbf{c}})
, but also for all the lengths
y_{\lambda}
of the multi-curve

(\Gamma_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
defined in §
3.2
.

4.1.
Trajectories in
T^{1}{\mathbf{S}}
and multiplication in
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})

It is a classical fact that
\mathcal{T}^{*}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}
may be seen as the space of discrete faithful
representations of the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(\mathbf{S})
into
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
(modulo
conjugacy). Moreover, if
Y\in\mathcal{T}^{*}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}
corresponds to the representation

\rho:\pi_{1}(\mathbf{S})\longrightarrow\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
, we can express all lengths thanks to the trace
of
\rho
:

(4.1)

\displaystyle 2\cosh\left(\frac{\ell_{Y}(\gamma)}{2}\right)=|{\rm Tr}\,\rho(\gamma)|

(where
\rho(\gamma)\in\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
is the image under
\rho
of the homotopy class of

\gamma
). Our method to express lengths in the new coordinates consists in providing an explicit
expression for
\rho(\gamma)
in (
4.1
), by writing a representative of the homotopy
class of
\gamma
as a succession of orthogonal segments.

We recall that the unit tangent bundle
T^{1}\mathbb{H}^{2}
itself can be identified with

\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
. We can then identify different geometric actions on
T^{1}\mathbb{H}^{2}
with the
right-multiplication by a specific matrix:
a^{t}
is the action of the geodesic flow at time
t
,

w^{t}
the conjugate of the geodesic flow by a rotation of angle
\pi/2
, and
k^{\theta}
the
rotation of angle
\theta
by
k^{\theta}
, where

(4.2)

\displaystyle a^{t}=\begin{pmatrix}e^{\frac{t}{2}}&0\\
0&e^{-\frac{t}{2}}\end{pmatrix}\qquad w^{t}=\begin{pmatrix}\cosh\left(\frac{t}{2}\right)&\sinh\left(\frac{t}{2}\right)\\
\sinh\left(\frac{t}{2}\right)&\cosh\left(\frac{t}{2}\right)\end{pmatrix}\qquad k^{\theta}=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)&\sin\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)\\
-\sin\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)&\cos\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)\end{pmatrix}.

These moves are represented on Figure
17
.

Figure 17
.
The moves corresponding to the matrices
a^{t}
,
w^{t}
and
k^{\theta}
.

It is important to note that, while
a^{t}
always has non-negative entries, this is true of
w^{t}
only if
t\geq 0
. In what follows, a lot of effort
is devoted to using
w^{t}
only for
t\geq 0
.

4.2.
Expression of
\ell_{Y}({\mathbf{c}})
in the new coordinates

Let us now focus on finding an expression for
\ell_{Y}(\mathbf{c})
in terms of

(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})
. We shall do so component by component, i.e. provide an expression for
the
i
th component
\mathbf{c}_{i}
of
\mathbf{c}
for
i\in\{1,\ldots,\mathfrak{n}\}
. Oftenwise, we
will focus on one component
\mathbf{c}_{i}
at a time, in which case we will drop the mention of
i

to lighten notations. This shall always be mentioned, to avoid any confusion.

4.2.1.
Piecewise geodesic representative of
{\mathbf{c}_{i}}

First, we recall that we saw in (
2.7
) that the homotopy class of
{\mathbf{c}}^{\mathrm{op}}_{i}
can be
written, for any index
q\in\Theta^{i}
, as

{\mathbf{c}}^{\mathrm{op}}_{i}=p(q)\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}p(\sigma q)\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\ldots\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}p(\sigma^{n_{i}-1}q)

where, for any
q^{\prime}
,
p(q^{\prime})
is the concatenation of the bar
B_{q^{\prime}}
and the simple portion

\mathcal{I}_{q^{\prime}}
.

Consider a point
Y\in\mathcal{T}^{*}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}
. As suggested in Remark
2.8
, we can pick our
representative so that
\beta
is a multi-geodesic. We then consider the representative of the
homotopy class of
{\mathbf{c}}_{i}
given by a cyclic succession of orthogonal geodesics segments:

(4.3)

\displaystyle\bar{p}(q)\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\bar{p}(\sigma(q))\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\ldots\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\bar{p}(\sigma^{n_{i}-1}q)

for a
q\in\Theta^{i}
, where
\bar{p}(q):=\overline{B}_{q}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\overline{\mathcal{I}}_{q}.
Each bar

\overline{B}_{q}
is now an orthogeodesic from
\beta_{\lambda(\sigma^{-1}q)}
to

\beta_{\lambda(q)}
.

For the rest of §
4.2
, it will be convenient to use the relabelling of
\Theta

introduced in §
2.5.2
, which allows us to identify
\Theta^{i}
with the cyclic set

\mathbb{Z}_{n_{i}}=\mathbb{Z}\diagup n_{i}\mathbb{Z}
. Through this identification, going through the elements

k\in\mathbb{Z}_{n_{i}}
corresponds to going through the elements
\sigma^{k}q
in (
4.3
).
We will therefore write
\overline{B}(i,k)=\overline{B}(\sigma^{k}q)
,

\overline{\mathcal{I}}(i,k)=\overline{\mathcal{I}}(\sigma^{k}q)
,
\theta(i,k)=\theta(\sigma^{k}q)
etc. Following
Notation
3.3
, we will also write
L(i,k)=L(\sigma^{k}q)
and

\epsilon L(i,k)=\epsilon L(\sigma^{k}q)
. As a consequence, the consecutive lengths of all the
segments encountered in (
4.3
) are (up to cyclic permutation)

L(i,1),\theta(i,1),\ldots,L(i,n_{i}),\theta(i,n_{i}).

The sign of
\epsilon L(i,k)
tells us in which direction the bar
\overline{B}(i,k)
is taken at the
step
k
.

4.2.2.
Lift of
{\mathbf{c}}
to the universal cover

We now consider a lift of the closed path (
4.3
) to the universal cover of
\mathbf{S}
,
identified with a subset of the hyperbolic plane. For each index
q\in{\mathbf{\Theta}}^{i}
, let

\tilde{\beta}_{q}\subset\mathbb{H}^{2}
be a lift of the geodesic
\beta_{\lambda(q)}
such that, if

\tilde{B}_{\sigma^{-1}q,q}
denotes the oriented orthogeodesic going from

\tilde{\beta}_{\sigma^{-1}q}
to
\tilde{\beta}_{q}
, then
\tilde{B}_{\sigma^{-1}q,q}
projects
down to
\overline{B}_{q}
on the surface.

Whilst there was a priori no natural orientation for the multi-curve
\beta
on

\mathbf{S}
, we can now orient the lifts
\tilde{\beta}_{q}
so that the bar

\tilde{B}_{\sigma^{-1}q,q}
arrives on the right of
\tilde{\beta}_{q}
for each

q\in\mathbf{\Theta}^{i}
with
\sign(q)=+
and on the left otherwise. We then make the following
crucial observation.

Lemma 4.1
.

The geodesics
(\tilde{\beta}_{q})_{q\in\mathbf{\Theta}^{i}}
are disjoint and, for any

q\in\mathbf{\Theta}^{i}
,
\tilde{\beta}_{q}
and
\tilde{\beta}_{\sigma q}
are
aligned.
\tilde{\beta}_{\sigma q}
is on the left of
\tilde{\beta}_{q}
if and only if
\sign(q)=+
.

We denote by
\tilde{\mathcal{I}}_{q}\subset\tilde{\beta}_{q}
the segment between the endpoint of

\tilde{B}_{\sigma^{-1}q,q}
and the origin of
\tilde{B}_{q,\sigma q}
. Then the segment

\tilde{\mathcal{I}}_{q}
projects down to the segment
\overline{\mathcal{I}}_{q}
on the surface. If we call

\tilde{p}(q)=\tilde{B}_{\sigma^{-1}q,q}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\tilde{\mathcal{I}}(q)
, then the infinite
concatenation
\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}_{q\in{\mathbf{\Theta}}^{i}}\;\tilde{p}(q)
of orthogonal geodesics
segments in the hyperbolic plane projects down to (
4.3
) on the surface. We shall
denote by
\tilde{\mathbf{c}}_{i}
the infinite geodesic homotopic to

\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}_{q\in{\mathbf{\Theta}}^{i}}\;\tilde{p}(q)
in the hyperbolic plane.

Notation 4.2
.

The notation
\tilde{B}_{mn}
, for the moment only defined for
n=\sigma m
, may be
extended to arbitrary
m,n\in{\mathbf{\Theta}}^{i}
. We call
\tilde{B}_{mn}\subset\mathbb{H}^{2}
the
oriented orthogeodesic segment from
\tilde{\beta}_{m}
to
\tilde{\beta}_{n}
. We denote by

L_{mn}>0
its length and
z_{mn}\in\tilde{\beta}_{n}
its terminus.

4.2.3.
Expression of the length of
\mathbf{c}_{i}
as a trace

Starting from (
4.3
) we now find a formula expressing each

\ell_{Y}({\mathbf{c}}_{i})
as the trace of a product of matrices.

Lemma 4.3
.

For
i\in\{1,\ldots,\mathfrak{n}\}
, the length of the component

{\mathbf{c}}_{i}
is given by the formula

(4.4)

\cosh\left(\frac{\ell_{Y}({\mathbf{c}}_{i})}{2}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\,{\rm Tr}\left(a_{1}^{i}b_{1}^{i}\ldots a_{n_{i}}^{i}b_{n_{i}}^{i}\right)

where the matrices
a_{k}^{i},b_{k}^{i}
are defined by

(4.5)

a_{k}^{i}=a^{\epsilon L(i,k)}=\begin{pmatrix}e^{\frac{\epsilon L(i,k)}{2}}&0\\
0&e^{-\frac{\epsilon L(i,k)}{2}}\end{pmatrix}\qquad b_{k}^{i}=w^{\theta(i,k)}=\begin{pmatrix}\cosh\left(\frac{\theta(i,k)}{2}\right)&\sinh\left(\frac{\theta(i,k)}{2}\right)\\
\sinh\left(\frac{\theta(i,k)}{2}\right)&\cosh\left(\frac{\theta(i,k)}{2}\right)\end{pmatrix}.

Proof.

For the proof, let us fix a value of
i
. As explained at the beginning of this section, the
elements of
\Theta^{i}
are labelled
(i,k)
with
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{n_{i}}
. To lighten notations, we shall
omit the mention of
i
, for instance writing
n_{i}=n
,
L_{k}=L(i,k)
,
\theta(i,k)=\theta_{k}
,
etc.

Let
v
denote the unit vector tangent to the bar
\overline{B}_{1}=\overline{B}(i,1)
based at
its origin. We consider a lift
\tilde{v}
of
v
in
T^{1}\mathbb{H}^{2}
identified with

\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
. The decomposition (
4.3
) into a succession of orthogonal
geodesic segments precisely tells us that

\tilde{v}a_{1}b_{1}\ldots a_{n}b_{n}=\rho({\mathbf{c}})\tilde{v}
. This implies that

(4.6)

\displaystyle\rho({\mathbf{c}})=\tilde{v}a_{1}b_{1}\ldots a_{n}b_{n}\tilde{v}^{-1}.

In light of (
4.1
), we obtain

\cosh(\ell_{Y}({\mathbf{c}})/2)=\frac{1}{2}\,|{\rm Tr}\left(a_{1}b_{1}\ldots a_{n}b_{n}\right)|
, which
is our claim up to the presence of the absolute value.

To prove the proposition, we need to argue that we can remove the absolute
value. In order to do so, we realise the matrix
a_{1}b_{1}\ldots a_{n}b_{n}
as
the endpoint of a continuous path

\tilde{a}_{1}\diamond\tilde{b}_{1}\diamond\ldots\diamond\tilde{a}_{n}\diamond\tilde{b}_{n}

in
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
starting at the identity
matrix. More precisely, for any
k
, let us define the paths

\displaystyle\tilde{a}_{k}:[0,L_{k}]

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})

\displaystyle\tilde{b}_{k}:[0,\theta_{k}]

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle\longmapsto a^{\sign(k)t}

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle\longmapsto w^{t},

which respectively correspond to the geodesic motion along the bar
\overline{B}_{k}
(in positive or
negative time depending on
\sign(k)
), and the parallel transport of a vector orthogonal to

\overline{\mathcal{I}}_{k}
. Note that the endpoint of
\tilde{a}_{k}
and
\tilde{b}_{k}
are the matrices

a_{k}
and
b_{k}
. We then define the path

\tilde{a}_{1}\diamond\tilde{b}_{1}\diamond\ldots\diamond\tilde{a}_{n}\diamond\tilde{b}_{n}
as the
concatenation of the successive paths with matching endpoints
t\mapsto\tilde{a}_{1}(t)
,

t\mapsto a_{1}\tilde{b}_{1}(t)
,
t\mapsto a_{1}b_{1}\tilde{a}_{2}(t)
,

t\mapsto a_{1}b_{1}a_{2}\tilde{b}_{2}(t)
,
\ldots
, and
t\mapsto a_{1}b_{1}\ldots a_{n}\tilde{b}_{n}(t)
.

Thanks to our prescription of orientation rules, the path
{\mathbf{c}}^{\mathrm{op}}_{i}
traced in
\mathbf{S}
may be lifted to
a path
{\mathbf{c}}^{\mathrm{nl}}_{i}
traced in the unit tangent bundle
T^{1}{\mathbf{S}}
,
projecting down to
{\mathbf{c}}^{\mathrm{op}}_{i}
, satisfying the following:
{\mathbf{c}}^{\mathrm{nl}}_{i}
is made of
unit vectors that point to the left of each oriented segment
\overline{\mathcal{I}}_{k}
, and are tangent to
the bars
\overline{B}_{k}
. We call this a
nice lift
of
{\mathbf{c}}^{\mathrm{op}}_{i}
. To

{\mathbf{c}}^{\mathrm{nl}}_{i}
, we may apply the following statement.

Lemma 4.4
.

Suppose we have a piecewise smooth closed path
(\gamma,v)
drawn in
T^{1}{\mathbf{S}}
, where
the path
t\mapsto\gamma(t)\in{\mathbf{S}}
is non-contractible, in minimal position, and

v(t)\in T_{\gamma(t)}^{1}{\mathbf{S}}
is always on the left of
\gamma
. Then there is a homotopy
from
(\gamma,v)
to
(\gamma_{\mathrm{g}},v_{\mathrm{n}})
, where
\gamma_{\mathrm{g}}
is the
closed geodesic homotopic to
\gamma
and
v_{\mathrm{n}}(t)
is the left-normal vector to

\gamma_{g}(t)
.

We can then conclude that the path
c_{i}^{\mathrm{nl}}
in
T^{1}\mathbf{S}
can be continuously deformed to

\displaystyle[0,\ell_{Y}(\mathbf{c}_{i})]

\displaystyle\rightarrow T^{1}{\mathbf{S}}

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle\mapsto v_{0}w^{t}

where
v_{0}
is normal to the geodesic representative of
\mathbf{c}_{i}
and lies on its left.
As a consequence, the path
v\tilde{a}_{1}\diamond\tilde{b}_{1}\diamond\ldots\diamond\tilde{a}_{n}\diamond\tilde{b}_{n}
can also be continuously deformed
to
t\mapsto v_{0}w^{t}
.
Each path in this continuous family leads to the same value of
{\rm Tr}\,\rho({\mathbf{c}}_{i})
.
Since
the trace of
w^{\ell(\mathbf{c}_{i})}
is positive, we must have
{\rm Tr}(a_{1}b_{1}\ldots a_{n}b_{n})>0
as well, proving the proposition.
∎

4.2.4.
The formula for the length of
\mathbf{c}_{i}

Expanding (
4.4
), we can deduce the following expression for the length
of
{\mathbf{c}}_{i}
.
We shall use a shorthand notation for hyperbolic trigonometric functions.

Notation 4.5
.

We denote
\hyp_{+}:=\cosh
,
\hyp_{-}:=\sinh
.
For
\delta=(\delta_{1},\ldots,\delta_{n})\in\{\pm 1\}^{n}
and

t=(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we set

(4.7)

\displaystyle\hyp_{\delta}(t):=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\hyp_{\delta_{i}}(t_{i}).

We prove the following.

Theorem 4.6
.

For any
Y\in\mathcal{T}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}^{*}
of coordinates

(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})
, any
i\in\{1,\ldots,\mathfrak{n}\}
,

\displaystyle\cosh\left(\frac{\ell_{Y}({\mathbf{c}}_{i})}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\delta\in\{\pm 1\}^{n_{i}}\\
\delta_{1}\ldots\delta_{n_{i}}=+1\end{subarray}}\hyp_{\delta}\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)\cosh\left(\frac{\rho^{\delta}\cdot\epsilon L}{2}\right)

where

\rho^{\delta}_{k}=\prod_{1\leq j<k}\delta_{j}

(for
k=1
this empty product is equal to
1
),

\epsilon L=(\epsilon L(i,1),\ldots,\epsilon L(i,n_{i}))
,
and
\cdot
denotes the scalar product, so that

\rho^{\delta}\cdot\epsilon L=\sum_{k=1}^{n_{i}}\epsilon_{k}L_{k}\rho^{\delta}_{k}
.

Note that this result holds for any choice of openings of the intersections of
\mathbf{c}
, and
provides different expressions for the length of
\mathbf{c}
in different coordinate systems
depending on that choice.

Proof.

As in the previous proof, we momentarily drop all references to the index
i
.
By equation (
4.4
), the desired quantity can be expressed as

\frac{1}{2}\,{\rm Tr}\left(A_{1}\ldots A_{n}\right)
where for any
k
,
A_{k}=a_{k}b_{k}
, and
a_{k},b_{k}
are defined in (
4.5
). Calculating the product, we find

\displaystyle A_{k}

\displaystyle=a_{k}b_{k}=\begin{pmatrix}\cosh\left(\frac{\theta_{k}}{2}\right)e^{\epsilon_{k}L_{k}/2}&\sinh\left(\frac{\theta_{k}}{2}\right)e^{\epsilon_{k}L_{k}/2}\\
\sinh\left(\frac{\theta_{k}}{2}\right)e^{-\epsilon_{k}L_{k}/2}&\cosh\left(\frac{\theta_{k}}{2}\right)e^{-\epsilon_{k}L_{k}/2}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\hyp_{+}\left(\frac{\theta_{k}}{2}\right)E_{+1}\left(\frac{\epsilon_{k}L_{k}}{2}\right)+\hyp_{-}\left(\frac{\theta_{k}}{2}\right)E_{-1}\left(\frac{\epsilon_{k}L_{k}}{2}\right)

with the matrices
E_{\pm 1}
defined by

(4.8)

E_{+1}(s)=\begin{pmatrix}e^{s}&0\\
0&e^{-s}\end{pmatrix}\qquad\qquad E_{-1}(s)=\begin{pmatrix}0&e^{s}\\
e^{-s}&0\end{pmatrix}.

We need to understand products of these matrices. Using the fact that

\displaystyle E_{+1}(s)E_{+1}(s^{\prime})=E_{+1}(s+s^{\prime})\qquad\qquad E_{+1}(s)E_{-1}(s^{\prime})=E_{-1}(s+s^{\prime})

\displaystyle E_{-1}(s)E_{+1}(s^{\prime})=E_{-1}(s-s^{\prime})\qquad\qquad E_{-1}(s)E_{-1}(s^{\prime})=E_{+1}(s-s^{\prime})

one proves by a straightforward induction that for any
s\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\delta\in\{\pm 1\}^{n}
,

E_{\delta_{1}}(s_{1})\ldots E_{\delta_{n}}(s_{n})=E_{\delta_{1}\ldots\delta_{n}}(\rho^{\delta}\cdot s)

where
\rho^{\delta}_{k}
is defined in the statement of the Lemma. Therefore, expanding the product of
the matrices
A_{k}
, we obtain

(4.9)

\cosh\left(\frac{\ell_{Y}({\mathbf{c}}_{i})}{2}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\,\sum_{\delta\in\{\pm 1\}^{n}}\hyp_{\delta}\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right){\rm Tr}\left(E_{\delta_{1}\ldots\delta_{n}}\left(\frac{\rho^{\delta}\cdot\epsilon L}{2}\right)\right)

which allows us to conclude, since
{\rm Tr}(E_{+1}(s))=2\cosh(s)
and
{\rm Tr}(E_{-1}(s))=0
.

∎

Motivated by Proposition
4.6
, we introduce the following
function
M_{n}
.

Notation 4.7
.

For an integer
n\geq 1
,
x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
and
t=(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we define

(4.10)

\displaystyle M_{n}(x,t)

\displaystyle=2\argcosh\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\delta\in\{\pm 1\}^{n}\\
\delta_{1}\ldots\delta_{n}=+1\end{subarray}}\hyp_{\delta}\left(\frac{t}{2}\right)\cosh\left(\frac{\rho^{\delta}\cdot x}{2}\right).

Then, Theorem
4.6
tells us that

(4.11)

\displaystyle\ell_{Y}({\mathbf{c}}_{i})=M_{n_{i}}\left(\epsilon L(i,1),\ldots,\epsilon L(i,n_{i}),\theta(i,1),\ldots,\theta(i,n_{i})\right)

where we recall that
\epsilon L(i,k)
is short-hand for
\epsilon(i,k)L(i,k)
. Adding the
lengths of all components of
{\mathbf{c}}
, we obtain

(4.12)

\displaystyle\ell_{Y}({\mathbf{c}})=\sum_{i=1}^{\mathfrak{n}}M_{n_{i}}\left(\epsilon L(i,1),\ldots,\epsilon L(i,n_{i}),\theta(i,1),\ldots,\theta(i,n_{i})\right).

4.3.
Parameters
\tau

In the following section, we shall apply similar techniques to find the expression of the lengths

(\ell_{Y}(\Gamma_{\lambda}))_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
of the pair of pants decomposition constructed in §
3.2
. As in the case of
\mathbf{c}
, we can realise the path
\Gamma_{\lambda}
by going
along a succession of bars
B_{q}
and simple portions
\mathcal{I}_{q}
, now with different orientation
rules (this was already observed in Remark
3.6
). This motivates the following
definitions.

For the rest of this section (and hence until the end of §
10
), we shall assume that

all intersections are opened the first way
.
As a consequence, the multi-curve
\beta
is
oriented. This convention will lighten notations for the expressions of
\ell_{Y}(\Gamma_{\lambda})
;
however, the methods we develop can be adapted to other situations.

4.3.1.
Definition

Let
q,q^{\prime}\in\Theta
be two indices such that
\lambda(q)=\lambda(q^{\prime})
and

\sign(q)=\sign(q^{\prime})=:\epsilon
, i.e. both bars
B_{q}
and

B_{q^{\prime}}
arrive on the same component of
\beta
and on the same side. We denote as

\mathcal{K}_{qq^{\prime}}
the oriented segment on
\beta
going from the terminus point

t(B_{q})
to the terminus point
t(B_{q^{\prime}})
, following the orientation of
\beta
if
\epsilon=+
, and its reverse orientation otherwise.

Once the bars
{B}_{q},{B}_{q^{\prime}}
have glided to the orthogeodesics

\overline{B}_{q},\overline{B}_{q^{\prime}}
, the segment
\mathcal{K}_{qq^{\prime}}
glides to a new segment

\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{qq^{\prime}}
, and we denote by
\tau_{qq^{\prime}}
its length.
Thanks to the Useful Remark (Remark
2.11
),
\tau_{qq^{\prime}}
is always strictly positive.

(a)

\epsilon=+
.

(b)

\epsilon=-
.

Figure 18
.
The segment
\mathcal{K}_{qq^{\prime}}
.

In the special case when a segment
\mathcal{K}_{qq^{\prime}}
does not contain any other point
t(B_{q^{\prime\prime}})
in its
interior, with
B_{q^{\prime\prime}}
also arriving on the same side as
B_{q}
and
B_{q^{\prime}}
, then we simply denote
by
\mathcal{K}_{q}
this segment. This notation (where
q^{\prime}
