has been removed) is justified by the remark that
there is exactly one
q^{\prime}
with this property. We then denote
\tau_{q}:=\tau_{qq^{\prime}}
(or, when
convenient,
\tau(q)
).

4.3.2.
Relation between
\tau
and
\theta

Each parameters
\tau_{qq^{\prime}}
is a sum of a certain number of parameters
\theta_{q^{\prime\prime}}
: there
exists a (uniquely defined) set of indices
\Theta_{\tau}(qq^{\prime})\subseteq\Theta
such that

(4.13)

\displaystyle\tau_{qq^{\prime}}=\sum_{q^{\prime\prime}\in\Theta_{\tau}(qq^{\prime})}\theta_{q^{\prime\prime}},

reflecting a partition of
\mathcal{K}_{qq^{\prime}}
into

\mathcal{K}_{qq^{\prime}}=\bigsqcup_{q^{\prime\prime}\in\Theta_{\tau}(qq^{\prime})}\mathcal{I}_{q^{\prime\prime}}
.

In the case of
\tau_{q}
, we adopt as slightly simpler notation, writing that there exists a set of
indices
\Theta_{\tau}(q)\subseteq\Theta
such that

(4.14)

\displaystyle\tau_{q}=\sum_{q^{\prime}\in\Theta_{\tau}(q)}\theta_{q^{\prime}}.

For a set
W\subseteq\Theta
, we let
\Theta_{\tau}(W):=\bigcup_{q\in W}\Theta_{\tau}(q)
.
Note that this union is not necessarily disjoint.

4.4.
Expression of the boundary lengths in the new coordinates

We are now ready to find the expression of the length of the curves
\Gamma_{\lambda}
for

\lambda\in\Lambda
. We recall that we assumed, for the sake of simplicity, that the intersections
have been opened the first way (see Remark
4.9
for the general case).

4.4.1.
Case where
\Gamma_{\lambda}
is a component of
\beta

First, we note that in the special case where the curve
\Gamma_{\lambda}
is a component of the
multi-curve
\beta
, i.e. when
\lambda\in\Lambda^{\beta}
, then we can simply write

y_{\lambda}=\ell_{Y}(\beta_{\lambda})
, which has the simple expression in terms of the parameters

\vec{\theta}
:

(4.15)

\displaystyle y_{\lambda}=\sum_{q:\lambda(q)=\lambda}\theta_{q}=\sum_{q\in\Theta_{t}(\lambda)}\theta_{q},

where we recall the definition of the set
\Theta_{t}(\lambda)
from Notation
2.16
.

4.4.2.
Polygonal curve associated to
\Gamma_{\lambda}

Let us now fix
\lambda\in\Lambda^{\Gamma}=\Lambda\setminus\Lambda^{\beta}
(i.e.
\Gamma_{\lambda}
is not a
component of
\beta
). The length of
\Gamma_{\lambda}
does not depend on its orientation; we orient
it so that, if
j:=\step(\lambda)
is the step of the construction of the pair of pants decomposition at which

\Gamma_{\lambda}
appears, then
\mathbf{P}_{j}
lies on the
left
of
\Gamma_{\lambda}
.

Figure 19
.
Following two (non-necessarily distinct) boundary components
\Gamma_{\lambda}
and

\Gamma_{\lambda^{\prime}}
of
\mathbf{S}
near a bar
B_{q}
with
\sign(q)=+
.

Let us first assume that
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}
, i.e.
\lambda
is a boundary component of

S
. Examining the local picture at each intersection and following the boundary of the
regular neighbourhood of the loop
\mathbf{c}
(as represented in Figure
19
), we obtain
that we can write the boundary component
\Gamma_{\lambda}
as an alternating succession of
bars
B_{q}
and segments
\mathcal{K}_{q}
defined in §
4.3
, i.e. as

(4.16)

\bar{p}_{1}^{\lambda}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\ldots\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\bar{p}_{m(\lambda)}^{\lambda}

for some integer
m(\lambda)
, where each piece
\bar{p}_{k}^{\lambda}
is a concatenation

\overline{B}_{q}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{q}
for some
q=q_{k}^{\lambda}\in\Theta
.

Remark 4.8
.

We also notice that
\sign q_{k}^{\lambda}=(-1)^{k}
, and in particular the integer
m(\lambda)
is
even, but this will not play any role in the discussion.

More generally, if
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{in}}
, then we realise
\Gamma_{\lambda}
as a boundary component
of the surface
\mathbf{S}_{j}
explored at the step
j
of the construction of the pair of pants
decomposition. We then obtain that
\Gamma_{\lambda}
can, too, be written under the form
(
4.16
), but now with bars

\bar{p}_{k}^{\lambda}=\overline{B}_{q}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{qq^{\prime}}
for some
q=q_{k}^{\lambda}

and
q^{\prime}={q_{k}^{\prime\lambda}}\in\Theta
. See Figure
20
for an example.

Figure 20
.
An example of the two new boundary components
\Gamma_{\lambda}
for a case where
r=2
,
and their description in terms of a succession of bars and segments
\mathcal{K}
. We use the shorthand
notation
\tau_{ij}^{\epsilon}:=\tau_{(i,\epsilon),(j,\epsilon)}
.

This expression for the homotopy class of
\Gamma_{\lambda}
is the equivalent of (
4.3
)
for the curve
\mathbf{c}_{i}
and will, as before, be our starting point to compute the length of

\Gamma_{\lambda}
. The lengths appearing in this decomposition are the lengths of the bars

\overline{B}_{q}
, which are the same lengths
L_{q}
as before, now together with the lengths

\tau_{qq^{\prime}}
of the segments
\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{qq^{\prime}}
.

The important observation here is that, as we go along the curve
\Gamma_{\lambda}
, we follow a
succession of geodesic segments, only making
right turns
(as seen on Figure
19
,
and in the orientation convention of
\tau_{qq^{\prime}}
in §
4.3
). Such a path will be called a

polygonal curve
in Definition
8.1
.

Remark 4.9
.

If we open the intersections both ways, we can still obtain a description of
\Gamma_{\lambda}
as a
polygonal curve (a succession of orthogonal geodesic segments making only right turns), but the
notations for the orientations of the bars and the parameters
\tau
need to be adapted. In
particular, we no longer have alternate signs for the bars, and
m(\lambda)
is not necessarily
even (see for instance a figure-eight opened the second way).

4.4.3.
Re-labelling

Let us now relabel the segments and lengths appearing in the decomposition (
4.16
)
following the order in which they occur in
\Gamma_{\lambda}
. We shall therefore denote

\overline{B}^{\lambda}_{k}:=\overline{B}_{q_{k}^{\lambda}}
and

\mathcal{K}^{\lambda}_{k}:=\mathcal{K}_{q_{k}^{\lambda}q_{k}^{\prime\lambda}}
, and call
L^{\lambda}_{k}
,
\tau^{\lambda}_{k}

their respective lengths. Note that both these lengths are always positive quantities, by definition
for
L^{\lambda}_{k}
, and in virtue of the Useful Remark for
\tau^{\lambda}_{k}
.
We denote as
\vec{L}^{\lambda}
and
\vec{\tau}^{\lambda}
the vectors

\vec{L}^{\lambda}=(L_{1}^{\lambda},\ldots,L^{\lambda}_{m(\lambda)})
and

\vec{\tau}^{\lambda}=(\tau^{\lambda}_{1},\ldots,\tau^{\lambda}_{m(\lambda)})
.

4.4.4.
Expression of the length

We now follow quite closely the discussion of §
4.1
.

Lemma 4.10
.

For any
Y\in\mathcal{T}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}^{*}
, any
\lambda\in\Lambda^{\Gamma}
, we have

(4.17)

\cosh\left(\frac{\ell_{Y}(\Gamma_{\lambda})}{2}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\,{{\rm Tr}\left(a_{1}^{\lambda}b_{1}^{\lambda}\ldots a_{m(\lambda)}^{\lambda}b_{m(\lambda)}^{\lambda}\right)}

where, for any index
k
,
a_{k}^{\lambda}:=A(L_{k}^{\lambda})
and
b_{k}^{\lambda}:=A(\tau_{k}^{\lambda})
with

(4.18)

A(s):=w^{s}k^{-\frac{\pi}{2}}=\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\begin{pmatrix}e^{\frac{s}{2}}&-e^{-\frac{s}{2}}\\
e^{\frac{s}{2}}&e^{-\frac{s}{2}}\end{pmatrix}.

Proof.

The proof is the same as the proof of Lemma
4.3
. The new path
in
T^{1}\mathbf{S}
that we consider is represented on
Figure
21
.

Figure 21
.
The path in
T^{1}\mathbf{S}
followed in the proof of Lemma

4.10
.

The key argument to remove the absolute value is that this path has the
property required by Lemma
4.4
, namely that the vectors always
point to its left.
∎

Expanding the trace, we can deduce the following formula giving the lengths of the
curves
\Gamma_{\lambda}
.

Theorem 4.11
.

For any
Y\in\mathcal{T}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}^{*}
, any
\lambda\in\Lambda^{\Gamma}
, we have

\displaystyle\cosh\left(\frac{\ell_{Y}(\Gamma_{\lambda})}{2}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\alpha\in\{\pm 1\}^{m(\lambda)}}{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\ldots\alpha_{m(\lambda)}}\hyp_{-\nabla\alpha}\left(\frac{L}{2}\right)\exp\left(\frac{\alpha\cdot\tau}{2}\right)

where
(\nabla\alpha)_{k}:=\alpha_{k}/{\alpha_{k-1}}=\alpha_{k}{\alpha_{k-1}}
with the
convention
\alpha_{0}=\alpha_{m(\lambda)}
.

Remark 4.12
.

For
m=1
this reduces to

\displaystyle\cosh\left(\frac{\ell_{Y}(\Gamma)}{2}\right)=\sinh\left(\frac{L_{1}}{2}\right)\sinh\left(\frac{\tau_{1}}{2}\right).

Proof.

To simplify notations, we shall remove the dependency on
\lambda
from the
name of the matrices and lengths.
By equation (
4.17
), the announced quantity can be
expressed as

\frac{1}{2}\,{{\rm Tr}\left(A(L_{1})A(\tau_{1})\ldots A(L_{m})A(\tau_{m})\right)}

where
A(s)
is the matrix introduced in (
4.18
). Calculating the
product, we find

\displaystyle A(L)A(\tau)=\begin{pmatrix}e^{\frac{\tau}{2}}\sinh\left(\frac{L}{2}\right)&-e^{-\frac{\tau}{2}}\cosh\left(\frac{L}{2}\right)\\
e^{\frac{\tau}{2}}\cosh\left(\frac{L}{2}\right)&-e^{-\frac{\tau}{2}}\sinh\left(\frac{L}{2}\right)\end{pmatrix}=\cosh\left(\frac{L}{2}\right)e_{-1}\left(\frac{\tau}{2}\right)+\sinh\left(\frac{L}{2}\right)e_{+1}\left(\frac{\tau}{2}\right)

with the matrices
e_{\pm 1}
defined by

e_{+1}(s)=\begin{pmatrix}e^{s}&0\\
0&-e^{-s}\end{pmatrix}\qquad\qquad e_{-1}(s)=\begin{pmatrix}0&-e^{-s}\\
e^{s}&0\end{pmatrix}.

We can use once again the matrices
E_{-1},E_{+1}
introduced in equation (
4.8
), and note the relations

\displaystyle e_{+1}(s)e_{+1}(s^{\prime})=E_{+1}(s+s^{\prime})\qquad\qquad e_{+1}(s)e_{-1}(s^{\prime})=-E_{-1}(s-s^{\prime})

\displaystyle e_{-1}(s)e_{+1}(s^{\prime})=E_{-1}(-s-s^{\prime})\qquad\qquad e_{-1}(s)e_{-1}(s^{\prime})=-E_{+1}(s^{\prime}-s)

and

\displaystyle E_{+1}(s)e_{+1}(s^{\prime})=E_{-1}(s)e_{-1}(s^{\prime})=e_{+1}(s+s^{\prime})

\displaystyle E_{+1}(s)e_{-1}(s^{\prime})=E_{-1}(s)e_{+1}(s^{\prime})=e_{-1}(s^{\prime}-s).

A straightforward induction then shows that for any
s\in\mathbb{R}^{2k}
and

\delta\in\{\pm 1\}^{2k}
,

e_{\delta_{1}}(s_{1})e_{\delta_{2}}(s_{2})\ldots e_{\delta_{2k}}(s_{2k})=\delta_{2}\delta_{4}\ldots\delta_{2k}\,E_{\pi(\delta)}(\alpha^{\delta}\cdot s),

where
\pi(\delta)=\prod_{k}\delta_{k}
and
\alpha^{\delta}_{k}:=\prod_{j\leq k}\delta_{j}
,
and that for any
s\in\mathbb{R}^{2k+1}
and
\delta\in\{\pm 1\}^{2k+1}
,

e_{\delta_{1}}(s_{1})e_{\delta_{2}}(s_{2})\ldots e_{\delta_{2k+1}}(s_{2k+1})=\delta_{2}\delta_{4}\ldots\delta_{2k}\,e_{\pi(\delta)}(\pi(\delta)\,\alpha^{\delta}\cdot s).

Therefore, in the case
m=2k
,

\displaystyle\cosh\left(\frac{\ell(\Gamma)}{2}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\,\sum_{\delta\in\{\pm 1\}^{2k}}\delta_{2}\delta_{4}\ldots\delta_{m}\hyp_{-\delta}\left(\frac{L}{2}\right){\rm Tr}\left(E_{\pi(\delta)}\left(\frac{\alpha^{\delta}\cdot\tau}{2}\right)\right)

which implies that

\displaystyle\cosh\left(\frac{\ell(\Gamma)}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\delta\in\{\pm 1\}^{m}\\
\pi(\delta)=+1\end{subarray}}\delta_{2}\delta_{4}\ldots\delta_{m}\hyp_{-\delta}\left(\frac{L}{2}\right)\cosh\left(\frac{{\alpha^{\delta}\cdot\tau}}{2}\right)

since
{\rm Tr}(E_{+1}(s))=2\cosh(s)
and
{\rm Tr}(E_{-1}(s))=0
.
By expanding
\cosh\left(\frac{{\alpha^{\delta}\cdot\tau}}{2}\right)
into exponential functions, and letting

\alpha_{k-1}\alpha_{k}=\delta_{k}=\alpha_{k}/\alpha_{k-1}
, we obtain the
claimed formula.

In the case
m=2k+1
, we conclude similarly, now using

\displaystyle\cosh\left(\frac{\ell(\Gamma)}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\delta\in\{\pm 1\}^{m}\\
\pi(\delta)=+1\end{subarray}}\delta_{2}\delta_{4}\ldots\delta_{m-1}\hyp_{-\delta}\left(\frac{L}{2}\right)\sinh\left(\frac{{\alpha^{\delta}\cdot\tau}}{2}\right).

∎

4.4.5.
Reformulation and final outcome

Motivated by Theorem
4.11
, we now introduce the
following notation.

Notation 4.13
.

For an integer
m\geq 1
and
x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m})
,
t=(t_{1},\ldots,t_{m})
, we define

(4.19)

\displaystyle Q_{m}(x,t)=2\argcosh\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\alpha\in\{\pm 1\}^{m}}{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\ldots\alpha_{m}}\hyp_{-\nabla\alpha}\left(\frac{x}{2}\right)\exp\left(\frac{\alpha\cdot t}{2}\right)

and

(4.20)

\displaystyle F_{m}(x,t)

\displaystyle=\frac{\cosh\Big(\frac{1}{2}Q_{m}(x,t)\Big)}{\exp\Big(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n=1}^{m}t_{n}\Big)}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\alpha\in\{0,1\}^{m}}(-1)^{\alpha_{1}+\ldots+\alpha_{m}}\hyp_{-\partial\alpha}\left(\frac{x}{2}\right)\exp(-\alpha\cdot t)

with
(\partial\alpha)_{n}:=1
if
\alpha_{n-1}=\alpha_{n}
and
-1
if

\alpha_{n-1}\not=\alpha_{n}
.

Remark 4.14
.

Note that we go from (
4.19
) to (
4.20
) by the change of indices

(\alpha_{n})\rightsquigarrow(\frac{\alpha_{n}+1}{2})
.

We have proved that

(4.21)

\displaystyle\ell_{Y}(\Gamma_{\lambda})=Q_{m(\lambda)}(\vec{L}^{\lambda},\vec{\tau}^{\lambda}).

We observe that the relation between the functions
Q_{m}
and
F_{m}
can be rewritten

(4.22)

\displaystyle U(Q_{m}(x,t))=\sum_{n=1}^{m}t_{n}+2\log F_{m}(x,t),

for the function

(4.23)

\displaystyle U(x)=2\log\cosh\left(\frac{x}{2}\right)=x-2\log 2+\mathcal{O}(e^{-x}).

The final outcome of the calculation then is

(4.24)

\displaystyle\ell_{Y}(\Gamma_{\lambda})=\sum_{n=1}^{m(\lambda)}\tau^{\lambda}_{n}-2\log 2+2\log F_{m(\lambda)}(\vec{L}^{\lambda},\vec{\tau}^{\lambda})+\mathcal{O}(e^{-\ell_{Y}(\Gamma_{\lambda})}).

4.4.6.
Set of indices associated to
\lambda

Recall from (
4.14
) (the labelling by
q
being replaced by
(\lambda,k)
) that the
lengths
\tau^{\lambda}_{k}
can be expressed by

(4.25)

\displaystyle\tau^{\lambda}_{k}=\sum_{q\in\Theta_{\tau}(\lambda,k)}\theta_{q},

for some uniquely defined set of indices

\Theta_{\tau}(\lambda,k)\subseteq\Theta
.
Now, we define the following index notation.

Notation 4.15
.

For
\lambda\in\Lambda^{\Gamma}
, let

(4.26)

\Theta_{\ell}(\lambda):=\bigcup_{k=1}^{m(\lambda)}\Theta_{\tau}(\lambda,k)\subseteq\Theta.

We extend the notation
\Theta_{\ell}(\lambda)
to the indices
\lambda\in\Lambda^{\beta}
by
letting
\Theta_{\ell}(\lambda):=\Theta_{t}(\lambda)
the set of indices introduced in
Notation
2.16
. If
W
is a subset of
\Lambda
we denote

(4.27)

\displaystyle\Theta_{\ell}(W):=\bigcup_{\lambda\in W}\Theta_{\ell}(\lambda).

For any
\lambda\in\Lambda
,
\Theta_{\ell}(\lambda)
denotes the set of indices
q\in\Theta

necessary to express the length
\ell_{Y}(\Gamma_{\lambda})
.

Remark 4.16
.

Keep in mind that the sets
\Theta_{\ell}(\lambda)
are not disjoint: in fact, each
q\in\Theta

may belong to two sets
\Theta_{\ell}(\lambda)
with
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}
, expressing the fact
that each simple portion
\mathcal{I}_{q}
has a boundary component of
\mathbf{S}
on its left and one on
its right. As a consequence, one has an inclusion
(\Theta_{\ell}(W))^{c}\subseteq\Theta_{\ell}(W^{c})

(where
V^{c}
denotes the complement of the set
V
), which in general is strict.

5.
Expression of the volume functions as pseudo-convolutions of FR functions

Let us now, in this brief section, come back to our initial objective,
i.e. proving Theorem
1.23
, and reformulate it using
the new notions and formulae presented in §
2
to

4
.
More precisely, we shall explain how to compute the volume functions defined
in (
1.13
) by

\mathcal{J}:\ell\mapsto\int_{\ell_{Y}({\mathbf{c}})=\ell}\prod_{j\in V}f_{j}(x_{j})\prod_{j=1}^{k}x_{i_{j}}\delta(x_{i_{j}}-x_{i_{j}^{\prime}})\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}^{\mathrm{WP}}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}(\mathbf{x},Y)}{\,\mathrm{d}\ell}

as
pseudo-convolutions
of functions in the class
\mathcal{F}
– a notion that will be formalized
in §
6
.

We recall that, in the integral above,
V
is a subset of
\partial\mathbf{S}
. By our
conventions introduced in §
3.3
, we identify
\partial\mathbf{S}
with

\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}
, and hence
V
with a subset of
\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}\subseteq\Lambda
.
We write
V^{\beta}:=V\cap\Lambda^{\beta}
and
V^{\Gamma}:=V\cap\Lambda^{\Gamma}
, so that
V^{\beta}
are the
components of
V
that are components of the multi-curve
\beta
, and
V^{\Gamma}
those that are not.

5.1.
Change of variables

Let us change variables using our Jacobian computation, and more precisely its Dirac variant,
Corollary
3.13
. We obtain that:

\mathcal{J}(\ell)=2^{r}\int_{\ell_{Y}({\mathbf{c}})=\ell}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-4.0mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-4.5mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-5.0mu{\rm{l}}}}_{\mathfrak{D}}(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})\prod_{\lambda\in V}f_{\lambda}(y_{\lambda})\frac{\prod_{\lambda\in V^{\beta}}\sinh\left(\frac{y_{\lambda}}{2}\right)\prod_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{in}}^{\beta}}\sinh^{2}\left(\frac{y_{\lambda}}{2}\right)}{\prod_{\lambda\in V^{\Gamma}}\sinh\left(\frac{y_{\lambda}}{2}\right)}\\
T_{V,\mathrm{m}}(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})\prod_{i=1}^{r}\sinh(L_{i})\frac{\,\mathrm{d}^{r}\vec{L}\,\mathrm{d}^{2r}\vec{\theta}}{\,\mathrm{d}\ell}

where
\mathfrak{D}
is the domain of variation of the new variables
(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})
, studied
in §
3.5
, and
T_{V,\mathrm{m}}
is an explicit distribution which we shall leave as
such. The integral
\mathcal{J}(\ell)
can be rewritten under the simpler form

(5.1)

\mathcal{J}(\ell)=2^{r}\int_{\ell_{Y}({\mathbf{c}})=\ell}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-4.0mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-4.5mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-5.0mu{\rm{l}}}}_{\mathfrak{D}}(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in V^{\beta}_{+}\end{subarray}}g_{\lambda}(y_{\lambda})\prod_{\lambda\in V^{\Gamma}}u_{\lambda}(y_{\lambda})\,T_{V,\mathrm{m}}(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})\prod_{i=1}^{r}\sinh(L_{i})\frac{\,\mathrm{d}^{r}\vec{L}\,\mathrm{d}^{2r}\vec{\theta}}{\,\mathrm{d}\ell},

by setting
V^{\beta}_{+}:=V^{\beta}\cup\Lambda_{\mathrm{in}}^{\beta}
and, for
\lambda\in\Lambda
,

\displaystyle g_{\lambda}(y):=f_{\lambda}(y)\sinh\left(\frac{y}{2}\right)\qquad\text{and}\qquad u_{\lambda}(y):=\frac{f_{\lambda}(y)}{\sinh\left(\frac{y}{2}\right)}

where
f_{\lambda}
is already defined for
\lambda\in V\subseteq\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}
, and is extended to

\lambda\in\Lambda
by letting

\begin{cases}f_{\lambda}(y):=1&\text{for }\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}\setminus V\\
f_{\lambda}(y):=\sinh\left(\frac{y}{2}\right)&\text{for }\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{in}}.\end{cases}

5.2.
Expansion of the product in the integral

Let us now focus on the factor

(5.2)

\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in V^{\beta}_{+}\end{subarray}}g_{\lambda}(y_{\lambda})\prod_{V^{\Gamma}}u_{\lambda}(y_{\lambda})

appearing in the integral
\mathcal{J}
.

5.2.1.
The functions
g_{\lambda}

For technical considerations related to cancellations at zero in the integral, let us first rewrite
for each
\lambda\in V^{\beta}_{+}

g_{\lambda}(y):=\tilde{g}_{\lambda}(y)\chi_{\textrm{z}}(y)^{2},

where
\chi_{\textrm{z}}
is the function introduced in Notation
1.22
. We notice that we can apply
the Friedman–Ramanujan hypothesis to every term
\tilde{g}_{\lambda}
appearing in the product
above. Indeed, for
\lambda\in V^{\beta}
, by the hypothesis on the functions

(f_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in V}
in Notation
1.22
, we have that

\tilde{g}_{\lambda}\in\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{K}_{\lambda},\mathrm{N}_{\lambda}}
and, if
p_{\lambda}
denotes its principal
term,

(5.3)

|\tilde{g}_{\lambda}(y)-p_{\lambda}(y)\,e^{y}|\leq\|\tilde{g}_{\lambda}\|_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{K}_{\lambda},\mathrm{N}_{\lambda}}}(1+y)^{\mathrm{N}_{\lambda}-1}e^{y/2}

where
p_{\lambda}
is a polynomial of degree
\leq\mathrm{K}_{\lambda}-1
.
For
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{in}}^{\beta}
, we simply notice that

\tilde{g}_{\lambda}(y)=\sinh^{2}(y/2)/\chi_{\textrm{z}}(y)^{2}
which belongs to
\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{K}_{\lambda},\mathrm{N}_{\lambda}}

for the integers
(\mathrm{K}_{\lambda},\mathrm{N}_{\lambda}):=(1,1)
, thanks to the fact that
\sinh^{2}(y/2)
has a
zero of order
2
at
0
. Its principal term is
p_{\lambda}(y)e^{y}
with
p_{\lambda}(y):=1/4
. We
can write the Friedman–Ramanujan hypothesis explicitely for these terms and obtain

(5.4)

\displaystyle|\tilde{g}_{\lambda}(y)-p_{\lambda}(y)\,e^{y}|\leq\|\tilde{g}_{\lambda}\|_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{K}_{\lambda},\mathrm{N}_{\lambda}}}e^{y/2}

where
\|\tilde{g}_{\lambda}\|_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{K}_{\lambda},\mathrm{N}_{\lambda}}}
is a fixed universal constant.

We pick a set of indices
W^{\beta}\subseteq V^{\beta}_{+}
for which we take the remainder term in the
approximations (
5.3
) and (
5.4
). This allows us to expand the product as a sum of terms of the form

\prod_{\lambda\in V^{\beta}_{+}}\chi_{\textrm{z}}(y_{\lambda})^{2}\prod_{\lambda\in V^{\beta}_{+}\setminus W^{\beta}}p_{\lambda}(y_{\lambda})e^{y_{\lambda}}\prod_{\lambda\in W^{\beta}}\|\tilde{g}_{\lambda}\|_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{K}_{\lambda},\mathrm{N}_{\lambda}}}\,r_{\lambda}(y_{\lambda})

where for all
\lambda\in W^{\beta}
,

|r_{\lambda}(y_{\lambda})|\leq(1+y_{\lambda})^{\mathrm{N}_{\lambda}-1}e^{y_{\lambda}/2}
.

We then use the fact that, for
\lambda\in\Lambda^{\beta}
,
y_{\lambda}
is a linear combination of
the components of the vector
\vec{\theta}
, given by the simple expression (
4.15
):

(5.5)

y_{\lambda}=\sum_{q\in\Theta_{\ell}(\lambda)}\theta_{q}.

Note that the sets
\Theta_{\ell}(\lambda)
for
\lambda\in V^{\beta}_{+}\setminus W^{\beta}
are disjoint and
their union is
\Theta_{\ell}(V^{\beta}_{+}\setminus W^{\beta})
(note that this is not a priori the case for
the sets
\Theta_{\ell}(\lambda)
for
\lambda\in\Lambda
, and we here use the fact that

\lambda\in\Lambda^{\beta}
). We obtain that we can further expand the product as a sum of
contribution of the form

(5.6)

\mathrm{fn}((\tilde{g}_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in V^{\beta}})\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in V^{\beta}_{+}\end{subarray}}\chi_{\textrm{z}}(y_{\lambda})^{2}\prod_{\lambda\in V^{\beta}_{+}\setminus W^{\beta}}\prod_{q\in\Theta_{\ell}(\lambda)}\theta_{q}^{\mathrm{K}_{q}^{\lambda}}\prod_{q\in\Theta_{\ell}(V^{\beta}_{+}\setminus W^{\beta})}e^{\theta_{q}}\prod_{\lambda\in W^{\beta}}r_{\lambda}(y_{\lambda})

where for
\lambda\in V^{\beta}_{+}\setminus W^{\beta}
,

\sum_{q\in\Theta_{\ell}(\lambda)}\mathrm{K}_{q}^{\lambda}\leq\mathrm{K}_{\lambda}-1
. In particular, for

\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{in}}^{\beta}
,
\mathrm{K}_{q}^{\lambda}=0
.
We also note that the constant in front can be bounded by
\prod_{\lambda\in V^{\beta}}\|\tilde{g}_{\lambda}\|_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{K}_{\lambda},\mathrm{N}_{\lambda}}}
.

5.2.2.
The functions
u_{\lambda}

Similarly, applying the Friedman–Ramanujan hypothesis to the functions

\tilde{g}_{\lambda}(y)=f_{\lambda}(y)\sinh(y/2)/\chi_{\textrm{z}}(y)^{2}\in\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{K}_{\lambda},\mathrm{N}_{\lambda}}
for

\lambda\in V^{\Gamma}
allows us to replace the functions
u_{\lambda}
by an approximation,
this time by the polynomial function
p_{\lambda}
coming from the principal term of

\tilde{g}_{\lambda}
, up to an exponentially decaying error. More precisely, using the relation

u_{\lambda}(y)=\frac{4\chi_{\textrm{z}}(y)^{2}}{(1-e^{-y})^{2}}e^{-y}\tilde{g}_{\lambda}(y)

together with the hypothesis (
1.12
) on the function
\chi_{\textrm{z}}
, we see that

|u_{\lambda}(y)-p_{\lambda}(y)|\leq 32\|\tilde{g}_{\lambda}\|_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{K}_{\lambda},\mathrm{N}_{\lambda}}}(1+y)^{\mathrm{N}_{\lambda}-1}e^{-y/2}.

We now replace each term in the product by this expansion, and obtain it can be rewritten as a sum
of terms of the form

\prod_{\lambda\in V^{\Gamma}\setminus W^{\Gamma}_{0}}p_{\lambda}(y_{\lambda})\prod_{\lambda\in W^{\Gamma}_{0}}32\|\tilde{g}_{\lambda}\|_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{K}_{\lambda},\mathrm{N}_{\lambda}}}\,r_{\lambda}^{0}(y_{\lambda})

where
W^{\Gamma}_{0}\subseteq V^{\Gamma}
and
|r_{\lambda}^{0}(y)|\leq(1+y)^{\mathrm{N}_{\lambda}-1}e^{-y/2}
for all

\lambda\in W^{\Gamma}_{0}
.

We now express
p_{\lambda}(y_{\lambda})
as a linear combination of monomials, and use the expression
for
y_{\lambda}
in terms of
(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})
obtained in §
4.4
. Note that, for
these terms which are not components of
\beta
, the expression is more complex, and not a simple
linear combination. More precisely, by (
4.24
),

(5.7)

y_{\lambda}=\sum_{n=1}^{m(\lambda)}\tau_{n}^{\lambda}-2\log 2+2\log F_{m(\lambda)}(\vec{L}^{\lambda},\vec{\tau}^{\lambda})+\mathcal{O}\left(e^{-y_{\lambda}}\right)

where
\vec{L}^{\lambda}=(L_{1}^{\lambda},\ldots,L_{m(\lambda)}^{\lambda})
and

\vec{\tau}^{\lambda}=(\tau_{1}^{\lambda},\ldots,\tau_{m(\lambda)}^{\lambda})
are the lengths of the
bars and segments
\mathcal{K}
appearing in the description of
\Gamma_{\lambda}
as a polygonal curve, and

F_{m(\lambda)}
is the function defined in (
4.20
). We can further express the parameters

\tau
appearing in the sum in (
5.7
) in terms of
\vec{\theta}
using
(
4.25
) which states that

\tau_{n}^{\lambda}=\sum_{q\in\Theta_{\tau}(\lambda,n)}\theta_{q}.

The set of indices involved in writing
\vec{\tau}^{\lambda}
is

\Theta_{\ell}(\lambda)=\bigcup_{n=1}^{m(\lambda)}\Theta_{\tau}(\lambda,n)
.
Altogether, we obtain that the product of the
u_{\lambda}
terms can be expressed as a sum of terms
of the form

(5.8)

\mathrm{fn}((\tilde{g}_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in V^{\Gamma}})\prod_{\lambda\in V^{\Gamma}\setminus W^{\Gamma}}\Big[(\log F_{m(\lambda)}(\vec{L}^{\lambda},\vec{\tau}^{\lambda}))^{d_{\lambda}}\prod_{q\in\Theta_{\ell}(\lambda)}\theta_{q}^{\mathrm{K}_{q}^{\lambda}}\Big]\prod_{\lambda\in W^{\Gamma}}r_{\lambda}(\vec{L},\vec{\theta}^{\lambda})

where:

•

W^{\Gamma}\subseteq V^{\Gamma}
is the set of indices with exponential decay (it
contains the set
W^{\Gamma}_{0}
above, but can contain more elements due to the exponentially
decaying term in (
5.7
));

•

for
\lambda\in W^{\Gamma}
,
\vec{\theta}^{\lambda}=(\theta_{q})_{q\in\Theta_{\ell}(\lambda)}
is
the set of
\theta
parameters required to express
y_{\lambda}

and

(5.9)

|r_{\lambda}(\vec{L},\vec{\theta}^{\lambda})|\leq(1+y_{\lambda})^{\mathrm{N}_{\lambda}-1}e^{-y_{\lambda}/2};

•

for
\lambda\in V^{\Gamma}\setminus W^{\Gamma}
,

\sum_{q\in\Theta_{\ell}(\lambda)}\mathrm{K}_{q}^{\lambda}+\,\mathrm{d}_{\lambda}\leq\mathrm{K}_{\lambda}-1
;

•

the constant factor is bounded by
\prod_{\lambda\in V^{\Gamma}}\|\tilde{g}_{\lambda}\|_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{K}_{\lambda},\mathrm{N}_{\lambda}}}
.

5.2.3.
Conclusion and substitution in the integral

Taking equations (
5.1
), (
5.6
) and (
5.8
), we obtain that the
integral
\mathcal{J}(\ell)
can be rewritten as a fixed sum of terms of the form

(5.10)

\begin{split}&\mathrm{fn}((\tilde{g}_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in V},r)\int_{\ell_{Y}(\mathbf{c})=\ell}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-4.0mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-4.5mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-5.0mu{\rm{l}}}}_{\mathfrak{D}}(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})\prod_{\lambda\in V^{\beta}_{+}}\chi_{\textrm{z}}(y_{\lambda})^{2}\prod_{q\in\Theta_{\ell}(V_{+}\setminus W)}\theta_{q}^{\mathrm{K}_{q}}\prod_{q\in\Theta_{\ell}(V^{\beta}_{+}\setminus W^{\beta})}e^{\theta_{q}}\\
&\prod_{\lambda\in V^{\Gamma}\setminus W^{\Gamma}}(\log F_{m(\lambda)}(\vec{L}^{\lambda},\vec{\tau}^{\lambda}))^{d_{\lambda}}\prod_{\lambda\in W}r_{\lambda}(\vec{L},\vec{\theta}^{\lambda})\,T_{V,\mathrm{m}}(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})\prod_{i=1}^{r}\sinh(L_{i})\frac{\,\mathrm{d}^{r}\vec{L}\,\mathrm{d}^{2r}\vec{\theta}}{\,\mathrm{d}\ell},\end{split}

where:

•

V_{+}:=V\cup\Lambda_{\mathrm{in}}^{\beta}
and
W:=W^{\beta}\cup W^{\Gamma}\subseteq V_{+}
;

•

\mathrm{K}_{q}:=\sum_{\lambda\in V_{+}\setminus W,\Theta_{\ell}(\lambda)\ni q}\mathrm{K}_{q}^{\lambda}
(with

\mathrm{K}_{q}^{\lambda}
defined to be
0
if non already defined);

•

|r_{\lambda}(\vec{L},\vec{\theta}^{\lambda})|\leq(1+y_{\lambda})^{\mathrm{N}_{\lambda}-1}e^{\alpha_{\lambda}y_{\lambda}/2}
with
\alpha_{\lambda}=1
if
\lambda\in W^{\beta}
and
-1
if

\lambda\in W^{\Gamma}

•

the constant factor is bounded by
\mathrm{fn}(r)\prod_{\lambda\in V}\|\tilde{g}_{\lambda}\|_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{K}_{\lambda},\mathrm{N}_{\lambda}}}
.

Note that we have

(5.11)

\sum_{q\in\Theta_{\ell}(V_{+}\setminus W)}\mathrm{K}_{q}+\sum_{\lambda\in V^{\Gamma}\setminus W^{\Gamma}}d_{\lambda}\leq\sum_{\lambda\in V}(\mathrm{K}_{\lambda}-1).

5.3.
Active and neutral parameters

Our final aim is to prove that the integral
\mathcal{J}
is a Friedman–Ramanujan function following the
method sketched in §
1.3.8
to prove that a convolution
f_{1}\ast f_{2}
is a
Friedman–Ramanujan function. We recall that, in this case, the idea is to observe that

\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{K}_{1}+\mathrm{K}_{2}}(f_{1}\ast f_{2})=\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{K}_{1}}f_{1}\ast\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{K}_{2}}f_{2}
. Hence, if we choose the
indices
\mathrm{K}_{1}
and
\mathrm{K}_{2}
so that
\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{K}_{i}}f_{i}
are elements of
\mathcal{R}
(which we can do if

f_{i}\in\mathcal{F}
), then we can easily deduce by an upper bound that

\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{K}_{1}+\mathrm{K}_{2}}(f_{1}\ast f_{2})\in\mathcal{R}
, which in turn implies that
f_{1}\ast f_{2}\in\mathcal{F}
.

Now, if a function
f_{i}
is already an element of
\mathcal{R}
, we do not need to cancel its principal
term; in other words, we do not need to apply the operator
\mathcal{L}
to
f_{i}
in order to establish the
Friedman–Ramanujan property for
f_{1}\ast f_{2}
(we can take
\mathrm{K}_{i}=0
). In our language, this means
that the variable
x_{i}
corresponding to
f_{i}
in the convolution

f_{1}\ast f_{2}(x)=\int_{x_{1}+x_{2}=x}f_{1}(x_{1})f_{2}(x_{2})\frac{\,\mathrm{d}x_{1}\,\mathrm{d}x_{2}}{\,\mathrm{d}x}
is a
neutral
parameter
. To the contrary, a variable which appears with a principal term in the integral (and
will therefore need to be cancelled by applying the operator
\mathcal{L}
) will be called an
active
parameter
.

Notation 5.1
.

We partition the set of
\Theta
into sets of
active and neutral parameters

\Theta=\Theta_{\mathrm{ac}}\sqcup\Theta_{\mathrm{nt}}
where

(5.12)

\displaystyle\Theta_{\mathrm{ac}}:=\{q\in\Theta_{\ell}(V^{\beta}_{+})\,:\,q\notin\Theta_{\ell}(W)\}.

We denote as
\vec{\theta}_{\mathrm{ac}}=(\theta_{q})_{q\in\Theta_{\mathrm{ac}}}
and

\vec{\theta}_{\mathrm{nt}}=(\theta_{q})_{q\in\Theta_{\mathrm{nt}}}
the components of

\vec{\theta}=(\theta_{q})_{q\in\Theta}
in those two sets of indices. We will call

neutral parameters
the components of
\vec{\theta}_{\mathrm{nt}}
together with the vector

\vec{L}=(L_{1},\ldots,L_{r})
, and
active parameters
the components of
\vec{\theta}_{\mathrm{ac}}
.

In the following, the convolution argument will concern the variables
\vec{\theta}_{\mathrm{ac}}
, while
\vec{L}
and

\vec{\theta}_{\mathrm{nt}}
are kept fixed. We will not need to apply the operator
\mathcal{L}
with respect to the neutral
variables, because their contribution does not yield a principal term in the product (
5.10
).

Remark 5.2
.

The reason why we do not need to work on the neutral parameters is that they will appear without
a factor
2
in our comparison estimate, Proposition
9.3
. We have already
commented on the importance of factors of
2
in Proposition
1.30
, a simple
comparison estimate. We saw in Lemma
3.27
how double-filling loops (for which all
parameters are neutral) can easily be proven to be Friedman–Ramanujan remainders, by direct
inequalities, without any need to apply the operator
\mathcal{L}
.

We now set aside the neutral parameters in (
5.10
) and consider them as
constants. The focus of the remainder of the paper is thus on integrals of the form

(5.13)

\displaystyle\mathbf{Int}(\ell)=\int_{h(\vec{\theta}_{\mathrm{ac}})=\ell}\varphi_{0}(\vec{\theta}_{\mathrm{ac}})\prod_{q\in\Theta_{\mathrm{ac}}}\theta_{q}^{\mathrm{K}_{q}}e^{\theta_{q}}\,\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\vec{\theta}_{\mathrm{ac}}}{\,\mathrm{d}\ell}

where:

•

all neutral variables
\vec{L},\vec{\theta}_{\mathrm{nt}}
are fixed;

•

we have the upper bound

(5.14)

\sum_{q\in\Theta_{\mathrm{ac}}}\mathrm{K}_{q}\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in V^{\beta}_{+}\\
\Theta_{\ell}(\lambda)\not\subseteq\Theta_{\ell}(W)\end{subarray}}(\mathrm{K}_{\lambda}-1)\leq\sum_{\lambda\in V^{\beta}_{+}\setminus W}(\mathrm{K}_{\lambda}-1);

•

the weight function
\varphi_{0}
is defined as

(5.15)

\displaystyle\varphi_{0}(\vec{\theta}_{\mathrm{ac}})={\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-4.0mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-4.5mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-5.0mu{\rm{l}}}}_{\mathfrak{D}}(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})\prod_{\lambda\in V^{\beta}_{+}}\chi_{\textrm{z}}(y_{\lambda})^{2}\prod_{\lambda\in V^{\Gamma}\setminus W^{\Gamma}}(\log F_{m(\lambda)}(\vec{L}^{\lambda},\vec{\tau}^{\lambda}))^{d_{\lambda}}

and, for each
\lambda\in V^{\Gamma}\setminus W^{\Gamma}
,
d_{\lambda}\leq\mathrm{K}_{\lambda}-1
;

•

the level function
h
is the length function of
\mathbf{c}
,

(5.16)

\displaystyle h(\vec{\theta}_{\mathrm{ac}})=\ell_{Y}({\mathbf{c}})=\sum_{i=1}^{\mathfrak{n}}\ell_{Y}({\mathbf{c}}_{i}).

It will be useful in the following to keep track of the quantities we have taken out of the integral
due to depending only on neutral variables:

(5.17)

\mathrm{fn}((\tilde{g}_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in V},r)\prod_{q\in\Theta_{\ell}(V_{+})\cap\Theta_{\mathrm{nt}}}\theta_{q}^{\mathrm{K}_{q}}\prod_{\lambda\in W}r_{\lambda}(\vec{L},\vec{\theta}_{\mathrm{nt}})\,T_{V,\mathrm{m}}(\vec{L},\vec{\theta}_{\mathrm{nt}})\prod_{i=1}^{r}\sinh(L_{i}).

Comparing with the forthcoming definition (
6.3
), this appears as the

(h,\varphi_{0})
-convolution of the Friedman–Ramanujan functions

(\theta_{q}^{\mathrm{K}_{q}}e^{\theta_{q}})_{q\in\Theta_{\mathrm{ac}}}
. The reason why we use the word
convolution

is that the function
h(\vec{\theta}_{\mathrm{ac}})
behaves in many respects like the sum

\sum_{q\in\Theta_{\mathrm{ac}}}\theta_{q}
(plus a function of the neutral variables), and the function

\varphi_{0}
will be shown to have “small” derivatives in some sense, so it can be thought of as
close to being constant. The purpose of §
6
is to make these notions precise.

6.
Pseudo-convolutions and stability of the FR hypothesis

In this section, we introduce a notion of
pseudo-convolution

f_{1}\star\ldots\star f_{n}|^{h}_{\varphi}
of functions
(f_{i})_{1\leq i\leq n}
with the level
function
h
and the weight
\varphi
, which generalizes the usual convolution

f_{1}\ast\ldots\ast f_{n}
, and is motivated by the form of (
5.13
). We then study the way
the operator
\mathcal{L}=\id-\mathcal{P}
acts on pseudo-convolutions in §
6.2
. This will provide statements of the following kind: if

f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}
are Friedman–Ramanujan functions, and if
(h,\varphi)
satisfy adequate
assumptions, then
f_{1}\star\ldots\star f_{n}|^{h}_{\varphi}
is also a Friedman–Ramanujan function; see
Theorem
6.28
. Roughly speaking, the function
h(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
is assumed to be close to

x_{1}+\ldots+x_{n}+C
and
\varphi
to a constant (in a certain topology defined in §
6.3
).

6.1.
Definition of the notion of pseudo-convolution

We make the following definition.

Definition 6.1
.

Take
n\geq 1
. Let
\varphi:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a locally bounded, measurable function,
and
h
be a real-valued mesurable function defined on a set containing the support

\operatorname{supp}(\varphi)
of
\varphi
. We assume that for every compact subset
K\subset\mathbb{R}
, the set

h^{-1}(K)\cap\operatorname{supp}(\varphi)
has finite measure.

We call
(h,\varphi)
-convolution
of
n
locally integrable functions

f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
the pushforward of the product measure

\varphi(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\prod_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}(x_{i})\,\mathrm{d}x_{i}
by the map
h
. In other words, it is
the distribution
\nu
on
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
defined by the fact that, for every continuous function

F:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
with compact support,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}F(x)\nu(\mathrm{d}x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}F(h(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}))\,\varphi(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\prod_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}(x_{i})\,\mathrm{d}x_{i}.

In our applications, the functions
h
and
\varphi
will always be much more than measurable. More
precisely, throughout this article, we suppose the following.

Notation 6.2

(Assumptions on
(\varphi,h)
)
.

Take
a>0
. We assume the following.

•

\varphi:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
function of support included in

(a,+\infty)^{n}
.

•

h:[a,+\infty)^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
function.

•

For any index
1\leq i\leq n
and any fixed

\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{i}=(x_{j})_{j\not=i}\in[a,+\infty)^{n-1}
, the function

\displaystyle h_{i}^{\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{i}}:\,x_{i}\mapsto h(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})

is a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-diffeomorphism from
[a,+\infty)
onto its image; we denote

\ell\mapsto h_{i}^{-1}(\ell,\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{i})
its inverse.

•

The derivative

(\ell,\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{i})\mapsto\partial_{\ell}h_{i}^{-1}(\ell,\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{i})
is bounded on
its domain of definition.

The following proposition follows directly from the change of variable formula:

Proposition 6.3
.

Under the hypotheses of Notation
6.2
, the
(h,\varphi)
-convolution
\nu
of any
n

locally integrable functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}
admits a density with respect to the Lebesgue
measure, with the following expression

(6.1)

\displaystyle\frac{d\nu}{\,\mathrm{d}\ell}(\ell)=\int\varphi\big(x_{1},\ldots,h_{i}^{-1}(\ell,\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{i}),\ldots,x_{n}\big)f_{i}(h_{i}^{-1}(\ell,\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{i}))\prod_{j\not=i}f_{j}(x_{j})\,\partial_{\ell}h_{i}^{-1}(\ell,\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{i})\,\mathrm{d}\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{i}

where
\,\mathrm{d}\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{i}:=\prod_{j\not=i}\,\mathrm{d}x_{j}
. In particular this is independent of the choice of
the index
i
.

Notation 6.4
.

The expression (
6.1
) will be denoted
f_{1}\star\ldots\star f_{n}|^{h}_{\varphi}
.

If
x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
are related by the implicit relation
h(x)=\ell
, we will denote

\frac{\,\mathrm{d}x_{1}\ldots\,\mathrm{d}x_{n}}{\,\mathrm{d}\ell}=\frac{\,\mathrm{d}x}{\,\mathrm{d}\ell}
any of the equivalent expressions of the
measure

(6.2)

\displaystyle\frac{\,\mathrm{d}x_{1}\ldots\,\mathrm{d}x_{n}}{\,\mathrm{d}\ell}=\frac{\,\mathrm{d}x}{\,\mathrm{d}\ell}:=\partial_{\ell}h_{i}^{-1}(\ell,\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{i})\,\mathrm{d}\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{i}.

This corresponds to the
disintegration of the Lebesgue measure
\,\mathrm{d}x=\,\mathrm{d}x_{1}\ldots\,\mathrm{d}x_{n}
on the level-sets of
h
. Thus,
we can write

(6.3)

\displaystyle f_{1}\star\ldots\star f_{n}|^{h}_{\varphi}(\ell)=

\displaystyle\int_{h(x)=\ell}\varphi(x)\prod_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}(x_{i})\,\frac{\,\mathrm{d}x}{\,\mathrm{d}\ell}.

Example 6.5
.

The usual convolution corresponds to
h(x)=x_{1}+\ldots+x_{n}
, and either
\varphi\equiv 1
, or

\varphi(x)={\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-4.0mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-4.5mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-5.0mu{\rm{l}}}}_{x_{1}\geq 0,\ldots,x_{n}\geq 0}
(in the case of convolutions of functions
defined on
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
).

Remark 6.6

(Associativity)
.

The associativity of the usual convolution follows from associativity of
the addition. For a general function
h
, the associativity rule may be replaced by formulae such
as

\displaystyle f_{1}\star\ldots\star f_{n}|^{h}_{\varphi}(\ell)=\int f_{2}\star\ldots\star f_{n}|^{h_{x_{1}}}_{\varphi_{x_{1}}}(\ell)f_{1}(x_{1})\,\mathrm{d}x_{1}

where
\varphi_{x_{1}}
(and similarly
h_{x_{1}}
) is the function of
n-1
variables defined by
freezing the first variable to
x_{1}
:

\varphi_{x_{1}}(x_{2},x_{3},\ldots,x_{n}):=\varphi(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots,x_{n}).

This simply expresses the fact that we can do the convolution

f_{2}\star\ldots\star f_{n}|^{h_{x_{1}}}_{\varphi_{x_{1}}}
with respect to
x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}
(while

x_{1}
is kept fixed) and then integrate with respect to
f_{1}(x_{1})\,\mathrm{d}x_{1}
, to obtain the
pseudo-convolution
f_{1}\star\ldots\star f_{n}|^{h}_{\varphi}
.

In the rest of the article, we will need to use this associativity rule for multiple variables and
pairs
(h,\varphi)
. In order to avoid cumbersome notations, we will often omit the index
x_{1}

in the freezed functions
h_{x_{1}}
and
\varphi_{x_{1}}
when the context allows to avoid any confusion.

6.2.
Action of the operator
\mathcal{L}_{\ell}
on pseudo-convolution

In §
1.3.7
we defined two operators acting on locally integrable functions,
\mathcal{P}_{x}=\int_{0}^{x}
(taking a primitive) and
\mathcal{L}_{x}=\id-\mathcal{P}_{x}
. Since we are dealing with functions of several variables
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
, the subscript

x_{i}
will serve to indicate that the primitive is taken with respect to the variable
x_{i}
.

The goal of this section is to compare

\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{K}_{1}+\ldots+\mathrm{K}_{n}}_{\ell}(f_{1}\star\ldots\star f_{n}|^{h}_{\varphi})
with

(\mathcal{L}_{x_{1}}^{\mathrm{K}_{1}}f_{1})\star\ldots\star(\mathcal{L}_{x_{n}}^{\mathrm{K}_{n}}f_{n})|^{h}_{\varphi}
. With the usual
convolution on functions defined on
\mathbb{R}
, we have the exact identity

(6.4)

\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{K}_{1}+\ldots+\mathrm{K}_{n}}_{\ell}(f_{1}*\ldots*f_{n})=(\mathcal{L}_{x_{1}}^{\mathrm{K}_{1}}f_{1})*\ldots*(\mathcal{L}_{x_{n}}^{\mathrm{K}_{n}}f_{n}).

Note that, in the case of functions defined on
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
only, there are additional boundary
terms coming from
x_{i}=0
in this relation (we leave this observation as an exercise to the reader).

For general functions
h
and
\varphi
, the two expressions

\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{K}_{1}+\ldots+\mathrm{K}_{n}}_{\ell}(f_{1}\star\ldots\star f_{n}|^{h}_{\varphi})
and

(\mathcal{L}_{x_{1}}^{\mathrm{K}_{1}}f_{1})\star\ldots\star(\mathcal{L}_{x_{n}}^{\mathrm{K}_{n}}f_{n})|^{h}_{\varphi}
will not be
equal. We provide in Theorem
6.16
a detailed expression for the error term produced by
replacing one by the other.

We saw in §
1.3.8
how the identity (
6.4
) can be exploited to prove
the stability of the Friedman–Ramanujan hypothesis by convolution. In §
6.4
,
we will use the results of this section to provide a similar stability result, under certain
hypotheses on the level function
h
and the weight
\varphi
.

6.2.1.
Basic calculus in one variable

We gather in the following lemmas the behavior of
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{L}
under change of variables and multiplication. The change of variable formula yields:

Lemma 6.7
.

Let
h:(0,+\infty)\rightarrow(h_{\inf},+\infty)
be a

\mathcal{C}^{1}
-diffeomorphism with
h_{\inf}\geq 0
, and
h^{-1}
be its inverse. Then, for any continuous
function
G:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\ell}(G\circ h^{-1})=(\mathcal{P}_{x}G)\circ h^{-1}+R_{x}G\circ h^{-1}

where
R_{x}G(x):=\mathcal{P}_{x}(G(\partial_{x}h-1))
.

Proof.

This is just a way of writing

\int_{h_{\inf}}^{\ell}G(h^{-1}(u))\,\mathrm{d}u=\int_{0}^{h^{-1}(\ell)}G(t)\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{0}^{h^{-1}(\ell)}G(t)(h^{\prime}(t)-1)\,\mathrm{d}t.

It is important to note that
\ell\mapsto G(h^{-1}(\ell))
is only defined on
(h_{\inf},+\infty)
, so the operator
\mathcal{P}_{\ell}
should, a priori, be defined as
\int_{h_{\inf}}^{\ell}
. However, if we extend
G(h^{-1}(\ell))
to be
0
if
\ell\leq h_{\inf}
, the formula remains true taking
\mathcal{P}_{\ell}=\int_{0}^{\ell}
.
∎

Integration by parts can be written in the following form:

Lemma 6.8
.

For any continuous function
G:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and any
\mathcal{C}^{1}
function

\varphi:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{x}(\varphi G)=\varphi\,\mathcal{P}_{x}G-\mathcal{P}_{x}\big(\partial_{x}{\varphi}\,\mathcal{P}_{x}G\big).

Proof.

This just says that

\int_{0}^{x}\varphi(t)G(t)\,\mathrm{d}t=\varphi(x)\int_{0}^{x}G(t)\,\mathrm{d}t-\int_{0}^{x}\varphi^{\prime}(t)\Big(\int_{0}^{t}G(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\Big)\,\mathrm{d}t

which is indeed true by integration by parts.
∎

By combining both previous formulas and iterating them, we obtain:

Proposition 6.9
.

With the notations of Lemma
6.7
and
6.8
, for any
\mathrm{K}\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{K}}_{\ell}((\varphi G)\circ h^{-1})=\,

\displaystyle(\varphi\,\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{K}}_{x}G)\circ h^{-1}

\displaystyle-\sum_{t=0}^{\mathrm{K}-1}\mathcal{L}_{\ell}^{\mathrm{K}-1-t}\big((\varphi\,R_{x}(\mathcal{L}_{x}^{t}G))\circ h^{-1}\big)

\displaystyle+\sum_{t=0}^{\mathrm{K}-1}\mathcal{L}_{\ell}^{\mathrm{K}-1-t}\mathcal{P}_{\ell}\big(\partial_{\ell}(\varphi\circ h^{-1})\,\mathcal{P}_{\ell}(\mathcal{L}_{x}^{t}G\circ h^{-1})\big).

Proof.

For
\mathrm{K}=1
, a combination of Lemmas
6.7
and
6.8
(w.r.t. the variable

\ell
) yields

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\ell}((\varphi G)\circ h^{-1})

\displaystyle=(\varphi G)\circ h^{-1}-\mathcal{P}_{\ell}(\varphi\circ h^{-1}\cdot G\circ h^{-1})

\displaystyle\overset{\ref{l:product}}{=}(\varphi G)\circ h^{-1}-\varphi\circ h^{-1}\cdot\mathcal{P}_{\ell}(G\circ h^{-1})+\mathcal{P}_{\ell}\big(\partial_{\ell}(\varphi\circ h^{-1})\,\mathcal{P}_{\ell}(G\circ h^{-1})\big)

\displaystyle\overset{\ref{l:Icompo}}{=}(\varphi\,\mathcal{L}_{x}G)\circ h^{-1}-(\varphi\,R_{x}G)\circ h^{-1}+\mathcal{P}_{\ell}\big(\partial_{\ell}(\varphi\circ h^{-1})\,\mathcal{P}_{\ell}(G\circ h^{-1})\big)

which gives the expression of the commutator
[L,T]
for
with
L=\mathcal{L}
and
TG=(\varphi G)\circ h^{-1}
:

\displaystyle[L,T]G=-(\varphi R_{x}G)\circ h^{-1}+\mathcal{P}_{\ell}\big(\partial_{\ell}(\varphi\circ h^{-1})\,\mathcal{P}_{\ell}(G\circ h^{-1})\big).

We then use the algebraic identity

\displaystyle L^{\mathrm{K}}T=TL^{\mathrm{K}}+\sum_{t=0}^{\mathrm{K}-1}L^{\mathrm{K}-1-t}[L,T]L^{t}.

to obtain the claimed formula for general
\mathrm{K}
.
∎

6.2.2.
Application to the first variable

Proposition
6.9
allows us to compare

\mathcal{L}_{\ell}^{\mathrm{K}}(f_{1}\star\ldots\star f_{n}|^{h}_{\varphi})
with

(\mathcal{L}_{x_{1}}^{\mathrm{K}}f_{1})\star f_{2}\ldots\star f_{n}|^{h}_{\varphi}
, which is a first step towards
the aim of this subsection. We prove the following.

Proposition 6.10
.

For any family of continuous functions
(f_{j})_{1\leq j\leq n}
and any integer
\mathrm{K}\geq 1
,

(6.5)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\ell}^{\mathrm{K}}\big(f_{1}\star\ldots\star f_{n}|^{h}_{\varphi}\big)(\ell)=

\displaystyle\;(\mathcal{L}_{x_{1}}^{\mathrm{K}}f_{1})\star f_{2}\star\ldots\star f_{n}|^{h}_{\varphi\frac{\partial h}{\partial x_{1}}}(\ell)+\mathcal{L}_{\ell}^{\mathrm{K}}\big(f_{1}\star\ldots\star f_{n}|^{h}_{\varphi(1-\frac{\partial h}{\partial x_{1}})}\big)(\ell)

(6.6)

\displaystyle-\sum_{t=0}^{\mathrm{K}-1}\mathcal{L}_{\ell}^{\mathrm{K}-1-t}\Big(\int f_{2}\star\ldots\star f_{n}|^{h_{x_{1}}}_{\varphi\frac{\partial h}{\partial x_{1}}R_{x_{1}}(\mathcal{L}^{t}_{x_{1}}f_{1})}(\ell)\,\mathrm{d}x_{1}\Big)

(6.7)

\displaystyle+\sum_{t=0}^{\mathrm{K}-1}\mathcal{L}_{\ell}^{\mathrm{K}-1-t}\mathcal{P}_{\ell}\Big(\int f_{2}\star\ldots\star f_{n}|^{h_{x_{1}}}_{\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x_{1}}\mathcal{P}_{x_{1}}(\mathcal{L}_{x_{1}}^{t}f_{1}\frac{\partial h}{\partial x_{1}})}(\ell)\,\mathrm{d}x_{1}\Big).

Remark 6.11
.

For the usual convolution, we have
h(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})=x_{1}+\ldots+x_{n}
and

\varphi=1
, so
\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x_{1}}=0
and
\frac{\partial h}{\partial x_{1}}=1

(and hence
R_{x_{1}}=0
). We see that only the first term remains:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\ell}^{\mathrm{K}}(f_{1}\ast\ldots\ast f_{n})=(\mathcal{L}_{x_{1}}^{\mathrm{K}}f_{1})\ast f_{2}\ast\ldots\ast f_{n}.

In the case
\varphi(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})={\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-4.0mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-4.5mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-5.0mu{\rm{l}}}}_{x_{1}\geq 0,\ldots,x_{n}\geq 0}
, we get a boundary
term with a Dirac mass at
x_{1}=0
, it corresponds to the term (
6.7
).

Proof.

We start by writing our pseudo-convolution as:

(6.8)

\displaystyle f_{1}\star\ldots\star f_{n}|^{h}_{\varphi}(\ell)=\int\varphi(h_{1}^{-1}(\ell,\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1}),\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1})\,f_{1}(h_{1}^{-1}(\ell,\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1}))\prod_{i=2}^{n}f_{i}(x_{i})\,\partial_{\ell}h_{1}^{-1}(\ell,\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1})\,\mathrm{d}x_{2}\ldots\,\mathrm{d}x_{n}.

We break (
6.8
) into two terms, writing

\displaystyle\partial_{\ell}h_{1}^{-1}(\ell,\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1})=1+\partial_{\ell}h_{1}^{-1}(\ell,\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1})\Big(1-\frac{\partial h}{\partial x_{1}}(h_{1}^{-1}(\ell,\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1}))\Big).

We observe that the second term on the right-hand-side yields exactly the second announced term
in (
6.5
). We are therefore left to apply the operator
\mathcal{L}_{\ell}^{\mathrm{K}}
to the function

\int\varphi(h_{1}^{-1}(\ell,\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1}),\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1})\,f_{1}(h_{1}^{-1}(\ell,\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1}))\prod_{i=2}^{n}f_{i}(x_{i})\,\mathrm{d}x_{2}\ldots\,\mathrm{d}x_{n}

which we do by applying Proposition
6.9
with respect to the variable
x_{1}
(while

x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}
are frozen parameters) and the function
h=h_{1}
. We obtain:

(6.9)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\ell}^{\mathrm{K}}\big((\varphi f_{1})\circ h_{1}^{-1}\big)=\,

\displaystyle\varphi\circ h_{1}^{-1}\,\big(\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{K}}_{x_{1}}f_{1}\big)\circ h_{1}^{-1}

(6.10)

\displaystyle-\sum_{t=0}^{\mathrm{K}-1}\mathcal{L}_{\ell}^{\mathrm{K}-1-t}\big(\varphi\circ h_{1}^{-1}\,\big(R_{x_{1}}(\mathcal{L}_{x_{1}}^{t}f_{1})\big)\circ h_{1}^{-1}\big)

(6.11)

\displaystyle+\sum_{t=0}^{\mathrm{K}-1}\mathcal{L}_{\ell}^{\mathrm{K}-1-t}\mathcal{P}_{\ell}\big(\partial_{\ell}(\varphi\circ h_{1}^{-1})\,\mathcal{P}_{\ell}(\mathcal{L}_{x_{1}}^{t}f_{1}\circ h_{1}^{-1})\big).

Integrating the terms (
6.9
) and (
6.10
) along

x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}
respectively yield the first term of (
6.5
) and the term
(
6.6
) in the theorem. For the last term (
6.11
), we use the
chain rule

\partial_{\ell}(\varphi\circ h_{1}^{-1})=\Big(\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x_{1}}\circ h_{1}^{-1}\Big)\,\partial_{\ell}h_{1}^{-1},

and the change of variable

\mathcal{P}_{\ell}(\mathcal{L}_{x_{1}}^{t}f_{1}\circ h_{1}^{-1})=\mathcal{P}_{x_{1}}\Big(\mathcal{L}_{x_{1}}^{t}f_{1}\,\frac{\partial h}{\partial x_{1}}\Big)\circ h_{1}^{-1}

which leads to the claim once again by integration.
∎

Notation 6.12
.

For
1\leq j\leq n
,
x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
and
0\leq t\leq\mathrm{K}-1
, introduce the functions

\displaystyle F^{\mathrm{K}}_{j}(x)=\mathcal{L}_{x_{j}}^{\mathrm{K}}f_{j}(x_{j})\,\frac{\partial h}{\partial x_{j}}(x)

\displaystyle G_{j}(x)=f_{j}(x_{j})\Big(1-\frac{\partial h}{\partial x_{j}}(x)\Big)

\displaystyle H^{t}_{j}(x)=-R_{x_{j}}(\mathcal{L}^{t}_{x_{j}}f_{j})(x_{j})\,\frac{\partial h}{\partial x_{j}}(x)

\displaystyle I_{j}^{t}(x)=\mathcal{P}_{x_{j}}\Big(\mathcal{L}_{x_{j}}^{t}f_{j}\frac{\partial h}{\partial x_{j}}\Big)(x).

We can rephrase Proposition
6.10
as follows:

Corollary 6.13
.

For any family of continuous functions
(f_{j})_{1\leq j\leq n}
and any integer
\mathrm{K}\geq 1
,

\mathcal{L}_{\ell}^{\mathrm{K}}(f_{1}\star\ldots\star f_{n}|^{h}_{\varphi})
is the sum of the following functions, or
of the images of these functions under some power
\mathcal{L}_{\ell}^{t}
where
0\leq t\leq\mathrm{K}-1
:

•

\int f_{2}\star\ldots\star f_{n}|^{h_{x_{1}}}_{T_{1}\varphi}(\ell)\,\mathrm{d}x_{1}
where the
operator
T_{1}
is the multiplication by the function
F^{\mathrm{K}}_{1},G_{1}
or
H_{1}^{t}
with

0\leq t\leq\mathrm{K}-1
; or

•

\mathcal{P}_{\ell}\big(\int f_{2}\star\ldots\star f_{n}|^{h_{x_{1}}}_{T_{1}\varphi}(\ell)\,\mathrm{d}x_{1}\big)

where
T_{1}=I_{1}^{t}\partial_{1}
with
0\leq t\leq\mathrm{K}-1
, i.e.
T_{1}
is the derivative with
respect to
x_{1}
followed by multiplication by
I_{1}^{t}
.

Proof.

We observe that by the associativity rule, Theorem
6.10
can also be
written

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\ell}^{\mathrm{K}}\big(f_{1}\star\ldots\star f_{n}|^{h}_{\varphi}\big)(\ell)=

\displaystyle\;\int f_{2}\star\ldots\star f_{n}|^{h_{x_{1}}}_{F_{1}^{K}\varphi}(\ell)\,\mathrm{d}x_{1}+\mathcal{L}_{\ell}^{\mathrm{K}}\Big(\int f_{2}\star\ldots\star f_{n}|^{h_{x_{1}}}_{G_{1}\varphi}(\ell)\,\mathrm{d}x_{1}\Big)

\displaystyle+\sum_{t=0}^{\mathrm{K}-1}\mathcal{L}_{\ell}^{\mathrm{K}-1-t}\int f_{2}\star\ldots\star f_{n}(\ell)|^{h_{x_{1}}}_{H_{1}^{t}\varphi}\,\mathrm{d}x_{1}

\displaystyle+\sum_{t=0}^{\mathrm{K}-1}\mathcal{L}_{\ell}^{\mathrm{K}-1-t}\mathcal{P}_{\ell}\Big(\int f_{2}\star\ldots\star f_{n}|^{h_{x_{1}}}_{I_{1}^{t}\partial_{1}\varphi}(\ell)\,\mathrm{d}x_{1}\Big).

The conclusion then follows directly.
∎

6.2.3.
The multi-variable result

We shall now successively apply Corollary
6.13
in the variables
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
. In order to do so, the following notations for multi-indices will be helpful.

Notation 6.14

(Multi-indices)
.

If
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{n}
is a multi-index, we use the standard notations

|\alpha|=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}
,
\partial^{\alpha}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\partial_{i}^{\alpha_{i}}
(with

\partial_{i}=\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}
) and
\alpha\cdot x=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}x_{i}
.

Most of the time, all of the coefficients will be
\alpha_{i}=0
or
1
, that is to say, we will
manipulate differential operators of order at most one in each variable. This motivates the
following convention.

Notation 6.15
.

If
\pi
is a subset of
\{1,\ldots,n\}
, we identify
\pi
with the multi-index

\alpha={\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-4.0mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-4.5mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-5.0mu{\rm{l}}}}_{\pi}
(the indicator function of
\pi
), and hence denote

\partial^{\pi}=\partial^{{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-4.0mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-4.5mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-5.0mu{\rm{l}}}}_{\pi}}=\prod_{i\in\pi}\partial_{i}
. Then,

{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-4.0mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-4.5mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-5.0mu{\rm{l}}}}_{\pi}\cdot x=\sum_{i\in\pi}x_{i}
.

We now have the following.

Theorem 6.16
.

For any family of continuous functions
(f_{j})_{1\leq j\leq n}
and any integers

(\mathrm{K}_{j})_{1\leq j\leq n}
with
\mathrm{K}_{j}\geq 1
, if
\mathrm{K}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathrm{K}_{j}
, then the function

\mathcal{L}_{\ell}^{\mathrm{K}}(f_{1}\star\ldots\star f_{n}|^{h}_{\varphi})
can be expressed as the sum of the
following functions, or their images under
\mathcal{L}_{\ell}^{t}\mathcal{P}_{\ell}^{t^{\prime}}
for
0\leq t\leq\mathrm{K}-n

and
0\leq t^{\prime}\leq n
,

(6.12)

\displaystyle\mathbf{Int}_{\pi}(\ell):=\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}h(x)=\ell\end{subarray}}\partial^{\pi_{0}}\varphi(x)\prod_{j=1}^{n}\partial^{\pi_{j}}\Phi_{j}(x)\frac{\,\mathrm{d}x}{\,\mathrm{d}\ell}

where:

•

\pi=(\pi_{j})_{0\leq j\leq n}
is a family of disjoint subsets of
\{1,\ldots,n\}
such
that
j\notin\pi_{j}
for all
j
;

•

if
B:=\bigsqcup_{j=0}^{n}\pi_{j}
, then for any
1\leq j\leq n
,

(6.13)

\Phi_{j}=\begin{cases}F^{\mathrm{K}_{j}}_{j},G_{j}\text{ or }H_{j}^{t^{\prime\prime}}\text{ with }0\leq t^{\prime\prime}\leq\mathrm{K}_{j}-1&\text{if }j\not\in B\\
I_{j}^{t^{\prime\prime}}\text{ with }0\leq t^{\prime\prime}\leq\mathrm{K}_{j}-1&\text{if }j\in B.\end{cases}

Remark 6.17
.

The set
B
corresponds to the set of “bad” parameters, i.e. parameters for which
\Phi_{j}=I_{j}^{t^{\prime\prime}}

(we will see in §
6.4.2
why these terms are more problematic). It is also
the set of variables w.r.t. which a derivative appears in (
6.12
).

Remark 6.18
.

The number of possible terms of form (
6.12
) is
\mathrm{fn}(n,\mathrm{K})
.

The proof is an induction on the number of variables to which the operators are applied (Corollary

6.13
corresponding to one variable). More precisely, we prove the following result, and
deduce Theorem
6.16
by taking
k=n
.

Lemma 6.19
.

With the notations of Theorem
6.16
, for any
k\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, the function

\mathcal{L}_{\ell}^{\mathrm{K}_{1}+\ldots+\mathrm{K}_{k}}(f_{1}\star\ldots\star f_{n}|^{h}_{\varphi})
can be expressed as
the sum of the following functions, or their images under
\mathcal{L}_{\ell}^{t}\mathcal{P}_{\ell}^{t^{\prime}}
for

0\leq t\leq\sum_{j=1}^{k}(\mathrm{K}_{j}-1)
and
0\leq t^{\prime}\leq k
,

(6.14)

\displaystyle\mathbf{Int}_{\pi}^{k}(\ell):=\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}h(x)=\ell\end{subarray}}\partial^{\pi_{0}}\varphi(x)\prod_{j=1}^{k}\partial^{\pi_{j}}\Phi_{j}(x)\prod_{j=k+1}^{n}f_{j}(x_{j})\frac{\,\mathrm{d}x}{\,\mathrm{d}\ell}

where:

•

\pi=(\pi_{j})_{0\leq j\leq k}
is a family of disjoint subsets of
\{1,\ldots,k\}
such
that
\pi_{j}
contains only integers
\geq j+1
for all
j
;

•

if
B:=\bigsqcup_{j=0}^{k}\pi_{j}
, then for any
1\leq j\leq k
,
\Phi_{j}
satisfies
(
6.13
).

Proof.

As announced, this is done by induction on
k
. For
k=1
, we see that the announced
expression fits with Corollary
6.13
. If
T_{1}
is a multiplication operator by

\Phi_{1}\in\{F_{1}^{K_{1}},G_{1},H_{1}^{t^{\prime\prime}}\}
, we take
\pi_{0}=\pi_{1}=\emptyset
. If

T_{1}=I_{1}^{t^{\prime\prime}}\,\partial_{1}
, we take
\Phi_{1}=I_{1}^{t^{\prime\prime}}
,
\pi_{0}=\{1\}
and
\pi_{1}=\emptyset
.

Assume the result is known for
k
. We apply Corollary
6.13
with respect to the
variable
x_{k+1}
, to express the image under
\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{K}_{k+1}}
of the function
(
6.14
), corresponding to a given data set
\pi=(\pi_{0},\ldots,\pi_{k})
. Application of
Corollary
6.13
gives rise to four different kind of terms, depending on the nature of
an operator
T_{k+1}
that modifies the function under the integral. For the various situations,
we examine how the data evolves to a new set of data

\pi^{\prime}=(\pi^{\prime}_{0},\pi^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\pi^{\prime}_{k},\pi^{\prime}_{k+1})
, for which we check that the conditions of the
theorem are still satisfied.

First, for the terms for which
T_{k+1}
is a multiplication operator by a function
\Phi_{k+1}

equal to
F^{\mathrm{K}_{k+1}}_{k+1}
,
G_{k+1}
or
H_{k+1}^{t^{\prime\prime}}
, we take

\pi^{\prime}_{0}=\pi_{0}
,
\pi_{j}^{\prime}=\pi_{j}
for
j\leq k
and
\pi_{k+1}^{\prime}=\emptyset
.

The last terms are those for which
T_{k+1}=I_{k+1}^{t^{\prime\prime}}\partial_{{k+1}}
. We need to examine
the effect of applying this operator to an expression of the form

\partial^{\pi_{0}}\varphi\prod_{j=1}^{k}\partial^{\pi_{j}}\Phi_{j}
. We use the Leibniz rule to
write

(6.15)

I_{k+1}^{t^{\prime\prime}}\partial_{{k+1}}\Big(\partial^{\pi_{0}}\varphi\prod_{j=1}^{k}\partial^{\pi_{j}}\Phi_{j}\Big)=I_{k+1}^{t^{\prime\prime}}\partial_{{k+1}}\partial^{\pi_{0}}\varphi\prod_{j=1}^{k}\partial^{\pi_{j}}\Phi_{j}+I_{k+1}^{t^{\prime\prime}}\partial^{\pi_{0}}\varphi\,\partial_{x_{k+1}}\Big(\prod_{j=1}^{k}\partial^{\pi_{j}}\Phi_{j}\Big).

Then, we observe that those two terms can be expressed in the announced form, letting

\Phi_{k+1}=I_{k+1}^{t^{\prime\prime}}
,
\pi^{\prime}_{k+1}=\emptyset
, and

•

for the first term,
\pi^{\prime}_{0}=\pi_{0}\cup\{k+1\}
and
\pi^{\prime}_{j}=\pi_{j}
for
1\leq j\leq k
;

•

for the second term, we take
\pi^{\prime}_{0}=\pi_{0}
and apply the Leibniz rule to the product

\prod_{j}\partial^{\pi_{j}}\Phi_{j}
, to write it as a sum of different contributions; then, for

1\leq j\leq k
, we take one of the
\pi^{\prime}_{j}
to be equal to
\pi_{j}\cup\{k+1\}
(corresponding to
the index which is hit by the Leibniz rule) and leave all the others are untouched
(
\pi^{\prime}_{j}=\pi_{j}
).

∎

6.3.
The classes of functions
E^{(a)}
and
\mathcal{E}^{(a)}

Let us introduce classes of functions which will be helpful to quantify the distance between a

(h,\varphi)
pseudo-convolution and the usual convolution.

6.3.1.
Definition

For now, and the rest of the paper, we fix
a>0
.

Definition 6.20
.

We let
E_{n}^{(a)}
be the vector space of functions
h:[a,+\infty)^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

such that:

•

h
is of class
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
;

•

for every multi-index
\alpha\in\{0,1\}^{n}
such that
\alpha\not=(0,\ldots,0)
,

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in[a,+\infty)^{n}}\Big\{e^{\alpha\cdot x}|\partial^{\alpha}h(x)|\Big\}<+\infty.

This space is endowed with the seminorms

\lVert h\rVert_{\alpha,a}:=\sup\{e^{\alpha\cdot x}|\partial^{\alpha}h(x)|,x\in[a,+\infty)^{n}\}
.

Notice that, in this definition, we only control partial derivatives of
h
that are of
order at most one in each variable.

Notation 6.21
.

Define
L_{n}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})=x_{1}+\ldots+x_{n}
to be the linear functional associated to the usual
convolution.

In what follows, we will prove results for pseudo-convolutions assuming that
h
is close to
L_{n}

in a certain sense; the definition below is used to quantify this.

Definition 6.22
.

We let
\mathcal{E}_{n}^{(a)}
be the affine space of functions
h
such that
h-L_{n}\in E_{n}^{(a)}
.
Equivalently,
h
belongs in
\mathcal{E}_{n}^{(a)}
if and only if

•

h
is of class
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
;

•

for every multi-index
\alpha\in\{0,1\}^{n}
such that
|\alpha|\geq 2
,

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in[a,+\infty)^{n}}\Big\{e^{\alpha\cdot x}|\partial^{\alpha}h(x)|\Big\}<+\infty

•

for every
j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
,

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in[a,+\infty)^{n}}\Big\{e^{x_{j}}|\partial_{j}h(x)-1|\Big\}<+\infty.

6.3.2.
Core examples

Our main examples of such functions will be the following.

Notation 6.23
.

For
\beta=(\beta_{\epsilon})_{\epsilon\in\{\pm\}^{n}}
a family of real numbers, we define

h_{\beta}(x)=2\log\Big(\sum_{\epsilon\in\{\pm\}^{n}}\beta_{\epsilon}\hyp_{\epsilon}\left(\frac{x}{2}\right)\Big).

Proposition 6.24
.

We assume that
\beta
is not identically
0
and that, for all
\epsilon
,
\beta_{\epsilon}\geq 0
.
Then,
h_{\beta}\in\mathcal{E}_{n}^{(a)}
for any
a>0
.
Moreover, there exists
C=C(a,n)
, independent of
\beta
, such that

(6.16)

\displaystyle\sup_{\alpha\not=(0,\ldots,0)}\lVert h_{\beta}-L_{n}\rVert_{\alpha,a}\leq C.

Corollary 6.25
.

The same result holds for the family

(6.17)

\tilde{h}_{\beta}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})=2\argcosh\Big(\sum_{\epsilon\in\{\pm\}^{n}}\beta_{\epsilon}\hyp_{\epsilon}\left(\frac{x}{2}\right)\Big)

if we impose the additional condition that
\beta_{(1,\ldots,1)}\geq 1
.

Proof of Proposition
6.24
.

For
y\in\mathbb{R}
, introduce

g_{\pm}(y)=e^{-y/2}\hyp_{\pm}(y)=\frac{1\pm e^{-y}}{2}.

Let
g_{\epsilon}(x)=\prod_{j=1}^{n}g_{\epsilon_{j}}(x_{j})
. We have

\displaystyle h_{\beta}(x)=L_{n}(x)+2\log\Big(\sum_{\epsilon\in\{\pm\}^{n}}\beta_{\epsilon}g_{\epsilon}(x)\Big).

Note that

|\partial g_{+}(y)|=\frac{e^{-y}}{2}\leq e^{-y}g_{+}(y)

and, if
y\geq a>0
,

|\partial g_{-}(y)|=\frac{e^{-y}}{2}\leq\frac{1}{1-e^{-a}}e^{-y}g_{-}(y).

It follows that the
constant
C=(1-e^{-a})^{-n}
satisfies, for all
\alpha\in\{0,1\}^{n}
and
x\in[a,+\infty)^{n}
,

(6.18)

\displaystyle|\partial^{\alpha}g_{\epsilon}(x)|\leq C\,e^{-\alpha\cdot x}g_{\epsilon}(x).

For
\alpha\in\{0,1\}^{n}
with
|\alpha|=k\geq 1
, the partial derivative

\partial^{\alpha}\log\Big(\sum_{\epsilon\in\{\pm\}^{n}}\beta_{\epsilon}g_{\epsilon}(x)\Big)
is a linear combination of terms of the form

(6.19)

\displaystyle\frac{\prod_{j=1}^{k}\Big(\sum_{\epsilon\in\{\pm\}^{n}}\beta_{\epsilon}\partial^{\pi_{j}}g_{\epsilon}(x)\Big)}{\Big(\sum_{\epsilon\in\{\pm\}^{n}}\beta_{\epsilon}g_{\epsilon}(x)\Big)^{k}}

where
\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{k}
form a partition of
\{i,\alpha_{i}=1\}
.
For each
j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
, (
6.18
) implies

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{\epsilon\in\{\pm\}^{n}}\beta_{\epsilon}|\partial^{\pi_{j}}g_{\epsilon}(x)|}{\sum_{\epsilon\in\{\pm\}^{n}}\beta_{\epsilon}g_{\epsilon}(x)}\leq C\exp\Big(-\sum_{i\in\pi_{j}}\alpha_{i}x_{i}\Big).

As a result, the term (
6.19
) is bounded by
C^{k}\exp(-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}x_{i})
,
proving the proposition.
∎

Let us now prove the corollary.

Proof of Corollary
6.25
.

We write
\tilde{h}_{\beta}=V(h_{\beta})
where

V(x)=2\argcosh(e^{\frac{x}{2}})=x+2\log 2+\omega(e^{-x})

is the reciprocal of the function
U
that appeared in (
4.22
). By
\omega(e^{-x})
we
mean a function whose derivatives are all
\mathcal{O}(e^{-x})
. It is straightforward to check that

\tilde{h}
satisfies the same derivative estimates as
h
, provided we know beforehand that

\sum_{\epsilon\in\{\pm\}^{n}}\beta_{\epsilon}\hyp_{\epsilon}\left(\frac{x}{2}\right)
stays bounded away from
1
on

[a,+\infty)^{n}
(to avoid the singularity of
\argcosh
at
1
). The condition

\beta_{(1,\ldots,1)}\geq 1
implies that

\sum_{\epsilon\in\{\pm\}^{n}}\beta_{\epsilon}\hyp_{\epsilon}\left(\frac{x}{2}\right)\geq\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\right)\geq\exp\Big(\frac{na}{2}\Big)>1

which is enough to conclude.
∎

6.3.3.
Useful properties

First, we make the following immediate observation.

Remark 6.26
.

If
\pi=(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{N})
is a partition of
\{1,\ldots,n\}
, and if for all
j\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
, the functions

(x_{i})_{i\in\pi_{j}}\mapsto h_{j}((x_{i})_{i\in\pi_{j}})
are in
\mathcal{E}_{|\pi_{j}|}^{(a)}
, then following
function is an element of
\mathcal{E}_{n}^{(a)}
:

(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\mapsto\sum_{j=1}^{N}h_{j}((x_{i})_{i\in\pi_{j}}).

It may also be useful to note the following simple implication of the definition of
\mathcal{E}_{n}^{(a)}
:

Proposition 6.27
.

Assume that (
6.16
) holds for all
\alpha\in\{0,1\}^{n}

such that
|\alpha|=1
. Then there exists
l_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
such that, for all

x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\in[a,+\infty)^{n}
,

|h(x)-L_{n}(x)-l_{0}|\leq C\sum_{i=1}^{n}e^{-x_{i}}.

Proof.

First, we observe that if a function
g:[a,+\infty)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
satisfies

|g(t)-g(s)|\leq Ce^{-t}
for all
t<s
, by Cauchy’s rule, it admits a limit
g(+\infty)
at
infinity, which satisfies

|g(t)-g(+\infty)|\leq Ce^{-t}.

This property holds for instance if
|g^{\prime}(t)|\leq Ce^{-t}
, and is stable under taking limits of
sequences of functions. Applied to
g=h-L_{n}
successively in each variable, denoting

l_{0}=g(+\infty,\ldots,+\infty)
, this yields

\displaystyle|g(x)-l_{0}|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}|g(x_{1},\ldots,x_{i-1},x_{i},+\infty,\ldots,+\infty)-g(x_{1},\ldots,x_{i-1},+\infty,+\infty,\ldots,+\infty)|

which implies that
|g(x)-l_{0}|\leq C\sum_{i=1}^{n}e^{-x_{i}}
.
∎

6.4.
Stability of the Friedman–Ramanujan hypothesis by pseudo-convolution

We now have the ingredients to state a general result, according to which
f_{1}\star\ldots\star f_{n}|^{h}_{\varphi}
is a Friedman–Ramanujan function if
f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}
are so, under certain assumptions on
h
on

\varphi
. In particular, we will prove Theorem
6.28
– which is not yet the result used to prove Theorem
1.23
, but may be considered as a preliminary version, and provides a nice abstract statement:

Theorem 6.28
.

Let
a>0
. We assume that
h\in\mathcal{E}_{n}^{(a)}
and that

\varphi\in E_{n}^{(a)}
with
\operatorname{supp}\varphi\subset(a,+\infty)^{n}
. If
(f_{j})_{1\leq j\leq n}

are continuous functions in
\mathcal{F}
, then
f_{1}\star\ldots\star f_{n}|^{h}_{\varphi}\in\mathcal{F}
.

Recall that we always assume that the conditions of Notation
6.2
hold.

6.4.1.
Quantitative hypotheses on the functions
h
,
\varphi
and
f_{j}

As we prove Theorem
6.28
, we will be able to give estimates on the
Friedman–Ramanujan norms of
f_{1}\star\ldots\star f_{n}|^{h}_{\varphi}
as a function of those of

f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}
(the precise bound is given in (
6.27
)). To this end, we introduce
more specific notation which will be useful to track everything in the proof.

Notation 6.29
.

Let
n\geq 1
and
a>0
. We assume the following.

(
h
)

The function
h
lies in
\mathcal{E}_{n}^{(a)}
. We denote
C_{1},C_{2}\geq 1
two
constants such that

\displaystyle\forall\alpha\in\{0,1\}^{n}\setminus\{(0,\ldots,0)\},

\displaystyle\quad\lVert h-L_{n}\rVert_{\alpha,a}\leq C_{1}

\displaystyle\forall j\in\{1,\ldots,n\},

\displaystyle\quad\Big|\frac{\partial h}{\partial x_{j}}\Big|\leq C_{2}.

By the hypothesis from Notation
6.2
there exists a constant
C_{2}^{\prime}
such that

\forall j\in\{1,\ldots,n\},\quad 0<\Big|\frac{\partial x_{j}}{\partial h}\Big|\leq C_{2}^{\prime}.

We let
C_{0}=\max(C_{1},C_{2},C_{2}^{\prime})
.
By Proposition
6.27
, there exists a
l_{0}\geq 1
such that

(6.20)

\displaystyle\forall x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\in[a,+\infty)^{n},\quad\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\leq h(x)+l_{0}.

(
\varphi
)

The function
\varphi
is supported in
(a,+\infty)^{n}
and

\varphi\in E_{n}^{(a)}
. Let
C_{3}\,\geq 0
be a constant such that, for every multi-index

\alpha\in\{0,1\}^{n}
with
\alpha\not=(0,\ldots,0)
,
\lVert\varphi\rVert_{\alpha,a}\leq C_{3}
.

(
f
)

For
j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
,
f_{j}\in\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{K}_{j},\mathrm{N}_{j}}
is a continuous
function. We assume w.l.o.g. that
\mathrm{K}_{j}\leq\mathrm{N}_{j}
and
\lVert f_{j}\rVert_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{K}_{j},\mathrm{N}_{j}}}=1
. By
Remark
1.29
, for
x_{j}\geq 0
and
0\leq m<\mathrm{K}_{j}
,

\displaystyle|\mathcal{L}^{m}f_{j}(x_{j})|\leq M_{\mathrm{K}_{j},\mathrm{N}_{j}}(1+x_{j})^{\mathrm{K}_{j}-1}e^{x_{j}}

\displaystyle|\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{K}_{j}}f_{j}(x_{j})|\leq M_{\mathrm{K}_{j},\mathrm{N}_{j}}(1+x_{j})^{\mathrm{N}_{j}-1}e^{\frac{x_{j}}{2}}.

We let
\mathrm{K}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathrm{K}_{j}
,
\mathrm{N}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathrm{N}_{j}
and
M_{\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N}}^{\times}:=\prod_{j=1}^{n}M_{\mathrm{K}_{j},\mathrm{N}_{j}}
.

Remark 6.30
.

Comparison estimates
such as (
6.20
), comparing
\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}

with
h(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
, are crucial throughout the paper.

6.4.2.
Estimates on the functions
F_{j}^{\mathrm{K}_{j}}
,
G_{j}
,
H_{j}^{t}
and
I_{j}^{t}

We can deduce from our hypotheses some bounds on the functions introduced in Notation

6.12
, which appear when computing
\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{K}}_{\ell}(f_{1}\star\ldots f_{n}|_{\varphi}^{h})
. This
will allow us to foresee some key elements of the proof of Theorem
6.28
.

Lemma 6.31
.

Under the assumptions
(
h
)
and
(
f
)
from Notation
6.29
, for

j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, for any set
\pi_{j}\subset\{1,\ldots,n\}
such that
j\notin\pi_{j}

and any
x\in[a,+\infty)^{n}
,

•

if
\Phi_{j}=F^{\mathrm{K}_{j}}_{j},G_{j}
or
H_{j}^{t}
with
0\leq t<\mathrm{K}_{j}
,

(6.21)

|\partial^{\pi_{j}}\Phi_{j}(x)|\leq C_{0}^{2}M_{\mathrm{K}_{j},\mathrm{N}_{j}}(1+x_{j})^{\mathrm{N}_{j}}e^{\frac{x_{j}}{2}}\prod_{i\in\pi_{j}}e^{-x_{i}}

•

if
\Phi_{j}=I_{j}^{t}
with
0\leq t<\mathrm{K}_{j}
,

(6.22)

|\partial^{\pi_{j}}\Phi_{j}(x)|\leq C_{0}^{2}M_{\mathrm{K}_{j},\mathrm{N}_{j}}(1+x_{j})^{\mathrm{N}_{j}}e^{x_{j}}\prod_{i\in\pi_{j}}e^{-x_{i}}.

Proof.

If
\pi_{j}=\emptyset
, it is a straightforward computation that

\begin{cases}|F^{\mathrm{K}_{j}}_{j}(x)|\leq C_{2}M_{\mathrm{K}_{j},\mathrm{N}_{j}}(1+x_{j})^{\mathrm{N}_{j}-1}e^{\frac{x_{j}}{2}},\\
|G_{j}(x)|\leq C_{1}M_{\mathrm{K}_{j},\mathrm{N}_{j}}(1+x_{j})^{\mathrm{K}_{j}-1},\\
|H^{t}_{j}(x)|\leq C_{1}C_{2}M_{\mathrm{K}_{j},\mathrm{N}_{j}}(1+x_{j})^{\mathrm{K}_{j}}\end{cases}

and

\displaystyle|I^{t}_{j}(x)|\leq C_{2}M_{\mathrm{K}_{j},\mathrm{N}_{j}}(1+x_{j})^{\mathrm{K}_{j}-1}e^{x_{j}}.

We then use the fact that
\mathrm{K}_{j}\leq\mathrm{N}_{j}
and
C_{1},C_{2}\leq C_{0}
. When
\pi_{j}
is non-empty,
the additional exponential factors come from the hypothesis on the derivatives of
h
.
∎

We here see the crucial phenomenon at play for the proof of Theorem
6.28
: compared
to the original upper bound
|f_{j}(x_{j})|\leq M_{\mathrm{K}_{j},\mathrm{N}_{j}}(1+x_{j})^{\mathrm{K}_{j}-1}e^{x_{j}}
, we have
gained in (
6.21
) a factor
e^{-x_{j}/2}
when replacing the function
f_{j}
by the
function
F^{\mathrm{K}_{j}}_{j}
,
G_{j}
or
H^{t}_{j}
.
Unfortunately, the estimate we obtain on the function
I^{t}_{j}
does not constitute an improvement
compared to the initial growth-rate of
f_{j}
.

In this case however, an exponential factor
e^{-x_{j}/2}
will be gained elsewhere, from the
derivatives of the other functions. Remember that we have noticed in Remark
6.17
that the
set of “bad” indices
B
for which the function
I_{j}^{t}
appears corresponds to the set of
derivatives that appear elsewhere in the integral. We have seen in the lemma above that such
derivatives will create a decay reducing the growth of
I_{j}^{t}
.

We obtain the following bound on the quantity

\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{K}}(f_{1}\star\ldots\star f_{n}\|^{h}_{\varphi})
.

Proposition 6.32
.

Under the assumptions
(
h
)
and
(
f
)
from Notation
6.29
,

(6.23)

|\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{K}}(f_{1}\star\ldots\star f_{n}\|^{h}_{\varphi})(\ell)|\leq\sum_{\pi}\sum_{0\leq t+t^{\prime}\leq\mathrm{K}}|\mathcal{L}_{\ell}^{t}\mathcal{P}_{\ell}^{t^{\prime}}\mathbf{Int}_{\pi}(\ell)|

and

(6.24)

|\mathbf{Int}_{\pi}(\ell)|\leq C_{0}^{2n}M_{\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N}}^{\times}\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}h(x)=\ell\end{subarray}}|\partial^{\pi_{0}}\varphi(x)|\prod_{j\in B\setminus\pi_{0}}e^{-x_{j}}\prod_{j\in B}(1+x_{j})^{\mathrm{N}_{j}}e^{x_{j}}\prod_{j\not\in B}(1+x_{j})^{\mathrm{N}_{j}}e^{\frac{x_{j}}{2}}\frac{\,\mathrm{d}x}{\,\mathrm{d}\ell}.

Proof.

The bound (
6.23
) is a direct consequence of the expression (
6.12
)
together with the triangle inequality. Recall that

\displaystyle\mathbf{Int}_{\pi}(\ell)=\int_{h(x)=\ell}\partial^{\pi_{0}}\varphi(x)\prod_{j=1}^{n}\partial^{\pi_{j}}\Phi_{j}(x)\,\frac{\,\mathrm{d}x}{\,\mathrm{d}\ell}

where
\pi_{0},\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{n}
are disjoint subsets of
\{1,\ldots,n\}
such that

j\notin\pi_{j}
, and
B=\bigsqcup_{j=0}^{n}\pi_{j}
. We then use Lemma
6.31
on
each term, remembering that
\Phi_{j}
is in the first situation if
j\notin B
and the second if

j\in B
. We conclude by noticing that

\prod_{j=1}^{n}\prod_{i\in\pi_{j}}e^{-x_{i}}=\prod_{j\in B\setminus\pi_{0}}e^{-x_{j}}
.
∎

Remark 6.33
.

The previous statement is not completely optimal, but we have decided to
line up all the estimates on the worst possible case. For later use, it will also be useful to
lose again in optimality in (
6.24
), and write that:

(6.25)

\begin{split}|\mathbf{Int}_{\pi}(\ell)|\leq C_{0}^{2n}M^{\times}_{\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N}}\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}h(x)=\ell\end{subarray}}|\partial^{\pi_{0}}\varphi(x)|\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\notin\pi_{0}\end{subarray}}e^{-\frac{x_{j}}{2}}\prod_{j=1}^{n}(1+x_{j})^{\mathrm{N}_{j}}e^{x_{j}}\frac{\,\mathrm{d}x}{\,\mathrm{d}\ell}.\end{split}

If we compare with (
6.24
), we note that we have accepted to waste a factor

e^{-\frac{x_{j}}{2}}
for
j\in B\setminus\pi_{0}
. The resulting upper bound only depends on
\pi_{0}

and not on
B
(both coincide if
\pi_{0}=B
).

6.4.3.
End of the proof of Theorem
6.28

We are now ready to finish the proof of Theorem
6.28
by using Assumption

(
\varphi
)
to bound (
6.24
).

Proof.

Given that we now assume that

|\partial^{\pi_{0}}\varphi(x)|\leq C_{3}e^{-\sum_{j\in\pi_{0}}x_{j}}
, we obtain that for any
term
\pi
in (
6.23
),

(6.26)

|\mathbf{Int}_{\pi}(\ell)|\leq C_{3}\,C_{0}^{2n}M^{\times}_{\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N}}\int_{h(x)=\ell}\prod_{j=1}^{n}(1+x_{j})^{\mathrm{N}_{j}}e^{\frac{x_{j}}{2}}\frac{\,\mathrm{d}x}{\,\mathrm{d}\ell}.

We now explicit the level-set integration and use the comparison estimate
(
6.20
). We obtain that

|\mathbf{Int}_{\pi}(\ell)|\leq C_{3}\,C_{0}^{2n}M^{\times}_{\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N}}\int_{\sum_{j=2}^{n}x_{j}\leq\ell+l_{0}}\prod_{j=1}^{n}(1+x_{j})^{\mathrm{N}_{j}}e^{\frac{x_{j}}{2}}\Big|\frac{\partial x_{1}}{\partial h}\Big|\prod_{j=2}^{n}\,\mathrm{d}x_{j}

which, by assumption on the inverse derivatives of
h
, implies

|\mathbf{Int}_{\pi}(\ell)|\leq C_{3}\,C_{0}^{2n}C_{2}^{\prime}M^{\times}_{\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N}}(1+\ell+l_{0})^{\mathrm{N}}e^{\frac{\ell+l_{0}}{2}}\int_{\sum_{j=2}^{n}x_{j}\leq\ell+l_{0}}\prod_{j=2}^{n}\,\mathrm{d}x_{j}.

As a consequence,

|\mathbf{Int}_{\pi}(\ell)|\leq C_{3}\,C_{0}^{2n+1}M^{\times}_{\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N}}(1+\ell+l_{0})^{\mathrm{N}+n-1}e^{\frac{\ell+l_{0}}{2}}.

We have thus proven that the functions
\mathbf{Int}_{\pi}
are in the class
\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{N}+n}
. From
Proposition
6.32
,

\mathcal{L}_{\ell}^{\mathrm{K}}(f_{1}\star\ldots\star f_{n}|^{h}_{\varphi})
is a sum of such functions
(or images thereof under powers of
\mathcal{P}_{\ell}
or
\mathcal{L}_{\ell}
), hence

\mathcal{L}_{\ell}^{\mathrm{K}}(f_{1}\star\ldots\star f_{n}|^{h}_{\varphi})\in\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{N}+n}
. Proposition

1.27
then implies that
f_{1}\star\ldots\star f_{n}|^{h}_{\varphi}
belongs in

\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N}+n}
. The proof furthermore gives

(6.27)

\displaystyle\lVert f_{1}\star\ldots\star f_{n}|^{h}_{\varphi}\rVert_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N}+n}}\leq C_{3}\,C_{0}^{2n+1}\mathrm{fn}(n,\mathrm{K})(1+l_{0})^{\mathrm{N}+n-1}e^{\frac{l_{0}}{2}}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\lVert f_{j}\rVert_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{K}_{j},\mathrm{N}_{j}}}.

∎

6.5.
Problems in the geometric application of Assumption
(
h
)
and

(
\varphi
)

Having noticed at the end of §
5
that the integral (
5.13
) is the

(h,\varphi_{0})
-convolution of Friedman–Ramanujan functions (with
\varphi_{0}
and
h
specified
in (
5.15
) and (
5.16
) respectively), we now need to examine the possibility to
apply Theorem
6.28
. Unfortunately, the functions
h
and
\varphi_{0}
do not satisfy
the required assumptions, which explains why we will need two additional sections §
7
and §
8
to solve these issues.

6.5.1.
Problem with Assumption
(
h
)

The explicit expression of

\ell_{Y}({\mathbf{c}})
was obtained in (
4.12
). By virtue of Corollary
6.25

and Remark
6.26
, for each parameter
\vec{L}
, we have that the function

h:\vec{\theta}\mapsto\ell_{Y}({\mathbf{c}})

lies in the set
\mathcal{E}^{(a)}_{2r}

provided we restrict all the
\theta_{q}
to be
\geq a
. If
we now fix the neutral parameters
\vec{\theta}_{\mathrm{nt}}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\Theta_{\mathrm{nt}}}
and consider

(6.28)

\displaystyle h:\vec{\theta}_{\mathrm{ac}}\mapsto\ell_{Y}({\mathbf{c}}),

then it is easy to check that the restriction of this function to
\mathbb{R}_{\geq a}^{\Theta_{\mathrm{ac}}}
belongs
to
\mathcal{E}^{(a)}_{\#\Theta_{\mathrm{ac}}}
.

We have already remarked that the non-crossing variables
\theta_{q}
stay positive, as consequence of
the Useful Remark
2.11
. As a consequence, in the purely non-crossing case (when all
variables are non-crossing), Assumption
(
h
)
is satisfied.

If there exists some crossing variables, then they may take positive or negative values, in which
case we are out of the domain of application of Corollary
6.25
.

\rightsquigarrow
Section
7
will be devoted to solving this issue by choosing a
different representative of the homotopy class of
{\mathbf{c}}
. This will provide an
expression of
h
which falls in a class
\mathcal{E}
with respect to newly defined variables. The reader
might wish to focus upon first read on the purely non-crossing case, and hence skip §
7.2
and §
7.3
.

6.5.2.
Problem with Assumption
(
\varphi
)

The coefficients arising in the
expression of
Q_{m(\lambda)}
, obtained in (
4.19
) and (
4.21
), are equal to

\pm 1
, thus not of constant sign. As a consequence, we cannot use Proposition
6.24

nor Corollary
6.25
to deduce that
Q_{m(\lambda)}
belongs to the class

\mathcal{E}_{m(\lambda)}
with respect to the variables

(\tau^{\lambda}_{1},\ldots,\tau^{\lambda}_{m(\lambda)})
. We cannot either deduce that the functions

\log F_{m(\lambda)}
are in the class
E_{m(\lambda)}
.

\rightsquigarrow
Section §
8
will be devoted to estimating the derivatives of
F_{m(\lambda)}
.

Again this will necessitate changing the representative of the homotopy class of
\Gamma_{\lambda}
.

6.6.
Variants of Theorem
6.28

In the geometric applications of these techniques, we shall not use Theorem
6.28

directly, but several variants. Because these variants become quite technical, we present them by
gradually modifying the assumptions and conclusions. The reader is invited to skip this section at
first read, and come back to it when the necessity for these variants becomes clear.

6.6.1.
First variant: comparison estimates

First, we loosen the decay hypothesis on
\varphi
, and replace it by a new assumption.

Notation 6.34

(Assumption
(
\varphi
-CE)
)
.

Assume that the smooth function
\varphi
is supported on
(a,+\infty)^{n}
and there exists
positive functions
Z_{j}:\mathbb{R}_{>0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{>0}
,
1\leq j\leq n
, such that:

•

for all
\pi_{0}\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}
, all
x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\in(a,+\infty)^{n}
,

|\partial^{\pi_{0}}\varphi(x)|\leq C_{3}\,\prod_{j\in\pi_{0}}\frac{1}{Z_{j}^{1/2}(x_{j})};

•

there exists
l_{0}>0
satisfying (
6.20
) and such that, for any

\pi_{0}\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}
,

(6.29)

\displaystyle 2\sum_{j=1}^{n}x_{j}\leq h(x)+\sum_{j\not\in\pi_{0}}x_{j}+\sum_{j\in\pi_{0}}\log Z_{j}+l_{0}.

The important point, here, is that the functions
(Z_{j})_{1\leq j\leq n}
controlling the decay
of the derivatives of
\varphi
