can be incorporated in a comparison estimate for the function
h
.

Remark 6.35
.

The initial result, Theorem
6.28
, may be recovered by taking

Z_{j}=e^{x_{j}}
, in which case the comparison estimate (
6.29
) coincides with (
6.20
).

Theorem 6.36
.

Let
a>0
. We assume that the assumptions
(
h
)
,

(
f
)
and
(
\varphi
-CE)
hold. Then,

f_{1}\star\ldots\star f_{n}|^{h}_{\varphi}\in\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N}+n}
, and the estimate
(
6.27
) holds.

Proof.

We do not reproduce the proof, completely identical to the proof of Theorem
6.28
.
A crucial point is again the comparison estimate (
6.29
), which may be
rewritten on the level-set
\{h(x)=\ell\}
in exponential form

\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{n}e^{x_{j}}\prod_{j\in\pi_{0}}\frac{1}{Z_{j}^{1/2}}\prod_{j\not\in\pi_{0}}e^{-\frac{x_{j}}{2}}\leq e^{\frac{l_{0}}{2}}e^{\frac{\ell}{2}}.

This means that the
gain
\prod_{j\in\pi_{0}}Z_{j}^{-1/2}\prod_{j\not\in\pi_{0}}e^{-x_{j}/2}
on the left-hand side
results in a growth of size
e^{\ell/2}
instead of the expected
e^{\ell}
on the right-hand side,
leading to the fact that the resulting function is a Friedman–Ramanujan remainder.
∎

6.6.2.
Second variant: Linear forms

In our geometric applications,

\varphi(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
will actually be split into a product involving linear forms, as
follows:

Notation 6.37
.

Let
\varphi
be of the form:

(6.30)

\displaystyle\varphi(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})=\phi(\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{m})\,\psi(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})

where, for all
1\leq i\leq m
,
\tau_{i}
is a linear form, and more precisely

\tau_{i}(x)=\sum_{j\in\Theta(i)}x_{j}
for a subset
\Theta(i)\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}
. For

\tilde{\pi}\subseteq\{1,\ldots,m\}
, we denote

\Theta(\tilde{\pi}):=\bigcup_{i\in\tilde{\pi}}\Theta(i)
.

Remark 6.38
.

The original result corresponds to the case where
m=n
,
\Theta(i)=\{i\}
so that
\tau_{i}(x)=x_{i}
,
and
\psi=1
.

Let us adapt Theorem
6.16
to this new form of the density
\varphi
. The motivation
for this upcoming variant may be roughly explained as follows: suppose that the derivatives

\partial^{\tilde{\pi}}\phi(\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{m})
are bounded by

(6.31)

\displaystyle|\partial^{\tilde{\pi}}\phi(\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{m})|\leq C_{3}\,e^{-\sum_{i\in\tilde{\pi}}\tau_{i}}=C_{3}\,e^{-\sum_{i\in\tilde{\pi}}\sum_{j\in\Theta(i)}x_{j}}

for any
\tilde{\pi}\subseteq\{1,\ldots,m\}
.
Then, once the term
\partial^{\tilde{\pi}}\phi
appears in the application of
Corollary
6.13
, there is no further need to gain exponential decay with respect to the variables
x_{j}
with
j\in\Theta(\tilde{\pi})
.
We wish to avoid this extra work, not only to optimize the proof, but also because in the geometric application the higher order derivatives of
\phi
will be difficult to calculate. Thus, we copy the proof of Theorem
6.16
, but once

\phi
has been differentiated with respect to a variable
x_{j}
(say, for
j\in\Theta(i)
), we
stop applying our operators to the other variables
x_{j^{\prime}}
with
j^{\prime}\in\Theta(i)
.
This leads to the following statement.

Theorem 6.39
.

For any family of continuous functions
(f_{j})_{1\leq j\leq n}
and any integers

(\mathrm{K}_{j})_{1\leq j\leq n}
with
\mathrm{K}_{j}\geq 1
, if
\mathrm{K}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathrm{K}_{j}
, then the function

\mathcal{L}_{\ell}^{\mathrm{K}}(f_{1}\star\ldots\star f_{n}|^{h}_{\varphi})
can be expressed as the sum of the
following functions, or their images under
\mathcal{L}_{\ell}^{t}\mathcal{P}_{\ell}^{t^{\prime}}
for
0\leq t\leq\mathrm{K}-n

and
0\leq t^{\prime}\leq n
,

(6.32)

\displaystyle\mathbf{Int}_{\tilde{\pi}_{0},\tilde{\pi},\mathcal{V},\pi}(\ell):=\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}h(x)=\ell\end{subarray}}\partial^{\tilde{\pi}}\phi(\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{m})\,\partial^{\pi_{0}}\psi(x)\prod_{j\in\mathcal{V}}\partial^{\pi_{j}}\Phi_{j}(x)\prod_{j\notin\mathcal{V}}f_{j}(x_{j})\frac{\,\mathrm{d}x}{\,\mathrm{d}\ell}

where:

•

\tilde{\pi}_{0}\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}
and
\tilde{\pi}\subseteq\{1,\ldots,m\}
are two
sets of same cardinal, with a bijection
\tilde{\pi}_{0}\ni j\mapsto i_{j}\in\tilde{\pi}
such that

j\in\Theta(i_{j})
for all
j\in\tilde{\pi}_{0}
;

•

\mathcal{V}
is a subset of
\{1,\ldots,n\}
containing
\tilde{\pi}_{0}
, and so that

\mathcal{V}\cup\Theta(\tilde{\pi})=\{1,\ldots,n\}
;

•

(\pi_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{V}\cup\{0\}}
is a family of disjoint subsets of

\mathcal{V}\setminus\tilde{\pi}_{0}
such that
j\notin\pi_{j}
for all
j
;

•

if
B:=\tilde{\pi}_{0}\sqcup\pi_{0}\sqcup\bigsqcup_{j\in\mathcal{V}}\pi_{j}
, then for any
j\in\mathcal{V}
,
\Phi_{j}
satisfies (
6.13
).

Remark 6.40
.

To go from Theorem
6.39
to Theorem
6.16
, let

\tilde{\pi}_{0}=\tilde{\pi}=\emptyset
,
\mathcal{V}=\{1,\ldots,n\}
.

We prove, once again, this result by proving the following (stronger) lemma for by induction on the
integer
0\leq k\leq n
. This leads to the claim taking
k=n
.

Lemma 6.41
.

For any
0\leq k\leq n
, the function

\mathcal{L}_{\ell}^{\mathrm{K}}(f_{1}\star\ldots\star f_{n}|^{h}_{\varphi})
can be expressed as the sum of the
following functions, or their images under
\mathcal{L}_{\ell}^{t}\mathcal{P}_{\ell}^{t^{\prime}}
for
0\leq t\leq\mathrm{K}-k

and
0\leq t^{\prime}\leq k
,

(6.33)

\displaystyle\mathbf{Int}_{\tilde{\pi}_{0},\tilde{\pi},\mathcal{V},\pi}^{k}(\ell):=\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}h(x)=\ell\end{subarray}}\partial^{\tilde{\pi}}\phi(\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{m})\,\partial^{\pi_{0}}\psi(x)\prod_{j\in\mathcal{V}}\partial^{\pi_{j}}\Phi_{j}(x)\prod_{j\notin\mathcal{V}}f_{j}(x_{j})\frac{\,\mathrm{d}x}{\,\mathrm{d}\ell}

where:

•

\tilde{\pi}_{0}\subseteq\{1,\ldots,k\}
and
\tilde{\pi}\subseteq\{1,\ldots,m\}
are two
sets of same cardinal, with a bijection
\tilde{\pi}_{0}\ni j\mapsto i_{j}\in\tilde{\pi}
such that

j\in\Theta(i_{j})
for all
j\in\tilde{\pi}_{0}
;

•

\mathcal{V}
is a subset of
\{1,\ldots,k\}
containing
\tilde{\pi}_{0}
, and so that

\{1,\ldots,k\}\subseteq\mathcal{V}\cup\Theta(\tilde{\pi})
;

•

(\pi_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{V}\cup\{0\}}
is a family of disjoint subsets of

\mathcal{V}\setminus\tilde{\pi}_{0}
such that
\pi_{j}
contains only integers
\geq j+1
for all
j
;

•

if
B:=\tilde{\pi}_{0}\sqcup\pi_{0}\sqcup\bigsqcup_{j\in\mathcal{V}}\pi_{j}
, then for any
j\in\mathcal{V}
,
\Phi_{j}
satisfies (
6.13
).

Proof.

For
k=0
, we take
\tilde{\pi}=\tilde{\pi}_{0}=\pi_{0}=\mathcal{V}=\emptyset
. Now assume the formula known at
rank
k
and let us prove it for
k+1
, by applying
\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{K}_{k+1}}
to an expression of the
form (
6.33
).

First, let us observe that, if
k+1\in\Theta(\tilde{\pi})
, then we can simply apply

\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{K}_{k+1}}
to the integral (
6.32
) and leave it unchanged; the result will
satisfy the requirements of the theorem at the rank
k+1
, with the same
\tilde{\pi}_{0}
,

\tilde{\pi}
,
\mathcal{V}
,
(\pi_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{V}}
.

Let us now assume that
k+1\notin\Theta(\tilde{\pi})
. As before, we apply Corollary

6.13
to (
6.33
) with respect to the variable
x_{{k+1}}
. For each term,
we explicit how the data
(\tilde{\pi}_{0},\tilde{\pi},\mathcal{V},(\pi_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{V}})
evolves to a new
set of data
(\tilde{\pi}_{0}^{\prime},\tilde{\pi}^{\prime},\mathcal{V}^{\prime},(\pi_{j}^{\prime})_{j\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}})
which satifies the
conditions at the next rank. Note that, here, we will always take
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}=\mathcal{V}\cup\{k+1\}
and

\pi_{k+1}^{\prime}=\emptyset
.

In the three first cases,
T_{k+1}
is a multiplication operator by some function

\Phi_{k+1}
equal to
F^{\mathrm{K}_{{k+1}}}_{{k+1}}
,
G_{{k+1}}
or
H_{{k+1}}^{t}
. We take
\tilde{\pi}_{0}^{\prime}=\tilde{\pi}_{0}
,
\tilde{\pi}^{\prime}=\tilde{\pi}
, and
\pi_{j}^{\prime}=\pi_{j}
for

j\in\mathcal{V}
.

Otherwise,
T_{k+1}=I_{{k+1}}^{t}\partial_{{k+1}}
. In this case, we let

\Phi_{{{k+1}}}=I_{{k+1}}^{t}
. When we apply
\partial_{k+1}
to an expression of the form

\partial^{\tilde{\pi}}\phi(\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{m})\,\partial^{\pi_{0}}\psi(x)\prod_{j\in\mathcal{V}}\partial^{\pi_{j}}\Phi_{j}(x)
and then multiply it by

I_{k+1}^{t}=\partial^{\pi_{k+1}^{\prime}}\Phi_{k+1}
, by the Leibniz rule we obtain three types of
terms.

•

Terms of the form

(6.34)

\displaystyle\partial^{\tilde{\pi}}\partial_{{i}}\phi(\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{m})\,\partial^{\pi_{0}}\psi(x)\prod_{j\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}\partial^{\pi_{j}}\Phi_{j}(x)

for
i\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
with
k+1\in\Theta(i)
. We note that by hypothesis

i\notin\tilde{\pi}
. We take
\tilde{\pi}_{0}^{\prime}=\tilde{\pi}_{0}\cup\{k+1\}
,

\tilde{\pi}^{\prime}=\tilde{\pi}\cup\{i\}
, and leave the
(\pi_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{V}}
unchanged.

•

A term of the form

(6.35)

\displaystyle\partial^{\tilde{\pi}}\phi(\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{m})\;\partial^{\pi_{0}}\partial_{{k+1}}\psi(x)\prod_{j\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}\partial^{\pi_{j}}\Phi_{j}(x)

for which we set
\tilde{\pi}_{0}^{\prime}=\tilde{\pi}_{0}
,
\tilde{\pi}^{\prime}=\tilde{\pi}
,
\pi_{0}^{\prime}=\pi_{0}\cup\{k+1\}
, and leave the
(\pi_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{V}}
unchanged.

•

Finally, a term of the form

(6.36)

\displaystyle\partial^{\tilde{\pi}}\phi(\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{m})\;\partial^{\pi_{0}}\psi(x)\;\partial^{{k+1}}\Big(\prod_{j\in\mathcal{V}}\partial^{\pi_{j}}\Phi_{j}\Big)(x)\Phi_{k+1}(x)

which is itself a sum of terms coming from re-applying the Leibniz rule. For each of these
terms, we leave
\tilde{\pi}_{0}
,
\tilde{\pi}
and
\pi_{0}
unchanged, add
k+1
to exactly one of
the
\pi_{j}
with
j\in\mathcal{V}
(corresponding to the index which is hit by the Leibniz rule),
leaving the others unchanged.

∎

We deduce from Theorem
6.39
the following proposition replacing Proposition

6.32
:

Lemma 6.42
.

Under the assumptions
(
h
)
and
(
f
)
,

\displaystyle|\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{K}}(f_{1}\star\ldots\star f_{n}|^{h}_{\varphi})(\ell)|\leq\sum_{\tilde{\pi}_{0},\tilde{\pi},\mathcal{V},\pi}\sum_{0\leq t+t^{\prime}\leq\mathrm{K}+n}|\mathcal{L}^{t}\mathcal{P}^{t^{\prime}}\mathbf{Int}_{\tilde{\pi}_{0},\tilde{\pi},\mathcal{V},\pi}(\ell)|

and
|\mathbf{Int}_{\tilde{\pi}_{0},\tilde{\pi},\mathcal{V},\pi}(\ell)|
can be bounded above by

\displaystyle C_{0}^{2n}M^{\times}_{\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N}}\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}h(x)=\ell\end{subarray}}|\partial^{\tilde{\pi}}\phi(\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{m})||\partial^{\pi_{0}}\psi(x)|\prod_{j\in\mathcal{V}\setminus(\tilde{\pi}_{0}\cup\pi_{0})}e^{-\frac{x_{j}}{2}}\prod_{j=1}^{n}(1+x_{j})^{\mathrm{N}_{j}}e^{x_{j}}\frac{\,\mathrm{d}x}{\,\mathrm{d}\ell}.

The following proposition inputs an assumption about
\varphi
to find upper bounds on the terms
appearing in Lemma
6.42
.

Notation 6.43

(Assumption
(
\varphi
-CE-form)
)
.

Assume that
\varphi(x)=\phi(\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{m})\psi(x)
where:

•

\psi
satisfies Assumption
(
\varphi
)
, namely it is supported in
(a,+\infty)^{n}
and there exists a constant
C_{3}
such that, for any
\pi\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}
,

\sup_{x\in(a,+\infty)^{n}}e^{\sum_{j\in\pi}x_{j}}|\partial^{\pi}\psi|\leq C_{3}.

•

\phi
satisfies Assumption
(
\varphi
-CE)
w.r.t. its variables

(\tau_{i})_{1\leq i\leq m}
, i.e. there exists positive functions

Z_{i}:\mathbb{R}_{>0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{>0}
,
1\leq i\leq m
, such that:

–

for all
\tilde{\pi}\subseteq\{1,\ldots,m\}
,

(6.37)

\displaystyle|\partial^{\tilde{\pi}}\phi(\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{m})|\leq C_{4}\,\prod_{i\in\tilde{\pi}}\frac{1}{Z_{i}^{1/2}(\tau_{i})};

–

there exists a function
\mathcal{H}(x)
such that for all
\tilde{\pi}\subseteq\{1,\ldots,m\}
,
we have the comparison estimate

(6.38)

\displaystyle 2\mathcal{H}(x)\leq h(x)+\sum_{j\not\in\Theta(\tilde{\pi})}x_{j}+\sum_{i\in\tilde{\pi}}\log Z_{i}(\tau_{i}).

Remark 6.44
.

On the level-set
\{h(x)=\ell\}
, the comparison estimate can be rewritten under the
multiplicative form

(6.39)

\displaystyle\prod_{j\not\in\Theta(\tilde{\pi})}e^{-\frac{x_{j}}{2}}\prod_{i\in\tilde{\pi}}\frac{1}{Z_{i}^{1/2}(\tau_{i})}\leq e^{\frac{\ell}{2}}e^{-\mathcal{H}(x)}.

Proposition 6.45
.

Assume that
(
f
)
,
(
h
)
from Notation

6.29
and Assumption

(
\varphi
-CE-form)
are satisfied. Then a term of the form (
6.32
) may be bounded by

\displaystyle C_{0}^{2n}C_{3}C_{4}\,M^{\times}_{\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N}}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\|f_{j}\|_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{K}^{j},\mathrm{N}^{j}}}(1+\ell+l_{0})^{\mathrm{N}}e^{\frac{\ell}{2}}

\displaystyle\int_{h(x)=\ell}e^{-\mathcal{H}(x)}e^{\sum_{j=1}^{n}x_{j}}\frac{\,\mathrm{d}x}{\,\mathrm{d}\ell}.

As a consequence, in order to prove the Friedman-Ramanujan property, it will be enough to bound the
terms appearing in Proposition
6.45
.

7.
Erasing of crossing parameters in the length of
\mathbf{c}

In this section, a hyperbolic metric
Y
on
\mathbf{S}
is fixed. We consider our multi-loop

{\mathbf{c}}=({\mathbf{c}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{c}}_{\mathfrak{n}})
. The section is devoted to solving
the first issue raised in §
6.5
.

We recall that, for
q\in{{\Theta}}
, if
\sign(q)\sign(\sigma q)=-1
, we call
q
a U-turn index,
and otherwise a crossing index. We denote as
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\Theta
the set of crossing
indices. We shall solve issues related to situations when some crossing parameters
\theta_{q}
, with

q\in\mathcal{C}
, assume negative values.

7.1.
Idea and organization of this section

An example of a crossing variable is represented in Figure
22
. In Figure

22(a)
, the crossing parameter
\theta_{q}
is positive, and the length-function

\ell_{Y}(\mathbf{c})
belongs to an appropriate class
\mathcal{E}
, which means that the argument
we have set up in §
6
can be used to establish Theorem
1.16
. This is not the
case in Figure
22(b)
unfortunately, when
\theta_{q}\leq 0
.

(a)

When
\theta_{1}^{+}>0
.

(b)

When
\theta_{1}^{+}<0
.

Figure 22
.
A simple example with a crossing parameter
\theta_{1}^{+}
. The dotted curve is the element
of the homotopy class of
\mathbf{c}_{i}
constructed in §
3
to express

\ell_{Y}(\mathbf{c}_{i})
.

In order to solve this issue, depending on the metric
Y
, we shall find a new representative of
the homotopy class of a component
{\mathbf{c}}_{i}
of
\mathbf{c}
, and introduce new

positive
variables
\tilde{\theta}_{q}
, such that the expression of
\ell_{Y}({\mathbf{c}}_{i})

in those new variables now belongs to the desired class of functions
\mathcal{E}
. The idea is that, when
a crossing variable is negative, we can rectify the problem by a basic move: if

\sign(q)\sign(\sigma q)=+1
and
\theta_{q}<0
, the
basic move
consists in replacing

\overline{B}_{\sigma^{-1}q,q}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\overline{\mathcal{I}}_{q}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\overline{B}_{q,\sigma q}
by the homotopic orthogeodesic
\overline{B}_{\sigma^{-1}q,\sigma q}
from

\beta_{\sigma^{-1}q}
to
\beta_{\sigma q}
. The segment
\overline{\mathcal{I}}_{q}
is then said to be

erased
, and the variable
\theta_{q}
is an erased variable. On Figure
23
we
show a basic move in the case
\sign(q)=\sign(\sigma q)=+1
. We then perform these basic moves
iteratively to get rid of all negative crossing variables.

Figure 23
.
The basic move consists in replacing the dotted path with the dashed line in the
homotopy class of
\mathbf{c}_{i}
.

We start this section by studying families of aligned geodesics in
\mathbb{H}^{2}
and their
orthogeodesics. We prove a key lemma called the
staircase inequality
in §
7.2.2
. We then construct the erasing procedure in §
7.2.3
; see Proposition

7.4
. Once we lift our closed geodesic
\mathbf{c}_{i}
, loops
\beta
and bars
B
to
the hyperbolic plane
\mathbb{H}^{2}
as done in §
4.2.2
, we can apply Proposition
7.4

to them. This yields a partition of the Teichmüller space, as well as a new set of parameters

\tilde{\theta}_{q}
on each element of this partition, as detailed in §
7.3
. The new
length
\ell_{Y}(\mathbf{c}_{i})
is expressed in these new parameters in §
7.4
. We then
introduce a partition of unity adapted to this construction in §
7.6
.

For readers who decide to focus on the purely non-crossing case, it suffices to read §
7.5
-
7.6
.

7.2.
Families of aligned geodesics and their orthogeodesics

Let us group together a few key observations about families of aligned geodesics and their
orthogeodesics, which will be key to this section.
In this whole subsection, we shall consider the following set-up. For an integer
M\geq 2
,

\gamma_{0},\ldots,\gamma_{M}
are
M+1
oriented geodesics in the hyperbolic plane. We assume
that they are pairwise aligned, and that
\gamma_{i+1}
is on the left of
\gamma_{i}
. We define

\overline{B}_{ij}
to be the oriented orthogeodesics from
\gamma_{i}
to
\gamma_{j}
, of length
denoted by
L_{ij}>0
, and
z_{ij}\in\gamma_{j}
(resp.
z_{ji}\in\gamma_{i}
) the endpoint
(resp. origin) of
\overline{B}_{ij}
.

7.2.1.
The
3
-geodesic lemma

First, we prove the following elementary observation.

Lemma 7.1
.

Let
j\in\{1,\ldots,M-1\}
. Then,

(7.1)

\vec{\mathrm{d}}(z_{j,j+1},z_{j-1,j+1})\geq 0\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\vec{\mathrm{d}}(z_{j+1,j},z_{j-1,j})\geq 0\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\vec{\mathrm{d}}(z_{j+1,j-1},z_{j,j-1})\geq 0

and, in terms of absolute distances,

(7.2)

|\vec{\mathrm{d}}(z_{j+1,j},z_{j-1,j})|>|\vec{\mathrm{d}}(z_{j+1,j-1},z_{j,j-1})|+|\vec{\mathrm{d}}(z_{j,j+1},z_{j-1,j+1})|.

Proof.

These results stem from the elementary study of the polygon of consecutive vertices
z_{j-1,j+1}
,

z_{j,j+1}
,
z_{j+1,j}
,
z_{j-1,j}
,
z_{j,j-1}
,
z_{j+1,j-1}
represented in Figure

24
.

Figure 24
.
Illustration of the proof of Lemma
7.1
.

Equation (
7.1
) is obtained by observing that there exists no
hyperbolic hexagon with
5
inner angles equal to
\pi/2
and one equal to
3\pi/2
, and hence the
polygon is a self-intersecting right-angled hexagon. Then, (
7.2
) is a direct
consequence of the classic trigonometric formula for self-intersecting right-angled hexagons of
consecutive side-lengths
(\ell_{i})_{1\leq i\leq 6}
(see e.g.
[
5
, Theorem 2.4.4]
):

\displaystyle\cosh(\ell_{3})=\cosh(\ell_{1})\cosh(\ell_{5})+\cosh(\ell_{6})\sinh(\ell_{1})\sinh(\ell_{5})>\cosh(\ell_{1}+\ell_{5}).

∎

7.2.2.
The staircase inequality

Let us now prove an inequality which we will refer to as the staircase inequality,
illustrated in Figure
25
.

Figure 25
.
The geometric situation for the staircase inequality.

Lemma 7.2
.

Let
M\geq 2
.
We introduce the distances

\theta_{j}=\vec{\mathrm{d}}(z_{j-1,j},z_{j+1,j}),\quad\mu_{j}=\vec{\mathrm{d}}(z_{M,j},z_{j+1,j}),\quad\nu_{M}=\vec{\mathrm{d}}(z_{M-1,M},z_{0,M}).

Assume that
\theta_{j}\geq 0
for
1\leq j\leq M-2
, that
\theta_{M-1}<0
, and that
\mu_{j}>0

for
0\leq j\leq M-2
. Then,

(7.3)

\displaystyle|\theta_{M-1}|>\sum_{j=1}^{M-2}\theta_{j}+\mu_{0}+\nu_{M}.

Remark 7.3
.

The inequality (
7.3
) can also be written in the form

(7.4)

\displaystyle-\sum_{j=1}^{M-1}\theta_{j}>\mu_{0}+\nu_{M}.

Proof.

We apply Lemma
7.1
to the geodesics
\gamma_{j-1}
,
\gamma_{j}
and

\gamma_{M}
. We obtain that

(7.5)

\displaystyle u_{j}:=\vec{\mathrm{d}}(z_{M,j},z_{j-1,j})>\vec{\mathrm{d}}(z_{M,j-1},z_{j,j-1})+\vec{\mathrm{d}}(z_{j,M},z_{j-1,M}),

all of these quantities being positive thanks to the hypothesis
\mu_{j-1}>0
and
(
7.1
).

For
2\leq j\leq M
, going along
\gamma_{j-1}
, we have the identity

\displaystyle\vec{\mathrm{d}}(z_{M,j-1},z_{j,j-1})=\vec{\mathrm{d}}(z_{M,j-1},z_{j-2,j-1})+\vec{\mathrm{d}}(z_{j-2,j-1},z_{j,j-1})=u_{j-1}+\theta_{j-1},

and for
j=1
,

\vec{\mathrm{d}}(z_{M,0},z_{1,0})=\mu_{0}
.
As a consequence, (
7.5
) can be rewritten in terms of the sequence
(u_{j})_{j}
as:

\begin{cases}u_{j}>u_{j-1}+\theta_{j-1}+\vec{\mathrm{d}}(z_{j,M},z_{j-1,M})&(j\geq 2)\\
u_{1}>\mu_{0}+\vec{\mathrm{d}}(z_{1,M},z_{0,M})&(j=1).\end{cases}

The result follows by induction, using that
u_{M-1}=|\theta_{M-1}|
and

\nu_{M}=\sum_{j=1}^{M-1}\vec{\mathrm{d}}(z_{j,M},z_{j-1,M}).

∎

7.2.3.
Erasing the negative crossings

We are now ready to prove the main result of this section, the contruction of the erasing
procedure. We denote as
\vec{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{M-1}
the vector

\theta_{j}=\vec{\mathrm{d}}(z_{j-1,j},z_{j+1,j})
for all
1\leq j\leq M-1
, and
\vec{L}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{M}

the vector of components
L_{j,j+1}
,
0\leq j<M
. We note that the geometry of our family of
aligned geodesics is entirely determined by the values of the parameters
\vec{L},\vec{\theta}

(which belong in a subset of
\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{M}\times\mathbb{R}^{M-1}
).

Proposition 7.4
.

Let
M\geq 2
and
\vec{L}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{M}
be a fixed
length-vector. There exists a partition
(P_{\xi})_{\xi\in\Xi_{M}}
of
\mathbb{R}^{M-1}
and a family

(I(\xi))_{\xi\in\Xi_{M}}
of subsets

I(\xi)=\{0=\varphi_{\xi}(0)<\varphi_{\xi}(1)<\ldots<\varphi_{\xi}(K_{\xi}+1)=M\}

such that the following holds. Let
\xi\in\Xi_{M}
. For any
\vec{\theta}\in P_{\xi}
,

(a)

for any
1\leq j\leq K_{\xi}
,

\vec{\mathrm{d}}(z_{\varphi_{\xi}(j-1),\varphi_{\xi}(j)},z_{\varphi_{\xi}(j+1),\varphi_{\xi}(j)})\geq 0
;

(b)

for
0\leq j\leq K_{\xi}
, if we set

\displaystyle\mu_{\varphi_{\xi}(j)}:=\vec{\mathrm{d}}(z_{\varphi_{\xi}(j+1),\varphi_{\xi}(j)},z_{\varphi_{\xi}(j)+1,\varphi_{\xi}(j)})

\displaystyle\nu_{\varphi_{\xi}(j+1)}:=\vec{\mathrm{d}}(z_{\varphi_{\xi}(j+1)-1,\varphi_{\xi}(j+1)},z_{\varphi_{\xi}(j),\varphi_{\xi}(j+1)})

then both of these distances are positive;

(c)

for
0\leq j\leq K_{\xi}
,

(7.6)

-\sum_{\varphi_{\xi}(j)<k<\varphi_{\xi}(j+1)}\theta_{k}\geq\mu_{\varphi_{\xi}(j)}+\nu_{\varphi_{\xi}(j+1)}.

Intuitively, this statement uses a combination of basic moves, together with the staircase
inequality, to extract a path homotopic to the initial path, but with only positive turns. This is
illustrated in Figure
26
. Moving forward, we will use Proposition

7.4
to
erase
the variables
\theta_{k}
with
k\notin I(\xi)
. This includes
all of the negative
\theta_{k}
, but also the
\theta_{k}
that are “not positive enough” to
compensate for the existence of negative ones. Inequality
7.6
says that the sum of
erased
\theta_{k}
is negative, and gives a lower bound on its absolute value.

Figure 26
.
Illustration of the statement of Proposition
7.4
.

Remark 7.5
.

The partition is indexed by some set
\Xi_{M}
, the definition of which will be determined in the
proof, but is of no particular interest. It is worth noting from the proof that the elements

P_{\xi}
of the partition are products of intervals.

Remark 7.6
.

We have fixed the lengths
\vec{L}
because the whole discussion depends implicitly on the value
of
\vec{L}
, although not apparent in the notation.
The dependency on
\vec{L}
shall not be relevant to our purposes.

Proof.

We shall prove the proposition by induction on the integer
M
, using a succession of basic
moves. If
M=2
, the partition of
\mathbb{R}
is
\mathbb{R}_{<0}\sqcup\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
. More precisely, the sets

I
are as follows.

•

If
\theta_{1}\geq 0
, there is nothing to be done, and we take
I=\{0,1,2\}
(i.e.

K=M-1=1
and
\varphi
is the identity).

•

If
\theta_{1}<0
, then we perform a basic move, that is, we take
I=\{0,2\}
. Inequality (
7.2
) then implies the claim that

|\theta_{1}|>\vec{\mathrm{d}}(z_{2,0},z_{1,0})+\vec{\mathrm{d}}(z_{1,2},z_{0,2})=\mu_{0}+\nu_{2}
.

Assume now that the construction has been achieved at the rank
M
, and extend it to the next
rank
M+1
, i.e. to a family of aligned geodesics
\gamma_{0},\ldots,\gamma_{M+1}
. We apply the
construction to the geodesics
\gamma_{0},\ldots,\gamma_{M}
and obtain a partition

(P_{\xi})_{\xi\in\Xi_{M}}
of
\mathbb{R}^{M-1}
and a family of sets

I(\xi)=\{0=\varphi_{\xi}(0)<\varphi_{\xi}(1)<\ldots<\varphi_{\xi}(K_{\xi}+1)=M\}

satisfying the properties (a), (b), (c). Let us now construct a partition

(P_{\xi^{\prime}}^{\prime})_{\xi^{\prime}\in\Xi_{M+1}}
of
\mathbb{R}^{M}
and a family of sets
I^{\prime}(\xi^{\prime})
(associated to a
function
\varphi_{\xi^{\prime}}^{\prime}
and an integer
K_{\xi^{\prime}}^{\prime}
) satisfying (a), (b), (c) at the rank

M+1
.

We shall partition

\mathbb{R}^{M}=\mathbb{R}^{M-1}\times\mathbb{R}=\bigsqcup_{\xi\in\Xi_{M}}(P_{\xi}\times\mathbb{R})

by partitionning each individual
P_{\xi}\times\mathbb{R}
. Let
\xi\in\Xi_{M}
be fixed – to
simplify notations, we drop the mention of
\xi
in
\varphi_{\xi}
and
K_{\xi}
. Let us cut the

\mathbb{R}
-component of
P_{\xi}\times\mathbb{R}
(related to the value of
\theta_{M}
) depending on the
relative positions of the points
z_{\varphi(K),M}
and
z_{M+1,M}
along
\gamma_{M}
.

•

If the segment
[z_{\varphi(K),M},z_{M+1,M}]
is positively oriented, there is
nothing to be done, and we simply let
I^{\prime}=I(\xi)\cup\{M+1\}
(in which case
K^{\prime}=K+1
).

•

Otherwise, using (
7.1
), we observe that for any
k\in\{1,\ldots,K\}
,

\displaystyle\vec{\mathrm{d}}(z_{M+1,\varphi(k)},z_{\varphi(k+1),\varphi(k)})\geq 0\quad\Rightarrow\quad\forall j\geq k,\quad\vec{\mathrm{d}}(z_{M+1,\varphi(j)},z_{\varphi(j-1),\varphi(j)})>0

\displaystyle\vec{\mathrm{d}}(z_{\varphi(k-1),\varphi(k)},z_{M+1,\varphi(k)})\geq 0\quad\Rightarrow\quad\forall j\leq k,\quad\vec{\mathrm{d}}(z_{\varphi(j+1),\varphi(j)},z_{M+1,\varphi(j)})>0.

We use this to establish the existence of a uniquely defined
K^{\prime}\in\{0,\ldots,K\}
,
represented in Figure
27
, such that the following two conditions hold:

(7.7)

\begin{split}\forall 0\leq j\leq K,\quad\big(\vec{\mathrm{d}}(z_{M+1,\varphi(j)},z_{\varphi(j+1),\varphi(j)})\geq 0&\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad j\geq K^{\prime}\big)\\
\forall 1\leq j\leq K,\quad\big(\vec{\mathrm{d}}(z_{\varphi(j-1),\varphi(j)},z_{M+1,\varphi(j)})>0&\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad j\leq K^{\prime}\big).\end{split}

Figure 27
.
In order to highlight the construction of the integer
K^{\prime}
, we have only represented
the geodesics
\gamma_{\varphi(j)}
for
0\leq j\leq K+1
as well as
\gamma_{M+1}
, and
the relevant orthogeodesics.

We then let
I^{\prime}=\{\varphi(0),\ldots,\varphi(K^{\prime}),M+1\}
, a set associated to
the integers
K^{\prime}
and the function
\varphi^{\prime}
which coincides with
\varphi
for
j\leq K^{\prime}

and is equal to
M+1
at
K^{\prime}+1
.

Let us now construct the partition in more detail. We temporarily revert to notations depending
on
\xi
for clarity. For any
K^{\prime}\in\{0,\ldots,K_{\xi}+1\}
, we denote as
E_{K^{\prime}}
the set of
values of
\theta_{M}\in\mathbb{R}
such that:

•

\vec{\mathrm{d}}(z_{\varphi_{\xi}(K_{\xi}),M},z_{M+1,M})\geq 0
if
K^{\prime}=K_{\xi}+1
;

•

the set of conditions (
7.7
) holds if
K^{\prime}
is an integer in

\{0,\ldots,K_{\xi}\}
.

We can then write

\mathbb{R}=\bigsqcup_{K^{\prime}=0}^{K_{\xi}+1}E_{K^{\prime}}
for each individual
\xi\in\Xi_{M}
. This yields the
partition

\mathbb{R}^{M}=\bigsqcup_{\xi^{\prime}\in\Xi_{M+1}}P^{\prime}_{\xi^{\prime}},\quad\text{where}\quad\Xi_{M+1}=\{(\xi,K^{\prime})\,:\,\xi\in\Xi_{M},0\leq K^{\prime}\leq K_{\xi}+1\}

and
P^{\prime}_{\xi^{\prime}}:=P_{\xi}\times E_{K^{\prime}}
for
\xi^{\prime}=(\xi,K^{\prime})\in\Xi_{M+1}
.

Our choice of
K^{\prime}
straighforwardly implies that (a) and (b) are satisfied. The condition (c) is
automatic in the case
K^{\prime}=K+1
, and is the last thing that we need to check when
K^{\prime}\leq K
.

By the induction hypothesis and the construction of
I^{\prime}
, we already know that (
7.6
)
holds for
j=1,\ldots,K^{\prime}-1
, and we just need to check it for
j=K^{\prime}
. That is, we need to prove
that

-\sum_{\varphi(K^{\prime})<k<M+1}\theta_{k}\geq\vec{\mathrm{d}}(z_{M+1,\varphi(K^{\prime})},z_{\varphi(K^{\prime})+1,\varphi(K^{\prime})})+\vec{\mathrm{d}}(z_{M,M+1},z_{\varphi(K^{\prime}),M+1}).

In order to do so, we write

(7.8)

\sum_{\varphi(K^{\prime})<k<M+1}\theta_{k}=\sum_{j=K^{\prime}+1}^{K+1}\theta_{\varphi(j)}+\sum_{j=K^{\prime}}^{K}\sum_{\varphi(j)<k<\varphi(j+1)}\theta_{k}.

In order to bound the first term, we apply the staircase inequality, Lemma
7.2

(under the form (
7.4
)), to the geodesics
\gamma_{\varphi(j)}
for

K^{\prime}\leq j\leq K
and
\gamma_{M+1}
. The conditions (a) and (b) allow to verify the
hypotheses. We obtain

(7.9)

\begin{split}&-\sum_{j=K^{\prime}+1}^{K}\vec{\mathrm{d}}(z_{\varphi(j-1),\varphi(j)},z_{\varphi(j+1),\varphi(j)})-\vec{\mathrm{d}}(z_{\varphi(K),M},z_{M+1,M})\\
&>\vec{\mathrm{d}}(z_{M+1,\varphi(K^{\prime})},z_{\varphi(K^{\prime}+1),\varphi(K^{\prime})})+\vec{\mathrm{d}}(z_{M,M+1},z_{\varphi(K^{\prime}),M+1}).\end{split}

We then relate each of the terms on the left-hand-side to a
\theta_{\varphi(j)}
by writing for

j\leq K

\begin{split}\theta_{\varphi(j)}&=\vec{\mathrm{d}}(z_{\varphi(j)-1,\varphi(j)},z_{\varphi(j)+1,\varphi(j)})\\
&=\vec{\mathrm{d}}(z_{\varphi(j)-1,\varphi(j)},z_{\varphi(j-1),\varphi(j)})+\vec{\mathrm{d}}(z_{\varphi(j-1),\varphi(j)},z_{\varphi(j+1),\varphi(j)})+\vec{\mathrm{d}}(z_{\varphi(j+1),\varphi(j)},z_{\varphi(j)+1,\varphi(j)})\\
&=\nu_{\varphi(j)}+\vec{\mathrm{d}}(z_{\varphi(j-1),\varphi(j)},z_{\varphi(j+1),\varphi(j)})+\mu_{\varphi(j)}\end{split}

and similarly

\theta_{\varphi(K+1)}=\theta_{M}=\vec{\mathrm{d}}(z_{M-1,M},z_{M+1,M})=\nu_{\varphi(K+1)}+\vec{\mathrm{d}}(z_{\varphi(K),M},z_{M+1,M}).

Equation (
7.9
) then becomes

(7.10)

\begin{split}&-\sum_{j=K^{\prime}+1}^{K+1}\theta_{\varphi(j)}+\sum_{j=K^{\prime}+1}^{K}\mu_{\varphi(j)}+\sum_{j=K^{\prime}}^{K}\nu_{\varphi(j+1)}\\
&>\vec{\mathrm{d}}(z_{M+1,\varphi(K^{\prime})},z_{\varphi(K^{\prime}+1),\varphi(K^{\prime})})+\vec{\mathrm{d}}(z_{M,M+1},z_{\varphi(K^{\prime}),M+1}).\end{split}

Now, to bound the second sum in (
7.8
), we apply the induction hypothesis to
K^{\prime}\leq j\leq K
, which yields:

(7.11)

\displaystyle-\sum_{\varphi(j)<k<\varphi(j+1)}\theta_{k}>\mu_{\varphi(j)}+\nu_{\varphi(j+1)}.

Summing (
7.10
) with (
7.11
) for all
K^{\prime}\leq j\leq K
allows to obtain, due
to (
7.8
), that

\displaystyle-\sum_{\varphi(K^{\prime})<k<M+1}\theta_{k}>\vec{\mathrm{d}}(z_{M+1,\varphi(K^{\prime})},z_{\varphi(K^{\prime}+1),\varphi(K^{\prime})})+\vec{\mathrm{d}}(z_{M,M+1},z_{\varphi(K^{\prime}),M+1})+\mu_{\varphi(K^{\prime})}.

(we notice that all but one of the terms
\mu_{\varphi(j)}
and
\nu_{\varphi(j+1)}
vanish). The
conclusion then follows, observing that

\vec{\mathrm{d}}(z_{M+1,\varphi(K^{\prime})},z_{\varphi(K^{\prime}+1),\varphi(K^{\prime})})+\mu_{\varphi(K^{\prime})}=\vec{\mathrm{d}}(z_{M+1,\varphi(K^{\prime})},z_{\varphi(K^{\prime})+1,\varphi(K^{\prime})}).

∎

Remark 7.7
.

We can apply a similar construction to families of aligned geodesics ordered in the opposite
order, i.e. such that
\gamma_{i+1}
is now on the
right
of
\gamma_{i}
. Importantly, in
this case, we do
not
reverse the direction of the hypotheses (b) (otherwise, the
construction would be exactly the same and of no additional interest). See Figure

28
.

Figure 28
.
Two examples with
M=3
and positive or negative
crossings.

7.3.
Crossing intervals and erasing of negative crossing parameters

In this section, we lift the geodesics
\beta
to the hyperbolic plane, and apply Proposition

7.4
to erase all negative crossing variables. This yields newly defined
positive

variables
(\tilde{\theta}_{q})_{q\in I(\xi)}
. We will rely on the periodic re-indexation of

\Theta
introduced in §
2.5
as well as the lifting from §
4.2.2
.

The forthcoming discussion, in the case of purely non-crossing eights, is essentially empty.

7.3.1.
Decomposition of
\Theta^{i}
in crossing intervals

Let
i\in\{1,\ldots,\mathfrak{n}\}
.
We decompose the set
\Theta^{i}\sim\mathbb{Z}_{n_{i}}
according to the signs of its elements.

Notation 7.8
.

We let
I_{1}^{+},\ldots,I_{p}^{+}\subseteq\Theta^{i}
denote the connected components (maximal
subintervals) of
\{q\in\Theta^{i},\sign(q)=+\}
, and
I_{1}^{-},\ldots,I_{p}^{-}\subseteq\Theta^{i}

the connected components of the complement. We call the intervals
I_{j}^{\delta}

crossing
intervals
(
j=1,\ldots,p
,
\delta=\pm
).
Actual crossings
correspond to intervals

I_{j}^{\delta}
of length
\geq 2
.

Remark 7.9
.

If the function
\sign
is constant on
\Theta^{i}
, then
p=1
(there is only one crossing
interval), and we either have
I_{1}^{+}=\emptyset
or
I_{1}^{-}=\emptyset
. In all other cases,
due to the cyclicity of
\Theta^{i}\sim\mathbb{Z}_{n_{i}}
, the crossing intervals alternate in signs, and
hence the number of positive and negative crossing intervals are equal.

This decomposition of
\Theta^{i}
in crossing intervals yields the partition

\Theta^{i}=\bigsqcup_{j=1}^{p}\bigsqcup_{\delta=\pm}I_{j}^{\delta},

which allows us to identify

\mathbb{R}^{\Theta^{i}}\simeq\prod_{j=1}^{p}\prod_{\delta=\pm}\mathbb{R}^{I_{j}^{\delta}}.

Note that crossing intervals
I_{j}^{\delta}
(
j\in\mathbb{Z}
) can also be defined as subsets of

{\mathbf{\Theta}}^{i}
,
after pre-composing with the projection map
\mathbb{Z}\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{n_{i}}
. In that case we have

I_{j+p}^{\delta}=I_{j}^{\delta}+n_{i}
, where
p
is the number of positive crossing intervals
(equivalently, of negative crossing intervals).

7.3.2.
Application of the erasing procedure to one crossing interval

Let
j\in\{1,\ldots,p\}
be an actual crossing. We write

I_{j}^{\delta}=\{q_{0}+1,\ldots,q_{0}+M\}
, where
M=\#I_{j}^{\delta}\geq 2
. By Lemma

4.1
, we can apply Proposition
7.4
(and Remark
7.7
if

\delta=-
) to the family of
M+1
aligned geodesics
\tilde{\beta}(q)
with

q_{0}\leq q\leq q_{0}+M
, and obtain:

•

a partition
(P_{\xi}^{o})_{\xi\in\Xi_{M}}
of
\mathbb{R}^{\{q_{0}+1,\ldots,q_{0}+M-1\}}
;

•

subsets
\{q_{0}=\varphi_{\xi}(0)<\varphi_{\xi}(1)<\ldots<\varphi_{\xi}(K_{\xi}+1)=q_{0}+M\}
of

I_{j}^{\delta}\cup\{q_{0}\}

satisfying the properties (a), (b), (c). We then define:

•

\Xi(I_{j}^{\delta})
as a copy of
\Xi_{M}
;

•

for
\xi=\xi_{j}^{\delta}\in\Xi(I_{j}^{\delta})
,
P_{\xi_{j}^{\delta}}=P_{\xi}^{o}\times\mathbb{R}
, so
that
(P_{\xi_{j}^{\delta}})_{\xi_{j}^{\delta}\in\Xi(I_{j}^{\delta})}
is a partition of

\mathbb{R}^{I_{j}^{\delta}}
;

•

for
\xi\in\Xi(I_{j}^{\delta})
,

I_{j}^{\delta}(\xi):=\{\varphi_{\xi}(1)<\ldots<\varphi_{\xi}(K_{\xi}+1)\}
, which is a
subinterval of
I_{j}^{\delta}
.

We extend this definition to the case where the crossing is not an actual crossing, by setting

(P_{\xi_{j}^{\delta}})_{\xi_{j}^{\delta}\in\Xi(I_{j}^{\delta})}
to be the trivial partition

\mathbb{R}^{I_{j}^{\delta}}=\mathbb{R}
indexed by a singleton set
\Xi(I_{j}^{\delta})
, and

I_{j}^{\delta}(\xi)=I_{j}^{\delta}
in that case.

Remark 7.10
.

Keep in mind that by construction, for any
\xi\in\Xi(I_{j}^{\delta})
, the interval

I_{j}^{\delta}(\xi)
always contains the right endpoint
q_{0}+M
of
I_{j}^{\delta}
, which is by
definition a U-turn. The sets
P_{\xi_{j}^{\delta}}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{I_{j}^{\delta}}
all contain a full copy
of
\mathbb{R}
for the rightmost point of
I_{j}^{\delta}
, because the U-turn variables do not need to be
controlled, by virtue of the Useful Remark, Remark
2.11
. Indeed, it is automatic that
the segments
[z_{q_{0}-1,q_{0}},z_{\varphi(1),q_{0}}]
and
[z_{\varphi(K),q_{0}+M},z_{q_{0}+M+1,q_{0}+M}]
are
positively oriented.

Remark 7.11
.

In the case described in Remark
7.9
, the
construction is slightly different at the last step of the induction. If

[z_{\varphi(K),n_{i}},z_{n_{i}+1,n_{i}}]
is positively oriented, we proceed as normal, but if

[z_{\varphi(K),n_{i}},z_{n_{i}+1,n_{i}}]
is negatively oriented, then we simply take

I(\xi)=\emptyset
.

Remark 7.12
.

The construction is done in the universal cover. Since the covering group acts by isometries, if
we replace
I
by
I+n_{i}
, then the picture is translated by a hyperbolic isometry, namely the
translation of axis
\tilde{\mathbf{c}}_{i}
and of translation length
\ell({\mathbf{c}}_{i})
.
This remark implies that the construction done in the universal cover projects down to

{\mathbf{S}}
, and that the subsets
I_{j}^{\delta}(\xi_{j}^{\delta})
satisfy the invariance property

I_{j+p}^{\delta}(\xi_{j+p}^{\delta})=I_{j}^{\delta}(\xi_{j}^{\delta})+n_{i}
.

7.3.3.
New representative of the homotopy class of
\mathbf{c}_{i}

Let us show how our construction will be used to resolve the issue caused by the crossing interval

I_{j}^{\delta}
, raised in §
6.5
.

Notation 7.13
.

Let
\xi\in\Xi(I_{j}^{\delta})
. For
q=\varphi_{\xi}(k)\in I_{j}^{\delta}(\xi)
, we consider the points

G_{q}:=z_{\varphi_{\xi}(k-1),\varphi_{\xi}(k)}\quad\text{and}\quad D_{q}:=z_{\varphi_{\xi}(k+1),\varphi_{\xi}(k)}.

We denote as
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{q}
the segment

[G_{q},D_{q}]
on
\tilde{\beta}(q)
.

By property (a), the segment
\tilde{\mathcal{I}}^{\prime}_{q}
is positively oriented for every

q\in I_{j}^{\delta}(\xi)
. We observe that the portion

\tilde{p}_{q_{0}+1}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\ldots\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\tilde{p}_{q_{0}+M}
of the infinite path

\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}_{q\in{\mathbf{\Theta}}^{i}}\;\tilde{p}(q)
is homotopic, with endpoints gliding along

\tilde{\beta}(q_{0})
and
\tilde{\beta}(q_{0}+M)
, to the path

(7.12)

\displaystyle\tilde{B}_{q_{0},\varphi_{\xi}(1)}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{\varphi_{\xi}(1)}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\ldots\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\tilde{B}_{\varphi_{\xi}(k-1),\varphi_{\xi}(k)}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{\varphi_{\xi}(k)}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\tilde{B}_{\varphi_{\xi}(j),\varphi_{\xi}(j+1)}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\ldots\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{\varphi_{\xi}(K)}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\tilde{B}_{\varphi_{\xi}(K),q_{0}+M}.

This is the path represented in Figure
27
. We therefore see that Proposition

7.4
allows to replace our original representation of
\tilde{\mathbf{c}}_{i}
, where
the segments
\tilde{\mathcal{I}}(q)
could be oriented in any direction, to a new one where the
segments
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{\varphi_{\xi}(k)}
are positively oriented.

7.3.4.
Erasing of all crossing intervals in
\Theta^{i}

Let us now apply the erasing procedure simultanously to all crossing intervals in
\Theta^{i}
.

Notation 7.14
.

We define for the component
\mathbf{c}_{i}
the set

(7.13)

\Xi^{i}:=\prod_{1\leq j\leq p}\prod_{\delta=\pm}\Xi(I_{j}^{\delta}).

We denote as
\xi^{i}=(\xi_{j}^{\delta})_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j\leq p\\
\delta=\pm\end{subarray}}
the elements of

\Xi^{i}
, and for such a
\xi^{i}
, we let

(7.14)

P_{\xi^{i}}:=\prod_{j=1}^{p}\prod_{\delta=\pm}P_{\xi_{j}^{\delta}}\subseteq\prod_{j=1}^{p}\prod_{\delta=\pm}\mathbb{R}^{I_{j}^{\delta}}=\mathbb{R}^{\Theta^{i}}

and

(7.15)

I(\xi^{i})=\bigsqcup_{j=1}^{p}\bigsqcup_{\delta=\pm}I_{j}^{\delta}(\xi_{j}^{\delta}),\quad\quad\tilde{I}(\xi^{i})=\bigsqcup_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\bigsqcup_{\delta=\pm}I_{j}^{\delta}(\xi_{j}^{\delta}).

We denote
n_{\xi^{i}}=\#I(\xi^{i})
.

The collection
(P_{\xi^{i}})_{\xi^{i}\in\Xi^{i}}
then forms a partition of
\mathbb{R}^{\Theta^{i}}
. The set

I(\xi^{i})
is a subset of
\Theta^{i}
while
\tilde{I}(\xi^{i})
is the corresponding
n_{i}\mathbb{Z}
-periodic
subset of
{\mathbf{\Theta}}^{i}
. Because
I_{j}^{\delta}(\xi_{j}^{\delta})
always has the same right
endpoint as
I_{j}^{\delta}
,
I(\xi^{i})
contains all the U-turn variables in
\Theta^{i}
.

7.3.5.
New variables
\tilde{\theta}_{q}
and crossing inequality

For
\xi^{i}\in\Xi^{i}
, the sets
I(\xi^{i})
and
\tilde{I}(\xi^{i})
can be described by an increasing
function
(\varphi_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
with values in
\mathbf{\Theta}^{i}
such that

\varphi_{k+\#I(\xi^{i})}=\varphi_{k}+n_{i}
.

Notation 7.15
.

Let
\xi^{i}\in\Xi^{i}
. For
q=\varphi_{k}\in I(\xi^{i})
, we denote as:

•

G_{q}:=z_{\varphi_{k-1},\varphi_{k}}
and

D_{q}:=z_{\varphi_{k+1},\varphi_{k}}
;

•

\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{q}
the segment
[G_{q},D_{q}]
on
\tilde{\beta}(q)
, and
\tilde{\theta}_{q}
its length;

•

\tilde{L}_{q}=L_{\varphi_{k-1},\varphi_{k}}
the length of the orthogeodesic

\tilde{B}_{\varphi_{k-1},\varphi_{k}}
.

By property (a), the segment
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{q}
is always positively oriented and hence
\tilde{\theta}_{q}\geq 0
.

We can now adapt (
7.12
) and now simultaneous erase all crossing
intervals. Indeed, the infinite concatenation

\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\big(\tilde{B}_{\varphi_{k-1},\varphi_{k}}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{\varphi_{k}}\big)

gives an infinite curve in the hyperbolic plane, which projects down to a curve homotopic to the
lift
\tilde{\mathbf{c}}_{i}
of
\mathbf{c}_{i}
to
\mathbb{H}
. The fact that the new path does no longer
go along
\tilde{\mathcal{I}}(q)
for
q\in\tilde{I}(\xi^{i})
motivates the following terminology.

Definition 7.16
.

The variables
\theta_{q}
for
q\in\Theta^{i}\setminus\tilde{I}(\xi^{i})
are said to be

erased
.

We can then restate the property (c) from Proposition
7.4
in the following equivalent
way.

Lemma 7.17
.

For any component
i
,

(7.16)

\displaystyle\sum_{q\in\Theta^{i}}\theta_{q}<\sum_{q\in I(\xi^{i})}\tilde{\theta}_{q}.

Proof.

We simply notice that property (c) states that

(7.17)

\displaystyle\sum_{q\in\Theta^{i}\setminus I(\xi^{i})}\theta_{q}<-\sum_{k=1}^{\#I(\xi^{i})}(\nu_{\varphi_{k+1}}+\mu_{\varphi_{k}})

with the notation
\mu,\nu
from Proposition
7.4
, and observe that by definition,

(7.18)

\displaystyle\nu_{\varphi_{k}}+\tilde{\theta}_{\varphi_{k}}+\mu_{\varphi_{k}}=\theta_{\varphi_{k}}.

∎

Remark 7.18
.

In the case described in Remarks
7.9
and
7.11
, when the sign function is constant on

\Theta^{i}
, if
\xi
is such that
I(\xi)=\emptyset
, then (
7.16
) means that

\displaystyle\sum_{q\in\Theta^{i}}\theta_{q}<0.

In this case it is sufficient to consider the geodesic representative of
{\mathbf{c}}_{i}
, and we
won’t need to express the length
\ell_{Y}({\mathbf{c}}_{i})
in coordinates.

7.3.6.
Product over all components
\mathbf{c}_{i}

Let us now group our constructions for the different components

\mathbf{c}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{c}_{\mathfrak{n}}
of the multiloop
\mathbf{c}
. In order to do so, we let

\Xi:=\prod_{i=1}^{\mathfrak{n}}\Xi^{i}
. We shall consider the cartesian product of the partitions

P_{\xi^{i}}
, that is, for
\xi=(\xi^{1},\ldots,\xi^{\mathfrak{n}})\in\Xi
, we let

(7.19)

\displaystyle P_{\xi}=\prod_{i=1}^{\mathfrak{n}}P_{\xi^{i}}\quad\text{and}\quad I(\xi)=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{\mathfrak{n}}I(\xi^{i})\subseteq\Theta.

The sets
(P_{\xi})_{\xi\in\Xi}
then form a partition of

\prod_{i=1}^{\mathfrak{n}}\mathbb{R}^{\Theta^{i}}=\mathbb{R}^{\Theta}
. Each
P_{\xi}
is a product of intervals.

We shall say that the variables
\theta_{q}
for
q\not\in I(\xi)
are
erased
. The set
I(\xi)

contains all U-turn variables. As a consequence, in the purely non-crossing case, we have

I(\xi)=\Theta
for all
\xi
, and all the previous discussion is empty.

7.4.
New expression for the length

The erasing procedure immediately yields a new expression for
\ell_{Y}({\mathbf{c}})
, true for

\vec{\theta}\in P_{\xi}
, which replaces (
4.12
).

Proposition 7.19
.

Let
\xi\in\Xi
. As soon as
\vec{\theta}\in P_{\xi}
, we have

(7.20)

\displaystyle\ell_{Y}({\mathbf{c}})=\sum_{i=1}^{\mathfrak{n}}M_{n_{\xi^{i}}}((\epsilon\tilde{L}_{q})_{q\in I(\xi^{i})},(\tilde{\theta}_{q})_{q\in I(\xi^{i})})

where
n_{\xi^{i}}=\#I(\xi^{i})
,
\epsilon\tilde{L}_{q}:=\sign(q)\tilde{L}_{q}
and
M_{n}
is defined in
(
4.10
).

Proof.

For each
1\leq i\leq\mathfrak{n}
, we apply Proposition
4.6
to the
representative

\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\big(\tilde{B}_{\varphi_{k-1},\varphi_{k}}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{\varphi_{k}}\big)
of the homotopy class of
\tilde{\mathbf{c}}_{i}
on the subset
P_{\xi^{i}}

of
\mathbb{R}^{\Theta^{i}}
.
∎

Note that, as long as
\vec{\theta}\in P_{\xi}
, each variable
\tilde{\theta}_{q}
with

q\in I(\xi)
is positive. As a consequence, this new formula solves the first issue raised in §
6.5
.

When using formula (
7.20
) later, we shall consider all the variables
\theta_{q}
with

q\not\in I(\xi)
as additional neutral parameters. The following observation will then be useful.

Lemma 7.20
.

Let
\xi\in\Xi
. On the set
P_{\xi}
, for any
q\in I(\xi)
,

(7.21)

\tilde{L}_{q}=\mathrm{fn}((\theta_{q^{\prime}})_{q^{\prime}\not\in I(\xi)},\vec{L})\quad\text{and}\quad\tilde{\theta}_{q}=\theta_{q}+\mathrm{fn}((\theta_{q^{\prime}})_{q^{\prime}\not\in I(\xi)},\vec{L}).

Proof.

Let
\xi\in\Xi
. For
1\leq i\leq\mathfrak{n}
, let us represent
\xi^{i}\in\Xi^{i}
by a function

(\varphi_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
. For
q\in I(\xi^{i})
, the length
\tilde{L}_{q}
is the length of the
orthogeodesic from
\tilde{\beta}(\varphi_{k-1})
to
\tilde{\beta}(\varphi_{k})
, and therefore can
be expressed as a function of
\vec{L}
and the
\theta_{q^{\prime}}
with
q^{\prime}\in\Theta^{i}
such that

\varphi_{k-1}<q^{\prime}<\varphi_{k}
. This implies that
q^{\prime}\notin I(\xi)
, which was our claim.

Similarly, the quantities
\nu_{\varphi_{k}},\mu_{\varphi_{k}}
can be expressed solely in terms
of the
\theta_{q^{\prime}}
with
q^{\prime}\not\in I(\xi)
. However, we have seen in (
7.18
) that

\theta_{\varphi_{k}}=\nu_{\varphi_{k}}+\tilde{\theta}_{\varphi_{k}}+\mu_{\varphi_{k}}
, which
allows us to conclude.
∎

As a consequence, if we fix
(\theta_{q})_{q\not\in I(\xi^{i})}
as well as
\vec{L}
, and view the
expression (
7.20
) as a function of
(\tilde{\theta}_{q})_{q\in I(\xi^{i})}
, then Proposition

6.24
holds for this new length function.

Remark 7.21
.

An intuition as to why the variables
\theta_{q}
taking negative values yield additional neutral
parameters can be found by examining Figure
22
. Recall that we have motivated
in §
5.3
the fact that the lengths
\vec{L}
are neutral parameters by observing that,
when we describe the path
\mathbf{c}_{i}
as the concatenation

\mathbf{c}_{i}^{\mathrm{op}}=\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}_{q\in{{\Theta}}^{i}}\;p(q)
, each bar is visited twice, which mean
that there is a factor of
2
in front of each
L_{j}
in our comparison estimates.

We see in the example of Figure
22
that, when
\theta_{1}^{+}<0
, the portion

\mathcal{I}_{1}^{+}
is visited three times by the path
\mathbf{c}_{i}^{\mathrm{op}}
, instead of once
when
\theta_{1}^{+}\geq 0
. This means that we do not need to apply the operator
\mathcal{L}
to cancel
exponential growth related to the parameter
\theta_{1}^{+}
, or, in other words, that it is a neutral
parameter.

7.5.
The purely non-crossing case

The reader only interested in the purely non-crossing case can read the rest of the paper,
considering that
\Xi
is a set with one element denoted
\xi=(\xi^{1},\ldots,\xi^{\mathfrak{n}})
, and that
in what follows we let
I(\xi)=\Theta
,
I(\xi^{i})=\Theta^{i}
for
i\in\{1,\ldots,\mathfrak{n}\}
. In this
case we have
\tilde{\theta}_{q}=\theta_{q}
for all
q\in\Theta
, as well as

\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{q}=\overline{\mathcal{I}}_{q}=[G_{q},D_{q}]
.

7.6.
A partition of unity on Teichmüller space

We recall that the space of interest in this article is the Teichmüller space

\mathcal{T}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}^{\ast}
, which we have equipped with a new set of coordinates

(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{r}\times\mathbb{R}^{\Theta}
. We have introduced a partition

\mathbb{R}^{\Theta}=\bigsqcup_{\xi\in\Xi}P_{\xi}
that we wish to use on the parameter

\vec{\theta}
. Because our methods require some smoothness, it will be more convenient to partition
the values of
\vec{\theta}
using a partition of unity, which we shall now construct. Note that
the entire discussion above is dependent on the length vector
\vec{L}
, although this has not been
made explicit in the notation.

Notation 7.22
.

Let
\chi_{\mathcal{C},0}
and
\chi_{\mathcal{C},\infty}
denote two smooth functions on
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
such that:

•

\chi_{\mathcal{C},0}+\chi_{\mathcal{C},\infty}\equiv 1
;

•

\chi_{\mathcal{C},0}
is supported in
[0,2\log 2]
and identically equal to
1
on
[0,\log 2]
.

Definition 7.23
.

For
\xi\in\Xi
and
\mathcal{Q}\subseteq I(\xi)
, we define a cut-off function on Teichmüller space,

(7.22)

\displaystyle\Psi_{\xi,\mathcal{Q}}(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})={\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-4.0mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-4.5mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-5.0mu{\rm{l}}}}_{P_{\xi}}(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})\prod_{q\in\mathcal{Q}}\chi_{\mathcal{C},\infty}(\tilde{\theta}_{q})\prod_{q\not\in\mathcal{Q}}\chi_{\mathcal{C},0}(\tilde{\theta}_{q}).

By construction,

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\xi\in\Xi\\
\mathcal{Q}\subseteq I(\xi)\end{subarray}}\Psi_{\xi,\mathcal{Q}}\equiv 1.

As announced in §
7.4
, we associate to each partition element some additional neutral
variables.

Notation 7.24

(Neutral variables associated with
(\xi,\mathcal{Q})
)
.

For
\xi\in\Xi
and
\mathcal{Q}\subseteq I(\xi)
, we let

(7.23)

\displaystyle\Theta_{\mathrm{nt}}(\xi,\mathcal{Q})=\Theta\setminus\mathcal{Q}.

Note that, on the support of the cut-off function
\Psi_{\xi,\mathcal{Q}}
, the variables

(\tilde{\theta}_{q})_{q\in\Theta\setminus\mathcal{Q}}
stay in
[0,2\log 2]
, so that we do not need to
gain exponential decay in those variables. They can therefore be considered to be neutral
variables.

8.
The lengths of boundary curves

8.1.
Objective and plan of the section

Recall that, in Proposition
4.11
, we have obtained expressions for the lengths
of the pair of pants decomposition
(\Gamma_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
in our new coordinates

(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})
on the Teichmüller space
\mathcal{T}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}^{*}
. This new expression reads

\ell_{Y}(\Gamma_{\lambda})=Q_{m}(\vec{L}^{\lambda},\vec{\tau}^{\lambda})
, where

\vec{L}^{\lambda}=(L^{\lambda}_{1},\ldots,L^{\lambda}_{m})
and

\vec{\tau}^{\lambda}=(\tau^{\lambda}_{1},\ldots,\tau^{\lambda}_{m})
are the parameters
described in §
4.4.2
,
m=m(\lambda)
, and the functions
F_{m}
,
Q_{m}
are
defined in (
4.20
) by

(8.1)

\displaystyle F_{m}(\vec{L},\vec{\tau})=\frac{\cosh(\frac{1}{2}Q_{m}(\vec{L},\vec{\tau}))}{\exp(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\tau_{j})}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\alpha\in\{0,1\}^{m}}(-1)^{|\alpha|}\hyp_{-\partial\alpha}\left(\frac{\vec{L}}{2}\right)e^{-\alpha\cdot\vec{\tau}}

with
(\partial\alpha)_{j}=1
if
\alpha_{j}=\alpha_{j-1}
and
-1
if
\alpha_{j}\not=\alpha_{j-1}
.

The aim of this section is to study the function
F_{m}
, and in particular its logarithmic
derivatives, which will appear when we apply our key argument to the
integral (
5.13
). The formula (
8.1
) is problematic because of its alternating
signs: it is not clear which terms dominate the sum, nor is it that the formula gives a positive
function. In fact, the domain
\mathfrak{D}
of
(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})
is precisely defined by the
fact that the formula giving
\cosh(\ell_{Y}(\Gamma_{\lambda})/2)
is greater than
1
for all

\lambda\in\Lambda
, as seen in Proposition
3.21
.

We work around this problem introducing a
favorable region
, i.e. a region of
\mathfrak{D}
on
which
F_{m}
is well-approximated by its term with
\alpha=(0,\ldots,0)
, equal to

\prod_{j=1}^{m}\sinh(L_{j}/2)
. This domain is defined in terms of the
heights of cells

introduced in §
8.4.1
. We provide estimates on the derivatives of
F_{m}
true
inside the favorable region in Proposition
8.17
. Outside the favorable region, the height
of one (or several) cells must be bounded – we
neutralize
such cells. The neutralization
procedure is to low-height cells what the erasing procedure was to negative
\theta
parameters in
§
7
. The description of the neutralization procedure while dealing
simultanously with all of the boundary components
(\Gamma_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}

requires some technical considerations, addressed in §
8.5
. In §
8.6
, we finally obtain a partition of unity of the Teichmüller space so that we
understand the length of
\Gamma_{\lambda}
and its derivatives on every piece of the partition.

8.2.
Polygonal curves, neutralization, derivation

In §
4.4
, we have used a key observation on the structure of the curves
\Gamma_{\lambda}

to compute their lengths: the fact that they can be viewed as a succession of perpendicular
geodesic segments of alternating lengths
L_{j}^{\lambda},\tau_{j}^{\lambda}
, turning the same direction
each time (see Figure
20
). We shall introduce a notion of polygonal curve, which
encapsulates this key property of
\Gamma_{\lambda}
. Let us first provide this definition without
reference to a hyperbolic metric. Subsequently in §
8.3
, a hyperbolic metric will be given,
and we will work with preferred “geodesic representatives”.

8.2.1.
Definition of polygonal curves

In the following, we fix a base surface
\mathbf{S}
and an oriented multi-curve
\beta
on

\mathbf{S}
.

Definition 8.1
.

A polygonal curve
\Gamma
(on the surface
{\mathbf{S}}
and based on

\beta
) is an oriented closed path without self-intersections, formed by a cyclic sequence

(J_{j},K_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{m}}
of oriented segments where, for all
j\in\mathbb{Z}_{m}
,

•

J_{j}
meets the multi-curve
\beta
at its endpoints and not in its interior, and is
homotopically non-trivial with gliding endpoints along
\beta
;

•

K_{j}
is a subsegment of
\beta
, possibly with reversed orientation;

•

J_{j}
arrives at
K_{j}
on the right at the point
t(J_{j})=o(K_{j})
, and
K_{j}
arrives at

J_{j+1}
on the right at the point
t(K_{j})=o(J_{j+1})
.

Note that the multi-curve
\beta
is essential to this definition. The segments
J_{j}
and
K_{j}

play different roles and will therefore be named differently.

Definition 8.2
.

The segments
K_{j}
are called the
cells
of the polygonal curve
\Gamma
, and the
J_{j}

its
bridges
. We denote

\Cell(\Gamma)=\{K_{j},j\in\mathbb{Z}_{m}\}
and
\Br(\Gamma)=\{{J}_{j},j\in\mathbb{Z}_{m}\}
.

For a cell
K=K_{j}\in\Cell(\Gamma)
, the bridges
adjacent to
K
refer to the bridges

J_{j}
and
J_{j+1}
. We respectively call these bridges the
bridge arriving on
K
and the

bridge coming out of
K
.

Notation 8.3
.

We call
m
the
combinatorial length
of
\Gamma
, denoted
|\Gamma|
.

By convention, for
m=0
, a polygonal curve of combinatorial length
0
is a simple curve that does
not intersect the family
\beta
(but may be homotopic to a component of
\beta
). In particular,
polygonal curves of combinatorial length
0
do not have any cells.

Example 8.4
.

We have seen in §
4.4.2
that each of the curves

(\Gamma_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
in the pair of pants decomposition defined in §
3.2
is a polygonal curve based on
\beta
, where:

•

the bridges
J_{j}
are among the bars
B_{q}
;

•

the cells
K_{j}
are among the segments
\mathcal{K}_{qq^{\prime}}
defined in §
4.3
.

The family
(\Gamma_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
will be the main example to which the whole
discussion applies. For
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}
, we can even say that the corresponding cells are
among the segments
(\mathcal{K}_{q})_{q\in\Theta}
.

Note that the components
(\beta_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda^{\beta}}
are polygonal curves of
combinatorial length
0
.

8.2.2.
Neutralizing a cell

Let
\Gamma=(J_{j},K_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{m}}
be a polygonal curve on
\mathbf{S}
of combinatorial length

m\geq 2
. Let
j\in\mathbb{Z}_{m}
. We may construct a new polygonal curve
\Gamma^{\prime}
by replacing the
sequence
(J_{j},K_{j},J_{j+1})
in
\Gamma
by one single bridge

\tilde{J}_{j}:=J_{j}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}K_{j}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}J_{j+1}
(or a smoothened version thereof). This
operation is represented in Figure
29(a)
. We observe that the derived
polygonal curve
\Gamma^{\prime}
we obtain is homotopic to
\Gamma
and that its combinatorial length is

m-1
.

(a)

One cell.

(b)

Several cells.

Figure 29
.
Neutralization of one or several cells.

Definition 8.5
.

The polygonal curve
\Gamma^{\prime}
is said to be
derived from
\Gamma
by neutralizing the
cell
K_{j}
. We call
\tilde{J}_{j}
the
roof over
K_{j}
. We denote

\tilde{K}_{j-1}:={K}_{j-1}
,
\tilde{K}_{j+1}:={K}_{j+1}
the
new cells of
\Gamma^{\prime}
.

The motivation for the name “new cells” will become apparent when we take geodesic
representatives. The new polygonal curve
\Gamma^{\prime}
satisfies

•

\Br(\Gamma^{\prime})=\{\tilde{J}_{j}\}\cup(\Br(\Gamma)\setminus\{J_{j},J_{j+1}\})
;

•

\Cell(\Gamma^{\prime})=\{\tilde{K}_{j-1},\tilde{K}_{j+1}\}\cup(\Cell(\Gamma)\setminus\{K_{j-1},K_{j},K_{j+1}\})
.

If now
\Gamma
is a polygonal curve of combinatorial length
1
, we define
\tilde{J}_{1}
as the
simple closed curve
J_{1}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}K_{1}
(or a smoothened verson thereof). Then

\Gamma^{\prime}=\tilde{J}_{1}
is a polygonal curve of combinatorial length
0
homotopic to
\Gamma
.

8.2.3.
Simultaneous neutralization of several cells.

We now consider an interval
I=\{a,\ldots,b\}
of
\mathbb{Z}_{m}
of length
1\leq\#I<m
. Let us
define

\tilde{J}_{I}:=J_{a}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}K_{a}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\ldots\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}K_{b}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}J_{b+1}

(or a smoothened version thereof) and call
\tilde{J}_{I}
the
roof over
(K_{j})_{j\in I}
. A
new polygonal curve
\Gamma^{\prime}
may be obtained by replacing the sequence

(J_{a},K_{a},\ldots,K_{b},J_{b+1})
by
\tilde{J}_{I}
, as represented in Figure

29(b)
. We then say that
\Gamma^{\prime}
is
derived from
\Gamma
by
neutralizing simultaneously all the
(K_{j})_{j\in I}
.

We denote
\tilde{K}_{a-1}:={K}_{a-1}
,
\tilde{K}_{b+1}:={K}_{b+1}
and call them
new
cells
of
\Gamma^{\prime}
. The new polygonal curve
\Gamma^{\prime}
then satisfies:

•

\Br(\Gamma^{\prime})=\{\tilde{J}_{I}\}\cup(\Br(\Gamma)\setminus\{J_{a},J_{a+1},\ldots,J_{b+1}\})
;

•

\Cell(\Gamma^{\prime})=\{\tilde{K}_{a-1},\tilde{K}_{b+1}\}\cup(\Cell(\Gamma)\setminus\{K_{a-1},K_{a},\ldots,K_{b+1}\})
.

We extend these definitions to the case
I=\mathbb{Z}_{m}
, now taking
\Gamma^{\prime}
to be a simple curve
freely homotopic to the concatenation

J_{1}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}K_{1}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\ldots\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}J_{m}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}K_{m}
, so that it does not
intersect
\beta
. Then,
\Gamma^{\prime}
is polygonal curve of combinatorial length
0
.

In all cases,
\Gamma^{\prime}
is homotopic to
\Gamma
, and its combinatorial length now is
m-\#I
. The
two notions of neutralization coincide when
I=\{j\}
, and the polygonal curve derived by
neutralizing simultanously all the
(K_{j})_{j\in I}
is the same as the one derived by successively
neutralizing the cells
K_{a},\ldots,K_{b}
(in whichever order).

8.3.
Geodesic representative and length inequalities

We now endow
{\mathbf{S}}
with a hyperbolic metric
Y\in\mathcal{T}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}^{*}
, and consider polygonal
curves as geometric objects.

8.3.1.
Geodesic representative

As suggested in Remark
2.8
, we can assume by choosing the right representative in
the Teichmüller space of
\mathbf{S}
that
\beta
is a multi-geodesic, which then implies that
the cells
K_{j}
are geodesic segments. We replace each bridge
J_{j}
by its orthogeodesic
representative
\bar{J}_{j}
. As the segments
J_{j}
glide to their orthogeodesic representatives

\bar{J}_{j}
, the segments
K_{j}
glide to new segments
\bar{K}_{j}
whose endpoints match those of the
family
\bar{J}_{j}
. We call
(\bar{J}_{j},\bar{K}_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{m}}
the
piecewise geodesic
representative
of
\Gamma
. This allows us to define the maps

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}^{*}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{>0}

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}^{*}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{>0}

\displaystyle Y

\displaystyle\mapsto\ell_{Y}(J_{j}):=\ell(\bar{J}_{j})

\displaystyle Y

\displaystyle\mapsto\ell_{Y}(K_{j}):=\ell(\bar{K}_{j}).

Note that now
\bar{J}_{j}
and
\bar{K}_{j}
form a right angle at their meeting point, and so do

\bar{K}_{j}
and
\bar{J}_{j+1}
.

In the following, unless specified otherwise, we always take geodesic representatives of
polygonal curves, and we suppress all the bars
\bar{.}
from the notation.

We notice that the discussion in §
4.4
is valid for any polygonal curve, not only the
family
(\Gamma_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
of boundary components of our pair of pants
decomposition. In particular, if we introduce the short-hand notations

(8.2)

L_{j}:=\ell_{Y}(J_{j})\quad\text{and}\quad\tau_{j}:=\ell_{Y}(K_{j})

then we can apply Proposition

4.11
to any polygonal curve
\Gamma
, and obtain that
\ell_{Y}(\Gamma)=Q_{m}(\vec{L},\vec{\tau})
for the length-vectors
\vec{L}=(L_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{m}}
and

\vec{\tau}=(\tau_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{m}}
.

8.3.2.
Neutralization and lengths

Let us now examine the geometric impact of neutralizing cells in a polygonal curve
\Gamma
.

First, we observe that if
\Gamma^{\prime}
is obtained by neutralization of a cell
K_{j}
in a polygonal
curve
\Gamma
, then, due to taking geodesic representatives, the new cell
\tilde{K}_{j-1}
is
strictly included in
{K}_{j-1}
, and the new cell
\tilde{K}_{j+1}
in
{K}_{j+1}
(see Figure

30
). This justifies the name “new cells” introduced in
Definition
8.5
. This is also the case when neutralizing several cells simultanously.

We extend the notation (
8.2
) to the definitions introduced in §
8.2.2
and
8.2.3
related to new cells and bridges, so that:

•

\tilde{L}_{j}
,
\tilde{\tau}_{j-1}
and
\tilde{\tau}_{j+1}
respectively denote the lengths of
the new bridge
\tilde{J}_{j}
and the new cells
\tilde{K}_{j-1}
and
\tilde{K}_{j+1}
obtained
when we neutralize the cell
K_{j}
;

•

\tilde{L}_{I}
,
\tilde{\tau}_{a-1}
and
\tilde{\tau}_{b+1}
respectively denote the lengths of
the new bridge
\tilde{J}_{I}
and the new cells
\tilde{K}_{a-1}
and
\tilde{K}_{b+1}
obtained
when we neutralize simultanously
(K_{j})_{j\in I}
for
I=\{a,\ldots,b\}
.

(a)

One cell.

(b)

Several cells.

Figure 30
.
Geodesic representative of a polygonal curve and of a neutralization.

Importantly, if
\Gamma^{\prime}
is a polygonal curve derived from
\Gamma
, because
\Gamma^{\prime}
is homotopic
to
\Gamma
, we have

Q_{|\Gamma|}(\vec{L},\vec{\tau})=\ell_{Y}(\Gamma)=\ell_{Y}(\Gamma^{\prime})=Q_{|\Gamma^{\prime}|}(\vec{L}^{\prime},\vec{\tau}^{\prime})

where
\vec{L}^{\prime},\vec{\tau}^{\prime}
are the length parameters of
\Gamma^{\prime}
. As a consequence, we obtain
multiple distinct rewritings of the length of the same closed geodesic, depending on which
polygonal curve we use,
\Gamma
, or any derived polygonal curve
\Gamma^{\prime}
. Our goal in the
following will be to neutralize enough cells so that the expression of
the length is well-behaved and can be easily understood.

We make the following observation.

Lemma 8.6
.

Let
\Gamma
be a polygonal curve of combinatorial length
m
, and
I=\{a,\ldots,b\}
be an
interval of
\mathbb{Z}_{m}
. Then,

(8.3)

\begin{split}\tilde{L}_{I}&=\mathrm{fn}(L_{a},\ldots,L_{b+1},\tau_{a},\ldots,\tau_{b})\\
\tilde{\tau}_{a-1}&=\tau_{a-1}-\mathrm{fn}(L_{a},\ldots,L_{b+1},\tau_{a},\ldots,\tau_{b})\\
\tilde{\tau}_{b+1}&=\tau_{b+1}-\mathrm{fn}(L_{a},\ldots,L_{b+1},\tau_{a},\ldots,\tau_{b}).\end{split}

This is a straightforward consequence of applying the following elementary lemma to the highlighted
right-angled polygon in Figure
30
.

Lemma 8.7
.

Let
m\geq 3
.
In any right-angled convex polygon
(J_{j},K_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{m}}
, any three consecutive lengths can be
written as functions of the
2m-3
others:

(\ell(K_{j}),\ell(J_{j}),\ell(K_{j+1}))=\mathrm{fn}\big((\ell(J_{k}))_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{m}\setminus\{j\}},(\ell(K_{k}))_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{m}\setminus\{j,j+1\}}\big).

Proof of Lemma
8.7
.

When
m=3
, this is a direct consequence of the basic formulas for convex right-angled hexagons (see
e.g.
[
5
, Theorem 2.4.1 (i-ii)]
). For
m>3
, we obtain the result by induction,
introducing a right-angled hexagon at each step.
∎

8.4.
Favorable regions for studying the length of a polygonal curve

We are now ready to study the variations of the function

(8.4)

\displaystyle F_{m}(\vec{L},\vec{\tau})=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\alpha\in\{0,1\}^{m}}(-1)^{|\alpha|}\hyp_{-\partial\alpha}\left(\frac{\vec{L}}{2}\right)e^{-\alpha\cdot\vec{\tau}}.

We observe that the first term, corresponding to
\alpha=(0,\ldots,0)
, has the coefficient

\hyp_{-1}\left(\frac{\vec{L}}{2}\right)=\prod_{j=1}^{m}\sinh\left(\frac{L_{j}}{2}\right).

We shall identify a “favourable” region of the Teichmüller space where this term dominates all
others, so that
\cosh(\ell_{Y}(\Gamma)/2)
is comparable to

\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\tau_{j})\right)\prod_{j=1}^{m}\sinh(L_{j}/2)
.

8.4.1.
Height of a cell

First, we introduce a geometric quantity associated to each cell, called its
height
. We will
see in §
8.4.2
how this is the relevant quantity one needs to introduce to describe the
variations of the function
F_{m}
.

Definition 8.8
.

Let
\Gamma
be a polygonal curve of combinatorial length
m
, and
j\in\mathbb{Z}_{m}
. The
height
of the cell
K_{j}
is defined as

(8.5)

Z^{\Gamma}_{K_{j}}:=\tanh(L_{j})\tanh(L_{j+1})\,e^{\tau_{j}}.

We also use the short-hand notation
Z_{j}:=Z^{\Gamma}_{K_{j}}
, when convenient.

Note that, if
m=1
, the height is defined as
Z_{1}=\tanh^{2}(L_{1})e^{\tau_{1}}
. If
m=0
, the polygonal
curve has no cell, so there is no notion of height.

We prove the following bounds on the height
Z_{j}
of a cell.

Lemma 8.9
.

For
m\geq 2
,
1\leq Z_{j}\leq e^{\tau_{j}}
and upon neutralizing the cell
K_{j}
, we have

(8.6)

\tilde{L}_{j}\leq\log Z_{j}+L_{j}+L_{j+1}.

Proof.

The upper bound
Z_{j}\leq e^{\tau_{j}}
is a direct consequence of the definition. The two other
bounds can be obtained from the trigonometric formula for right-angled hexagons, see
e.g.
[
5
, Theorem 2.4.1 (i)]
, which yield

(8.7)

\displaystyle\cosh(\tilde{L}_{j})=\sinh(L_{j})\sinh(L_{j+1})\cosh(\tau_{j})-\cosh(L_{j})\cosh(L_{j+1}).

Indeed,
\cosh(\tilde{L}_{j})\geq 0
together with (
8.7
) imply that

\displaystyle\tanh(L_{j})\tanh(L_{j+1})\cosh(\tau_{j})\geq 1

and hence
Z_{j}\geq 1
. Then, we rewrite (
8.7
) as

(\cosh(\tau_{j})-1)\sinh(L_{j})\sinh(L_{j+1})=\cosh(\tilde{L}_{j})+\cosh(L_{j}-L_{j+1})\geq\cosh(\tilde{L}_{j}).

Since
\cosh(\tau_{j})-1=2\sinh^{2}\left(\frac{\tau_{j}}{2}\right)\leq e^{\tau_{j}}/2
, this implies that

2\cosh(\tilde{L}_{j})\leq e^{\tau_{j}}\sinh(L_{j})\sinh(L_{j+1})=Z_{j}\cosh(L_{j})\cosh(L_{j+1})

which yields the result since
e^{x}/2\leq\cosh(x)\leq e^{x}
.
∎

We notice that (
8.6
) means that the height
Z_{j}
of a cell
K_{j}
can be used to bound
the length
\tilde{L}_{j}
of the roof over it, under the additional assumption that the lengths of
the adjacent bridges are bounded. More generally, the height of the cell
K_{j}
can be used to bound
the length of the orthogeodesic
W_{j}
constructed below and represented in Figure
31
.

Definition 8.10
.

For
j\in\mathbb{Z}_{m}
, we define the orthogeodesic
W_{j}
by:

•

W_{j}=K_{j}
if
L_{j}\geq 1
and
L_{j+1}\geq 1
;

•

W_{j}
is the orthogeodesic between
K_{j-1}
and
J_{j+1}
if
L_{j}<1
and
L_{j+1}\geq 1
;

•

W_{j}
is the orthogeodesic between
J_{j}
and
K_{j+1}
if
L_{j}\geq 1
and
L_{j+1}<1
;

•

W_{j}=\tilde{J}_{j}
otherwise, i.e. if
L_{j}<1
and
L_{j+1}<1
.

(a)

L_{j},L_{j+1}\geq 1
.

(b)

L_{j}<1
and
L_{j+1}\geq 1
.

(c)

L_{j}<1
and
L_{j+1}<1
.

Figure 31
.
The orthogeodesic
W_{j}
depending on the values of
L_{j}
and
L_{j+1}
.

We then have the following bound.

Lemma 8.11
.

For any
j
,
\ell(W_{j})\leq\log(Z_{j})+2
.

Proof.

If both
L_{j}
and
L_{j+1}
are smaller than one, then this follows from
(
8.6
). When both
L_{j}
and
L_{j+1}
are greater or equal to
1
, it is a
straightforward consequence of the definition and the fact that
\log(1/\tanh(1))<1
. In the
last cases, say
L_{j}<1
and
L_{j+1}\geq 1
, we use the hyperbolic formulas for right-angles
pentagons (see
[
5
, Theorem 2.3.4 (i)]
) to write

\cosh\ell(W_{j})=\sinh L_{j}\sinh\tau_{j}
, which once again yields the claim since

\log(2/\sinh(1))<1
.
∎

8.4.2.
Favourable regions and estimates on
F_{m}

We are ready to use our notion of height of a cell to identify a condition upon which the
leading term of
F_{m}
is the term
\alpha=(0,\ldots,0)
.

Lemma 8.12
.

For any polygonal curve
\Gamma
of combinatorial length
m\geq 1
, any
Y\in\mathcal{T}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}^{*}
, any multi-index
\alpha\in\{0,1\}^{m}
distinct from
(0,\ldots,0)
,

(8.8)

\displaystyle\frac{\hyp_{-\partial\alpha}\left(\frac{\vec{L}}{2}\right)}{\hyp_{-1}\left(\frac{\vec{L}}{2}\right)}e^{-\alpha\cdot\vec{\tau}}\leq\prod_{j\in\alpha}\frac{4}{Z_{K_{j}}^{\Gamma}}.

In particular, if all the
Z_{j}
go to
+\infty
, then for all
\alpha\not=(0,\ldots,0)
,

\frac{\hyp_{-\partial\alpha}\left(\frac{\vec{L}}{2}\right)}{\hyp_{-1}\left(\frac{\vec{L}}{2}\right)}e^{-\alpha\cdot\vec{\tau}}=o(1).

We can then deduce that
F_{m}(\vec{L},\vec{\tau})\sim\hyp_{-1}(\vec{L}/2)
and

\cosh(\ell_{Y}(\Gamma)/2)\sim\hyp_{-1}\left(\frac{\vec{L}}{2}\right)\exp(\frac{1}{2}(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\tau_{j}))
, as
quantified in the following statement.

Corollary 8.13
.

Let
\Gamma
be a polygonal curve of combinatorial length
m\geq 1
. For a real number

a\geq(m+3)\log 2
, if we assume

\forall j\in\mathbb{Z}_{m},\quad Z_{K_{j}}^{\Gamma}\geq e^{a}

then we have

(8.9)

\displaystyle F_{m}(\vec{L},\vec{\tau})\geq\frac{1}{4}\hyp_{-1}\left(\frac{\vec{L}}{2}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\cosh\left(\frac{\ell_{Y}(\Gamma)}{2}\right)\geq\frac{e^{ma/2}}{2^{m+2}}.

This motivates the following definitions.

Notation 8.14
.

In the following, we let
a=a(r)=\max((2r+3)\log(2),2\log(8\cosh(3/2)))
, where, recall,

r
is the number of intersections of our generalized eight. We also fix two smooth functions

\chi_{\Gamma,0},\chi_{\Gamma,\infty}
on
\mathbb{R}
, with the property that
\chi_{\Gamma,0}
is supported in

[-e^{a+1},e^{a+1}]
,
\chi_{\Gamma,0}\equiv 1
on
[-e^{a},e^{a}]
, and

\chi_{\Gamma,0}+\chi_{\Gamma,\infty}\equiv 1
.

Definition 8.15
.

Let
\Gamma
be a polygonal curve of combinatorial length
m\leq 2r
. We call
\Gamma
-
favourable region
the region of Teichmüller space

\{Y\in\mathcal{T}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}^{*}:\quad\forall j\in\mathbb{Z}_{m},Z_{K_{j}}^{\Gamma}>e^{a}\}.

Remark 8.16
.

In particular, using Corollary
8.13
and the fact that
e^{a/2}\geq 8\cosh 3/2
, we
obtain that, for any polygonal curve
\Gamma
, if
Y
is in the
\Gamma
-favorable region,
then
\ell_{Y}(\Gamma)\geq 3
.

Proof of Lemma
8.12
.

Let
m\geq 2
. For
\alpha\in\{0,1\}^{m}
such that
\alpha\neq(0,\ldots,0)
, let us write

\{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{m}:\alpha_{j}=1\}
as a disjoint union of intervals,

\displaystyle\{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{m}:\alpha_{j}=1\}=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{l}\{k_{i},\ldots,l_{i}\},

so that
\alpha_{j}=1
for
k_{i}\leq j\leq l_{i}
but
\alpha_{k_{i}-1}=0=\alpha_{l_{i}+1}
. Then, by
definition,
(\delta\alpha)_{j}=-1
when
j=k_{i}
or
l_{i}+1
and
+1
otherwise.
It follows that

\displaystyle\frac{\hyp_{-\partial\alpha}\left(\frac{\vec{L}}{2}\right)}{\hyp_{-1}\left(\frac{\vec{\vec{L}}}{2}\right)}\,e^{-\alpha\cdot\tau}

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{l}\coth\left(\frac{L_{k_{i}}}{2}\right)\coth\left(\frac{L_{l_{i}+1}}{2}\right)\prod_{k_{i}\leq j\leq l_{i}}e^{-\tau_{j}}.

We note that, for all
x\geq 0
, we have
\coth(x/2)\leq 2\coth(x)
. Therefore,

\frac{\hyp_{-\partial\alpha}\left(\frac{\vec{L}}{2}\right)}{\hyp_{-1}\left(\frac{\vec{L}}{2}\right)}\,e^{-\alpha\cdot\tau}\leq\prod_{i=1}^{l}4\coth(L_{k_{i}})\coth(L_{l_{i}+1})\prod_{k_{i}\leq j\leq l_{i}}e^{-\tau_{j}}.

We then inject
1\leq\prod_{k_{i}<j\leq l_{i}}4\coth^{2}(L_{j})
in
this equation, and obtain

\frac{\hyp_{-\partial\alpha}\left(\frac{\vec{L}}{2}\right)}{\hyp_{-1}\left(\frac{\vec{L}}{2}\right)}\,e^{-\alpha\cdot\tau}\leq\prod_{i=1}^{l}\prod_{k_{i}\leq j\leq l_{i}}4\coth(L_{j})\coth(L_{j+1})e^{-\tau_{j}}=\prod_{j\in\alpha}\frac{4}{Z_{j}}

which is our claim.
∎

Proof of Corollary
8.13
.

First we observe that, by hypothesis, for any
j
,
4/Z_{j}\leq 4e^{-a}\leq 2^{-m-1}
which is
smaller than
1
. As a consequence, for any
\alpha\neq(0,\ldots,0)
, we have

\prod_{j\in\alpha}4/Z_{j}\leq 2^{-m-1}
. To then deduce the
bound (
8.9
), we write the triangle inequality:

\displaystyle F_{m}(\vec{L},\vec{\tau})\geq\frac{1}{2}\hyp_{-1}\left(\frac{\vec{L}}{2}\right)\Big[1-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\{0,1\}^{m}\\
\alpha\not=(0,\ldots,0)\end{subarray}}\frac{\hyp_{-\partial\alpha}\left(\frac{\vec{L}}{2}\right)}{\hyp_{-1}\left(\frac{L}{2}\right)}e^{-\alpha\cdot\tau}\Big]\geq\frac{1}{4}\hyp_{-1}\left(\frac{\vec{L}}{2}\right)

because the sum above is bounded above by

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\{0,1\}^{m}\end{subarray}}2^{-m-1}=1/2
.
There only remains to prove the last claim, which can be obtained by writing

\displaystyle\cosh\left(\frac{\ell_{Y}(\Gamma)}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=e^{\sum_{j=1}^{m}\tau_{j}/2}F_{m}(\vec{L},\vec{\tau})\geq\frac{1}{4}e^{\sum_{j=1}^{m}\tau_{j}/2}\prod_{j=1}^{m}\sinh\left(\frac{L_{j}}{2}\right)

thanks to the bound on
F_{m}
. Then, since
\sinh(x/2)\geq\tanh(x)/2
for all
x\geq 0
,

\cosh\left(\frac{\ell_{Y}(\Gamma)}{2}\right)\geq\frac{1}{2^{m+2}}e^{\sum_{j=1}^{m}\tau_{j}/2}\prod_{j=1}^{m}\tanh(L_{j})=\frac{1}{2^{m+2}}\prod_{j=1}^{m}Z_{j}^{1/2}\geq\frac{e^{ma/2}}{2^{m+2}}.

∎

8.4.3.
Derivatives of
F_{m}

Let us now estimate the logarithmic derivatives of the function
F_{m}
within the favorable region.

Proposition 8.17
.

Let
\Gamma=(J_{j},K_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{m}}
be a polygonal curve of
combinatorial length
m\geq 1
. If the metric
Y
is in the
\Gamma
-favourable region, then for
any subset
\pi\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{m}
,

\displaystyle\Big|\prod_{j\in\pi}\frac{\partial}{\partial\tau_{j}}\big(\log F_{m}\big)(\vec{L},\vec{\tau})\Big|\leq\mathrm{fn}(m)\,\prod_{j\in\pi}\frac{1}{Z_{j}}.

Proof.

Let
k=\#\pi
. The partial derivative
\partial^{\pi}\log F_{m}
is a linear combination (with
universal coefficients depending only on
k
) of fractions of the form

\prod_{j=1}^{k}(\partial^{\pi_{j}}F_{m}/F_{m})
where the non-empty subsets
\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{k}
of

\mathbb{Z}_{m}
form a partition of
\pi
. We then write for each
j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{\pi_{j}}F_{m}}{F_{m}}=\sum_{\alpha\in\{0,1\}^{m}}(-1)^{|\alpha|}\frac{\hyp_{-\partial\alpha}\left(\frac{\vec{L}}{2}\right)\partial^{\pi_{j}}(e^{-\alpha\cdot\vec{\tau}})}{F_{m}}.

To bound the denominator, we use (
8.9
), which states that

F_{m}(\vec{L},\vec{\tau})\geq\frac{1}{4}\hyp_{-1}(\vec{L}/2)
