. For the numerator, we note that

\partial^{\pi_{j}}(e^{-\alpha\cdot\vec{\tau}})=0
unless
\alpha_{i}=1
for all
i\in\pi_{j}
, and in the
latter case we have

\partial^{\pi_{j}}(e^{-\alpha\cdot\vec{\tau}})=(-1)^{\#\pi_{j}}e^{-\alpha\cdot\vec{\tau}}
. In particular, the
term
\alpha=(0,\ldots,0)
does not contribute to the sum. Then,

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\partial^{\pi_{j}}F_{m}}{F_{m}}\right|\leq 4\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\{0,1\}^{m}\\
\alpha\neq(0,\ldots,0)\end{subarray}}\frac{\hyp_{-\partial\alpha}\left(\frac{\vec{L}}{2}\right)e^{-\alpha\cdot\vec{\tau}}}{\hyp_{-1}\left(\frac{\vec{L}}{2}\right)}\leq 2^{3m+2}\prod_{i\in\pi_{j}}\frac{1}{Z_{i}}

by Lemma
8.12
, using the fact that there are at most
2^{m}
terms in the sum.
Taking the product over
j=1,\ldots,k
yields

\prod_{j=1}^{k}\left|\frac{\partial^{\pi_{j}}F_{m}}{F_{m}}\right|\leq 2^{(3m+2)k}\prod_{i\in\pi}\frac{1}{Z_{i}}.

Summing over all partitions

\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{k}
of
\pi
, and noticing that
k\leq m
, yields the announced bound.
∎

8.4.4.
Decoration for one polygonal curve

We recall that our aim in this section is to provide a well-described formula for the length of a
polygonal curve
\Gamma
. Proposition
8.17
tells us that this is achieved as soon as our
metric
Y
lies in the
\Gamma
-favorable region. If this is not the case, then we shall replace

\Gamma
by a derived polygonal curve
\Gamma^{\prime}
obtained by neutralizing a cell
K
such that

Z_{K}^{\Gamma}\leq e^{a}
, and hope that
Y
now falls in the
\Gamma^{\prime}
-favourable region. Since
the combinatorial length strictly decreases under derivation, we can iterate this process finitely
many times until we reach a favourable region for a polygonal curve
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
derived from

\Gamma
. Since
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
is homotopic to
\Gamma
, this yields a well-behaved formula for the
length
\ell_{Y}(\Gamma)
in terms of the lengths of the cells and bridges of
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
.

The
decorations
, defined below, will serve to enumerate all possible cases in this recursive
process. Later on (§
8.6
), decorations will be used to construct a partition of unity
on the Teichmüller space: a decoration
\infty
on a cell
K
will mean that we consider the
region of Teichmüller space where the height
Z^{\Gamma}_{K}
is high, and on the opposite the
decoration
0
will mean that we consider the region where the height
Z^{\Gamma}_{K}
is low.

Let us provide the general definition of a decoration for a polygonal curve
\Gamma
.

Definition 8.18
.

A decorated polygonal curve
(\Gamma,d)
is a polygonal curve
\Gamma
of combinatorial length

|\Gamma|\geq 1
, together with a map
d:\Cell(\Gamma)\longrightarrow\{0,\infty\}
. A cell

K\in\Cell(\Gamma)
is called
favourable
if
d(K)=\infty
and
unfavourable
if

d(K)=0
. A decorated polygonal curve
(\Gamma,d)
is called
favourable
if all cells are
favourable.

A decorated polygonal curve
(\Gamma^{\prime},d^{\prime})
is said to be
derived from

(\Gamma,d)
if

\Gamma^{\prime}
is derived from
\Gamma
by neutralizing a cell
K\in\Cell(\Gamma)
such that

d(K)=0
. In addition, we ask that the map
d^{\prime}
coincides with
d
on

\Cell(\Gamma^{\prime})\cap\Cell(\Gamma)
.

The following definition allows us to consider the iterative derivation process described above.

Definition 8.19
.

Let
\Gamma
be a polygonal curve.
An
exhaustion
for
\Gamma
is a sequence of decorated polygonal curves
(\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d})=(\Gamma^{k},d^{k})_{1\leq k\leq t}
, such that

•

\Gamma^{1}=\Gamma
;

•

for every
k<t
,
(\Gamma^{k},d^{k})
is not favourable, and
(\Gamma^{k+1},d^{k+1})
is
derived from
(\Gamma^{k},d^{k})
;

•

for
k=t
,
(\Gamma^{t},d^{t})
is either favourable or of combinatorial length
=0
.

Remark 8.20
.

In particular, any polygonal curve of combinatorial length
0
is left untouched by the exhaustion
process. It follows that, if we apply this process to a polygonal curve
\beta_{\lambda}
with

\lambda\in\Lambda^{\beta}
(i.e. a component of
\beta
), then nothing happens.

Notation 8.21
.

For an exhaustion
(\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d})=(\Gamma^{k},d^{k})_{1\leq k\leq t}
of a
polygonal curve
\Gamma
, we denote as
(\Gamma^{\infty},d^{\infty})=(\Gamma^{t},d^{t})
the decorated
polygonal curve at the terminal step of the exhaustion.

8.5.
Families of polygonal curves and simultanous decorations

The integral (
5.13
), our main focus, simultanously involves all the lengths

(\ell_{Y}(\Gamma_{\lambda}))_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
through the function
{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-4.0mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-4.5mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-5.0mu{\rm{l}}}}_{\mathfrak{D}}
. We will
therefore need to consider simultaneously all the polygonal curves

(\Gamma_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
. This motivates the need to study families of polygonal
curves, which we do in this section.

In the following, our families are indexed by our set
\Lambda
introduced in §
3.3
to index our pair of pants decomposition, but the precise structure of

\Lambda
does not matter. The one thing that is important is that we have ordered the set
\Lambda

with the order relation
\lambda_{1}<\lambda_{2}
if
\step(\lambda_{1})<\step(\lambda_{2})
, where

\step(\lambda)
is the step of the construction of the pair of pants decomposition at which

\Gamma_{\lambda}
appears. The considerations below can be applied to any partially ordered set

\Lambda
.

8.5.1.
Compatibility of polygonal curves

When considering families of polygonal curves, we will need to understand how the different
polygonal curves in our family intersect one another. Without any hypothesis, two polygonal curves
can have quite wild intersections and combinatorics. The following notion of compatibility will be
key to restrict the possible behaviours and describe our families of polygonal curves.

Definition 8.22

(Compatibility of two polygonal curves)
.

Let
\Gamma_{1}
,
\Gamma_{2}
be two polygonal curves based on
\beta
. We say that
\Gamma_{1}
and

\Gamma_{2}
are
compatible
if:

•

if two cells
K^{1}\in\Cell(\Gamma_{1})
,
K^{2}\in\Cell(\Gamma_{2})
lie in the same component of

\beta

and have the same orientation
, then they are either disjoint or one is included in
the other;

•

two bridges
J^{1}\in\Br(\Gamma_{1})
,
J^{2}\in\Br(\Gamma_{2})
are either equal or
disjoint (in particular, they cannot have an endpoint in common unless they are equal).

As a consequence of our definition of polygonal curves, for any cells
K^{1},K^{2}
within one
component
\beta
and oriented the same way, the bridges adjacent to
K^{1}
and
K^{2}
all lie on
the same side of that component of
\beta
.
The compatibility condition then implies that any two compatible polygonal curves
\Gamma_{1}
and

\Gamma_{2}
are homotopic to simple curves
not intersecting one another
.

Definition 8.23

(Inclusion of cells)
.

Let
\Gamma_{1}
,
\Gamma_{2}
be two compatible polygonal curves. Let
K^{1}\in\Cell(\Gamma_{1})
and

K^{2}\in\Cell(\Gamma_{2})
be two cells of
\Gamma_{1}
,
\Gamma_{2}
respectively. We say that the
cell
K^{1}
is
included
in the cell
K^{2}
, and write
K^{1}\subseteq K^{2}
, if
K^{1}
is a subset
of
K^{2}
and their orientations are the same. We will say that the two cells
coincide
, and
write
K^{1}=K^{2}
, if
K^{1}\subseteq K^{2}
and
K^{2}\subseteq K^{1}
. In this case, we say that
\Gamma_{1}

and
\Gamma_{2}
have
a cell in common
. We say that
K^{1}
is strictly included in
K^{2}
, and
write
K^{1}\subsetneq K^{2}
, if
K^{1}\subseteq K^{2}
and
K_{1}\neq K_{2}
.

Because of the second item in Definition
8.22
, if
K^{1}=K^{2}
, then the bridges adjacent
to this common cell are equal.

Remark 8.24
.

The reason we insist on only comparing cells with the same orientation is that we want
the notion of inclusion to be purely topological, that is, independent on the choice of a
hyperbolic metric. If
K^{1}\subseteq K^{2}
for topological polygonal curves
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}
,
then, by the Useful Remark
2.11
, we have that the geodesic representatives
\bar{K}^{1}

and
\bar{K}^{2}
satisfy
\bar{K}^{1}\subseteq\bar{K}^{2}
in the sense of the inclusion of sets.

Definition 8.25
.

We say a family of polygonal
curves
(\Gamma_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
form a
compatible family
if

•

these polygonal curves are pairwise compatible;

•

for
K^{1}\in\Cell(\Gamma_{\lambda_{1}})
and
K^{2}\in\Cell(\Gamma_{\lambda_{2}})
, if

K^{1}\subsetneq K^{2}
, then
\lambda_{2}<\lambda_{1}
.

Example 8.26
.

By construction, the family
(\Gamma_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
of boundary components of our
pair of pants decomposition is a compatible family of polygonal curves.

Notation 8.27
.

For
\Gamma=(\Gamma_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
a compatible family of polygonal curves, we
let

\Cell(\Gamma)=\bigcup_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\Cell(\Gamma_{\lambda}),

where the union runs over
the
\lambda\in\Lambda
such that
|\Gamma_{\lambda}|\geq 1
. For
K\in\Cell(\Gamma)
, if

\lambda\in\Lambda
is such that
K\in\Cell(\Gamma_{\lambda})
, then we denote by
\tilde{J}(K)

the roof over
K
in
\Gamma_{\lambda}
and
Z_{K}^{\Gamma}:=Z_{K}^{\Gamma_{\lambda}}
the height of
K
.
We call
complexity
of
\Gamma
, and denote
|\Gamma|
, its number of cells.

Thanks to the compatibility condition,
\tilde{J}(K)
and
Z_{K}^{\Gamma}
do not depend on the choice of a

\lambda\in\Lambda
for which
K\in\Cell(\Gamma_{\lambda})
. The following property will allow us to
compare heights within a compatible family. It says that the height is (almost) increasing for the
inclusion relation.

Proposition 8.28
.

Consider a compatible family
\Gamma=(\Gamma_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
. Let

K,K^{\prime}\in\Cell(\Gamma)
, such that
K^{\prime}\subseteq K
. Then

(8.10)

\displaystyle Z_{K}^{\Gamma}\geq e^{-2}Z_{K^{\prime}}^{\Gamma}.

Proof.

Let us lift the polygonal curve to the hyperbolic plane. Let
K
and
K^{\prime}
denote two lifts of our
cells, such that
K^{\prime}\subseteq K
. Let
J_{-}
,
J_{+}
denote lifts of the bridges arriving on
K
and
coming out of
K
, and similarly
J_{-}^{\prime}
,
J_{+}^{\prime}
for
K^{\prime}
. By definition of the inclusion of cells,
all of those bridges lie on the same side of
K
. We denote as
L_{\pm}
and
L_{\pm}^{\prime}
the lengths of
these bridges, and
\tau
,
\tau^{\prime}
the lengths of the cells
K
,
K^{\prime}
.

Let
t_{\pm}
denote the distance between the origins of
J_{\pm}
and
J_{\pm}^{\prime}
along
K
. We shall
only prove the property when
t_{+},t_{-}>0
, i.e. the bridges
J_{\pm}
and
J_{\pm}^{\prime}
are disjoint –
the proof is simpler when one or both of these lengths is equal to
0
.

Let
\tilde{\beta}_{+}
,
\tilde{\beta}_{+}^{\prime}
denote the lifts of the multicurve
\beta
on which the
bridges
J_{+}
,
J_{+}^{\prime}
terminate. Since there is no right-angled quadrilateral in
\mathbb{H}
, these two
lifts must differ. Then, completing the picture with the orthogeodesic between
\beta_{+}
and

\beta_{+}^{\prime}
, we obtain a convex right-angled hexagon, see Figure
32
.

Figure 32
.
Illustration of the proof of Proposition
8.28
.

In a convex right-angled hexagon, the lengths
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}
of any two consecutive edges must
satisfy
\sinh(\ell_{1})\sinh(\ell_{2})\geq 1
. Indeed, one can cut the hexagon along one of its
orthogeodesics to obtain a right-angled pentagon with two consecutive edges of lengths

\ell_{1},\ell_{2}
, which then must satisfy the aforementioned inequality by
[
5
, Therorem
2.3.4(i)]
.

It then follows that
\sinh(t_{+})\sinh(L_{+})\geq 1
. If
L_{+}\leq 1
, then

\sinh(L_{+})\geq\tanh(L_{+})/e
and we hence obtain

(8.11)

e^{t_{+}}\tanh(L_{+})\geq 1/e.

Otherwise,
e^{t_{+}}\geq 1
and
\tanh(L_{+})\geq\tanh(1)\geq 1/e
, so the inequality
(
8.11
) also holds. By symmetry, (
8.11
) remains
true replacing all
+
by
-
.

Then, we write
\tau=\tau^{\prime}+t_{+}+t_{-}
, and hence

\displaystyle Z_{K}^{\Gamma}=\tanh(L_{+})\tanh(L_{-})\,e^{\tau^{\prime}+t_{+}+t_{-}}\geq\frac{e^{\tau^{\prime}}}{e^{2}}\geq e^{-2}\tanh(L_{+}^{\prime})\tanh(L_{-}^{\prime})e^{\tau^{\prime}}=e^{-2}Z_{K^{\prime}}^{\Gamma}

which was our claim.
∎

8.5.2.
Neutralization in a compatible family

In §
8.2.2
, we defined a notion of neutralization of a cell
K
for a single
polygonal curve. Now, we define and discuss the notion of neutralization of a cell
K
in a
compatible family of polygonal curves
\Gamma=(\Gamma_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
.

Definition 8.29
.

Let
\Gamma=(\Gamma_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
be a family of polygonal curves. Let

K\in\Cell(\Gamma)
.

For every
\lambda\in\Lambda
, we let
\Gamma^{\prime}_{\lambda}
be the polygonal curve derived from

\Gamma_{\lambda}
by simultaneously neutralizing all cells
K^{\prime}\in\Cell(\Gamma_{\lambda})
such that

K^{\prime}\subseteq K
. We say that the family
\Gamma^{\prime}=(\Gamma^{\prime}_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
is
derived
from
\Gamma
by neutralizing
K
.

We show, using the Useful Remark, that the notion of compatibility is stable by derivation.

Proposition 8.30
.

If
\Gamma
is a compatible family and
\Gamma^{\prime}
is derived from

\Gamma
by neutralizing a cell, then
\Gamma^{\prime}
is a compatible family.

Proof.

Let
\tilde{K}^{1},\tilde{K}^{2}\in\Cell(\Gamma^{\prime})
be two cells with non-empty intersection and oriented
the same way. We wish to prove that either
\tilde{K}^{1}\subseteq\tilde{K}^{2}
or

\tilde{K}^{2}\subseteq\tilde{K}^{1}
, and that the bridges adjacent to these cells are equal or disjoint.

For
i=1,2
, let
\lambda_{i}\in\Lambda
denote an index for which

\tilde{K}^{i}\in\Gamma^{\prime}_{\lambda_{i}}
. By definition,
\Gamma^{\prime}_{\lambda_{i}}
is derived from
\Gamma_{\lambda_{i}}

by neutralizing some cells, and therefore the cell
\tilde{K}^{i}
is contained in a cell
K^{i}
of

\Gamma_{\lambda_{i}}
.

By the compatibility condition, two lifts of
\Gamma_{\lambda_{1}}
and
\Gamma_{\lambda_{2}}
to
\mathbb{H}^{2}
cannot
intersect transversally. Consider two such lifts, say
(J_{j}^{1},K_{j}^{1})_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}
and

(J^{2}_{j},K^{2}_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}
, chosen so that
K_{0}^{1}
and
K_{0}^{2}
are respective lifts of
K^{1}
and

K^{2}
and
K_{0}^{1}\cap K_{0}^{2}\neq\emptyset
.

Let us consider the index

m=\inf\{j\geq 0:t(K^{1}_{j})\not=t(K^{2}_{j})\},

i.e. the first place where the two polygonal curves
\Gamma_{\lambda_{1}}
and
\Gamma_{\lambda_{2}}
start
disagreeing after meeting along
K_{0}^{1}
and
K_{0}^{2}
(see Figure
33
).
First, we observe that if
m=+\infty
, then
\Gamma_{\lambda_{1}}=\Gamma_{\lambda_{2}}
and thus

\Gamma^{\prime}_{\lambda_{1}}=\Gamma^{\prime}_{\lambda_{2}}
, which is enough to conclude.

If now
m<\infty
, then
J^{1}_{j}=J^{2}_{j}
for all
j\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
. Thus, if
m>0
, then

K^{1}_{m}
and
K^{2}_{m}
have the same origin and, in particular,
K^{1}_{m}
and
K_{m}^{2}
are two
distinct cells, with an intersection and oriented the same way. This is obvisouly also true by
hypothesis if
m=0
. Now, because
\Gamma_{\lambda_{1}}
and
\Gamma_{\lambda_{2}}
are compatible, we
either have
K_{m}^{2}\subsetneq K_{m}^{1}
and
\lambda_{1}<\lambda_{2}
, or
K_{m}^{1}\subsetneq K_{m}^{2}
and

\lambda_{2}<\lambda_{1}
– let us assume w.l.o.g. that we are in the first situation.

If we now consider the index

n=\sup\{j\leq 0,o(K^{1}_{j})\not=o(K_{j}^{2})\},

since we assumed that
m<+\infty
,
\Gamma_{\lambda_{1}}\neq\Gamma_{\lambda_{2}}
, and hence

n>-\infty
. By the same reasoning as above, the cells
K_{n}^{1}
and
K_{n}^{2}
are distinct, with
an intersection, and oriented the same way. Since we have now specified that

\lambda_{1}<\lambda_{2}
, we must have
K_{n}^{2}\subsetneq K_{n}^{1}
.

Figure 33
.
Example of two compatible polygonal curves under a neutralization procedure. The full
lines are the initial polygonal curves. The dotted lines are the new cells
\tilde{K}^{1}
and

\tilde{K}^{2}
that we need to compare, and the dashed lines the new bridges. On this example,
we have
m=0
and
n=-1
.

Because two lifts of
\Gamma_{\lambda_{1}}
and
\Gamma_{\lambda_{2}}
cannot cross transversally, our lift of

\Gamma_{\lambda_{2}}
then lies entirely on the right of our lift of
\Gamma_{\lambda_{1}}
, as represented in
Figure
33
.

Now, for
i=1,2
, let
\tilde{J}^{i}
denote the bridge coming out of
\tilde{K}^{i}
in

\Gamma_{\lambda_{i}}^{\prime}
. By definition of the neutralization procedure, the bridge
\tilde{J}^{i}
is the
orthogeodesic between the cell
K^{i}_{0}
and a cell
K_{j_{i}}^{i}
for an index
j_{i}\geq 0
, and the
cells
K_{j}^{i}
for
0<j<j_{i}
are neutralized. There are now two cases to consider.

•

If
j_{2}\geq m
, we can use the Useful Remark
2.11
to deduce that the origin of

\tilde{J}^{2}
lies on the right of the origin of
\tilde{J}^{1}
on
K_{0}^{1}
, and the bridges

\tilde{J}^{1}
and
\tilde{J}^{2}
are equal or do not cross, which is exactly our claim.

•

Otherwise,
j_{2}<m
, or, in other words, the two polygonal curves
\Gamma_{\lambda_{1}}
and

\Gamma_{\lambda_{2}}
coincide up until the index
j_{2}
. By definition of the neutralization for a
family of curve, if a cell
K^{2}_{j}
of
\Gamma_{\lambda_{2}}
is contained in a cell
K_{j}^{1}
of

\Gamma_{\lambda_{1}}
and if
K_{j}^{1}
is neutralized, then
K^{2}_{j}
must also be
neutralized. Therefore,
j_{1}\leq j_{2}
, and we can once again use the Useful Remark to conclude.

The same reasoning also allows to prove that the origin
o(\tilde{K}^{2})
lies on the left of

o(\tilde{K}^{1})
, and the bridges arriving on
\tilde{K}^{1}
and
\tilde{K}^{2}
are equal or disjoint.
∎

8.5.3.
Simultaneous decoration

We are now ready to extend the notion of decoration introduced in §
8.4.4
to
families of polygonal curves.

Definition 8.31
.

Let
\Gamma=(\Gamma_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
be a compatible family of
polygonal curves. A
(simultaneous) decoration
of
\Gamma
is a map

d:\Cell(\Gamma)\longrightarrow\{0,\infty\}
such that any cell common to several
\Gamma_{\lambda}
bears only one
decoration.

Definition 8.32
.

Let
(\Gamma,d)
be a decorated family. A cell
K\in\Cell(\Gamma)
is called
unfavourable

(for the decoration
d
) if
d(K)=0
or if
K\subseteq K^{\prime}
with
d(K^{\prime})=0
. It is called

favourable
otherwise. A
maximal unfavourable cell
is a cell which is unfavourable
and maximal for inclusion.

A simultaneous decoration
d
of the family
\Gamma
is called
favourable
if all its cells are
favourable.

By convention, recall that polygonal curves of combinatorial length
0
have no cells. As a
consequence, if
\Gamma=(\Gamma_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
contains only polygonal curves
such that
|\Gamma_{\lambda}|=0
, then
\Cell(\Gamma)=\emptyset
and in particular
\Gamma
is
favorable.

Definition 8.33
.

A decorated family
(\Gamma^{\prime},d^{\prime})
is said to be
derived from

(\Gamma,d)
if
\Gamma^{\prime}
is derived from

\Gamma
by neutralizing a maximal unfavourable cell and the map
d^{\prime}
coincides with
d
on all cells
in common between
\Gamma
and
\Gamma^{\prime}
.

Definition 8.34
.

Let
\Gamma=(\Gamma_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
be a compatible family of
polygonal curves. An
exhaustion
of
\Gamma
is a sequence of decorated families

(\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d})=(\Gamma^{k},d^{k})_{1\leq k\leq t}
, where:

•

\Gamma^{1}=\Gamma
;

•

for every
1\leq k\leq t
,
\Gamma^{k}=(\Gamma^{k}_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
is a
compatible family of polygonal curves;

•

for every
1\leq k<t
,
(\Gamma^{k},d^{k})
is not favourable, and

(\Gamma^{k+1},d^{k+1})
is derived from
(\Gamma^{k},d^{k})
;

•

for
k=t
,
(\Gamma^{t},d^{t})
is favourable.

We let
\Cell(\mathbf{\Gamma})=\bigcup_{k=1}^{t}\Cell(\Gamma^{k})
. We denote as

|\mathbf{\Gamma}|=\#\Cell(\mathbf{\Gamma})
the
complexity
of the
exhaustion
\mathbf{\Gamma}
. If
K\in\Cell(\mathbf{\Gamma})
, we denote
\mathbf{d}(K)=d^{k}(K)

for any
k
such that
K\in\Cell(\Gamma^{k})
.

Note that, by construction, the value of the decoration
\mathbf{d}(K)=d^{k}(K)
is independent from
the choice of an index
k
such that
K\in\Cell(\Gamma^{k})
. This is also true of the roof over a
cell
\tilde{J}(K)
and height of a cell
Z_{K}^{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
introduced in Notation
8.27
.

Notation 8.35

(Terminal cells)
.

Let

(\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d})=(\Gamma^{k},d^{k})_{1\leq k\leq t}
be a sequence of decorated
families of polygonal curves. We denote as
(\Gamma^{\infty},d^{\infty})
the decorated polygonal
curve
(\Gamma^{t},d^{t})
. The cells of the last family
\Gamma^{\infty}
are called the
terminal
cells
of
(\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d})
. The set of terminal cells associated with

(\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d})
is denoted
\mathrm{Term}({\mathbf{\Gamma}},{\mathbf{d}})
, in other
words

\mathrm{Term}({\mathbf{\Gamma}},{\mathbf{d}}):=\Cell(\Gamma^{\infty}).

8.6.
Another partition of unity on Teichmüller space

Let us now focus on our compatible family of polygonal curves

\Gamma=(\Gamma_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
defined as the boundary components of the pair of
pants decomposition introduced in §
3.2
.

We recall that we have fixed a cutoff value
a=a(r)
and associated cutoff functions

\chi_{\Gamma,0},\chi_{\Gamma,\infty}
such that
\chi_{\Gamma,0}+\chi_{\Gamma,\infty}=1
in Notation
8.14
.

Notation 8.36
.

Let
({\mathbf{\Gamma}},{\mathbf{d}})=(\Gamma^{k},d^{k})_{1\leq k\leq t}
be an exhaustion of the
family
(\Gamma_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
. For a hyperbolic metric

Y\in\mathcal{T}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}^{*}
, we let

(8.12)

\displaystyle\Psi_{{\mathbf{\Gamma}},{\mathbf{d}}}(Y):=\prod_{K\in\Cell(\mathbf{\Gamma})}\chi_{\Gamma,\mathbf{d}(K)}({Z^{\mathbf{\Gamma}}_{K}}).

The function
\Psi_{{\mathbf{\Gamma}},{\mathbf{d}}}
is a cut-off function on the Teichmüller space

\mathcal{T}^{*}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}
. It achieves our purpose described throught this section. Indeed, if
Y\in\mathcal{T}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}^{*}
lies in the support of
\Psi_{\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d}}
, then for any cell
K\in\Cell(\mathbf{\Gamma})
,

•

if
\mathbf{d}(K)=0
, then
Z_{K}^{\mathbf{\Gamma}}\leq e^{a+1}
;

•

if
\mathbf{d}(K)=\infty
, then
Z_{K}^{\mathbf{\Gamma}}\geq e^{a}
.

We can extract from the family of functions

\Psi_{{\mathbf{\Gamma}},{\mathbf{d}}}
with
(\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d})
an exhaustion a
partition of unity on Teichmüller space :

Proposition 8.37
.

There exists a family of exhaustions
\mathfrak{E}
of the family
(\Gamma_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
such that

(8.13)

\displaystyle\forall Y\in\mathcal{T}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}^{*},\quad\sum_{({\mathbf{\Gamma}},{\mathbf{d}})\in\mathfrak{E}}\Psi_{{\mathbf{\Gamma}},{\mathbf{d}}}(Y)=1.

We omit the proof, a tedious but surpriseless recursive procedure on the number of cells of the
family of polygonal curves
(\Gamma_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
.

8.7.
Neutral variables associated with
({\mathbf{\Gamma}},{\mathbf{d}})
.

Fix an exhaustion
({\mathbf{\Gamma}},{\mathbf{d}})=(\Gamma^{k},d^{k})_{1\leq k\leq t}
of the
family
(\Gamma_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
of boundary components of our pair of pants
decomposition of
\mathbf{S}
. On the support of
\Psi_{{\mathbf{\Gamma}},{\mathbf{d}}}
, we shall
enlarge the set of neutral variables originally defined in §
5.3
, and hence diminish
the number of variables with respect to which we need to apply the operator
\mathcal{L}
under the
integral (
5.13
).

Amongst the terminal cells of
(\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d})
, some are particularly important.

Notation 8.38
.

We denote as
\mathrm{Term}_{\mathrm{BC}}(\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d})\subseteq\mathrm{Term}(\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d})
the set of cells of

(\Gamma_{\lambda}^{\infty})_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}^{\Gamma}}
. We call these cells
boundary terminal cells
.

We observe that, by definition of the neutralization procedure, any
K\in\mathrm{Term}_{\mathrm{BC}}(\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d})
is included in a
cell of the initial polygonal curve
(\Gamma_{\lambda}^{1})_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}^{\Gamma}}
. The cells of
this family are amongst the segments
(\mathcal{K}_{q})_{q\in\Theta}
defined in §
4.3
. We also
note that each segment
(\mathcal{K}_{q})_{q\in\Theta}
can contain at most one boundary terminal cell.

We now recall that, in §
4.3
, we have defined for each
q\in\Theta
a set

\Theta_{\tau}(q)\subseteq\Theta
such that we can write
the length
\tau_{q}
of
\mathcal{K}_{q}
as
\tau_{q}=\sum_{q^{\prime}\in\Theta_{\tau}(q)}\theta_{q^{\prime}}
.

Definition 8.39
.

Let
q\in\Theta
. We call
\mathcal{K}_{q}
a
surviving boundary segment
if
\mathcal{K}_{q}
contains a
boundary terminal cell. In that case, we let

(8.14)

\displaystyle Z_{{\mathbf{\Gamma}},{\mathbf{d}}}(\mathcal{K}_{q}):=Z_{K}^{{\mathbf{\Gamma}}}

where
K
is the unique boundary terminal cell contained in
\mathcal{K}_{q}
. We denote as
\Theta_{\mathrm{surv}}

the set of indices
q\in\Theta
such that
\mathcal{K}_{q}
is a surviving boundary segment and

\vec{\tau}_{\mathrm{surv}}=(\tau_{q})_{q\in\Theta_{\mathrm{surv}}}
.

Notation 8.40
.

We define additional
neutral variables
associated to the exhaustion

(\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d})
,

(8.15)

\displaystyle\Theta_{\mathrm{nt}}({\mathbf{\Gamma}},{\mathbf{d}}):=\bigcup_{q\in\Theta\setminus\Theta_{\mathrm{surv}}}\Theta_{\tau}(q).

For a vector
\vec{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{\Theta}
, we denote as

\vec{\theta}_{\mathrm{nt}}({\mathbf{\Gamma}},{\mathbf{d}})=(\theta_{q})_{q\in\Theta_{\mathrm{nt}}({\mathbf{\Gamma}},{\mathbf{d}})}\in\mathbb{R}^{\Theta_{\mathrm{nt}}(\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d})}
the restriction of the vector
\vec{\theta}
to the
indices in
\Theta_{\mathrm{nt}}({\mathbf{\Gamma}},{\mathbf{d}})
.

Remark 8.41
.

If a boundary component
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}
is reduced in the exhaustion procedure to a
component of combinatorial length
0
, i.e. if
|\Gamma^{\infty}_{\lambda}|=0
, then all variables

\theta_{q}
with
\lambda(q)=\lambda
are neutral variables.

Lemma
8.6
allows us to draw the following observations as to the variables
appearing in a terminal polygonal curve.

Lemma 8.42
.

Let
(\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d})
be an exhaustion of
(\Gamma_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
. Then,

•

for any
q\in\Theta_{\mathrm{surv}}
, if
K
is the unique boundary terminal cell contained in
\mathcal{K}_{q}
,

(8.16)

\displaystyle\tau_{q}=\ell(K)+\mathrm{fn}(\vec{L},\vec{\theta}_{\mathrm{nt}}({\mathbf{\Gamma}},{\mathbf{d}}));

•

any bridge
J\in\Br(\Gamma^{\infty})
of the terminal family of polygonal curves

\Gamma^{\infty}
satisfies

(8.17)

\displaystyle\ell(J)=\mathrm{fn}(\vec{L},\vec{\theta}_{\mathrm{nt}}({\mathbf{\Gamma}},{\mathbf{d}})).

8.8.
Structure and derivatives of the function

\Psi_{{\mathbf{\Gamma}},{\mathbf{d}}}(Y)

Later on, we will need to be able to estimate the derivatives of the function
\Psi_{{\mathbf{\Gamma}},{\mathbf{d}}}(Y)
with respect to certain variables.
We prove the following.

Proposition 8.43
.

We can rewrite
\Psi_{\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d}}(Y)
under the form

\Psi_{\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d}}(Y)=\tilde{\Psi}_{\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d}}(\vec{\tau},\vec{L},\vec{\theta}_{\mathrm{nt}}(\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d}))

for a smooth function

\tilde{\Psi}_{\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d}}:\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{\Theta}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{r}\times\mathbb{R}^{\Theta_{\mathrm{nt}}(\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d})}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
satisfying, for any set

\tilde{\pi}\subseteq\Theta
,

\Big|\frac{\partial^{|\tilde{\pi}|}\tilde{\Psi}_{\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d}}}{\prod_{q\in\tilde{\pi}}\partial\tau_{q}}\Big|\leq\mathrm{fn}(|\mathbf{\Gamma}|)\Big(\max_{0\leq k\leq|\tilde{\pi}|}\sup_{x\geq 0}\big(|\bar{\chi}_{\Gamma,0}^{(k)}(x)|+|\bar{\chi}_{\Gamma,\infty}^{(k)}(x)|\big)\Big)^{|\mathbf{\Gamma}|}

where
\bar{\chi}_{\Gamma,d}(x):=\chi_{\Gamma,d}(e^{x})
for
d\in\{0,\infty\}
.

Recall that the quantity
|\mathbf{\Gamma}|
is the complexity of
\mathbf{\Gamma}
, i.e. its number
of cells, which is bounded above by
2r\#\Lambda\leq 6r^{2}
.

Proof.

Let us first make a useful observation. For any cell
K\in\Cell(\mathbf{\Gamma})
, there exists

K^{1}\in\Cell(\Gamma^{1})
containing
K
. As a consequence,
K=K^{1}\setminus I_{K}
for a
union of intervals
I_{K}
, corresponding to some intervals that were removed in the neutralization
procedure. Recalling that the cells of
\Gamma^{1}
are amongst the collection of segments

(\mathcal{K}_{qq^{\prime}})_{q,q^{\prime}\in\Theta}
introduced in §
4.3
, we obtain that there exists a set of
indices
\Theta_{\Gamma}(K)\subseteq\Theta
such that

(8.18)

\ell_{Y}(K)=\sum_{q\in\Theta_{\Gamma}(K)}\tau_{q}-\ell_{Y}(I_{K}).

We now write

(8.19)

\displaystyle\Psi_{{\mathbf{\Gamma}},{\mathbf{d}}}(Y)=\prod_{K\in\Cell(\mathbf{\Gamma})}\bar{\chi}_{\Gamma,\mathbf{d}(K)}(\ell_{Y}(K)+T_{K})

where for
K\in\Cell(\mathbf{\Gamma})
,

•

\ell_{Y}(K)
is the length of the cell
K
;

•

if
J_{\pm}(K)
denote the two bridges adjacent to
K
, then

T_{K}=\log\tanh(\ell_{Y}(J_{-}(K)))+\log\tanh(\ell_{Y}(J_{+}(K))).

By (
8.18
) this is equal to

(8.20)

\displaystyle\Psi_{{\mathbf{\Gamma}},{\mathbf{d}}}(Y)=\prod_{K\in\Cell(\mathbf{\Gamma})}\bar{\chi}_{\Gamma,\mathbf{d}(K)}\Big(\sum_{q\in\Theta_{\Gamma}(K)}\tau_{q}-\ell_{Y}(I_{K})+T_{K}\Big).

We prove, by the same method as Lemma
8.42
, that for all

K\in\Cell(\mathbf{\Gamma})
,

T_{K}-\ell_{Y}(I_{K})=\mathrm{fn}(\vec{L},\vec{\theta}_{\mathrm{nt}}(\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d})).

Calling
f_{K}
this function, define

\tilde{\Psi}_{\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d}}(\vec{\tau},\vec{L},\vec{\theta}_{\mathrm{nt}}(\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d})):=\prod_{K\in\Cell(\mathbf{\Gamma})}\bar{\chi}_{\Gamma,\mathbf{d}}\Big(\sum_{q\in\Theta_{\Gamma}(K)}\tau_{q}+f_{K}(\vec{L},\vec{\theta}_{\mathrm{nt}}(\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d}))\Big).

The upper bound on the derivatives is a simple application of the chain rule.
∎

9.
Proof of the main theorem for generalized eights

We are now ready to prove the main result, Theorem
1.23
, for generalized eights.

9.1.
Writing as a pseudo-convolution

We recall that, in §
5
, we have reduced the problem to the study of the integral
(
5.13
)

\displaystyle\mathbf{Int}(\ell)=\int_{h(\vec{\theta}_{\mathrm{ac}})=\ell}\varphi_{0}(\vec{\theta}_{\mathrm{ac}})\prod_{q\in\Theta_{\mathrm{ac}}}\theta_{q}^{\mathrm{K}_{q}}e^{\theta_{q}}\,\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\vec{\theta}_{\mathrm{ac}}}{\,\mathrm{d}\ell}

with the function
h
equal to the length of
\mathbf{c}
in the coordinates
(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})
,
see (
5.16
), and the density
\varphi_{0}
given in (
5.15
). Under this form, the
integral
\mathbf{Int}(\ell)
is already written as a pseudo-convolution of Friedman–Ramanujan
functions. However, as seen in §
6.5
, we cannot directly apply Theorem

6.28
(or its variants) to it to conclude to Theorem
1.23
.

We shall therefore input the considerations of §
7
and
8
. More
precisely, let us use the partitions of unity
(\Psi_{\xi,\mathcal{Q}})_{\xi\in\Xi,\mathcal{Q}\subseteq I(\xi)}

and
(\Psi_{\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d}})_{(\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d})\in\mathfrak{E}}
defined in §
7.6
and
8.6
to subdivide the integral into

\displaystyle{\mathbf{Int}}(\ell)=\sum_{(\xi,\mathcal{Q}),(\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d})}{\mathbf{Int}}_{(\xi,\mathcal{Q}),(\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d})}(\ell),

where
\xi
ranges over
\Xi
,
\mathcal{Q}
over the subsets of
I(\xi)
,
(\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d})

over the set of exhaustions
\mathfrak{E}
constructed in Proposition
8.37
, and

{\mathbf{Int}}_{(\xi,\mathcal{Q}),(\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d})}(\ell)=\int_{h(\vec{\theta}_{\mathrm{ac}})=\ell}\varphi_{0}(\vec{\theta}_{\mathrm{ac}})\Psi_{\xi,\mathcal{Q}}(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})\Psi_{\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d}}(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})\prod_{q\in\Theta_{\mathrm{ac}}}\theta_{q}^{\mathrm{K}_{q}}e^{\theta_{q}}\,\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\vec{\theta}_{\mathrm{ac}}}{\,\mathrm{d}\ell}.

We now further enlarge the set of neutral parameters.

Notation 9.1
.

We define the full set of neutral parameters as

\Theta_{\mathrm{nt}}^{\prime}=\Theta_{\mathrm{nt}}\cup\Theta_{\mathrm{nt}}(\xi,\mathcal{Q})\cup\Theta_{\mathrm{nt}}(\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d}),

where
\Theta_{\mathrm{nt}}(\xi,\mathcal{Q})
and
\Theta_{\mathrm{nt}}(\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d})
are defined in
(
7.23
) and (
8.15
) respectively. The
active variables
are

\vec{\theta}_{\mathrm{ac}}^{\prime}:=(\theta_{q})_{q\in\Theta_{\mathrm{ac}}^{\prime}}
with
\Theta_{\mathrm{ac}}^{\prime}=\Theta\setminus\Theta_{\mathrm{nt}}^{\prime}
. We denote

\vec{\theta}_{\mathrm{ac}}^{\sim}=(\tilde{\theta}_{q})_{q\in\Theta_{\mathrm{ac}}^{\prime}}
the set of modified
\theta
parameters
introduced in Notation
7.15
.

From now on, all the
neutral variables

\vec{\theta}_{\mathrm{nt}}^{\prime}:=(\theta_{q})_{q\in\Theta_{\mathrm{nt}}^{\prime}}
will be
frozen, and any function which depends only on the neutral variables may be treated as a
constant. Our variables will be the
\vec{\theta}_{\mathrm{ac}}^{\sim}
, which are always
\geq\log(2)
, and hence
circumvent the problems raised by crossings. We saw in Lemma
7.20
that

\tilde{\theta}_{q}
and
\theta_{q}
just differ by a constant (that is, a function of neutral
variables).

We shall therefore consider the restriction of the integral

{\mathbf{Int}}_{(\xi,\mathcal{Q}),(\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d})}
defined as

(9.1)

{\mathbf{Int}}^{\prime}_{(\xi,\mathcal{Q}),(\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d})}(\ell)=\int_{h(\vec{\theta}_{\mathrm{ac}}^{\sim})=\ell}\varphi_{0}(\vec{\theta}_{\mathrm{ac}})\Psi_{\xi,\mathcal{Q}}(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})\Psi_{\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d}}(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})\prod_{q\in\Theta_{\mathrm{ac}}^{\prime}}\theta_{q}^{\mathrm{K}_{q}}e^{\theta_{q}}\,\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\vec{\theta}_{\mathrm{ac}}^{\sim}}{\,\mathrm{d}\ell}.

Note that the only modification is that we have removed the functions

\theta_{q}^{\mathrm{K}_{q}}e^{\theta_{q}}
for
q\in\Theta_{\mathrm{nt}}^{\prime}\setminus\Theta_{\mathrm{nt}}
(now constant), and
restricted the integral to the variables
\vec{\theta}_{\mathrm{ac}}^{\sim}
, which are identical to
\vec{\theta}_{\mathrm{ac}}^{\prime}
, up to
an additive function of the neutral parameters.

We can now see the integral
{\mathbf{Int}}^{\prime}_{(\xi,\mathcal{Q}),(\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d})}(\ell)
as the

(h,\varphi)
-convolutions of the functions
\theta_{q}^{\mathrm{K}_{q}}e^{\theta_{q}}
, indexed by

q\in\Theta_{\mathrm{ac}}^{\prime}
of cardinal
n_{\mathrm{ac}}=\#\Theta_{\mathrm{ac}}^{\prime}
, where

•

the function
h
is now given by (
7.20
), which reads

\displaystyle h(\vec{\theta}_{\mathrm{ac}}^{\sim})=\ell_{Y}({\mathbf{c}})=\sum_{i=1}^{\mathfrak{n}}M_{n^{\prime}_{i}}\Big((\epsilon\tilde{L}_{q})_{q\in I(\xi^{i})},(\tilde{\theta}_{q})_{q\in I(\xi^{i})}\Big)

where
n_{i}^{\prime}:=\#I(\xi^{i})
is the number of non-erased
\theta
parameters in the
i
-th component

\mathbf{c}_{i}
, and
\tilde{L}_{q},\tilde{\theta}_{q}
are the modified parameters introduced in §
7.4
after erasing;

•

the function
\varphi
given by

(9.2)

\displaystyle\varphi(\vec{\theta}_{\mathrm{ac}}^{\sim})=\Psi_{\xi,\mathcal{Q}}(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})\,\Psi_{\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d}}(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})\,{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-4.0mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-4.5mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-5.0mu{\rm{l}}}}_{\mathfrak{D}}(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in V_{+}^{\beta}\end{subarray}}\chi_{\textrm{z}}(y_{\lambda})^{2}\prod_{\lambda\in V^{\Gamma}\setminus W^{\Gamma}}\Big(\log F_{m_{\lambda}}(\vec{L}^{\lambda},\vec{\tau}^{\lambda})\Big)^{d_{\lambda}}.

In the next two sections, we show that the functions
h
and
\varphi
have the structure described
in the second variant of Theorem
6.28
, presented in §
6.6.2
.

9.2.
Properties of the function
h

By definition, on the support of the function
\Psi_{\xi,\mathcal{Q}}
, we have

\tilde{\theta}_{q}\geq\log(2)
for every
q\in\Theta_{\mathrm{ac}}^{\prime}
. We can therefore apply Proposition

6.24
, Corollary
6.25
and Remark
6.26
and prove that the
function
h
, seen as a function of the variables
\vec{\theta}_{\mathrm{ac}}^{\sim}
, belongs in
the class
\mathcal{E}_{n_{\mathrm{ac}}}^{(\log 2)}
introduced in Definition
6.22
.
We can then deduce that the function
h
is of class
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
on the support of

\Psi_{\xi,\mathcal{Q}}
, and that on this set,

•

for every
q\in\Theta_{\mathrm{ac}}^{\prime}
,

\displaystyle|\partial_{q}h(\vec{\theta}_{\mathrm{ac}}^{\sim})-1|\leq Ce^{-\tilde{\theta}_{q}};

•

for every
\pi\subseteq\Theta_{\mathrm{ac}}^{\prime}
such that
|\pi|\geq 2
,

\displaystyle|\partial^{\pi}h(\vec{\theta}_{\mathrm{ac}}^{\sim})|\leq Ce^{-\sum_{q\in\pi}\tilde{\theta}_{q}},

where
C=C(\log 2,n)
is the constant from Proposition
6.24
. As a consequence, the
function
h
satisfies the hypothesis
(
h
)
defined in Notation
6.29
with
respect to the variables
\vec{\theta}_{\mathrm{ac}}^{\sim}
, with the constant
C
.

9.3.
Derivatives of the function
\varphi

We shall rewrite the function
\varphi
under the special form required in (
6.30
), i.e.
split it into a product
\varphi=\phi\times\psi
where

\displaystyle\psi(\vec{\theta}_{\mathrm{ac}}^{\sim})=\Psi_{\xi,\mathcal{Q}}(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})\,{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-4.0mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-4.5mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-5.0mu{\rm{l}}}}_{\mathfrak{D}}(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in V_{+}^{\beta}\end{subarray}}\chi_{\textrm{z}}(y_{\lambda})^{2}

and

\displaystyle\phi(\vec{\tau}_{\mathrm{surv}})=\Psi_{\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d}}(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})\prod_{\lambda\in V^{\Gamma}\setminus W^{\Gamma}}\Big(\log F_{m_{\lambda}}(\vec{L}^{\lambda},\vec{\tau}^{\lambda})\Big)^{d_{\lambda}}

where we recall that
\vec{\tau}_{\mathrm{surv}}
is defined in Definition
8.39
.
To see that
\phi
may be expressed as a function of the parameters
\vec{\tau}_{\mathrm{surv}}
(once we have set the
neutral parameters as constants), we refer to Lemma
8.42
. We prove the
following.

Proposition 9.2
.

The functions
\psi
and
\phi
are
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
on the support of

\Psi_{\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d}}
. Furthermore,

•

for any
\pi\subseteq\Theta_{\mathrm{ac}}^{\prime}
,

|\partial^{\pi}\psi|\leq\mathrm{fn}(r)e^{-\sum_{q\in\pi}\tilde{\theta}_{q}}
;

•

for any
\tilde{\pi}\subseteq\Theta_{\mathrm{surv}}
, if

d:=\sum_{\lambda\in V^{\Gamma}\setminus W^{\Gamma}}d_{\lambda}
,

\displaystyle\Big|\frac{\partial^{|\tilde{\pi}|}\phi}{\prod_{q\in\tilde{\pi}}\partial\tau_{q}}\Big|\leq\mathrm{fn}(r,d)\,\ell_{Y}(\mathbf{c})^{d}\prod_{\mathcal{K}\in\tilde{\pi}}\frac{1}{Z^{1/2}_{\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d}}(\mathcal{K})}.

These inequalities play the role of the derivative controls in Assumption

(
\varphi
-CE-form)
, see Notation
6.43
.

Proof.

Let us first establish the smoothness of
\psi
and
\phi
.

We study the discontinuity of the indicator function
{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-4.0mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-4.5mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-5.0mu{\rm{l}}}}_{\mathfrak{D}}
. By Remark

8.41
, if
\lambda\in\Lambda
satisfies
|\Gamma^{\infty}_{\lambda}|=0
, then

\Theta_{\ell}(\lambda)\subseteq\Theta_{\mathrm{nt}}^{\prime}
. In other words, if
\lambda\in\Lambda
satisfies

q\in\Theta_{\ell}(\lambda)
for an active parameter
q\in\Theta_{\mathrm{ac}}^{\prime}
, then we must have

|\Gamma^{\infty}_{\lambda}|\geq 1
. In that scenario, by definition of the exhaustion, all the cells

K\in\Cell(\Gamma^{\infty}_{\lambda})
must be favorable. Then, on the support of

\Psi_{\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d}}
, by definition, we must have
Z^{\Gamma}_{K}\geq e^{a}
for all
cells in
\Gamma_{\lambda}^{\infty}
; in other words,
Y
is in the
\Gamma_{\lambda}^{\infty}
-favorable
region. By Remark
8.16
, we obtain that
\ell_{Y}(\Gamma_{\lambda})\geq 3
. Combined with Proposition
3.21
, this yields that
\Psi_{\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d}}

vanishes near the discontinuities of the indicator function
{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-4.0mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-4.5mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-5.0mu{\rm{l}}}}_{\mathfrak{D}}
. In other words,
the derivatives of
{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-4.0mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-4.5mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-5.0mu{\rm{l}}}}_{\mathfrak{D}}
with respect to
\vec{\theta}_{\mathrm{ac}}^{\sim}
are identically equal to
zero on the support of
\Psi_{\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d}}
.

Let us now examine the regularity of
\chi_{\textrm{z}}(y_{\lambda})
for
\lambda\in V^{\beta}_{+}
. Let

q\in\Theta_{\mathrm{ac}}^{\prime}
with
q\in\Theta_{\ell}(\lambda)
(otherwise
\chi_{\textrm{z}}(y_{\lambda})
does not depend on

\theta_{q}
). Then there also exists
q^{\prime}
such that
q\in\Theta_{\tau}(q^{\prime})
: otherwise,

\beta_{\lambda}
would not be reached by any of the bars
B_{1},\ldots,B_{r}
, contradicting the fact
that the multi-loop
\mathbf{c}
was assumed to be connected, and not a simple curve. Necessarily,
the corresponding
\mathcal{K}_{q^{\prime}}
must be a surviving boundary segment (otherwise, we would have

q\in\Theta_{\mathrm{nt}}(\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d})
by definition of the set of neutral variables). This
implies that
e^{\tau_{q^{\prime}}}\geq Z_{{\mathbf{\Gamma}},{\mathbf{d}}}(\mathcal{K}_{q^{\prime}})\geq e^{a}
. Because

\beta_{\lambda}
contains the segment
\mathcal{K}_{q^{\prime}}
, we then have
y_{j}\geq\tau_{q^{\prime}}\geq a>1
. But
the function
\chi_{\textrm{z}}
is assumed to be constant on
[1,+\infty)
. Thus, we obtain that the
derivatives of
\chi_{\textrm{z}}(y_{\lambda})
with respect to
\vec{\theta}_{\mathrm{ac}}^{\sim}
are identically equal to zero on the
support of
\Psi_{\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d}}
.

The smoothness of the functions

\Psi_{\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d}}
,
\Psi_{\xi,\mathcal{Q}}
and

\log(F_{m_{\lambda}}(\vec{L}^{\lambda},\vec{\tau}^{\lambda}))
are immediate consequences of their
definitions.
To estimate the derivatives
\partial^{\tilde{\pi}}\phi
we use the Leibniz rules. The derivatives
of
\phi
will contain derivatives of
\log F_{m_{\lambda}}(\vec{L}^{\lambda},\tau^{\lambda})
and
derivatives of
\Psi_{{\mathbf{\Gamma}},{\mathbf{d}}}
. We have all tools to estimate both.

By Lemma
8.42
, the lengths of terminal cells of

({\mathbf{\Gamma}},{\mathbf{d}})
coincide (up to additive “constants”) with the components of

\vec{\tau}_{\mathrm{surv}}=(\tau_{q})_{q\in\Theta_{\mathrm{surv}}}
. We may therefore reformulate Proposition
8.17
as
follows: on the support of the test function
\Psi_{{\mathbf{\Gamma}},{\mathbf{d}}}
, for every

\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}^{\Gamma}
, for every set
\tilde{\pi}
of surviving boundary segments with

|\tilde{\pi}|\geq 1
, we have

\displaystyle\Big|\frac{\partial^{\tilde{\pi}}\log F_{m_{\lambda}}(\vec{L}^{\lambda},\vec{\tau}^{\lambda})}{\prod_{q\in\tilde{\pi}}\partial\tau_{q}}\Big|\leq\mathrm{fn}(m_{\lambda})\,\prod_{q\in\tilde{\pi}}\frac{1}{Z_{\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d}}(\mathcal{K}_{q})}.

As a consequence,

\displaystyle\Bigg|\frac{\partial^{\tilde{\pi}}\Big(\big(\log F_{m_{\lambda}}(\vec{L}^{\lambda},\vec{\tau}^{\lambda})\big)^{d_{\lambda}}\Big)}{\prod_{q\in\tilde{\pi}}\partial\tau_{q}}\Bigg|\leq\mathrm{fn}(m_{\lambda})\,d_{\lambda}^{|\tilde{\pi}|}\big|\log F_{m_{\lambda}}(\vec{L}^{\lambda},\vec{\tau}^{\lambda})\big|^{d_{\lambda}-|\tilde{\pi}|}\prod_{q\in\tilde{\pi}}\frac{1}{Z_{\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d}}(\mathcal{K}_{q})}.

Since
m_{\lambda}
is always less than
2r
, we can write
\max_{m_{\lambda}\leq 2r}\mathrm{fn}(m_{\lambda})=\mathrm{fn}(r)
.
By definition of
F_{m_{\lambda}}
,

\big|\log F_{m_{\lambda}}(\vec{L}^{\lambda},\vec{\tau}^{\lambda})\big|\leq Q_{m_{\lambda}}(\vec{L}^{\lambda},\vec{\tau}^{\lambda})=\ell_{Y}(\Gamma_{\lambda})\leq\ell_{Y}(\mathbf{c})

by Proposition
3.22
.
We then deduce that

(9.3)

\displaystyle\Bigg|\frac{\partial^{\tilde{\pi}}\Big(\big(\log F_{m_{\lambda}}(\vec{L}^{\lambda},\vec{\tau}^{\lambda})\big)^{d_{\lambda}}\Big)}{\prod_{q\in\tilde{\pi}}\partial\tau_{q}}\Bigg|\leq\mathrm{fn}(r,d_{\lambda})\,\ell_{Y}(\mathbf{c})^{d_{\lambda}}\prod_{q\in\tilde{\pi}}\frac{1}{Z_{\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d}}^{1/2}(\mathcal{K}_{q})}

using the trivial but later useful observation that

1/Z_{\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d}}(\mathcal{K}_{q})\leq 1/Z^{1/2}_{\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d}}(\mathcal{K}_{q})
.

Let us turn to the derivatives of
\Psi_{{\mathbf{\Gamma}},{\mathbf{d}}}
. It follows from
Proposition
8.43
, the bound
|\mathbf{\Gamma}|\leq 6r^{2}
and the fact that our
partition functions
\chi_{\Gamma,0}
,
\chi_{\Gamma,\infty}
are fixed by Notation
8.14
, that

\Big|\frac{\partial^{\tilde{\pi}}\Psi_{{\mathbf{\Gamma}},{\mathbf{d}}}}{\prod_{q\in\tilde{\pi}}\partial\tau_{q}}\Big|\leq\mathrm{fn}(r).

We then harmonize this upper bound with (
9.3
) by artificially introducing a
product on the right-hand side. In order to do so, we note that, if
q\in\tilde{\pi}
is a
surviving boundary segment, then it contains a terminal cell
K
. On the support of the derivative
above,
Z_{K}^{\Gamma^{\infty}}\leq e^{a+1}
which, by Proposition
8.28
, implies that

Z_{\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d}}(\mathcal{K}_{q})\leq e^{a+3}
. We obtain

(9.4)

\displaystyle\Big|\frac{\partial^{\tilde{\pi}}\Psi_{{\mathbf{\Gamma}},{\mathbf{d}}}}{\prod_{q\in\tilde{\pi}}\partial\tau_{q}}\Big|\leq\mathrm{fn}(r)\prod_{\mathcal{K}\in\tilde{\pi}}\frac{1}{Z^{1/2}_{\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d}}(\mathcal{K})}.

The bounds on the derivatives of
\psi
are trivial consequences of the Leibniz
rules together with the previous observations on the derivatives of
{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-4.0mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-4.5mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-5.0mu{\rm{l}}}}_{\mathfrak{D}}
and

\chi_{\textrm{z}}(y_{\lambda})
. The only derivatives appearing are the derivatives

\partial^{\pi}\Psi_{\xi,\mathcal{Q}}
, which are bounded by
\mathrm{fn}(\#\mathcal{Q},|\pi|)\leq\mathrm{fn}(r)
, and vanish
for
\tilde{\theta}_{q}\geq 2\log(2)
, and hence satisfy

|\partial^{\pi}\Psi_{\xi,\mathcal{Q}}|\leq\mathrm{fn}(r)4^{|\pi|}\prod_{q\in\pi}e^{-\tilde{\theta}_{q}}\leq\mathrm{fn}(r)\prod_{q\in\pi}e^{-\tilde{\theta}_{q}}

which leads to the claim.
∎

9.4.
Comparison estimate

Now, the last thing we need to check is the hypothesis (
6.38
) in Assumption

(
\varphi
-CE-form)
, i.e., the comparison estimate. It will take the form of the following
inequality.

Proposition 9.3
.

On the support of
\Psi_{\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d}}
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}\setminus W}\ell_{Y}(\Gamma_{\lambda})\leq\ell_{Y}(\mathbf{c})+\sum_{q\not\in\Theta_{\tau}(\tilde{\pi})}\tilde{\theta}_{q}+\sum_{q\in\tilde{\pi}}(\log Z_{\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d}}(\mathcal{K}_{q})+2)+\mathrm{fn}(r).

We can rewrite this inequality under the form (
6.38
) in terms of the variables

\vec{\theta}_{\mathrm{ac}}^{\sim}
with the comparison functions

\displaystyle\log Z_{q}

\displaystyle:=\log Z_{\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d}}(\mathcal{K}_{q})+2

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(\vec{\theta}_{\mathrm{ac}}^{\sim})

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}\Big(\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}\setminus W}\ell_{Y}(\Gamma_{\lambda})-\mathrm{fn}(r)\Big).

The proof of the proposition can be found in §
10
, see Proposition

10.19
. We first show how it allows to end the proof of our main theorem.

9.5.
Applying the operator
\mathcal{L}

We are now ready to apply the operator
\mathcal{L}
to the integral (
9.1
), in order to
prove that the result is a Friedman-Ramanujan remainder (an element of
\mathcal{R}_{w}
). By Proposition

1.27
, this implies that the desired function is a
Friedman-Ramanujan function, which is our claim. In order to write down the terms we obtain when we
apply the operator
\mathcal{L}
, we will use Proposition
6.45
, the second variant of
our stability result.

The integration variable
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
are the active parameters
\tilde{\theta}_{q}
with

q\in\Theta_{\mathrm{ac}}^{\prime}
(and in particular
n=n_{\mathrm{ac}}=\#\Theta_{\mathrm{ac}}^{\prime}
), and the linear forms

\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{m}
are the components of the vector
\vec{\tau}_{\mathrm{surv}}=(\tau_{q})_{q\in\Theta_{\mathrm{surv}}}
,
given by (
4.14
). The functions
h,\phi
and
\psi
are those described in §
9.2
and §
9.3
, and the functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}
are the functions

\tilde{\theta}_{q}\mapsto\theta_{q}^{\mathrm{K}_{q}}e^{\theta_{q}}
for
q\in\Theta_{\mathrm{ac}}^{\prime}
.

The previous discussion proves that, as a function of the variables
\vec{\theta}_{\mathrm{ac}}^{\sim}
, the functions

h
,
f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}
and
\varphi
satisfy Assumptions
(h)
,
(f)
and

(
\varphi
-CE-form)
from Notation
6.29
and
6.43
(provided we
prove the comparison estimate). The constant
\mathrm{K}
from Assumption
(
f
)
is

(9.5)

\displaystyle\mathrm{K}=\sum_{q\in\Theta_{\mathrm{ac}}^{\prime}}(\mathrm{K}_{q}+1)

which, by (
5.11
), is equal to

(9.6)

\mathrm{K}=\sum_{q\in\Theta_{\mathrm{ac}}^{\prime}}\mathrm{K}_{q}+n_{\mathrm{ac}}\leq\sum_{j\in V}(\mathrm{K}_{j}-1)+n_{\mathrm{ac}}\leq\sum_{j\in V}(\mathrm{K}_{j}-1)+2r.

We note that the functions
\theta_{q}^{\mathrm{K}_{q}}e^{\theta_{q}}
only have a principal term here (so we
could take
\mathrm{N}_{q}=0
), but because we assume that
\mathrm{K}_{q}\leq\mathrm{N}_{q}
in Assumption
(f)
,
we take
\mathrm{N}_{q}=\mathrm{K}_{q}
for all
q\in\Theta_{\mathrm{ac}}^{\prime}
.

We can then apply Proposition
6.45
. We obtain that applying
\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{K}}
to
(
9.1
) yields a sum of terms bounded by

(9.7)

\displaystyle\mathrm{fn}(r,\mathrm{K})\prod_{j\in V}\|\tilde{g}_{j}\|_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{K}_{j},\mathrm{K}_{j}}}(1+\ell)^{\mathrm{K}}e^{\frac{\ell}{2}}

\displaystyle\int{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-4.0mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-4.5mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-5.0mu{\rm{l}}}}_{\mathfrak{D}}(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})\prod_{\lambda\in V^{\beta}_{+}}\chi_{\textrm{z}}(y_{\lambda})^{2}\prod_{q\in\Theta_{\mathrm{ac}}^{\prime}}e^{\theta_{q}}\prod_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}\setminus W}e^{-y_{\lambda}/2}\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\vec{\theta}_{\mathrm{ac}}^{\sim}}{\,\mathrm{d}\ell}.

As a consequence, our goal will be achieved if we prove that the integral in (
9.7
) is
polynomially bounded, after integration with respect to all the frozen variables
\vec{\theta}_{\mathrm{nt}}^{\prime}
.

All the variables that were frozen are recorded in (
5.17
) and just after
(
9.1
). Re-integrating them in the calculation, we are lead to consider the product
of
\mathrm{fn}(r,\mathrm{K})\prod_{j\in V}\|\tilde{g}_{j}\|_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{K}_{j},\mathrm{K}_{j}}}
with

\displaystyle(1+\ell)^{\mathrm{K}}e^{\frac{\ell}{2}}\int_{h(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})=\ell}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-4.0mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-4.5mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-5.0mu{\rm{l}}}}_{\mathfrak{D}}(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})

\displaystyle\prod_{\lambda\in V^{\beta}_{+}}\chi_{\textrm{z}}(y_{\lambda})^{2}P(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})T_{V,\mathrm{m}}(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})\prod_{i=1}^{r}\sinh(L_{i})\frac{\,\mathrm{d}^{r}\vec{L}\,\mathrm{d}^{2r}\vec{\theta}}{\,\mathrm{d}\ell}.

where

P(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})=\prod_{q\in\Theta_{\ell}(V_{+})\cap\Theta_{\mathrm{nt}}}\theta_{q}^{\mathrm{K}_{q}}\prod_{q\in\Theta_{\mathrm{nt}}^{\prime}\setminus\Theta_{\mathrm{nt}}}\theta_{q}^{\mathrm{K}_{q}}\prod_{q\in\Theta_{\mathrm{ac}}}e^{\theta_{q}}\prod_{\lambda\in W}r_{\lambda}(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})\prod_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}\setminus W}e^{-y_{\lambda}/2}.

Recall that we have assumed that we have the decay

|r_{\lambda}(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})|\leq\begin{cases}(1+y_{\lambda})^{\mathrm{N}_{\lambda}-1}e^{y_{\lambda}/2}&(\lambda\in W^{\beta})\\
(1+y_{\lambda})^{\mathrm{N}_{\lambda}-1}e^{-y_{\lambda}/2}&(\lambda\in W^{\Gamma}).\end{cases}

We also remember that
\sum_{q\in\Theta_{\mathrm{ac}}}{\theta_{q}}\leq\sum_{\lambda\in V^{\beta}_{+}\setminus W^{\beta}}y_{\lambda}=\sum_{\lambda\in V^{\beta}\setminus W^{\beta}}y_{\lambda}+\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{in}}^{\beta}}y_{\lambda}
. It follows that

\displaystyle P(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})

\displaystyle\leq(1+\ell)^{\mathrm{N}^{\prime}}\prod_{\lambda\in V^{\beta}\setminus W^{\beta}}e^{y_{\lambda}}\prod_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{in}}^{\beta}}e^{y_{\lambda}}\prod_{\lambda\in W^{\beta}}e^{y_{\lambda}/2}\prod_{\lambda\in W^{\Gamma}}e^{-y_{\lambda}/2}\prod_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}\setminus W}e^{-y_{\lambda}/2}

for
\mathrm{N}^{\prime}\leq\sum_{\lambda\in\Theta_{\mathrm{nt}}^{\prime}}\mathrm{N}_{\lambda}
.
Therefore, since
(V^{\beta}\setminus W^{\beta})\sqcup(V^{\Gamma}\setminus W^{\Gamma})\subseteq\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}\setminus W
,

\displaystyle P(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})

\displaystyle\leq(1+\ell)^{\mathrm{N}^{\prime}}\prod_{\lambda\in V^{\beta}}e^{y_{\lambda}/2}\prod_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{in}}^{\beta}}e^{y_{\lambda}}\prod_{\lambda\in V^{\Gamma}}e^{-y_{\lambda}/2}.

Putting everything together, we have proved that the integral (
9.1
) is bounded by a
sum of terms of the form

\int_{h(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})=\ell}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-4.0mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-4.5mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-5.0mu{\rm{l}}}}_{\mathfrak{D}}(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})\frac{\prod_{\lambda\in V^{\beta}}\chi_{\textrm{z}}(y_{\lambda})e^{y_{\lambda}/2}\prod_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{in}}^{\beta}}\chi_{\textrm{z}}^{2}(y_{\lambda})e^{y_{\lambda}}}{\prod_{\lambda\in V^{\Gamma}}e^{y_{\lambda}/2}}T_{V,\mathrm{m}}(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})\prod_{i=1}^{r}\sinh(L_{i})\frac{\,\mathrm{d}^{r}\vec{L}\,\mathrm{d}^{2r}\vec{\theta}}{\,\mathrm{d}\ell},

multiplied by
\mathrm{fn}(r,\mathrm{K})\prod_{j\in V}\|\tilde{g}_{j}\|_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{K}_{j},\mathrm{K}_{j}}}(1+\ell)^{\mathrm{K}+\mathrm{N}^{\prime}}e^{\frac{\ell}{2}}
.
We shall now bound this by a quantity for which we can use our change of variables, Corollary

3.13
, backwards. Indeed, we note that
\chi_{\textrm{z}}(y)e^{y/2}\leq c\sinh(y/2)
for a
universal constant
c
, and hence the quantity above is smaller than

\displaystyle\mathrm{fn}(r)\int_{h(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})=\ell}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-4.0mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-4.5mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-5.0mu{\rm{l}}}}_{\mathfrak{D}}(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})\frac{\prod_{\lambda\in V^{\beta}}\sinh\left(\frac{y_{\lambda}}{2}\right)\prod_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{in}}^{\beta}}\sinh^{2}\left(\frac{y_{\lambda}}{2}\right)}{\prod_{\lambda\in V^{\Gamma}}\sinh\left(\frac{y_{\lambda}}{2}\right)}T_{V,\mathrm{m}}(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})\prod_{i=1}^{r}\sinh(L_{i})\frac{\,\mathrm{d}^{r}\vec{L}\,\mathrm{d}^{2r}\vec{\theta}}{\,\mathrm{d}\ell}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{fn}(r)\int_{h(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})=\ell}\prod_{j=1}^{k}x_{i_{j}}\delta(x_{i_{j}}-x_{i_{j}^{\prime}})\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}(Y)}{\,\mathrm{d}\ell}.

We have shown that this quantity is polynomially bounded in the weak sense in Corollary

3.25
, giving a bound

\displaystyle\int_{\ell_{Y}({\mathbf{c}})\leq\ell}\prod_{j=1}^{k}x_{i_{j}}\delta(x_{i_{j}}-x_{i_{j}^{\prime}})\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}^{\mathrm{WP}}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}(\mathbf{x},Y)\leq(3\ell)^{3\chi(\mathbf{S})}.

As a conclusion, for
\mathrm{K}=\sum_{j\in V}(\mathrm{K}_{j}-1)+2r
we have shown that

\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{K}}\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{N}}

where
\mathrm{N}=\mathrm{K}+\mathrm{N}^{\prime}+3\chi(\mathbf{S})+1\leq\sum_{j\in V}\mathrm{N}_{j}+3\chi(\mathbf{S})+1
, implying that
\mathcal{J}
belongs to

\mathcal{F}_{w}^{\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N}}
. Furthermore we have shown that

(9.8)

\displaystyle\lVert\mathcal{J}\rVert_{\mathcal{F}_{w}^{\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N}}}\leq\mathrm{fn}(g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}},\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N})\,\prod_{j\in V}\lVert\tilde{g}_{j}\rVert_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{K}_{j},\mathrm{N}_{j}}}.

This proves Theorem
1.23
.

9.6.
Theorem
9.4

As a step towards the version of §
12
, we add a small modification to Theorem
1.23
. The only difference is that we impose the lengths of some components
\mathbf{c}_{j}
to stay bounded.

Theorem 9.4
.

With the notations of Theorem
1.23
, let
W
be a subset of
\{1,\ldots,\mathfrak{n}\}
and

\kappa=(\kappa_{j})_{j\in W}
be positive real numbers. Then, the function

\mathcal{J}=\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{T},V,\mathrm{m},f,W,\kappa}
defined by

(9.9)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}(\ell)=\int_{\ell_{Y}({\mathbf{c}})=\ell}\prod_{j\in W}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-4.0mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-4.5mu{\rm{l}}}{1\mskip-5.0mu{\rm{l}}}}_{[0,\kappa_{j}]}(\ell_{Y}(\mathbf{c}_{j}))\prod_{j\in V}f_{j}(x_{j})\prod_{j=1}^{k}x_{i_{j}}\delta(x_{i_{j}}-x_{i_{j}^{\prime}})\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}^{\mathrm{WP}}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}(\mathbf{x},Y)}{\,\mathrm{d}\ell}

is a Friedman–Ramanujan function in the weak sense. More precisely, let
\rho[{\mathbf{T}},W]
be
the number of unshielded simple portions of the multi-loop
\mathbf{c}
that are not in

(\mathbf{c}_{j})_{j\in W}
,

\mathrm{K}=\sum_{j\in V\setminus W}(\mathrm{K}_{j}-1)+\rho[{\mathbf{T}},W]\quad\textrm{and}\quad\mathrm{N}=\sum_{j\in V}\mathrm{N}_{j}+3\chi(\mathbf{S})+1.

Then
\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{F}_{w}^{\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N}}
, and

(9.10)

\displaystyle\lVert\mathcal{J}\rVert_{\mathcal{F}_{w}^{\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N}}}\leq\mathrm{fn}(g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}},\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N})\,e^{\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j\in W}\kappa_{j}}\prod_{j\in V}\lVert\tilde{g}_{j}\rVert_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{K}_{j},\mathrm{N}_{j}}}.

Proof.

The proof of Theorem
9.4
follows exactly the same lines as the proof of Theorem

1.23
, except that the set of neutral variables is enlarged to

\Theta_{\mathrm{nt}}^{\prime}(W):=\Theta_{\mathrm{nt}}^{\prime}\cup\{q\in\Theta,\comp(q)\in W\}
(with the notation
\comp(q)
from
§
2.5.2
, indicating to which component
c_{i}
the index
q
belongs). The set of
active variables is therefore reduced to
\Theta\setminus\Theta_{\mathrm{nt}}^{\prime}(W)
.

Proposition
9.3
is modified as follows:

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}\setminus W}\ell_{Y}(\Gamma_{\lambda})\leq\ell_{Y}(\mathbf{c})+\sum_{q\notin\Theta_{\tau}(\tilde{\pi})}\tilde{\theta}_{q}+\sum_{q\in\tilde{\pi}}(\log Z_{\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d}}(\mathcal{K}_{q})+2)+\sum_{j\in W}\kappa_{j}+\mathrm{fn}(r)

and this last term is responsible for the extra factor
e^{\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j\in W}\kappa_{j}}
in the quantitative statement of Theorem
9.4
.
∎

Remark 9.5
.

Note that
g
, the genus of the large surface in which
\mathbf{S}
is embedded at the beginning
of the paper, does not appear in Theorems
1.23
and
9.4
.
However, a notable point is that the numbers
(\kappa_{j})_{j\in W}

may be taken to be functions of
g
. This will happen in the application to Theorem
1.6
, where we have constraints such as
\ell_{Y}(\mathbf{c}_{j})\leq\kappa_{j}
with
\kappa_{j}=\kappa\log g
, with
\kappa>0
arbitrarily small.

Remark 9.6
.

Another point is that the new exponents
\mathrm{K}
,
\mathrm{N}
are bounded above by the ones obtained in
the initial result, Theorem
1.23
. In particular, the property also holds with the
exponents from Theorem
1.23
.

10.
Proof of the crucial comparison estimate (Proposition
9.3
)

The proof of the comparison estimate, Proposition
9.3
, is divided in two parts: a
lower bound on
\ell_{Y}(\mathbf{c})
(presented in §
10.1
) and an upper bound on

\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}\setminus W^{\Gamma}}\ell_{Y}(\Gamma_{\lambda})
(in §
10.2
).

10.1.
Lower bound on
\ell_{Y}(\mathbf{c})

Let us first prove a lower bound on the length of
\mathbf{c}
. We first prove a general lemma, and
then proceed to the proof in §
10.1.2
.

10.1.1.
A lemma

Let us first prove a comparison inequality in a simple case.
Let
\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{M}
be
M
complete oriented geodesics in the hyperbolic plane, assumed
to be pairwise aligned, with
\gamma_{k+1}
on the right of
\gamma_{k}
for all
k
. Let us define
the following notations.

•

For
1\leq k\leq M-1
,
H_{k,k+1}
is the orthogeodesic segment from
\gamma_{k}
to

\gamma_{k+1}
, of length denoted as
\theta_{k}
, with endpoints
G_{k}\in\gamma_{k}
and

D_{k}\in\gamma_{k+1}
.

•

D_{0}
and
G_{M}
are two arbitrary points on
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{M}
respectively.

•

For
1\leq k\leq M
, we denote as
L_{k}
the distance between
D_{k-1}
and
G_{k}
.

We then prove the following.

Lemma 10.1
.

We have

(10.1)

\displaystyle e^{\dist(D_{0},G_{M})}\geq\frac{1}{4^{M-1}}\exp\Big(\sum_{k=1}^{M}L_{k}\Big)\prod_{k=1}^{M-1}E_{k}(\theta_{k})

where the functions
E_{k}
are defined by

E_{k}(\theta)=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2}\exp(\theta)&\text{if the geodesic from }D_{0}\text{ to }G_{M}\text{ crosses }H_{k,k+1}\\
\cosh\theta-1=2\sinh^{2}\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Proof.

The proof relies on the following classic formula for hyperbolic birectangles, see
[
5
, Equation
(2.3.2)]
. For a geodesic quadrilateral with consecutives sides
\rho_{1},t,\rho_{2},d

with right angles between
\rho_{1},t
and between
t,\rho_{2}
, we have :

(10.2)

\displaystyle\cosh d=\cosh\rho_{1}\cosh\rho_{2}\cosh t-\sinh\rho_{1}\sinh\rho_{2}.

We then obtain that:

•

if
\rho_{1}\rho_{2}\geq 0
,

(10.3)

\displaystyle e^{d}\geq\cosh d\geq\cosh\rho_{1}\cosh\rho_{2}(\cosh t-1)\geq\frac{1}{4}e^{\rho_{1}+\rho_{2}}(\cosh t-1);

•

and if
\rho_{1}\rho_{2}\leq 0
,

(10.4)

\displaystyle e^{|d|}\geq\cosh d\geq\cosh\rho_{1}\cosh\rho_{2}\cosh t\geq\frac{1}{2^{3}}e^{|\rho_{1}|+|\rho_{2}|+|t|}.

Let us now prove the result by induction on
M\geq 2
. For
M=2
, this is a direct consequence of
(
10.3
) and (
10.4
) applied to the quadrilateral of vertices
D_{0}
,

G_{1}
,
D_{1}
,
G_{2}
.

For
M>2
, assume the result known for
M-1
geodesics, and let us pass to rank
M
. We observe
that the geodesic from
D_{0}
to
G_{M}
has to cross successively

\gamma_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{M-1}
. Call
\tilde{G}_{M-1}
its intersection point with

\gamma_{M-1}
. The induction assumption at rank
M-1
tells us that

\displaystyle e^{\dist(D_{0},\tilde{G}_{M-1})}\geq\frac{1}{4^{M-2}}\exp\Big(\sum_{k=1}^{M-2}L_{k}+\dist(D_{M-2},\tilde{G}_{M-1})\Big)\prod_{k=1}^{M-2}E_{k}(\theta_{k}).

By the case
M=2
,

\displaystyle e^{\dist(\tilde{G}_{M-1},G_{M})}\geq\frac{1}{4}\exp(\dist(\tilde{G}_{M-1},G_{M-1})+L_{M})\,E_{M-1}(\theta_{M-1}).

The result at rank
M
follows by putting together the two previous inequalities, given that we have the triangular inequality

L_{M-1}=\dist(D_{M-2},G_{M-1})\leq\dist(D_{M-2},\tilde{G}_{M-1})+\dist(\tilde{G}_{M-1},G_{M-1}),

and the equality

\dist(D_{0},G_{M})=\dist(D_{0},\tilde{G}_{M-1})+\dist(\tilde{G}_{M-1},G_{M}).

∎

We will actually use the following variant.

Lemma 10.2
.

Let
\mathcal{Q}=\{\psi_{1}<\psi_{2}<\ldots<\psi_{j}\}\subseteq\{1,\ldots,M-1\}
. If we assume that

\theta_{k}\geq\log 2
for all
k\in\mathcal{Q}
, then if we define
\psi_{0}:=0
and
\psi_{j+1}:=M
,

(10.5)

\displaystyle\dist(D_{0},G_{M})\geq\sum_{k\in\mathcal{Q}}\theta_{k}+\sum_{k=0}^{j}\dist(D_{\psi_{k}},G_{\psi_{k+1}})-5(M-1)\log 2.

Proof.

The proof is the same as above, now noting that the hypothesis on
\mathcal{Q}
implies that

E_{k}(\theta_{k})\geq\frac{1}{8}e^{\theta_{k}}
for all
k\in\mathcal{Q}
.
∎

10.1.2.
The lower bound

Recall the partition of unity

\Psi_{\xi,\mathcal{Q}}
defined in Definition
7.23
, indexed by elements

\xi\in\Xi
and subsets
\mathcal{Q}
of the set of parameters
I(\xi)\subseteq\Theta
(which
intuitively corresponds to the set of “nice” variables
\theta_{q}
, i.e. the ones we do not erase).
We have the decomposition
I(\xi)=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{\mathfrak{n}}I(\xi^{i})
depending on the index of the
component
\mathbf{c}
we consider. Consider
\tilde{I}(\xi^{i})\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
the periodic extension of

I(\xi^{i})
, and let
\varphi^{i}:\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
be an increasing numbering of it:

\tilde{I}(\xi^{i})=\{\varphi_{k}^{i},k\in\mathbb{Z}\},\qquad\varphi_{k}^{i}<\varphi_{k+1}^{i}.

Denoting
n_{\xi^{i}}=\#I(\xi^{i})
, we have the periodicity property that

\varphi_{k+n_{\xi^{i}}}^{i}=\varphi_{k}^{i}+n_{i}
.

We also recall some notation related to the lifts of the geometric situation to the hyperbolic plane
introduced in §
4.2.2
. For a component
i\in\{1,\ldots,\mathfrak{n}\}
,

(\tilde{\beta}(q))_{q\in\mathbf{\Theta}_{i}}
is a sequence of lifts of geodesics in the family

\beta
, which satisfies the following periodicity property:
\tilde{\beta}(q+n_{i})
is the image of

\tilde{\beta}(q)
by the hyperbolic translation of axis
\tilde{\mathbf{c}}_{i}
and of length

\ell_{Y}({\mathbf{c}}_{i})
.

Last, recall the intervals
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{q}=[G_{q},D_{q}]
introduced in Notation
7.13
.

Notation 10.3
.

Throughout this section, we fix
\xi\in\Xi
and
\mathcal{Q}\subseteq I(\xi)
. For

i\in\{1,\ldots,\mathfrak{n}\}
, let
\mathcal{Q}_{i}:=\mathcal{Q}\cap I(\xi^{i})\subseteq\Theta^{i}\simeq\mathbb{Z}_{n_{i}}
. We
pick a numbering of
\mathcal{Q}_{i}

\mathcal{Q}_{i}=\{\psi_{1}^{i}<\ldots<\psi_{j_{i}}^{i}\}\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n_{i}\}.

Let
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{i}=\{\psi_{k}^{i}\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\subseteq\mathbf{\Theta}^{i}\simeq\mathbb{Z}
denote its
cyclic extension.

Note that, since
\mathcal{Q}^{i}\subseteq I(\xi^{i})
, the sequence
(\psi_{k}^{i})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
is an extraction
of
(\varphi_{k}^{i})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
.

Definition 10.4
.

We call
junction
any geodesic segment
[D_{\psi_{k}^{i}},G_{\psi_{k+1}^{i}}]
, with

i\in\{1,\ldots,\mathfrak{n}\}
and
k\in\{1,\ldots,j_{i}\}
. We denote
\Junc(\mathcal{Q})
the set of
all junctions. Two segments of
\Junc(\mathcal{Q})
will be said to be
disjoint
if they
intersect only transversally.

Remark that certain junctions are bars (if
\psi_{k+1}^{i}=\psi_{k}^{i}+1
).

Proposition 10.5
.

For any
Y
in the support of
\Psi_{\xi,\mathcal{Q}}
,

(10.6)

\displaystyle\ell_{Y}({\mathbf{c}})\geq\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda^{\beta}}\ell_{Y}(\beta_{\lambda})+\sum_{p\in\Junc(\mathcal{Q})}\ell(p)-12\,r\log 2.

Remark 10.6
.

The proof consists, for each
i\in\{1,\ldots,\mathfrak{n}\}
, in
constructing a representative
\mathbf{c}_{i}^{\mathrm{f}}
of the homotopy class of

\mathbf{c}_{i}
, made of the concatenation of the intervals
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{q}
and of junctions. Inequality
(
10.6
) means that

\mathbf{c}^{\mathrm{f}}=(\mathbf{c}^{\mathrm{f}}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{c}^{\mathrm{f}}_{\mathfrak{n}})

almost minimizes the length in its homotopy class.

Proof.

Let
Y
be a metric on the support of
\Psi_{\xi,\mathcal{Q}}
.
We prove this property component by component and then sum the different bounds. Let us therefore
fix
i\in\{1,\ldots,\mathfrak{n}\}
.

Denote by
\gamma_{k}
the complete orthogeodesic between
\tilde{\beta}_{\varphi_{k-1}^{i}}
and

\tilde{\beta}_{\varphi_{k}^{i}}
: it contains the geodesic segment

\tilde{B}_{\varphi_{k-1}^{i}\varphi_{k}^{i}}
. By construction of the set
I(\xi^{i})
, all these
geodesics are parallel, and can be oriented so that
\gamma_{k+1}
is on the right of

\gamma_{k}
. We are in the geometric situation described in §
10.1.1
, with
M=n_{\xi^{i}}+1
.
The distance between
\gamma_{k}
and
\gamma_{k+1}
is
\tilde{\theta}_{\varphi_{k}^{i}}
. The
orthogeodesic segment denoted by
H_{k,k+1}
in §
10.1.1
is

[G_{\varphi_{k}^{i}},D_{\varphi_{k}^{i}}]
.

The infinite geodesic
\tilde{\mathbf{c}}_{i}
intersects successively all geodesics

\gamma_{k}
. Calling
D_{0}
its intersection point with
\gamma_{1}
and
G_{n_{\xi^{i}}+1}
its
intersection with
\gamma_{n_{\xi^{i}}+1}
, we have
\ell_{Y}({\mathbf{c}}_{i})=\dist(D_{0},G_{n_{\xi^{i}}+1})
.
We can apply inequality (
10.5
) to the set of indices
\psi_{k}^{i}
, which applies
here because all the
\tilde{\theta}_{\psi_{k}^{i}}
are greater than
\log 2
on the support of

\Psi_{\xi,\mathcal{Q}}
. We obtain that

(10.7)

\displaystyle\ell_{Y}({\mathbf{c}}_{i})\geq\sum_{q\in\mathcal{Q}^{i}}\tilde{\theta}_{q}+\sum_{k=1}^{j_{i}}\dist(D_{\psi_{k}^{i}},G_{\psi_{k+1}^{i}})-5n_{i}\log 2.

By summing over
i\in\{1,\ldots,\mathfrak{n}\}
and using

\ell_{Y}({\mathbf{c}})=\sum_{i=1}^{\mathfrak{n}}\ell_{Y}({\mathbf{c}}_{i})
, we obtain:

\displaystyle\ell_{Y}({\mathbf{c}})\geq\sum_{q\in\mathcal{Q}}\tilde{\theta}_{q}+\sum_{i=1}^{\mathfrak{n}}\sum_{k=1}^{j_{i}}\dist(D_{\psi_{k}^{i}},G_{\psi_{k+1}^{i}})-10r\log 2.

Because the
\tilde{\theta}_{q}
are all smaller than
2\log 2
for
q\not\in\mathcal{Q}
, we may write

\displaystyle\sum_{q\in\mathcal{Q}}\tilde{\theta}_{q}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{q\in I(\xi)}\tilde{\theta}_{q}-2r\log 2

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{q\in\Theta}\theta_{q}-2r\log 2=\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda^{\beta}}\ell_{Y}(\beta_{\lambda})-2r\log 2

where we used (
7.16
) in the second line. This is the claim. Note that the right-hand
side represents the total length of the representative

\mathbf{c}^{\mathrm{f}}=(\mathbf{c}_{1}^{\mathrm{f}},\ldots,\mathbf{c}_{\mathfrak{n}}^{\mathrm{f}})

mentioned in Remark
10.6
.
∎

10.1.3.
The polygons
\mathrm{Pol}(q)

We observe that, for any
1\leq i\leq\mathfrak{n}
and
1\leq k\leq j_{i}
, the junction

J_{k}^{i}=[D_{\psi_{k}^{i}},G_{\psi_{k+1}^{i}}]
leaves from
\tilde{\beta}_{\psi_{k}^{i}}
and arrives at

\tilde{\beta}_{\psi_{k+1}^{i}}
. It is homotopic with gliding endpoints to the concatenation

\displaystyle\left(\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}_{q=\psi_{k}^{i}+1}^{\psi_{k+1}^{i}-1}\tilde{B}_{q-1,q}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\tilde{\mathcal{I}}_{q}\right)\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\tilde{B}_{\psi_{k+1}^{i}-1,\psi_{k+1}^{i}}.

Examining the points at infinity of the infinite geodesic containing a lift of
J_{k}^{i}
, and using
the fact that it has to cross both
\tilde{\beta}_{\psi_{k}^{i}}
and
\tilde{\beta}_{\psi_{k+1}^{i}}
,
we make the following two observations.

•

The junction
J_{k}^{i}
crosses all the geodesics
\tilde{\beta}_{q}
with
q\in\{\psi_{k}^{i}+1,\ldots,\psi_{k+1}^{i}\}
, assuming
q
is a crossing parameter.

•

The junction
J_{k}^{i}
crosses all the bars
\tilde{B}_{q}
with

q\in\{\psi_{k}^{i}+1,...,\psi_{k+1}^{i}\}
, assuming both
q-1
and
q
are U-turn parameters.

We deduce the following.

Lemma 10.7
.

Let
q\in\Theta
, and let
1\leq i\leq\mathfrak{n}
and
1\leq k\leq j_{i}
for which

q\in\{\psi_{k}^{i}+1,...,\psi_{k+1}^{i}\}
. There exists a contractible geodesic polygon

\mathrm{Pol}(q)
, which is bordered, on one side by the full bar
\tilde{B}_{q}
, on other sides, by
subsegments of
J_{k}^{i}
,
\tilde{\beta}_{q-1}
,
\tilde{\beta}_{q}
and possibly

\tilde{\beta}_{q-2}
,
\tilde{\beta}_{q+1}
,
\tilde{B}_{q-1}
and
\tilde{B}_{q+1}
, and so that
none of the geodesics
\tilde{\beta}_{q^{\prime}}
or the bars
\tilde{B}_{q^{\prime}}
meet the interior of

\mathrm{Pol}(q)
.

Proof.

The construction is made by exhaustion of all cases, depending on whether
q-1
and
q
are U-turn
parameters or crossing parameters.

•

If
q-1
and
q
are crossing parameters, we simply consider the polygon with sides
delimited by
\tilde{B}_{q}
, and subsegments of
\tilde{\beta}_{q}
,
\tilde{\beta}_{q-1}
and

J_{k}^{i}
, as represented by the shaded area in Figure
34
.

Figure 34
.
The polygon
\mathrm{Pol}(q)
when
q-1
and
q
are crossing parameters.

•

If
q-1
is a crossing parameter and
q
is a U-turn, then there are four cases to consider,
depending on whether
J_{k}^{i}
intersects
\tilde{B}_{q}
or not, and whether it first intersects

\tilde{B}_{q+1}
or
\tilde{\beta}_{q+1}
. See Figure
35
.

Figure 35
.
The polygon
\mathrm{Pol}(q)
in the mixed case.

•

The symmetric case, when
q-1
is a U-turn parameter and
q
a crossing parameter.

•

In the case when both
q-1
and
1
are U-turn parameters, there are also four cases to
consider, depending on whether
J_{k}^{i}
first intersects
\tilde{\beta}_{q-2}
or

\tilde{B}_{q-1}
, and
\tilde{\beta}_{q+1}
or
\tilde{B}_{q+1}
. See Figure
36
.

Figure 36
.
The polygon
\mathrm{Pol}(q)
when
q-1
and
q
are U-turn parameters.

∎

Remark 10.8
.

In the case the junction
J_{k}^{i}
intersects the bar
\tilde{B}_{q}
, the polygon
\mathrm{Pol}(q)
is not
convex but rather made of two disjoint true polygons
\mathrm{Pol}(q)=\mathrm{Pol}^{1}(q)\sqcup\mathrm{Pol}^{2}(q)
, one at
the left of the bar
\tilde{B}_{q}
, the other one at its right. It can also happen that the
polygon
\mathrm{Pol}(q)
is degenerate, which happens if and only if the junction
J_{k}^{i}
coincides with
the bar
\tilde{B}_{q}
.

Because of the presence of these contractible polygons
\mathrm{Pol}(q)
, all the bars
\tilde{B}_{q}
(or
their projections
\overline{B}_{q}
to
\mathbf{S}
), for
q\in\{\psi_{k}^{i}+1,...,\psi_{k+1}^{i}\}
, are
said to be
superseded
by the polygon
\mathrm{Pol}(q)
. Each bar in the family
\overline{B}
is
superseded twice: if
\iota:\Theta\rightarrow\Theta
is the involution switching signs, then

\overline{B}_{q}
is superseded by
\mathrm{Pol}(q)
and
\overline{B}_{\iota q}
by
\mathrm{Pol}(\iota q)
.

Remark 10.9
.

In the special case when
q\in\{\psi_{k}^{i}+1,\ldots,\psi_{k+1}^{i}-1\}
is a U-turn parameter,
then the junction
J_{k}^{i}
does not intersect
\tilde{\beta}_{q}
, and the entirety of the simple
portion
\tilde{\mathcal{I}}_{q}
is included in the boundary of
\mathrm{Pol}(q)
. We say that
\overline{\mathcal{I}}_{q}

is
shielded
by the junction
J_{k}^{i}
: there is a contractible component of

\mathbf{S}\setminus\mathbf{c}^{\mathrm{f}}
containing
\overline{\mathcal{I}}_{q}
in its boundary.

10.2.
Upper bound on
\ell_{Y}(\Gamma_{\lambda})

The upper bound will be related to neutralizations within the family of polygonal curves

(\Gamma_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
. We invite the reader to remind themselves of the notations
introduced in §
8
, and in particular the notion of height of a cell

Z^{\mathbf{\Gamma}}_{K}
from Notation
8.27
, the conventions related to simultanous
decorations from §
8.5.3
, and the notion of surviving boundary segment

\mathcal{K}_{q}
,
q\in\Theta_{\mathrm{surv}}
, and associated height
Z_{{\mathbf{\Gamma}},{\mathbf{d}}}(\mathcal{K}_{q})
,
introduced in Definition
8.39
.

10.2.1.
A useful lemma

The following lemma, illustrated in Figure
37
, is an obvious consequence of the
variational characterization of geodesics and orthogeodesics. Here we use Lemma
8.11
, which
allows us to compare certain orthogedesics with the height function introduced in Definition

8.5
.

Proposition 10.10
.

Let
\Gamma=(J_{j},K_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{m}}
be a polygonal curve. Let

I=\{a,\ldots,b\}
be an interval of
\mathbb{Z}_{m}
of length
\geq 2
.
For
x,y
be two points in the roof
\tilde{J}_{I}
over
(K_{i})_{i\in I}
, denote as

\gamma_{xy}
the subsegment of
\tilde{J}_{I}
between
x
and
y
. Let

\gamma=\gamma_{1}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\kappa_{1}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\gamma_{2}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\cdots\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\kappa_{n}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\gamma_{n+1}
(with
n\geq 1
) be a path joining
x
and
y
, homotopic to

\gamma_{xy}
, such that:

•

for
1\leq i\leq n+1
,
\gamma_{i}
is a piecewise geodesic path;

•

for
1\leq i\leq n
,
\kappa_{i}
is a geodesic segment contained in a segment
K_{m_{i}}

for a
m_{i}\in I
.

Then, for any arbitrary subset
\pi
of
\mathbb{Z}_{m}
,

(10.8)

\displaystyle\ell(\gamma_{xy})\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq n\\
\kappa_{i}\notin\pi\end{subarray}}\ell(\kappa_{i})+\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}\ell(\gamma_{i})+\sum_{j\in I\cap\pi}(\log Z^{\Gamma}_{K_{j}}+2),

where
\kappa_{i}\notin\pi
means that the segment
K_{m_{i}}
containing
\kappa_{i}
has index
m_{i}\not\in\pi
.

Figure 37
.
Illustration of the length inequality in a neutralized polygonal curve.

In practice,
\gamma
will be simple (which implies that the indices
m_{i}
are monotonically
ordered), and the paths
\gamma_{i}
will not intersect the cells
(K_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{m}}
except at their
endpoints.

Remark 10.11
.

If
x
is the origin of
\tilde{J}_{I}
, then we can replace in this upper bound
\gamma_{1}
by any
piecewise geodesic path
\gamma^{\prime}_{1}
, starting anywhere on
K_{a-1}
and ending at the origin of

\kappa_{1}
. A similar remark holds if
y
is the endpoint of
\tilde{J}_{I}
lying in
K_{b+1}
.

The same result also holds with
I=\mathbb{Z}_{m}
,
\tilde{J}_{I}
replaced by
\Gamma
, and

\gamma=\gamma_{1}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\kappa_{1}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\gamma_{2}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\cdots\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\kappa_{n}
a
piecewise geodesic curve (hence closed), freely homotopic to
\Gamma
(the only difference being
that there is the same number of
\kappa_{i}
and
\gamma_{i}
, i.e. (
10.8
) now runs for

1\leq i\leq n
for both sums).

Proof.

First we observe that, under the hypotheses of the statement, by the variational definition of the
geodesic,

(10.9)

\displaystyle\ell(\gamma_{xy})\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell(\kappa_{i})+\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}\ell(\gamma_{i}).

We then observe that the path
\gamma
has to intersect each of the segments
W_{j_{i}}
defined
in Definition
8.10
. More precisely, there is a portion of
\gamma
containing
\kappa_{i}
and homotopic to a portion of
W_{j_{i}}
.
Thus, we replace
\gamma
by a piecewise geodesic path in the same homotopy class, where each segment

\kappa_{i}
such that
m_{i}\in\pi
is suppressed and replaced by a portion of
W_{m_{i}}
. The result then follows from the minimizing property of
\gamma_{xy}

together with Lemma
8.11
.
∎

10.2.2.
New representants of
\Gamma_{\lambda}

In the spirit of the previous length
inequality, we construct a new representant to each free homotopy class
\Gamma_{\lambda}
, which
approximates its length well.

Notation 10.12
.

Throughout the rest of this section, we fix an exhaustion

(\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d})=(\Gamma^{k},d^{k})_{1\leq k\leq t}
of the family of
polygonal curves
\Gamma=(\Gamma_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
. For

\lambda\in\Lambda
, write

\Gamma^{k}_{\lambda}=(J_{j}^{\lambda,k},K_{j}^{\lambda,k})_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{n_{\lambda}^{k}}}.

For
k=t
the terminal polygonal curve, we simply denote

\Gamma^{\infty}_{\lambda}=(J_{j}^{\lambda},K_{j}^{\lambda})_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{n_{\lambda}}}
.

Remember that we also fixed a
\xi\in\Xi
and
\mathcal{Q}\subseteq I(\xi)
, see Notation
10.3
.

In the following we focus on the elements
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}^{\Gamma}
, that is to say, boundary
curves. Let us call
\gamma_{\lambda}
the closed geodesic homotopic to
\Gamma^{1}_{\lambda}
. For

k\in\{1,\ldots,t\}
there exists a closed annular region
\mathrm{A}_{\lambda}^{k}
bordered, on one side
by
\gamma_{\lambda}
, on the other side by the polygonal curve
