\Gamma^{k}_{\lambda}
.
Note that we have the inclusion
\mathrm{A}_{\lambda}^{k}\subseteq\mathrm{A}_{\lambda}^{k-1}
for
k\geq 2
.

The following lemma is an immediate consequence of our construction.

Lemma 10.13
.

For
k\in\{1,\ldots,t\}
, we can find a simple, piecewise geodesic representative

\tilde{\Gamma}_{\lambda}^{k}
of the free homotopy class
\Gamma_{\lambda}
, contained in the annulus

\mathrm{A}_{\lambda}^{k}
, written as a cyclic concatenation

(10.10)

\displaystyle\tilde{\Gamma}_{\lambda}^{k}=\gamma_{1}^{\lambda,k}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\kappa_{1}^{\lambda,k}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\ldots\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\gamma^{\lambda,k}_{s_{\lambda}^{k}}\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}\kappa^{\lambda,k}_{s_{\lambda}^{k}}

where:

•

\gamma_{j}^{\lambda,k}
is piecewise geodesic, made of portions of junctions and bridges of

\Gamma^{k}_{\lambda}
;

•

\kappa_{j}^{\lambda,k}
is a geodesic segment contained in one of the cells
of
\Gamma_{\lambda}^{k}
.

The representative
\tilde{\Gamma}_{\lambda}^{k}
is unique if we ask that the annulus

\tilde{\mathrm{A}}_{\lambda}^{k}
bordered by
\gamma_{\lambda}
and by
\tilde{\Gamma}_{\lambda}^{k}
does not
contain any junction in its interior.

In particular,
\gamma_{j}^{\lambda,k}
does not intersect the cells of
\Gamma_{\lambda}^{k}
except at
its endpoints.

For
k=t
, we write
\gamma_{j}^{\lambda}=\gamma_{j}^{\lambda,t}
and

\kappa_{j}^{\lambda}=\kappa_{j}^{\lambda,t}
for
1\leq j\leq s_{\lambda}=s_{\lambda}^{t}
.

10.2.3.
Length inequalities: junctions

We prove the following comparison between the lengths of the paths

(\gamma_{j}^{\lambda,1})_{1\leq j\leq s_{\lambda}^{1}}
and the total length of all junctions.

Lemma 10.14
.

We have that

\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}^{\Gamma}}\sum_{j=1}^{s_{\lambda}^{1}}\ell(\gamma_{j}^{\lambda,1})\leq\sum_{p\in\Junc(\mathcal{Q})}\ell(p).

Proof.

We observe that, for
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}^{\Gamma}
, the union of paths

\bigcup_{1\leq j\leq s_{\lambda}^{1}}\gamma_{j}^{\lambda,1}
is composed of disjoint subsegments of
junctions, that we call
(j_{l}^{\lambda})_{1\leq l\leq M_{\lambda}}
, and subsegments of the bridges of

\Gamma^{1}
, which we denote as
(b_{l}^{\lambda})_{1\leq l\leq m_{\lambda}}
. Since the bridges of

\Gamma^{1}
are bars
\overline{B}_{q}
, the paths
(b_{l}^{\lambda})_{1\leq l\leq m_{\lambda}}
are
subsegments of bars.

We claim that to each segment
b_{l}^{\lambda}
we can associate a subset
P(b_{l}^{\lambda})
satisfying
the following properties:

•

P(b_{l}^{\lambda})
is included in
\bigcup_{p\in\Junc(\mathcal{Q})}p
and disjoint from all

(j^{\lambda^{\prime}}_{l^{\prime}})_{\lambda^{\prime},1\leq l^{\prime}\leq M_{\lambda^{\prime}}}
;

•

\ell(b_{l}^{\lambda})\leq\ell(P(b_{l}^{\lambda}))
;

•

the
P(b_{l}^{\lambda})
are mutually disjoint for distinct
(l,\lambda)
.

Indeed,
b_{l}^{\lambda}
is included in the bar
\overline{B}_{q}
for an index
q
. This means that

b_{l}^{\lambda}
is one of the boundary segments of the polygon
\mathrm{Pol}(q)\cup\mathrm{Pol}(\iota q)
. The two
polygons
\mathrm{Pol}(q)
and
\mathrm{Pol}(\iota q)
share a side, namely
\overline{B}_{q}
. Because their union
still contains the subset
b_{l}^{\lambda}
of
\overline{B}_{q}
in its boundary, their intersection

\mathrm{Pol}(q)\cap\mathrm{Pol}(\iota q)
must be a polygon, the boundary of which consists of the union of:

•

the entire segment
b_{l}^{\lambda}
itself;

•

a subset of
\bigcup_{p\in\Junc(\mathcal{Q})}p
made of pieces of boundary of
\mathrm{Pol}(q)
in the
interior of
\mathrm{Pol}(\iota q)
, or pieces of boundary of
\mathrm{Pol}(\iota q)
in the interior of

\mathrm{Pol}(q)
;

•

and, perharps, some pieces of
\beta_{\lambda(q)}
or
\beta_{\lambda(q-1)}
, orthogonal to

b_{l}^{\lambda}
.

We define
P(b_{l}^{\lambda})
to be the set of points in the boundary of
\mathrm{Pol}(q)\cap\mathrm{Pol}(\iota q)

which belong in
\bigcup_{p\in\Junc(\mathcal{Q})}p
. By an enumuration of all cases, a few of which are
illustrated in Figure
38
, we check that this set satisfies the length inequality, using
either the variational characterization of geodesics or orthogeodesics.

Figure 38
.
The construction of
P(b_{l}^{\lambda})
in all cases where
q
and
\iota(q)
are crossing
parameters. The overall shaded area is the union of
\mathrm{Pol}(q)
and
\mathrm{Pol}(\iota(q))
, with the
intersection represented in green. The dotted lines are the irrelevant portions of the junctions
and bars, and the dashed lines the multi-curve
\beta
.

We can then write

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}^{\Gamma}}\sum_{j=1}^{s_{\lambda}^{1}}\ell(\gamma_{j}^{\lambda,1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}^{\Gamma}\\
1\leq l\leq M_{\lambda}\end{subarray}}\ell(j^{\lambda}_{l})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}^{\Gamma}\\
1\leq l\leq m_{\lambda}\end{subarray}}\ell(b_{l}^{\lambda})

\displaystyle\leq\ell\Big(\bigsqcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}^{\Gamma}\\
1\leq l\leq M_{\lambda}\end{subarray}}j_{l}^{\lambda}\sqcup\bigsqcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}^{\Gamma}\\
1\leq l\leq m_{\lambda}\end{subarray}}P(b_{l}^{\lambda})\Big)\leq\sum_{p\in\Junc(\mathcal{Q})}\ell(p)

because the
j^{\lambda}_{l}
and
P(b_{l}^{\lambda})
are disjoint subsegments of the set of junctions.
∎

10.2.4.
Length inequalities and double points

Let us now prove some length inequalities related to the portions of cells

(\kappa_{j}^{\lambda})_{\lambda,j}
. We observe that the sets

(\beta_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}^{\beta}}
and

(\kappa_{j}^{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}^{\Gamma},1\leq j\leq s_{\lambda}}
are included in
\beta
,
and that each point of
\beta
belongs to at most two of them. In particular,

(10.11)

\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}^{\beta}}\ell_{Y}(\beta_{\lambda})+\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}^{\Gamma}}\sum_{1\leq j\leq s_{\lambda}}\ell(\kappa_{j}^{\lambda})\leq 2\ell_{Y}(\beta).

This is also true if we restrict the left-hand-side to smaller sets.

Notation 10.15
.

In the rest of this section, we shall consider arbitrary subsets
V^{\Gamma}\subseteq\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}^{\Gamma}
,

V^{\beta}\subseteq\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}^{\beta}
,
\pi\subseteq\Theta_{\mathrm{surv}}
. We let

V=V^{\beta}\sqcup V^{\Gamma}
. We write
\kappa_{j}^{\lambda}\notin\pi
iff the cell containing

\kappa_{j}^{\lambda}
is not in
\pi
. We denote

\Theta_{\tau}(\pi):=\bigcup_{q\in\pi}\Theta_{\tau}(q)
the set of indices corresponding to the
segments
\mathcal{K}_{q}
that are in
\pi
.

Plugging in these subsets, (
10.11
) directly implies

(10.12)

\sum_{\lambda\in V^{\beta}}\ell_{Y}(\beta_{\lambda})+\sum_{\lambda\in V^{\Gamma}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j\leq s_{\lambda}\\
\kappa_{j}^{\lambda}\notin\pi\end{subarray}}\ell(\kappa_{j}^{\lambda})\leq 2\ell_{Y}(\beta).

The aim of this subsection is to improve this inequality by determining the points which are
counted twice.

Definition 10.16
.

We call a point in
\beta

simple
if it satisfies either of these conditions:

•

it belongs to
\bigcup_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{in}}^{\beta}}\beta_{\lambda}
and to at most one
interval
\kappa_{j}^{\lambda}
with
\lambda\in V^{\Gamma}
and

\kappa_{j}^{\lambda}\notin\pi
;

•

it belongs to
\bigcup_{\lambda\in V^{\beta}}\beta_{\lambda}
and to none of the intervals

\kappa_{j}^{\lambda}
with
\lambda\in V^{\Gamma}
and
\kappa_{j}^{\lambda}\notin\pi
;

•

or it belongs to
\bigcup_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}^{\beta}\setminus V^{\beta}}\beta_{\lambda}
.

Other points are called
double
. We call
D
the set of double points.

Obviously, if
|D|
stands for the 1-dimensional Lebesgue measure of
D
as a subset of
\beta
,

(10.13)

\sum_{\lambda\in V^{\beta}}\ell_{Y}(\beta_{\lambda})+\sum_{\lambda\in V^{\Gamma}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j\leq s_{\lambda}\\
\kappa_{j}^{\lambda}\notin\pi\end{subarray}}\ell(\kappa_{j}^{\lambda})\leq\ell_{Y}(\beta)+|D|.

We now prove a bound on
|D|
.

Proposition 10.17
.

We have that

|D|\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q\in\mathcal{Q}\cap\Theta_{\ell}(V)\\
q\notin\Theta_{\tau}(\pi)\end{subarray}}\tilde{\theta}_{q}.

The proof essentially revolves around the following lemma.

Lemma 10.18
.

All the following points are simple:

(a)

points in
\mathcal{K}_{q}
where
\mathcal{K}_{q}
is a cell of
\Gamma_{\lambda}
with
\lambda\notin V^{\Gamma}

or
\kappa_{l}^{\lambda}\in\pi
;

(a’)

points in
\bigcup_{\lambda\notin V^{\beta}}\beta_{\lambda}
;

(b)

for
q\in I(\xi)
, points in
\overline{\mathcal{I}}_{q}\setminus[G_{q},D_{q}]
;

(c)

points in
\overline{\mathcal{I}}_{q}
where
q\not\in I(\xi)
;

(d)

for
q\in I(\xi)\setminus\mathcal{Q}
, all points in
\overline{\mathcal{I}}_{q}
;

Proof of Lemma
10.18
.

Items (a), (a’) are obvious.

Item (b) is also straightforward, because there are two junctions arriving at
G_{q}
and
D_{q}
,
which are responsible for the points in
\overline{\mathcal{I}}_{q}\setminus[G_{q},D_{q}]
being simple.

For item (c), if
q\not\in I(\xi)
then there is a junction that crosses
\beta_{\lambda(q)}

transversally, say at a point
x_{q}
, which is a vertex both of
\mathrm{Pol}(q)
and of
\mathrm{Pol}(q+1)
. The
two intervals
[x_{q},o(\overline{\mathcal{I}}_{q+1})]
and
[x_{q},t(\overline{\mathcal{I}}_{q})]
consist of simple
points (see Figure
39
), and their union contains
\overline{\mathcal{I}}_{q}
.

Figure 39
.
Illustration of case (c) in the proof of Lemma
10.18
. The two highlighted
polygonals are
\mathrm{Pol}(q)
and
\mathrm{Pol}(q+1)
. The dashed path is
\overline{\mathcal{I}}_{q}
.

Regarding item (d), if
q\in(I(\xi)\setminus\mathcal{Q})\cap\mathcal{C}
, there is again a junction that
crosses
\beta_{\lambda(q)}
transversally, and we can argue as for Item (c). If

q\in(I(\xi)\setminus\mathcal{Q})\cap\mathcal{U}
we use Remark
10.9
, showing that the
points in
\overline{\mathcal{I}}_{q}
are simple.
∎

Proof of Proposition
10.17
.

We write
\beta=\bigcup_{q\in\Theta}\overline{\mathcal{I}}_{q}
, where the geodesic segments are just
defined as subsets of
\beta
, that is to say, we forget about their orientations. These intervals
may overlap: this is the case if some of the
\theta_{q}
were negative, or were positive but very
large. By Lemma
10.18
, the only possibly double points are contained in segments

[G_{q},D_{q}]
where
q\in\mathcal{Q}\cap\Theta_{\ell}(V)
with
q\notin\Theta_{\tau}(\pi)
. This
implies the claim if we recall that
\tilde{\theta}_{q}
is the length of
[G_{q},D_{q}]
.
∎

10.2.5.
The upper bound

We are now ready to state and prove our upper bound.

Proposition 10.19
.

Let
\xi\in\Xi
,
\mathcal{Q}\subseteq I(\xi)
, and
(\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d})
be an exhaustion of

(\Gamma_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
. For any arbitrary sets
V\subseteq\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}
and

\pi\subseteq\Theta_{\mathrm{surv}}
, on the support of
\Psi_{\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d}}
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda\in V}\ell_{Y}(\Gamma_{\lambda})\leq\ell_{Y}({\mathbf{c}})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q\in\mathcal{Q}\cap\Theta_{\ell}(V)\\
q\notin\Theta_{\tau}(\pi)\end{subarray}}\tilde{\theta}_{q}+\sum_{q\in\pi}(\log Z_{\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d}}(\mathcal{K}_{q})+2)+\mathrm{fn}(r)

where
\Theta_{\tau}(\pi)=\bigcup_{q\in\pi}\Theta_{\tau}(q)
.

Proof.

Denote
V^{\beta}=V\cap\Lambda^{\beta}
and
V^{\Gamma}=V\cap\Lambda^{\Gamma}
.
For
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}^{\Gamma}
and
k\in\{1,\ldots,t-1\}
, we define

\mathcal{S}_{\lambda}^{k}=\{K\in\Cell(\Gamma_{\lambda}^{k}):\exists K^{\prime}\in\Cell(\Gamma^{k+1}),K^{\prime}\subseteq K\},

the set of cells of
\Gamma_{\lambda}^{k}
that “survive” at step
k+1
of the exhaustion. Denote

\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}^{k}=\Cell(\Gamma_{\lambda}^{k})\setminus\mathcal{S}_{\lambda}^{k}
the set of cells of

\Gamma_{\lambda}^{k}
that are neutralized at step
k+1
.
By repeated application of Proposition
10.10
, we obtain that, for any
Y
in the
support of
\Psi_{\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d}}
,

\displaystyle\ell_{Y}(\Gamma_{\lambda})=\ell(\gamma_{\lambda})\leq\sum_{j=1}^{s_{\lambda}}\ell(\gamma_{j}^{\lambda})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j\leq s_{\lambda}\\
\kappa_{j}^{\lambda}\notin\pi\end{subarray}}\ell(\kappa_{j}^{\lambda})+\sum_{q\in\pi\cap\Theta_{\ell}(\lambda)}(\log Z_{\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d}}(\mathcal{K}_{q})+2)

and, for
k\in\{2,\ldots,t\}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{s_{\lambda}^{k}}\ell(\gamma_{j}^{\lambda,k})\leq\sum_{j=1}^{s_{\lambda}^{k-1}}\ell(\gamma_{j}^{\lambda,k-1})+\sum_{K\in\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}^{k-1}}(\log Z^{\mathbf{\Gamma}}_{K}+2).

Put together, this yields

\displaystyle\ell_{Y}(\Gamma_{\lambda})\leq\sum_{j=1}^{s_{\lambda}^{1}}\ell(\gamma_{j}^{\lambda,1})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j\leq s_{\lambda}\\
\kappa_{j}^{\lambda}\notin\pi\end{subarray}}\ell(\kappa_{j}^{\lambda})+\sum_{q\in\pi\cap\Theta_{\ell}(\lambda)}(\log Z_{{\mathbf{\Gamma}},{\mathbf{d}}}(\mathcal{K}_{q})+2)+\sum_{k=1}^{t-1}\sum_{K\in\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}^{k}}(\log Z^{\mathbf{\Gamma}}_{K}+2).

Let us now use the decoration to further estimate this length. If
k\leq t-1
, then for any cell

K\in\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}^{k}
, there is a cell
K^{\prime}
of
\Gamma^{k}
, containing
K
, and bearing the
decoration
0
for the exhaustion
(\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d})
. As a result, by definition of
the test function
\Psi_{\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d}}
, on its support, we have

\log Z^{\mathbf{\Gamma}}_{K^{\prime}}\leq a+1
. Proposition
8.28
then implies

\log Z^{\mathbf{\Gamma}}_{K}\leq a+3
.
We can then deduce from the previous bound, after summation over
\lambda\in V^{\Gamma}
,
that

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda\in V^{\Gamma}}\ell_{Y}(\Gamma_{\lambda})\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in V^{\Gamma}\\
1\leq j\leq s_{\lambda}^{1}\end{subarray}}\ell(\gamma_{j}^{\lambda,1})+\sum_{\lambda\in V^{\Gamma}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j\leq s_{\lambda}\\
\kappa_{j}^{\lambda}\notin\pi\end{subarray}}\ell(\kappa_{j}^{\lambda})+\sum_{q\in\pi}(\log Z_{\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d}}(\mathcal{K}_{q})+2)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq k\leq t-1\\
\lambda\in V^{\Gamma}\\
K\in\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}^{k}\end{subarray}}(a+9).

In the last sum, the number of terms in the triple sum

\sum_{k=1}^{t-1}\sum_{\lambda\in V^{\Gamma}}\sum_{K\in\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}^{k}}
is less than the total
number of cells of all
\Gamma_{\lambda}
with
\lambda\in V^{\Gamma}
; this is less than
4r
.

We now use Lemma
10.14
, equation (
10.13
) and Proposition

10.17
to bound the sum over
\gamma_{j}^{\lambda}
and
\kappa_{j}^{\lambda}
. We obtain that

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda\in V}\ell_{Y}(\Gamma_{\lambda})=\sum_{\lambda\in V^{\Gamma}}\ell_{Y}(\Gamma_{\lambda})+\sum_{\lambda\in V^{\beta}}\ell_{Y}(\beta_{\lambda})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{p\in\Junc(\mathcal{Q})}\ell(p)+\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda^{\beta}}\ell_{Y}(\beta_{\lambda})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q\in\mathcal{Q}\cap\Theta_{\ell}(V)\\
q\notin\Theta_{\tau}(\pi)\end{subarray}}\tilde{\theta}_{q}+\sum_{q\in\pi}(\log Z_{\mathbf{\Gamma},\mathbf{d}}(\mathcal{K}_{q})+2)+\mathrm{fn}(r).

To conclude, we use the lower bound proven at the beginning of this section, (
10.6
).
∎

11.
Arbitrary loop topologies

We now indicate how to prove Theorem
1.23
when the multi-loop
{\mathbf{c}}
is not a generalized eight.
In this case, the representatives of the homotopy class of
{\mathbf{c}}
in minimal position may not be isotopic to one another. We choose arbitrarily one such representative – for instance, by putting an auxiliary hyperbolic metric on
{\mathbf{S}}
and choosing the geodesic representative of
{\mathbf{c}}
(possibly perturbing it to get rid of multiple intersections).

Let us denote by
\mathcal{N}\subseteq\mathbf{S}
a regular neighbourhood of
{\mathbf{c}}
. The pair

(\mathcal{N},{\mathbf{c}})
is a generalized eight, and we may apply the construction of the pairs of
pants decomposition of §
3.2
. We keep the same notation

(\Gamma_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
for the pair of pants decomposition of
\mathcal{N}
, with the
indices
\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}=\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}^{\beta}\sqcup\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}^{\Gamma}
corresponding to the boundary components of

\mathcal{N}
, split into the ones which are, or not, components of the simple multi-loop
\beta
.

In
\mathbf{S}
, some of the curves
(\Gamma_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}}
may be
contractible. Proposition
1.30
tells us that the existence of contractible
\beta_{\lambda}
and

\Gamma_{\lambda}
should help to prove Theorem
1.23
, by making comparison estimates easier.
On the other hand, contractible boundary components cause two new difficulties.

•

While it is relatively easy to enumerate topological types of generalized eights, it seems
daunting to make a list of all topological types of multi-loops. We sidestep this difficulty by
only ever working with a fixed topological type, in this article. In the applications to spectral
gaps of random hyperbolic surfaces
[
4
]
, the discussion involves all possible topological
types appearing in the Selberg trace formula, but we condition the Weil–Petersson measure on the
set of tangle-free surfaces, in order to drastically reduce the number of local topological types
to consider.

•

When we vary the metric
Y
on
\mathbf{S}
and consider the piecewise geodesic representative
of
\mathbf{c}
, as done in §
4.2.1
, the orthogeodesics

(\overline{B}_{q})_{q\in\Theta}
may be ill-defined. This happens if
B_{q}
is a
problematic
bar
in the sense of Definition
11.4
.

11.1.
Diagrams and the reconstitution procedure

We recall the notion of diagram
\mathbf{D}
representing the multi-loop
\mathbf{c}
introduced in
Definition
2.19
. A diagram is the data of the simple multi-loop
\beta
and the bars

B=(B_{1},\ldots,B_{r})
.

11.1.1.
Simple portions of diagrams

We extend the notions of simple portions, introduced in Definition
1.21
for
multi-loops, to diagrams as follows: a
simple portion
of
\mathbf{D}
is a maximal
subsegment of
\beta
that does not meet the endpoints of the bars
B
. These simple portions may
be
shielded
or
unshielded
, following once again Definition
1.21
. The
(un)shielded simple portion of
{\mathbf{c}}
are in natural bijection with (un)shielded simple
portion of
\mathbf{D}
.

We do not apply this terminology to the bars
B_{k}
, because they do not play the same role as the
curves
\beta_{i}
: indeed, when we describe the isotopy class of
\mathbf{c}
by following its
diagram, all the bars are traversed twice, whilst each simple portion of
\beta
only once.

11.1.2.
Reconstitution procedure

Given a diagram
\mathbf{D}=(\beta,B)
, we may reconstitute a multi-loop
\mathbf{c}
stemming from

\mathbf{D}
by the following procedure, guided by the
orientation rules
in §
2.2.2
.

The endpoints of the bars cut
\beta
into
2r
non-oriented segments
\mathcal{I}_{j}
.
Pick a starting segment
\mathcal{I}_{j_{0}}
and pick arbitrarily one of the two possible orientations of
this segment. There a unique closed path starting with
\mathcal{I}_{j_{0}}
, obtained by concatenating
alternatively bars
B_{k}
and segments
\mathcal{I}_{j}
, and respecting the orientation
rules. If this path goes through all segments
\mathcal{I}_{j}
, then we obtain a loop (with one component)
stemming from
\mathbf{D}
. If not, select another segment
\mathcal{I}_{j}
that has not been traversed yet,
and repeat the procedure until exhaustion of all segments
\mathcal{I}_{j}
.

Starting from a diagram
\mathbf{D}
with
r
bars, and letting

\Theta_{\mathbf{D}}:=\{1,\ldots,r\}\times\{\pm\}
, applying the reconstitution procedure gives
us an orientation of each segment
\mathcal{I}_{j}
and their labelling by elements of
\Theta_{\mathbf{D}}
,
as well as two oriented copies
(B_{1}^{\pm},\ldots,B_{r}^{\pm})
of each bar, labelled by

\Theta_{\mathbf{D}}
.

If we start with a multi-loop
\mathbf{c}
and obtain a diagram
\mathbf{D}
by opening the
intersections (either way), the reconstitution procedure will give us back a multi-loop isotopic to

\mathbf{c}
(modulo orientation of each component).

The reader may check that a different choice of the original orientation of
\mathcal{I}_{j_{0}}
will just
reverse the orientation of the corresponding component of
\mathbf{c}
. This means that if a
multi-loop stemming from
\mathbf{D}
has
\mathfrak{n}
components, then the reconstitution procedure can
lead to
2^{\mathfrak{n}}
multi-loops stemming from
\mathbf{D}
, all having the same geometric image, and
differing only by orientation of each component.

It seems difficult to determine when two diagrams lead to homotopic multi-loops; we do not address
this problem in this paper. In the case of generalized eights, this problem does not occur and we can
state the following:

Remark 11.1
.

Let
\mathbf{D}
be a diagram. Assume that
{\mathbf{S}}\setminus\mathbf{D}
has no connected component which
is a disk. Then a multi-loop stemming from
\mathbf{D}
is a generalized eight and,
as a consequence,
\mathbf{D}
represents a generalized eight; all generalized eights leading to the
diagram
\mathbf{D}
are isotopic (modulo orientation of each component).

Note that each component of a multi-loop
\mathbf{c}
obtained by the reconstitution procedure may
be written by a concatenation such as the one we have obtained in §
2.5.1
,
see (
2.6
).

11.1.3.
Extended diagrams

We can extend the notation of diagram, discarding the condition that the bars do not self-intersect:

Definition 11.2
.

An extended diagram
\mathbf{D}
in
\mathbf{S}
is the data (modulo isotopy) of a collection of
simple disjoint loops
\beta
, and of a collection of bars
B=(B_{1},\ldots,B_{r})
, defined as
closed segments with distinct endpoints lying in
\beta
, and disjoint from
\beta
otherwise.

The reconstitution procedure of §
11.1.2
also applies to an extended diagram

\mathbf{D}
.
Simple portions
of an extended diagram are defined as for usual diagrams. A
simple portion of
\mathbf{D}
is said to be shielded (in
\mathbf{S}
) if it lies in the boundary of a
contractible component of
\mathbf{S}\setminus\mathbf{D}
.

Definition 11.3
.

Let
\mathbf{D}=(\beta,B)
be an extended diagram in
\mathbf{S}
, and let

\mathbf{c}=(\mathbf{c}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{c}_{\mathfrak{n}})
be a multi-loop obtained from
\mathbf{D}
by
the reconstitution procedure. We say that
\mathbf{D}

satisfies the minimality condition

if:

•

the components
\mathbf{c}_{i}
are non-contractible;

•

if
\tilde{\mathbf{c}}_{i}
is a lift of a component of
\mathbf{c}
in the universal cover of

\mathbf{S}
, then
\tilde{\mathbf{c}}_{i}
has no self-intersection;

•

any two lifts
\tilde{\mathbf{c}}_{i}
,
\tilde{\mathbf{c}}_{j}
of components of
\mathbf{c}
in
the universal cover of
\mathbf{S}
can intersect at most once (with the convention that a shared bar
counts for 1 intersection point).

Remark that if we start with a multi-loop
\mathbf{c}
in minimal position, as we always do, then
any diagram
\mathbf{D}
representing it automatically satisfies the minimality condition; so this
is a natural condition to ask for.

Definition 11.4
.

A bar of an extended diagram is
problematic
if it satisfies
either of the following two conditions:

•

one of its endpoints lies on a contractible loop
\beta_{i}
;

•

its two endpoints lie on one same loop
\beta_{i}
, and the bar is homotopically trivial with
endpoints gliding along
\beta_{i}
.

Definition 11.5
.

A bar of an extended diagram is
cylindrical
if it runs between
\beta_{i}
and
\beta_{j}
with
i\not=j
and
\beta_{i},\beta_{j}
are non-contractible, in the same homotopy class.

Cylindrical bars count among the non-problematic bars.

Definition 11.6
.

An extended diagram
\mathbf{D}
is said to be
good
if it has no problematic bar and if it satisfies the minimality condition.

11.2.
An auxiliary generalized eight: the skeleton

The forthcoming Proposition
11.13
says that, by opening intersections from a multi-loop

{\mathbf{c}}
, we can create a generalized eight that has length
smaller
than

{\mathbf{c}}
for any hyperbolic metric,
and still fills
{\mathbf{S}}
(or a surface
obtained by removing cylinders from
{\mathbf{S}}
). Such an auxilliary loop is called a

skeleton
; simple examples are represented in Figure
40
.

11.2.1.
Removing one disk

We first prove the following lemma.

Lemma 11.7
.

Let
\mathbf{D}
be a diagram filling
{\mathbf{S}}
. Assume that at least one of the connected
components of
{\mathbf{S}}\setminus\mathbf{D}
is homeomorphic to a disk. Then we can remove one of
the bars of
\mathbf{D}
so that the resulting diagram
\mathbf{D}^{\prime}

fills a surface
{\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}
obtained by removing cylinders from
\mathbf{S}
.

We recall that we have defined the ways to remove a bar in a diagram in §
2.4
. By
construction,
{\mathbf{S}}\setminus\mathbf{D}^{\prime}
has exactly one less embedded disk than

{\mathbf{S}}\setminus\mathbf{D}
among its connected components.

Proof.

If one of the loops
\beta_{i}
are contractible, it borders a disk
D_{i}
. We remove a bar
B_{k}

touching
\beta_{i}
(and another components
\beta_{j}
) in the second way, to obtain
\mathbf{D}^{\prime}
. The
filled surface
{\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}
has same Euler characteristic as
{\mathbf{S}}
. If
B_{k}
lies
inside
D_{i}
, then
\beta_{j}
is also contractible and obviously
{\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}={\mathbf{S}}
.
Otherwise, the bar
B_{k}
is outside the disk
D_{i}
. We have
{\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}={\mathbf{S}}
except
in one particular case, which occurs when
\beta_{i}=\beta_{j}
. In this case,
{\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}
is
obtained from
{\mathbf{S}}
by removing a cylinder.

Assume now that none of the
\beta_{i}
are contractible. Consider a contractible connected component
D
of
{\mathbf{S}}\setminus\mathbf{D}
: pick a bar
B_{k}
in the boundary of this contractible component and remove it the first way. Either
{\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}={\mathbf{S}}
, or
{\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}
is obtained from
{\mathbf{S}}
by removing a cylinder (this can only happen if the two sides of
B_{k}
touch the open disk
D
).
∎

Examples of both cases appearing in this lemma can be found in Figure
40
.

Figure 40
.
Three examples of (a) loops, (b) diagrams
D^{(1)}
and (c) skeletons (shown in solid lines). The diagram
D^{(2)}
is obtained by reincorporating the dashed bar to the skeleton. The two first examples are homotopic, non-isotopic loops.

11.2.2.
Skeleton
\mathbf{D}^{\prime}

By iteration of the process, and using Remark
11.1
, we obtain:

Corollary 11.8
.

For any diagram
\mathbf{D}
filling
{\mathbf{S}}
, we can remove bars so that the resulting
diagram
\mathbf{D}^{\prime}
fills a surface
{\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}
obtained by removing cylinders from
\mathbf{S}
,
and so that no connected components of
{\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}\setminus\mathbf{D}^{\prime}
is contractible. The
diagram
\mathbf{D}^{\prime}
represents a generalized eight
{\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}
.

Definition 11.9
.

Let
{\mathbf{c}}
be a multi-loop with
r
self-intersections, and
\mathbf{D}^{(1)}=(\beta,B)
be the
diagram obtained by opening all the intersections of
{\mathbf{c}}
the first way. We call

skeleton
of
{\mathbf{c}}
any diagram
\mathbf{D}^{\prime}=(\beta^{\prime},B^{\prime})
obtained from
\mathbf{D}^{(1)}
by
application of Corollary
11.8
. We denote as
r^{\prime}\leq r
its the number of bars and

\Theta^{\prime}=\{1,\ldots,r^{\prime}\}\times\{\pm\}
. We also refer to the generalized eight
{\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}

stemming from
\mathbf{D}^{\prime}
, obtained by removing intersections from
{\mathbf{c}}
, as a skeleton of

\mathbf{c}
.

Remark 11.10
.

We have
\chi(\mathbf{S})=\chi(\mathbf{S}^{\prime})=r^{\prime}
.

Remark 11.11
.

A skeleton is not uniquely defined, because it depends on the order in which we choose the intersection points to remove when iterating Lemma
11.7
. It suffices for us to know that a skeleton exists.
The forthcoming discussion is completely dependent on the choice of a skeleton.

Remark 11.12
.

Even when
\mathbf{S}
is connected, the surface
{\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}
may be disconnected, and some
connected components may be cylinders containing a component of
{\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}
which is a simple
curve. This happens, in particular, when
\beta_{i}
and
\beta_{j}^{\pm 1}
are non-contractible
curves bordering a cylinder containing a third curve
\beta_{k}
, in which case
\beta_{k}
becomes a
separate connected component of
\mathbf{S}^{\prime}
once all its adjacent bars are removed the first
way.

Note however that if a bar of
\mathbf{D}
joins two non-contractible curves
\beta_{i}
, and is not in the
boundary of a contractible component of
{\mathbf{S}}\setminus\mathbf{D}
, then this bar stays in each
skeleton
\mathbf{D}^{\prime}
. A skeleton
\mathbf{D}^{\prime}
never contains the cylindrical bars and
\mathbf{S}^{\prime}
may have more
connected components than
\mathbf{S}
, including cylinders (filled by a simple curve).

The multi-curve
\beta^{\prime}
corresponding to a skeleton
{\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}
is homotopic to the collection
of non-contractible components of
\beta
in
\mathbf{D}^{(1)}
. In particular, its number of components is
smaller.

11.2.3.
Length inequality and skeleton

We are now ready to prove our length inequality.

Proposition 11.13
.

Let
{\mathbf{c}}
be a multi-loop in minimal position, filling
{\mathbf{S}}
. Let

{\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}
be a skeleton of
{\mathbf{c}}
. Then, for any hyperbolic Riemannian metric
Y

on
{\mathbf{S}}
,

(11.1)

\displaystyle\ell_{Y}({\mathbf{c}}^{\prime})\leq\ell_{Y}({\mathbf{c}}).

The proof relies on the following lemma.

Lemma 11.14
.

Let
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
be two multi-loops differing by one third Reidemeister move. Let
c_{1}^{\prime}
be
obtained from
c_{1}
by removing some intersections (in the first or the second way). Then there
exists
c^{\prime}_{2}
, homotopic to
c_{1}^{\prime}
, obtained from
c_{2}
by removing some intersections.

Proof of Lemma
11.14
.

This can be checked by simple inspection of a finite number of cases. Two cases are shown in
Figure
41
.

Figure 41
.
Illustration of two cases arising in the proof of Lemma
11.14
.

∎

Proof of Proposition
11.13
.

A skeleton
{\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}
is obtained from
{\mathbf{c}}
by removing some intersections. Let

Y
be a hyperbolic Riemannian metric
Y
on
{\mathbf{S}}
and
\gamma
be the geodesic
representative of
{\mathbf{c}}
. By definition,
\ell_{Y}({\mathbf{c}})=\ell(\gamma)
. Since

\gamma
is a geodesic,
\gamma
is in minimal position: by the results of
[
7
]
it is
isotopic to
{\mathbf{c}}
up to a finite number of third Reidemeister moves.

For each Reidemeister move, we shall use Lemma
11.14
. We obtain a multi-loop

\gamma^{\prime}
homotopic to the skeleton
{\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}
, obtained from
\gamma
by opening some
intersections. In the homotopy class of
\gamma^{\prime}
, there is a representative
\gamma_{\varepsilon}

which just differs from
\gamma
in an
\varepsilon
-neighbourhood of the intersections (with

\varepsilon>0
arbitrarily small) and such that
\ell(\gamma_{\varepsilon})<\ell(\gamma)
. More
precisely,
\gamma_{\varepsilon}
may be obtained from local “switchings”, described as follows:
take embedded
\varepsilon
-disks
D_{1},\ldots,D_{r}
around each of the intersection points

a_{1},\ldots,a_{r}
, such that
D_{i}\cap\gamma
is the union of two simple geodesic segments
intersecting once, say
[x_{1},x_{3}]\cup[x_{2},x_{4}]
. The boundary
\partial D_{i}\cap\gamma

consists of the four cyclically ordered points
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}
, and the multi-loop

\gamma_{\epsilon}
with removed intersections is such that

D_{i}\cap\gamma_{\epsilon}=[x_{1},x_{2}]\cup[x_{2},x_{3}]
. Outside the disks
D_{i}
, we have

\gamma_{\epsilon}=\gamma
. By the triangle inequality,

\ell([x_{1},x_{2}]\cup[x_{2},x_{3}])<\ell([x_{1},x_{3}]\cup[x_{2},x_{4}])
. Equivalently,

\ell(\gamma_{\varepsilon})<\ell(\gamma)
. To prove the proposition, we combine the two
inequalities
\ell_{Y}({\mathbf{c}}^{\prime})\leq\ell(\gamma_{\varepsilon})
, and

\ell(\gamma_{\varepsilon})<\ell(\gamma)=\ell_{Y}({\mathbf{c}})
.
∎

11.3.
Good representative of
\mathbf{c}

The goal of this section is to construct a new representative
\mathbf{D}^{\mathrm{opt}}
of
{\mathbf{c}}
which is a
good extended diagram: recall that this means a diagram without problematic bars (Definition

11.4
) and satisfying the minimality condition (Definition
11.3
).

11.3.1.
Diagrams
\mathbf{D}^{(2)}
and
\mathbf{D}^{\prime\prime}

From the construction of
{\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}
, we see that there exists a diagram
\mathbf{D}^{(2)}
on
\mathbf{S}
,
representing
{\mathbf{c}}
, obtained by:

•

gluing to some cylinders to
\mathbf{S}^{\prime}
to obtain
\mathbf{S}
;

•

adding a collection of bars to the diagram
\mathbf{D}^{\prime}
in
\mathbf{S}
.

Remark 11.15
.

The diagram
\mathbf{D}^{(2)}
is in general different from
\mathbf{D}^{(1)}
, because

\mathbf{D}^{(1)}
was obtained by opening all intersections the first way, whereas
\mathbf{D}^{(2)}
is
obtained from
{\mathbf{c}}
by opening the second way certain intersections (running between
contractible components of
\beta
).

The added bars may be problematic or non-problematic (cylindrical or not). Since the components of

\beta^{\prime}
in
\mathbf{D}^{(2)}
are non-contractible, the problematic bars of
\mathbf{D}^{(2)}
are only of one
kind: they are homotopically trivial bars, running from a component of
\beta^{\prime}
to itself.

Recall that the (un)shielded simple portions of
{\mathbf{c}}
are in natural bijection with
(un)shielded simple portions of
\mathbf{D}^{(1)}
, and of
\mathbf{D}^{(2)}
. A simple portion of
\mathbf{D}^{\prime}
can be
written as a disjoint union of simple portion of
\mathbf{D}^{(2)}
, only one of which may be
unshielded. Since
\mathbf{D}^{\prime}
is a generalized eight, it has exactly
2\chi(\mathbf{S})
simple portions. We
deduce the following.

Lemma 11.16
.

\mathbf{D}^{(1)}
and
\mathbf{D}^{(2)}
have at most
2\chi(\mathbf{S})
unshielded simple portions.

Definition 11.17
.

A simple portion of
\mathbf{D}^{\prime}
which contains only shielded simple portions of
\mathbf{D}^{(2)}
will be
called
a priori neutral
.

We introduce a last auxilliary diagram.

Notation 11.18
.

Let
\mathbf{D}^{\prime\prime}
be the diagram obtained by removing from
\mathbf{D}^{(2)}
its problematic bars.

Lemma 11.19
.

\mathbf{D}^{\prime\prime}
is a good diagram filling
\mathbf{S}
.

11.3.2.
Diagram
\mathbf{D}^{\mathrm{opt}}

We now prove the following.

Theorem 11.20
.

Let
\mathbf{c}
be a multi-loop in minimal position. Assume that
\mathbf{c}
is connected and
not double-filling. Then there exists good extended diagram
\mathbf{D}^{\mathrm{opt}}
, containing all
the non-problematic bars of
\mathbf{D}^{(2)}
, such that
\mathbf{c}
is homotopic to a
multi-loop stemming from
\mathbf{D}^{\mathrm{opt}}
.

Remark 11.21
.

We may assume w.l.o.g. that
\mathbf{c}
is connected. If the surface
\mathbf{S}
filled by
\mathbf{c}
is not connected, the previous theorem applies in each connected component of
\mathbf{S}
.

An example of a loop which is not a generalized eight, and a good extended diagram for this loop,
is represented in Figure
42
.

(a)

The loop
\mathbf{c}
.

(b)

The diagram
\mathbf{D}^{\mathrm{opt}}
.

Figure 42
.
Example of Theorem
11.20
.

Proof.

We prove the result by induction on the total number
n
of problematic bars of

\mathbf{D}^{(2)}
. The case
n=0
is obvious as we can take
\mathbf{D}^{\mathrm{opt}}=\mathbf{D}^{(2)}
.

Assume that
n\geq 1
, and that the expected property is known until rank

n-1
. By hypothesis, the diagram
\mathbf{D}^{(2)}
contains a problematic bar

B_{q}
. Because the bar is problematic regardless of its orientation, we assume w.l.o.g. that

\sign(q)=+
. By construction of
\mathbf{D}^{(2)}
, the bar

B_{q}
goes from one component

\beta^{\prime}_{\lambda}
to itself and is homotopically trivial with gliding endpoints. Denote by

D
the topological disc bordered by
B_{q}
and

\beta^{\prime}_{\lambda}
. One of the simple portions adjacent to

B_{q}
is shielded. To fix ideas, we assume it is
\mathcal{I}_{q}
.

A representative
{\mathbf{c}}^{\mathrm{op}}
of the homotopy class of
\mathbf{c}
may be obtained by applying the
reconstitution procedure, and allows to write the homotopy class of
c
as an
alternating concatenation of bars and simple portions. Denote
i_{1}=\comp(q)
and

i_{2}=\comp(\iota q)
so that the bars
B_{q}
and
B_{\iota q}
belong to the components

{\mathbf{c}}^{\mathrm{op}}_{i_{1}}
and
{\mathbf{c}}^{\mathrm{op}}_{i_{2}}
of
{\mathbf{c}}^{\mathrm{op}}
respectively.

Recall that
B_{\iota q}
is the same bar as
B_{q}
with either the same or the reverse
orientation. We treat the case where it has reverse orientation, the other case being similar
modulo changes of notation. The local situation near the bar
B_{q}
is shown on Figure

43
.

(a)

B_{q}
and
{\mathbf{c}}^{\mathrm{op}}_{i_{1}}
.

(b)

B_{\iota q}
and
{\mathbf{c}}^{\mathrm{op}}_{i_{2}}
.

Figure 43
.
The local situation near the bar
B_{q}
.

We introduce a new surface
\mathbf{S}_{\circ}\subseteq\mathbf{S}
by taking a regular neighbourhood
\mathcal{N}
of

\mathbf{D}^{(2)}
in
\mathbf{S}
and gluing to
\mathcal{N}
all connected components of

\mathbf{S}\setminus\mathbf{D}^{(2)}
that are contractible, except the connected component contained
in the disc
D
and touching the bar
B_{q}
.

In the diagram
\mathbf{D}^{(2)}
, now viewed as a diagram on
\mathbf{S}_{\circ}
, the bar
B_{q}
is
non-problematic. We can therefore apply the induction hypothesis at rank
n-1
to the multi-loop

\mathbf{c}
on
\mathbf{S}_{\circ}
. We obtain a good extended diagram
\mathbf{D}^{\mathrm{opt}}_{\circ}
on
\mathbf{S}_{\circ}
,
containing all non-problematic bars of
(\mathbf{S}_{\circ},\mathbf{D}^{(2)})
(and in particular
B_{q}
), and
such that
\mathbf{c}
is homotopic on
\mathbf{S}_{\circ}
to a multi-loop stemming from
\mathbf{D}^{\mathrm{opt}}_{\circ}
.

In
\mathbf{S}_{\circ}
the diagram
\mathbf{D}^{\mathrm{opt}}_{\circ}
is good, and in
\mathbf{S}
it has exactly one problematic
bar, namely
B_{q}
. We shall define the desired diagram
\mathbf{D}^{\mathrm{opt}}
by replacing
B_{q}
by a new
non-problematic bar
B_{\mathrm{new}}
.

Applying the reconstitution procedure to
\mathbf{D}^{\mathrm{opt}}_{\circ}
, for each
1\leq i\leq\mathfrak{n}
, we obtain
a representative
\mathbf{c}_{i}^{\circ}
of the homotopy class of
{\mathbf{c}}_{i}
as a
concatenation of bars and simple portions of
\mathbf{D}^{\mathrm{opt}}_{\circ}
. The construction will consist in
replacing the two components
{\mathbf{c}}^{\circ}_{i_{1}}
and
{\mathbf{c}}^{\circ}_{i_{2}}
,
containing
B_{q}
and
B_{\iota q}
respectively, by new representatives

{\mathbf{c}}^{\mathrm{opt}}_{i_{1}}
and
{\mathbf{c}}^{\mathrm{opt}}_{i_{2}}
. The other components

({\mathbf{c}}^{\circ}_{i})_{i\neq i_{1},i_{2}}
will stay untouched.

Lifting the situation to the hyperbolic plane, for
l\in\{1,2\}
, we can write the lift

\tilde{\mathbf{c}}_{i_{l}}^{\circ}
of
\mathbf{c}_{i_{l}}^{\circ}
as an infinite concatenation

\mathord{\mathchoice{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}{\,\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\,}}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\tilde{p}_{k}^{l}
where, for each
k
,
\tilde{p}_{k}^{l}
is the concatenation
of lifts
\tilde{B}_{k}^{l}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{I}}_{k}^{l}
of a bar and a simple portion of

\mathbf{D}^{\mathrm{opt}}_{\circ}
. We pick the indices so that
\tilde{p}^{1}_{0}
and and
\tilde{p}_{0}^{2}
contain a lift
of
B_{q}
and
B_{\iota q}
respectively.

Denote by
\tilde{\beta}_{k}^{l}
the complete geodesic on
\mathbb{H}^{2}
containing
\tilde{\mathcal{I}}^{l}_{k}
. We have

\tilde{\beta}_{0}^{1}=\tilde{\beta}_{0}^{2}
and we call
m>0
the smallest integer such that

\tilde{\beta}_{m}^{1}\not=\tilde{\beta}_{m}^{2}
. By the minimality condition for

{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{opt}}_{\circ}
, all
k\in\{0,\ldots,m-1\}
are crossing indices. We define
the bar
\tilde{B}_{\mathrm{new}}
on
\mathbb{H}^{2}
as the segment going from
t(\tilde{B}^{1}_{m})
to

t(\tilde{B}^{2}_{m})
, and let
B_{\mathrm{new}}
be its projection to
\mathbf{S}
. See Figure

44
. The desired diagram
\mathbf{D}^{\mathrm{opt}}
is defined from
\mathbf{D}^{\mathrm{opt}}_{\circ}
by removing
B_{q}
and
adding the non-problematic bar
B_{\mathrm{new}}
.

Figure 44
.
The new bar
B_{\mathrm{new}}
replacing
B_{q}
.

∎

Remark 11.22
.

At each induction step the number of unshielded simple portions of
\mathbf{D}^{\mathrm{opt}}
in
\mathbf{S}

stays the same as for the original
\mathbf{D}^{(2)}
, hence it is smaller that
2\chi(\mathbf{S})
.

11.4.
Sketch of proof for general topologies

We now explain how to prove Theorem
1.23
for general multi-loops
\mathbf{c}
.

11.4.1.
Coordinates on Teichmüller spaces

Since the diagram
\mathbf{D}^{\prime}
represents a generalized eight
\mathbf{c}^{\prime}
in
\mathbf{S}^{\prime}
, we can use
it to define coordinates
(\vec{L},\vec{\theta})\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{r^{\prime}}\times\mathbb{R}^{\Theta^{\prime}}
on

\mathcal{T}^{*}_{g_{\mathbf{S}^{\prime}},n_{\mathbf{S}^{\prime}}}
.

The surface
\mathbf{S}
is obtained by gluing cylinders to
\mathbf{S}^{\prime}
. This gives rise to coordinates

(\vec{L},\vec{\theta},\vec{\alpha})
on
\mathcal{T}^{*}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}
, obtained by adding some twist
coordinates
\vec{\alpha}
along the boundary of these cylinders, and getting rid of an equivalent
number of parameters
\theta_{q}
that have become redundant (corresponding to components of
\beta^{\prime}

homotopic in
\mathbf{S}
). Note that
\mathcal{T}^{*}_{g_{\mathbf{S}^{\prime}},n_{\mathbf{S}^{\prime}}}
and
\mathcal{T}^{*}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}
have the same dimension,
that is,
3\chi(\mathbf{S})
.

Recall that certain simple portions of
\mathbf{c}^{\prime}
are declared to be
a priori
neutral
. The corresponding parameters
\theta_{q}
are
a priori neutral
coordinates. A
priori neutral parameters, as well as all the twist parameters
\vec{\alpha}
, will be neutral
parameters throughout this discussion.

Remark 11.23
.

If
\mathbf{c}
is double-filling, all parameters
(\theta_{q})_{q\in\Theta}
are a priori neutral.

11.4.2.
Expression of
\ell_{Y}(\mathbf{c})

Denote by
\mathbf{D}^{\mathrm{opt}}=(\beta^{\mathrm{opt}},B^{\mathrm{opt}})
the good extended diagram constructed in
Theorem
11.20
. The reconstitution procedure, applied to the diagram
\mathbf{D}^{\mathrm{opt}}
, leads to a
representative of the homotopy class of
{\mathbf{c}}
in the form of an alternance of bars and
segments similar to (
2.7
).

Since none of the bars of
\mathbf{D}^{\mathrm{opt}}
is homotopically trivial with gliding endpoints, we can replace
the writing (
2.7
) by a geodesic representative, as described in §
4.2.1
. Indeed, we pick the representative of the metric
Y\in\mathcal{T}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}^{*}
such
that
\beta^{\prime}
is a multi-geodesic, and glide the bars
B^{\mathrm{opt}}_{q}
to their orthogeodesic
representatives
\overline{B}_{q}
, and then take a representative of
\mathbf{c}
of the
form (
4.3
). In the special case of multiple components
\beta^{\prime}_{i}
in the same
homotopy class, their geodesic representatives are collapsed to the same curve, and the cylindrical
bars
\overline{B}_{q}
running between them are reduced to a point. Theorem

4.6
then provides an expression for the length of each component

{\mathbf{c}}_{i}
with
1\leq i\leq\mathfrak{n}
, with the bars and segments corresponding to those of

\mathbf{D}^{\mathrm{opt}}
.

11.4.3.
Rest of the proof

Once the expression for
\ell_{Y}(\mathbf{c})
is obtained, the proof is similar to the one for
generalized eights:

(1)

we get rid of negative crossing parameters using the erasing procedure from §
7
;

(2)

we write the integral in the new coordinates, with an additional set of a priori neutral
coordinates defined in §
11.4.1
(note that each non-neutral coordinate
\theta

coincides with the length of an unshielded simple portion of
\mathbf{D}^{\mathrm{opt}}
, up to addition of a function
of neutral variables);

(3)

we apply our results about generalized convolutions in the active variables

\Theta_{\mathrm{ac}}^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\Theta
;

(4)

we conclude with a comparison estimate, which is easier to prove now that some lengths

\ell(\Gamma_{\lambda})
(corresponding to contractible components) vanish.

Remark 11.24
.

The choice of
\mathrm{K}
in Theorem
1.23
is still given by equation (
9.6
), but
now the the a-priori-neutral parameters have been removed from the new set of active parameter

\Theta_{\mathrm{ac}}^{\prime\prime}
. Hence,

(11.2)

\displaystyle\mathrm{K}=\sum_{q\in\Theta_{\mathrm{ac}}^{\prime\prime}}(\mathrm{K}_{q}+1)\leq\sum_{j\in V^{\prime}}(\mathrm{K}_{j}-1)+\rho[\mathbf{T}]

where the sum
V^{\prime}\subseteq V
is the set of
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{BC}}^{\beta}\cap V
such that

\beta_{\lambda}
contains at least an unshielded simple portion of
\mathbf{c}
, as mentioned in
Remark
1.25
. In particular, we can take
\mathrm{K}=0
if
\rho[\mathbf{T}]=0
.

12.
Variant of the main theorem for lc-surfaces

Our core motivation for proving Theorem
1.23
is the spectral gap problem, i.e. proving
that
\lambda_{1}
is close to
1/4
for typical hyperbolic surfaces of high genus. We have explained
the link between these questions, and presented the arguments leading to our spectral gap result
(Theorem
1.6
), in
[
4
]
. In the proof, we need to restrict the Weil–Petersson
measure to the set of tangle-free surfaces. The presence of an indicator function in the restricted
integral causes problems, that are overcome by the introduction of a so-called
Moebius
function
, constructed in
[
3
]
. The
\mathbf{T}
-averages
\langle F\rangle_{g}^{{\mathbf{T}}}
of a test
function
F
, defined in (
1.1
), are then replaced by expressions of the form

(12.1)

\displaystyle\langle\mu\otimes F\rangle_{g,Q}^{{\mathfrak{T}}}:=\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\Bigg[\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{\zeta}\in\mathcal{G}(X)\\
\tau\in\mathcal{S}(X)\\
(\mathbf{\zeta},\tau)\sim{\mathfrak{T}}\end{subarray}}\mu(\tau)F(\ell(\mathbf{\zeta}))\Bigg]

where:

•

\mu
is the Moebius function constructed in
[
3
]
, defined on the “extended moduli
space” of metric spaces made of disjoint unions of circles and hyperbolic surfaces with
boundary;

•

\mathbf{\zeta}
is a geodesic loop of length
\ell(\mathbf{\zeta})
;

•

\tau
is a subset of
X
with various connected components, which may be either surfaces with geodesic boundary, or non-contractible simple closed geodesics;

•

\mathfrak{T}
is now a local topological type of pairs
(\mathbf{\zeta},\tau)
.

In the following, a
test function

F
is a measurable function
F:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
which is bounded and compactly supported.

The precise definition of
\mu
and of the “extended moduli space” is given in
[
3
, §2]
and partially recalled below. We also recall the new notion of local topological type
adapted to pairs
(\mathbf{\zeta},\tau)
, and finally explain how to extend Theorem
1.23
to the
average (
12.1
). This provides the required technical generalization to complete
the proof of Theorem
1.6
.

12.1.
c-surfaces

Let us recall the notion of c-surface, developed in more detail in
[
3
, §2]
. In a nutshell, a
c-surface is the disjoint union of a surface and of simple curves.

Let
\mathbb{N}_{0}=\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
be the set of non-negative integers. We order the pairs
(g,n)\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{2}

by the order relation:
(g,n)\leq(g^{\prime},n^{\prime})
if
2g+n-2<2g^{\prime}+n^{\prime}-2
, or if
2g+n-2=2g^{\prime}+n^{\prime}-2
and

g\leq g^{\prime}
. For
j\geq 1
, denote by
{\mathfrak{S}}^{(j)}
the set of
2j
-tuples

({\bf g},{\bf n})=((g_{1},n_{1}),\ldots,(g_{j},n_{j}))\in(\mathbb{N}_{0}^{2})^{j}
, satisfying
(g_{i},n_{i})=(0,2)
or

2g_{i}-2+n_{i}>0
for
1\leq i\leq j
, and
(g_{i},n_{i})\leq(g_{i+1},n_{i+1})
for
1\leq i<j
.

Definition 12.1
.

A
c-surface of signature

({\bf g},{\bf n})\in{\mathfrak{S}}^{(j)}
is a non-empty topological space
\mathbf{S}
with
j
connected components

\mathbf{S}=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{j}\tau_{i}
labelled from
1
to
j
, where:

•

if
(g_{i},n_{i})=(0,2)
, then
\tau_{i}
is a
1
-dimensional oriented manifold
diffeomorphic to a circle;

•

if
2g_{i}+n_{i}-2>0
, then
\tau_{i}
is a compact
2
-dimensional orientable manifold with
(or without) boundary, of signature
(g_{i},n_{i})
, with boundary components numbered by

\{1,\ldots,n_{i}\}
.

Notation 12.2
.

The c-surface
\mathbf{S}
can be decomposed as
\mathbf{S}=(c,\sigma)
,
where
c=(c_{1},\ldots,c_{j_{1}})
and
\sigma=(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{j_{2}})
are,
respectively, collections of
1
-dimensional oriented manifolds and
2
-dimensional oriented
compact manifolds with boundary (
j=j_{1}+j_{2}
is the total number of connected components). We
shall denote by
\mathfrak{c}(\mathbf{S})=j_{1}
the number of 1d components, and
\chi(\mathbf{S})
the total
absolute Euler characteristic, that is

\chi(\mathbf{S})=\sum_{i=1}^{j_{2}}\chi(\sigma_{i})\quad\text{where}\quad\chi(\sigma_{i})=2g_{j_{1}+i}+n_{j_{1}+i}-2.

We say that
\mathbf{S}
is purely
2-dimensional if
\mathfrak{c}(\mathbf{S})=0
, and purely 1-dimensional if
\chi(\mathbf{S})=0
.

Notation 12.3

(Boundary of a c-surface)
.

For
\mathbf{S}=(\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{j})=(c_{1},\ldots,c_{j_{1}},\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{j_{2}})
, if

\partial\sigma_{i}
is the boundary of the surface
\sigma_{i}
,
we let
\partial\mathbf{S}
be the (usual) boundary of
\mathbf{S}

\partial\mathbf{S}=\Big(\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{j_{1}}c_{i}\Big)\sqcup\Big(\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{j_{2}}\partial\sigma_{i}\Big).

The total number of boundary components of
\sigma
is defined as

n_{\sigma}:=\sum_{i=1}^{j_{2}}n_{j_{1}+i}.

Definition 12.4

(Sub-c-surface)
.

If
\mathbf{S}
is a c-surface, we call

sub-c-surface
of
\mathbf{S}
a subset
\mathbf{S}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbf{S}
which (for the induced topology and
differential structure) is a c-surface, and such that
\partial\mathbf{S}^{\prime}
is a multi-curve
in
\mathbf{S}
, i.e. its components are non-contractible and homotopically distinct.

Notation 12.5

(Geodesic representative)
.

Let
\mathbf{S}
be a c-surface of signature

({\bf g},{\bf n})
and
\mathbf{S}^{\prime}=(c^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})
be a sub-c-surface of
\mathbf{S}
. Assume
\mathbf{S}
is endowed with
a metric
Z\in\mathcal{T}^{*}_{{\bf g},{\bf n}}
, the Teichmüller space of Riemannian metrics on
\mathbf{S}
that
are hyperbolic on the 2-dimensional components. Then
\mathbf{S}^{\prime}
is isotopic to a geodesic
sub-c-surface
\mathbf{S}^{\prime}_{Z}=(c^{\prime}_{Z},\sigma^{\prime}_{Z})
: we mean by this a sub-c-surface with geodesic boundary,
that we call the
geodesic representative
of
\mathbf{S}^{\prime}
in
Z
.

We denote by
\mathcal{S}(Z)
the set of geodesic sub-c-surfaces of
\mathbf{S}
endowed with the metric
Z
.

In
[
3
, §2]
, we also defined the extended moduli space
\mathbf{M}
, which is – roughly speaking – the moduli space of all possible Riemannian structures on all
possible c-surfaces, with hyperbolic metrics on the 2d-components. In the context of Definition
12.5
,
\mathbf{S}^{\prime}_{Z}
may be seen as a representative of an element of
\mathbf{M}
.

The following notation will also be useful.

Notation 12.6
.

For
\mathbf{S}
as in Notation
12.3
, if now
\tilde{\partial}c_{i}:=c_{i}\times\{\pm\}

(interpreted as the two boundary curves of a cylinder containing
c_{i}
), we let

\tilde{\partial}\mathbf{S}=\Big(\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{j_{1}}\tilde{\partial}c_{i}\Big)\sqcup\Big(\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{j_{2}}\partial\sigma_{i}\Big).

The components of
\tilde{\partial}\mathbf{S}
are labelled by the set

(\{1,\ldots,j_{1}\}\times\{\pm\})\sqcup\{(i,l),1\leq i\leq j_{2},1\leq l\leq n_{i}\}.

When needed, we can put a total lexicographic order on this set. We let

n_{\mathbf{S}}:=2j+n_{\sigma}
. Notice that, listing all the boundary components in the natural
order, we can also identify

\tilde{\partial}\mathbf{S}=\tilde{\partial}c\sqcup\{1,\ldots,n_{\sigma}\}=(\{1,\ldots,j_{1})\times\{\pm\})\sqcup\{1,\ldots,n_{\sigma}\}.

12.2.
lc-surfaces and their local topological types

We enrich the notion of local topological type, to a notion designed to sort out expressions such as
(
12.1
). The topological object to consider is now
(\mathbf{S},\mathbf{\zeta},\tau)
,
where
\mathbf{\zeta}
is a loop and
\tau
is a c-surface, which together fill
\mathbf{S}
. Such a pair will
be called a lc-surface. We define these objects in
[
2
, §A.1]
.

Notation 12.7
.

To any
({\bf g},{\bf n})\in{\mathfrak{S}}^{(j)}
,
j\geq 0
,
we associate a
fixed
smooth oriented c-surface
\mathbf{S}
of signature
({\bf g},{\bf n})
. The data
of
({\bf g},{\bf n})
, or equivalently of
\mathbf{S}
, is called a
c-filling type
.

Definition 12.8
.

A
local lc-surface
is a triplet
(\mathbf{S},\mathbf{\zeta},\tau)
, where
\mathbf{S}
is a c-filling type,

\mathbf{\zeta}
is a loop in
\mathbf{S}
,
\tau
is a sub-c-surface of
\mathbf{S}
, and the pair
(\mathbf{\zeta},\tau)
fills

\mathbf{S}
.

Two local lc-surfaces
(\mathbf{S},\mathbf{\zeta},\tau)
and
(\mathbf{S}^{\prime},\mathbf{\zeta}^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})
are said to be
locally
equivalent
if
\mathbf{S}=\mathbf{S}^{\prime}
(i.e.

(\mathbf{g}_{\mathbf{S}},\mathbf{n}_{\mathbf{S}})=(\mathbf{g}_{\mathbf{S}^{\prime}},\mathbf{n}_{\mathbf{S}^{\prime}})
), and there exists a
positive homeomorphism
\psi:\mathbf{S}\rightarrow\mathbf{S}
, possibly permuting the boundary components
and connected components of
\mathbf{S}
, such that
\psi\circ\mathbf{\zeta}
is freely homotopic to
\mathbf{\zeta}^{\prime}

and
\psi(\tau)=\tau^{\prime}
. We ask that the homotopy between
\psi\circ\mathbf{\zeta}
and
\mathbf{\zeta}^{\prime}
preserves
the orientation, and that
\psi
respects the numbering of boundary components of
\tau
and

\tau^{\prime}
.

This defines an equivalence relation
\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.32297pt\hskip-4.32297pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{loc}$}\hskip-4.32297pt\hskip-3.8889pt\raisebox{3.27222pt}{\hbox{$\sim$}}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip 4.32297pt}}
on local lc-surfaces. Equivalence classes for this
relation are denoted as
[\mathbf{S},\mathbf{\zeta},\tau]_{\mathrm{loc}}
and called
local topological types
of
lc-surfaces.

Notation 12.9
.

If
(\mathbf{S},\mathbf{\zeta},\tau)
is a local lc-surface, we will denote by

\mathbf{S}_{\partial}\subseteq\mathbf{S}
the c-surface filled by the multi-loop
(\mathbf{\zeta},\partial\tau)
, and
\mathbf{S}_{\mathrm{in}}

its complement in
\mathbf{S}
.

Necessarily, the 1d-components of
\tau
which lie in the 2d-components of
\mathbf{S}
must intersect

\mathbf{\zeta}
, otherwise
\tau\cup\mathbf{\zeta}
would not fill
\mathbf{S}
. As a consequence,
\mathbf{S}_{\partial}
contains the 1d
part of
\tau
, and
\mathbf{S}_{\mathrm{in}}
is contained in the 2d-part of
\tau
.

Definition 12.10
.

Let
\mathfrak{T}=[\mathbf{S},\mathbf{\zeta},\tau]_{\mathrm{loc}}
be a local topological type. For a pair

(\gamma,\tau_{0})
formed by a loop
\gamma
and a sub-c-surface
\tau_{0}
on a hyperbolic
surface
X
, we denote as
S(\gamma,\tau_{0})
the surface filled by
(\gamma,\tau_{0})
in
X

(defined in details in
[
2
, Definition 4.1]
). Then,
(\gamma,\tau_{0})
is said to

belong to the local topological type

[\mathbf{S},\mathbf{\zeta},\tau]_{\mathrm{loc}}
if there exists a positive
homeomorphism
\phi:S(\gamma,\tau_{0})\rightarrow\mathbf{S}
such that the loops
\phi(\gamma)

and
\mathbf{\zeta}
are freely homotopic in
\mathbf{S}
, and such that
\phi(\tau_{0})=\tau
. In that case, we
write
(\gamma,\tau_{0})\sim\mathfrak{T}
.

Notation 12.11
.

For any
j\geq 1
, there is an natural bijection from the set of c-surfaces with

\mathfrak{c}(\mathbf{S})=0
to the set of c-surfaces with
\mathfrak{c}(\mathbf{S})=j
, which consists in
adding
j
1-dimensional connected components: we denote it by
\rho_{j}
.

Similarly, if
{\mathfrak{T}}=[{{\mathbf{S},\mathbf{\zeta},\tau}}]_{\mathrm{loc}}
is a local topological type of lc-surface such
that
\mathfrak{c}(\mathbf{S})=0
, we denote by
\rho_{j}{\mathfrak{T}}
the local topological type obtained by
adding
j
1-dimensional connected components to the local topological type
\mathfrak{T}
. If
\mathfrak{T}

fills
\mathbf{S}
, then
\rho_{j}\mathfrak{T}
fills
\rho_{j}\mathbf{S}
.

12.3.
Volume functions for local topological types of lc-surfaces

We now study expressions such as (
12.1
) and state analogues of Theorem

1.4
. As explained in
[
4
]
, to prove our spectral gap result, Theorem

1.6
, we can restrict attention to subsurfaces of
X
that disconnect the hyperbolic
surface
X
into a bounded number of components. This is formalised as follows.

For an integer
Q
, we denote as
{\mathrm{MC}}_{X}(Q)
the set of multi-curves which separate
X
into at
most
Q
connected components. Let us define
\mathcal{S}_{Q}(X)\subset\mathcal{S}(X)
as the set of geodesic
sub-c-surfaces of
X
, with a 1d-part belonging to
{\mathrm{MC}}_{X}(Q)
. For any test function
F
, any bounded
measurable function
\nu
on the extended moduli space
\mathbf{M}
, we let

(12.2)

\displaystyle\langle\nu\otimes F\rangle_{g,Q}^{{\mathfrak{T}}}:=\mathbb{E}_{g}^{\mathrm{\scriptsize{WP}}}\Bigg[\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\mathcal{G}(X)\\
\tau\in\mathcal{S}_{Q}(X)\\
(\gamma,\tau)\sim{\mathfrak{T}}\end{subarray}}\nu(\tau)F(\ell(\gamma))\Bigg].

Notation 12.12
.

In the following, the function
\nu
on
\mathbf{M}
will be assumed to satisfy the following
properties for some positive real numbers
\kappa,R
with
\kappa<R
.

•

If
\tau=(c_{1},\ldots,c_{j_{1}},\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{j_{2}})
is such that

\nu(\tau)\not=0
, then

–

for
1\leq i\leq j_{1}
,
\ell(c_{i})\leq\kappa
, where
\ell(c_{i})
is the length of
c_{i}

for the metric
\tau
;

–

for
1\leq i\leq j_{2}
,
\ell(\partial\sigma_{i})\leq 9R\chi(\sigma_{i})
, where

\ell(\partial\sigma_{i})
is the total boundary length of
\sigma_{i}
.

•

There exists a bound of the form

(12.3)

|\nu(\tau)|\leq\mathfrak{f}(\mathfrak{c}(\tau),\chi(\tau)).

We assume that
\mathfrak{f}(j,\chi)\leq\mathfrak{f}(j^{\prime},\chi^{\prime})
if
j\geq j^{\prime}
and

\chi\leq\chi^{\prime}
.

Remark 12.13
.

Notice that the above conditions imply that, for any
\tau
in the support of
\nu
,

(12.4)

\ell(\partial\tau)\leq\kappa\mathfrak{c}(\tau)+9R\chi(\tau).

If we further assume that
\tau
is a sub-c-surface of an ambiant c-surface
\mathbf{S}
with

\mathfrak{c}(\mathbf{S})=0
, then
\ell(\partial\tau)\leq 12R\chi(\mathbf{S})
since the maximal
number of components for a multi-curve in
\mathbf{S}
is
3\chi(\mathbf{S})
.

Example 12.14
.

The hypotheses above are satisfied for
\nu=\mu_{\kappa,R}
, the Moebius function
constructed in
[
3
]
, with the bound

(12.5)

\mathfrak{f}(j,\chi):=\frac{U_{1}(\chi)}{j!}e^{RU_{2}(\chi)}

where
U_{1}
and
U_{2}
are increasing sequences (for which we do not need an explicit expression).

The following generalizes Proposition
1.2
, with exactly the same proof:

Proposition 12.15
.

Let
{\mathfrak{T}}=[{{\mathbf{S},\mathbf{\zeta},\tau}}]_{\mathrm{loc}}
be a local topological type of lc-surface with

\mathfrak{c}(\mathbf{S})=0
. For any
j\geq 0
, there exists a continuous functions

V_{g,Q}^{\nu,{\mathfrak{T}},j}
such that, for any test function
F
,

\displaystyle\langle\nu\otimes F\rangle_{g,Q}^{{\rho_{j}{\mathfrak{T}}}}=\int_{0}^{+\infty}F(\ell)V_{g,Q}^{\nu,{\mathfrak{T}},j}(\ell)\,\mathrm{d}\ell.

We are now ready to state the Friedman–Ramanujan property that we will prove.

Theorem 12.16
.

Let
{\mathfrak{T}}=[{{\mathbf{S},\mathbf{\zeta},\tau}}]_{\mathrm{loc}}
be a local topological type of lc-surface such that

\mathfrak{c}(\mathbf{S})=0
. For any integers
Q,N\geq 0
, any large enough
g
, any
j\leq\log g
, there exists a
Friedman–Ramanujan function
h_{g,Q,N}^{\nu,{\mathfrak{T}},j}
such that, for any test function

F
, any
\eta>0
,

\displaystyle\Big|\langle\nu\otimes F\rangle_{g,Q}^{{\rho_{j}{\mathfrak{T}}}}-\frac{1}{g^{\chi(\mathbf{S})}}\int_{0}^{+\infty}F(\ell)h_{g,Q,N}^{\nu,{\mathfrak{T}},j}(\ell)\,\mathrm{d}\ell\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{fn}(Q,N,\chi(\mathbf{S}),\eta)\,M^{j}\mathfrak{f}(j,\chi(\tau))e^{13R\chi(\mathbf{S})}\frac{\lVert F(\ell)\,e^{(1+\eta)\ell}\rVert_{\infty}}{g^{N+1}}

where
M=\mathrm{fn}(Q,N,n_{\mathbf{S}},\kappa)=2^{2(Q+n_{\mathbf{S}})}\tilde{a}_{N}^{2}(e^{\kappa}-1)
with the constant

\tilde{a}_{N}
from Theorem
A.1
. More precisely, there exists

\mathrm{K}=\mathrm{K}(N,\chi(\mathbf{S}))
and
\mathrm{N}=\mathrm{N}(N,\chi(\mathbf{S}))
such that

h_{g,Q,N}^{\nu,{\mathfrak{T}},j}\in\mathcal{F}_{w}^{\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N}}
, and

(12.6)

\lVert h_{g,Q,N}^{\nu,{\mathfrak{T}},j}\rVert^{w}_{\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N}}}\leq\mathrm{fn}(Q,N,\chi(\mathbf{S}))\,\tilde{a}_{N}^{2j}\mathfrak{f}(j,\chi(\tau))\,e^{6R\chi(\mathbf{S})}.

It is essential in this statement that the exponents and constants are independent from the number
of 1d components
j
and the specific topology of
(\mathbf{\zeta},\tau)
amongst pairs filling
\mathbf{S}
.

12.4.
Writing down the volume functions for lc-local types

Let us now sketch the proof of Theorem
12.16
, which is very similar to the proof of
Theorem
1.23
.

As before, we start by writing down an explicit expression for the volume function

V_{g,Q}^{\nu,{\mathfrak{T}},j}
. We then use the asymptotic expansion from
[
1
, Theorem
1.1]
to obtain an approximate expression for this volume function, up to errors
decaying like
1/g^{N+1}
. The Friedman–Ramanujan property follows from a variant of Theorem

1.23
. The main new difficulty here is to control the dependency of the estimates on the
integer
j
, the number of 1d-components of our lc-local-type (as we will need to understand this
dependency well for the spectral gap result).

12.4.1.
Realizations of a lc-local type

In order to write down the volume functions
V_{g,Q}^{\nu,{\mathfrak{T}},j}
with a formula similar to
(
1.9
), we need an enumeration of the possible embeddings of the c-filling type
\mathbf{S}

into a surface
S_{g}
of genus
g
. We therefore extend from
[
2
, §4.5]
the notion of
realization of a filling type into
S_{g}
.

For the sake of convenience, we treat separately purely 2d and 1d c-filling types, before merging
the two definitions. Recall from Notation
12.3
that we can put an order on the labels of
the connected components of
\partial\mathbf{S}
.

Definition 12.17
.

Let
\mathbf{S}=(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{j})
be a purely 2-dimensional c-filling type, and let

g\geq 2
. We call
realization of
\mathbf{S}
in a connected surface
S_{g}
of genus
g

any pair
\mathfrak{R}=(\vec{I},\vec{g})
, where:

•

\vec{I}
is a partition of
\partial\mathbf{S}
into
\mathfrak{q}\geq 1
non-empty sets

I_{1},\ldots,I_{\mathfrak{q}}
, numbered such that
i\mapsto\min I_{i}
is an increasing
function;

•

we ask furthermore the partition to be
transitive
in the sense given below;

•

\vec{g}=(g_{1},\ldots,g_{\mathfrak{q}})
is a vector of non-negative integers;

•

if for any
1\leq i\leq\mathfrak{q}
, we denote
n_{i}:=\#I_{i}
and

\chi_{i}:=2g_{i}-2+n_{i}
, then
\chi_{i}>0
or
(g_{i},n_{i})=(0,2)
, and we have:

(12.7)

\chi(\mathbf{S})+\sum_{i=1}^{\mathfrak{q}}\chi_{i}=2g-2.

The set of realizations of
\mathbf{S}
in
S_{g}
is denoted as
R_{g}(\mathbf{S})
.

Recall that
\partial\mathbf{S}
comes naturally partitioned into

\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{j}\partial\sigma_{i}
. The partition
\vec{I}
being
transitive
means that
there does not exist a strict non-empty subset of
\partial\mathbf{S}
that can be realized simultanously
as a union of sets
(\partial\sigma_{i})_{1\leq i\leq j}
and as a union of sets

(I_{i})_{1\leq i\leq\mathfrak{q}}
.

Realizations are meant to enumerate all possible embeddings of a local type in a closed surface of
genus
g
(i.e. absolute Euler characteristic
2g-2
), modulo the action of the mapping class group
(
[
2
, §4.5]
). The transitivity assumption guarantees that the resulting surface is

connected
.

For a purely 1d c-filling type, i.e. a multi-curve, we have a similar definition for realizations of
a multicurve in a connected surface
S_{g,n}
of signature
(g,n)
.

Definition 12.18
.

Let
c=(c_{1},\ldots,c_{j})
be a purely 1-dimensional c-filling type. Let
(g,n)\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{2}
be
such that
2g-2+n>0
or
(g,n)=(0,2)
. We call
realization of
c
in a connected surface

S_{g,n}
of signature
(g,n)
any pair
\mathfrak{R}=(\vec{J},\vec{g})
, where:

•

\vec{J}
is a partition of
\{1,\ldots,n\}\sqcup\tilde{\partial}c
into

\mathfrak{q}\geq 1
non-empty sets
J_{1},\ldots,J_{\mathfrak{q}}
, numbered so that

i\mapsto\min J_{i}
is an increasing function;

•

we ask furthermore the partition to be
transitive
in the sense given below;

•

\vec{g}=(g_{1},\ldots,g_{\mathfrak{q}})
is a vector of non-negative integers;

•

if for any
1\leq i\leq\mathfrak{q}
, we denote
n_{i}:=\#J_{i}
and

\chi_{i}:=2g_{i}-2+n_{i}
, then
\chi_{i}>0
or
(g_{i},n_{i})=(0,2)
, and in the latter case,
J_{i}

must contain exactly one element of
\{1,\ldots,n\}
and one element of
\tilde{\partial}c
;

•

we have:

(12.8)

\sum_{i=1}^{\mathfrak{q}}\chi_{i}=2g-2+n.

The set of realizations of
(c_{1},\ldots,c_{j})
in
S_{g,n}
is denoted as

R^{1}_{g,n}(j)
.

We recall that here
\tilde{\partial}c=\{1,\ldots,j\}\times\{\pm\}
is the set of boundary
curves of the cylinders containing the multi-curve
c
.
Note that the superscript
1
refers to the fact that we consider realizations of 1d curves. If the
curves, instead of being numbered by
\{1,\ldots,j\}
, are numbered by an abstract set
B
of
cardinality
j
, we write
R^{1}_{g,n}(B)
. Similarly, if the boundary components of
S_{g,n}
are
indexed by a set
I
of cardinality
n
, we denote
S_{g,I}
and
R^{1}_{g,I}(B)
.

Realizations are meant to enumerate (modulo the action of the mapping class group) all possible
embeddings of a multi-curve with
j
components into a surface of signature
(g,n)
. In the fourth
point of the definition, we impose that two curves cannot be in the same homotopy class, but we
authorize the case where one of the curves is homotopic to a boundary component. In particular, if

(g,n)=(0,2)
we must have
j\leq 1
.

Because we are only interested in
connected
surfaces
S_{g,n}
, we again impose a
transitivity condition, defined as follows. Remark that

\{1,\ldots,n\}\sqcup\tilde{\partial}c
comes naturally partitioned into

\Big(\bigsqcup_{k=1}^{n}\{k\}\Big)\sqcup\Big(\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{j}(\{i\}\times\{\pm\})\Big).

The partition
\vec{J}
being
transitive
means that there does not exist a strict non-empty
subset of
\{1,\ldots,n\}\sqcup\tilde{\partial}c
that can be realized simultanously as a union
of sets
\{k\}
and
\{i\}\times\{\pm\}
as above, and as a union of sets
J_{i}
with

1\leq i\leq\mathfrak{q}
.

Remark that the definition makes sense for
j=0
, in this case we must have
\mathfrak{q}=1
.

The definitions above can be extended for realizations of an arbitrary c-filling types

\mathbf{S}=(c,\sigma)
(having 1d and 2d components
c
and
\sigma
) into a connected surface
S_{g}
. For
the purpose of controlling the dependency of volume functions on
j
, we opt for the following
formulation in two steps: we first realize the 2d part
\sigma
into
S_{g}
, and in a second step
we realize
c
in
S_{g}\setminus\sigma
.

Definition 12.19
.

Let
\mathbf{S}=(c,\sigma)
be a general c-filling type, separated into its 1d and 2d components

c=(c_{1},\ldots,c_{j})
and
\sigma
. Let
g\geq 2
. We call
realization of
\mathbf{S}
in a
connected surface
S_{g}
of genus
g
the data of

\mathfrak{R}=(\mathfrak{R}_{\sigma},\pi,(\mathfrak{R}_{l})_{1\leq l\leq\mathfrak{q}})
, where

•

\mathfrak{R}_{\sigma}=(\vec{I},\vec{g})=(I_{1},\ldots,I_{\mathfrak{q}},g_{1},\ldots,g_{\mathfrak{q}})
is a realization of
\sigma
in
S_{g}
with
\mathfrak{q}
components;

•

\pi
is a partition of
\{1,\ldots,j\}
into (possibly empty) sets

\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{\mathfrak{q}}
;

•

for each
1\leq l\leq\mathfrak{q}
,

\mathfrak{R}_{l}=(\vec{J}_{l},\vec{g}_{l})\in R^{1}_{g_{l},I_{l}}(\pi_{l})
is a realization of
(c_{i})_{i\in\pi_{l}}

in a connected surface
S_{l}=S_{g_{l},n_{l}}
of signature
(g_{l},n_{l})
, where
n_{l}=\#I_{l}
.

The set of realizations of
\mathbf{S}
in
S_{g}
is denoted as
R_{g}(\mathbf{S})
.

For such a realization, the integer
\mathfrak{q}
corresponds to the number of connected components
of
S_{g}\setminus\sigma
. If those components are called
S_{1},\ldots,S_{\mathfrak{q}}
, then
S_{l}

has
n_{l}
boundary components indexed by
I_{l}
. The set
\pi_{l}
corresponds to the curves in

(c_{1},\ldots,c_{j})
that lie inside the component
S_{l}
.

For
1\leq l\leq\mathfrak{q}
, we denote
\vec{J}_{l}=(J_{l}^{1},\ldots,J_{l}^{{\mathfrak{q}}_{l}})
,
i.e. we let
\mathfrak{q}_{l}
be the number of connected components of the surface

S_{l}\setminus(\bigcup_{i\in\pi_{l}}c_{i})
(some of those components may be peripheral annuli). Then

(J_{l}^{i})_{1\leq i\leq{\mathfrak{q}}_{l}}
forms a partition of

I_{l}\sqcup(\pi_{l}\times\{\pm\})
.

The collection
(J_{l}^{i})_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq l\leq{\mathfrak{q}},1\leq i\leq{\mathfrak{q}}_{l}\end{subarray}}

forms a partition of
\tilde{\partial}\mathbf{S}=\{1,\ldots,n_{\sigma}\}\sqcup\tilde{\partial}c
. Letting
n_{l}^{i}=\#J_{l}^{i}
, we have

\sum_{i=1}^{{\mathfrak{q}}_{l}}n_{l}^{i}=n_{l}+2|\pi_{l}|\quad\text{and}\quad\sum_{l=1}^{\mathfrak{q}}\sum_{i=1}^{{\mathfrak{q}}_{l}}n_{l}^{i}=n_{\sigma}+2j=n_{\mathbf{S}}.

Notation 12.20
.

With the notations of Definition
12.19
, for
Q\geq 0
an integer, we denote by

R_{g,Q}(\mathbf{S})
the set of realizations in
R_{g}(\mathbf{S})
for which

c\in\mathrm{MC}_{S_{g}}(Q)
, that is to say, the 1d part
c
disconnects
S_{g}
into at most
Q
connected
components.

Remark that the number of connected components of
S_{g}\setminus\mathbf{S}
coincides with

\sum_{l=1}^{\mathfrak{q}}{\mathfrak{q}}_{l}
. For a realization in
R_{g,Q}(\mathbf{S})
, by
definition, the surface
S_{g}\setminus c
has at most
Q
connected components. Each
connected component of
S_{g}\setminus\mathbf{S}
is either a connected component of
S_{g}\setminus c
,
or shares a boundary with one boundary component of
\sigma
. Hence,

\sum_{l=1}^{\mathfrak{q}}{\mathfrak{q}}_{l}\leq Q+n_{\sigma}.

12.4.2.
Volume function associated to a realization

For a realization
\mathfrak{R}\in R_{g}(\mathbf{S})
, we define the volume function
V_{\mathfrak{R}}(\mathbf{z})
,
which is a function of
n_{\mathbf{S}}=n_{\sigma}+2j
variables

\mathbf{z}=(z_{t})_{t\in\tilde{\partial}\mathbf{S}}
defined by

\displaystyle V_{\mathfrak{R}}(\mathbf{z}):=\prod_{l=1}^{{\mathfrak{q}}}\prod_{i=1}^{{\mathfrak{q}}_{l}}V_{g_{l}^{i},n_{l}^{i}}(\mathbf{z}_{J_{l}^{i}})\quad\text{where}\quad\mathbf{z}_{J_{l}^{i}}=(z_{t},t\in J_{l}^{i}),

where we recall that for any
(g,n)
with
2g-2+n>0
,
V_{g,n}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
is the total
volume of the moduli space of surfaces of signature
(g,n)
with boundary lengths

x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
, and

V_{0,2}(x_{1},x_{2})=\frac{1}{x_{1}}\delta(x_{1}-x_{2})

where
\delta
denotes the Dirac mass at
0
.

For
1\leq l\leq{\mathfrak{q}}
, let

J_{l}^{\mathrm{cyl}}:=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq{\mathfrak{q}}_{l}\\
\chi_{l}^{i}=0\end{subarray}}J_{l}^{i},

where
\chi_{l}^{i}=2g_{n}^{i}-2+n_{l}^{i}\geq 0
. We then define

J^{\mathrm{cyl}}:=\bigcup_{l=1}^{{\mathfrak{q}}}J_{l}^{\mathrm{cyl}}
. This corresponds to the set of
boundary components of
\mathbf{S}
that are glued to one another by cylinders in the
realization.

12.4.3.
Upper bound on volume functions

Let us prove a rough upper bound on the volume functions.

Lemma 12.21
.

For a fixed partition
(\vec{J}_{l})_{1\leq l\leq\mathfrak{q}}
of
\tilde{\partial}{\mathbf{S}}
as
above, the sum over all corresponding realizations possessing this datum satisfies, for a
universal constant
C
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{V_{g}}\sum_{\mathfrak{R}}\prod_{t\in\tilde{\partial}\mathbf{S}}z_{t}\,\,V_{\mathfrak{R}}(\mathbf{z})\leq\frac{C}{g^{\chi(\mathbf{S})}}\prod_{t\in\tilde{\partial}\mathbf{S}\setminus J^{\mathrm{cyl}}}2\sinh\left(\frac{z_{t}}{2}\right)\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\chi_{l}^{i}=0\\
J_{l}^{i}=\{t,t^{\prime}\}\end{subarray}}z_{t}\delta(z_{t}-z_{t^{\prime}})

to be interpreted as an inequality between two positive measures. The number of possible data
sets
(\vec{J}_{l})_{1\leq l\leq\mathfrak{q}}
is at most
2^{n_{\mathbf{S}}(Q+n_{\sigma})}
.

Proof.

The choice of
(\vec{J}_{l})_{1\leq l\leq\mathfrak{q}}
automatically fixes
{\mathfrak{q}}
,

\vec{I}
,
\pi
and
J^{\mathrm{cyl}}
. This reduces to a sum over all possible values of

g_{1},\ldots,g_{\mathfrak{q}}
and
\vec{g}_{l}
. This sum is controlled by
[
13
, Lemma
24]
:

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathfrak{R}}\prod_{t\in\tilde{\partial}\mathbf{S}}z_{t}\,\,V_{\mathfrak{R}}(\mathbf{z})\leq\prod_{l=1}^{{\mathfrak{q}}}W_{\chi_{l}}\prod_{t\in\tilde{\partial}\mathbf{S}\setminus J^{\mathrm{cyl}}}2\sinh\left(\frac{z_{t}}{2}\right)\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\chi_{l}^{i}=0\\
J_{l}^{i}=\{t,t^{\prime}\}\end{subarray}}z_{t}\delta(z_{t}-z_{t^{\prime}})

where, for
\chi>0
,

W_{\chi}=\begin{cases}V_{\frac{\chi}{2}+1,0}&\text{if }\chi\text{ is even};\\
V_{\frac{\chi+1}{2},1}&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

The product
\prod_{l=1}^{{\mathfrak{q}}}W_{\chi_{l}}
itself is bounded by

\mathcal{O}(V_{g}/g^{\chi(\mathbf{S})})
. Then,
2^{(n_{\sigma}+2j)(Q+n_{\sigma})}
is an upper bound on the number
of partitions of a set with
n_{\mathbf{S}}=n_{\sigma}+2j
elements into at most
Q+n_{\sigma}
subsets.
∎

Remark 12.22
.

If we want to further impose that
\mathbf{S}=(c,\sigma)
is realised as a sub-c-surface, we have to impose that
\chi_{j}>0
and
\chi_{l}^{i}>0
in Definitions
12.18
and
12.19
.
It is also possible to impose this restriction only on some elements
J_{j}
or
J_{l}^{i}
of the
partitions; that is to say, forbid certain components to be cylinders. Obviously, imposing
restrictions on the set of allowed realizations does not modify the following discussion.

12.5.
Proof of our main results

We now turn to the proof of the main results of this section, Proposition
12.15
and
Theorem
12.16
. We omit technical details that are similar to earlier
considerations: we focus on the main new difficulty, which is to control the dependency on the
integer
j
. This requires to go back to our previous work
[
1
]
, following the
dependency of the estimates in the number of boundary components. We refer to §
A

for these estimates.

Remark 12.23
.

At some point it seems necessary to assume some upper bound, say
j\ll g^{1/2}
. It is not clear
if this is strictly necessary, or only a consequence of our imperfect optimisation of the
estimates. In any case, the application to spectral gap results only requires
j\leq\log g
.

12.5.1.
Expression of the volume function

Let
{\mathfrak{T}}=[{{\mathbf{S},\mathbf{\zeta},\tau}}]_{\mathrm{loc}}
be a local topological type of lc-surface such that

\mathfrak{c}(\mathbf{S})=0
(thus,
n_{\sigma}=n_{\mathbf{S}}
). Recall that
\rho_{j}\mathfrak{T}
stands for the local
topological type obtained by adding
j
1-dimensional connected components to
\mathfrak{T}
. If
\mathfrak{T}

fills the c-surface
\mathbf{S}
, then
\rho_{j}\mathfrak{T}
fills the c-surface
\rho_{j}\mathbf{S}
and the decomposition
of
\rho_{j}\mathbf{S}
into 1d and 2d-components can be written as
\rho_{j}\mathbf{S}=(c_{1},\ldots,c_{j},\mathbf{S})
.

We now straightforwardly apply the method developed in
[
2
, Thm. 5.5 and Prop. 5.21]
to
establish Proposition
12.15
, and see that the volume function

V_{g,Q}^{\nu,{\mathfrak{T}},j}(\ell)
has the form

(12.9)

\displaystyle\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\vec{\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{j}\end{subarray}}\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{n_{\mathbf{S}}}\end{subarray}}\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}Y\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{S}|\mathbf{S}_{\partial})\\
\ell_{Y}({\mathbf{\zeta}})=\ell\end{subarray}}\phi(\mathbf{x},\vec{\alpha})\,\nu(\tau_{(\mathbf{x},Y)},\vec{\alpha})\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}^{\mathrm{WP}}_{g_{\mathbf{S}},n_{\mathbf{S}}}(\mathbf{x},Y)}{\,\mathrm{d}\ell}\,\mathrm{d}\vec{\alpha},

where, for
\vec{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{j})\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{j}
and
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{n_{\mathbf{S}}}
,

\phi(\mathbf{x},\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{j})=\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}\ldots\alpha_{j}}\Phi_{g,Q}^{\mathfrak{T},j}(\mathbf{x},\alpha_{1},\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{j},\alpha_{j})

and
\Phi_{g,Q}^{\mathfrak{T},j}
is defined by

(12.10)

\displaystyle\Phi_{g,Q}^{\mathfrak{T},j}(\mathbf{z}):=\frac{1}{m(\mathfrak{T})}\frac{1}{V_{g}}\prod_{t\in\tilde{\partial}\mathbf{S}}z_{t}\,\sum_{\mathfrak{R}\in R_{g,Q}(\rho_{j}\mathbf{S})}V_{\mathfrak{R}}(\mathbf{z}).

Recall from Notation
12.5
that for
Z=(\mathbf{x},Y)
,
\tau_{Z}\in\mathcal{S}(Z)
is the geodesic
representative of
\tau
for the metric
Z
. We should actually restrict the sum to realizations

\mathfrak{R}
such that
(c_{1},\ldots,c_{j},\tau)
stands as a sub-c-surface in
S_{g}
(see Remark

12.22
).

Careful attention needs to be paid to the domain of integration
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{S}|\mathbf{S}_{\partial})
.
Recall from Notation
12.9
that
\mathbf{S}=\mathbf{S}_{\partial}\sqcup\mathbf{S}_{\mathrm{in}}
where
\mathbf{S}_{\partial}
is filled by
\mathbf{\zeta}
and

\partial\tau
. By hypothesis, the function
Y\mapsto\nu(\tau_{(\mathbf{x},Y)},\vec{\alpha})
is invariant
by the mapping class group of
\tau
. Thus the pair

(\ell_{Y}(\mathbf{\zeta}),\nu(\tau_{(\mathbf{x},Y)},\vec{\alpha}))
, initially defined on the Teichmüller space

\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{S})
, is invariant by
\mathrm{MCG}(\mathbf{S}_{\mathrm{in}})
. It follows that the integral takes place on

\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{S}|\mathbf{S}_{\partial})
, defined as the quotient of
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{S})
by
\mathrm{MCG}(\mathbf{S}_{\mathrm{in}})
and the
Dehn twists along the shared boundary components of
\mathbf{S}_{\partial}
and
\mathbf{S}_{\mathrm{in}}
. The
combinatorial factor
m(\mathfrak{T})
is the number of diffeomorphisms of
\mathbf{S}_{\partial}
stabilising

(\mathbf{\zeta},\partial\tau)
, modulo homotopy.

12.5.2.
Rough upper bounds on the volume function associated to a type

Let us prove the following rough upper bound.

Lemma 12.24
.

Let
\mathfrak{T}=[\mathbf{S},\mathbf{\zeta},\tau]_{\mathrm{loc}}
be a local topological type of lc-surface such that

\mathfrak{c}(\mathbf{S})=0
. For any large enough
g
, any
j,Q\geq 0
, any test function
F
, any

0<\eta\leq 1/9
,

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}}F(\ell)V_{g,Q}^{\nu,\mathfrak{T},j}(\ell)\,\mathrm{d}\ell\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{fn}(\chi(\mathbf{S}),\eta)2^{(n_{\mathbf{S}}+2j)(Q+n_{\mathbf{S}})}(e^{\kappa}-1)^{j}\mathfrak{f}(j,\chi(\tau))e^{13R\chi(\mathbf{S})}\frac{\lVert F(\ell)\,e^{(1+\eta)\ell}\rVert_{\infty}}{g^{\chi(\mathbf{S})}}.

Proof.

We use the formula for the volume function and the upper bound from
Lemma
12.21
. In order to control the exponential terms, we note that, in each term
appearing with associated lengths
(z_{t})_{t\in\tilde{\partial}\mathbf{S}}
, if

\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n_{\mathbf{S}}})
is the vector of boundary lengths of the surface
\mathbf{S}
filled by

(\mathbf{\zeta},\tau)
and
\vec{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{j})
the vector of the 1d-component,
then

(12.11)

\frac{1}{2}\sum_{t\in\tilde{\partial}\mathbf{S}}z_{t}=\sum_{j=1}^{j}\alpha_{j}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\mathbf{S}}}x_{i}.

By a straightfoward adaptation from Proposition

1.30
, for every hyperbolic metric
Y
on
\mathbf{S}
,

(12.12)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\mathbf{S}}}x_{i}\leq\ell_{Y}(\mathbf{\zeta})+\ell_{Y}(\partial\tau)\leq\ell_{Y}(\mathbf{\zeta})+12R\chi(\mathbf{S})

on the support of
